CCEP Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (The inward rectifier potassium channel, IK1, active during diastole.): IK1 has high open probability at negative potentials and dominates ventricular resting membrane conductance, holding it close to the potassium equilibrium potential. INa is closed at rest and only activates transiently during phase 0. ICaL contributes to the plateau, not the resting potential. If is essentially absent in working ventricular myocytes and instead drives diastolic depolarization in nodal pacemaker cells.
  2. The correct answer is C (The fast voltage-gated sodium channel, Nav1.5, encoded by SCN5A.): Nav1.5 opens within a fraction of a millisecond once threshold is reached, producing the large inward INa that generates the steep phase 0 upstroke and rapid conduction velocity in Purkinje and working myocardial fibers. The L-type calcium channel activates more slowly and underlies the plateau rather than the upstroke. Potassium channels carry outward current and cannot produce a rapid inward depolarizing current. The funny current is far too small and slow to account for a 200 V/s upstroke.
  3. The correct answer is B (The hyperpolarization-activated funny current, If, carried by HCN channels.): If activates on hyperpolarization during phase 4 and produces a slow inward sodium/potassium current that drives diastolic depolarization toward threshold in sinoatrial pacemaker cells. Ivabradine selectively inhibits HCN channels, slowing the rate of diastolic depolarization and reducing heart rate without affecting myocardial contractility. INa underlies the fast upstroke rather than pacemaking. IKr and ICaL contribute to repolarization and the plateau, respectively, not to funny-current-driven automaticity.
  4. The correct answer is D (The rapid delayed rectifier potassium current, IKr, encoded by KCNH2.): IKr, carried by the hERG channel encoded by KCNH2, is a major driver of phase 3 repolarization, and many macrolides and other QT-prolonging drugs block this channel, delaying repolarization and prolonging the QT interval. Ito shapes the early notch in phase 1, not the terminal repolarization phase. IK1 stabilizes the resting potential during diastole rather than driving active repolarization. ICaT contributes to nodal pacemaking and is not the target of drug-induced acquired long QT syndrome.
  5. The correct answer is B (Slowed conduction velocity from reduced intercellular electrical coupling.): Connexin 43 forms the gap junction channels that electrically couple adjacent ventricular myocytes, and reduced expression lowers intercellular conductance, slowing impulse propagation and increasing susceptibility to reentry. This effect is independent of sodium channel function, so upstroke velocity within a single cell is not directly increased. Refractory period is a property of channel gating kinetics, not intercellular coupling. Resting membrane potential is set by potassium conductance, not by connexin expression.
  6. The correct answer is A (Near the apex, close to the central fibrous body.): The compact AV node sits near the apex of the triangle of Koch, adjacent to the central fibrous body, just before the conduction system penetrates to form the bundle of His. The coronary sinus ostium and the tricuspid annulus form the base of the triangle, an area used for slow pathway ablation rather than the node itself. The tendon of Todaro is a border of the triangle, not a location outside it. The AV node is a right-sided structure and is not adjacent to the mitral annulus.
  7. The correct answer is D (The central fibrous body, separating atrial and ventricular myocardium.): The His bundle penetrates the central fibrous body, the only normal electrical connection between atria and ventricles, before descending along the membranous septum and dividing into bundle branches. The crista terminalis is a right atrial ridge unrelated to the conduction system's ventricular course. The moderator band carries the right bundle branch distally but is not where the His bundle penetrates. The cavotricuspid isthmus is the substrate for typical atrial flutter, not a conduction pathway for the His bundle.
  8. The correct answer is C (The left anterior fascicle and the left posterior fascicle.): The left bundle branch divides into an anterior fascicle, which is thinner and more susceptible to block, and a posterior fascicle, which is thicker with dual blood supply, together producing the characteristic axis shifts seen with fascicular block. The right bundle branch is a separate structure arising directly from the bundle of His, not a subdivision of the left bundle. Neither the crista terminalis nor the sinoatrial or atrioventricular nodes are fascicles of the ventricular conduction system.
  9. The correct answer is A (Rapid, near-simultaneous distribution of the impulse to ventricular myocardium.): The Purkinje network consists of specialized fast-conducting fibers that spread the impulse widely and nearly simultaneously across the subendocardium, producing coordinated ventricular contraction and the multiple early breakthrough sites seen on mapping. The dominant pacemaker function belongs to the sinoatrial node, not the Purkinje system. Decremental, rate-protective conduction is a property of the AV node, which the Purkinje fibers lie distal to. The Purkinje network is a ventricular structure and does not couple atrial myocytes.
  10. The correct answer is B (Myocardial sleeves, atrial muscle extending several centimeters into the vein.): Pulmonary vein myocardial sleeves contain atrial muscle fibers with abnormal automaticity and short refractory periods that commonly initiate atrial fibrillation, making them the primary target of pulmonary vein isolation. The ligament of Marshall is a separate epicardial structure near the left atrial appendage, not intravenous myocardium. The crista terminalis lies in the right atrium and is unrelated to the pulmonary veins. The septum secundum contributes to the interatrial septum, not the pulmonary venous myocardium.
  11. The correct answer is D (The tricuspid valve annulus and the inferior vena cava.): The cavotricuspid isthmus is the region of right atrial tissue bounded by the tricuspid valve annulus anteriorly and the inferior vena cava posteriorly, and it forms the critical zone of slow conduction in typical atrial flutter, making it the ablation target. The fossa ovalis and coronary sinus ostium are nearby landmarks but do not themselves define the isthmus borders. The crista terminalis and sinoatrial node lie elsewhere in the right atrium. The left atrial appendage and mitral annulus are left-sided structures unrelated to typical flutter.
  12. The correct answer is C (The right atrium, with its ostium near the tricuspid annulus.): The coronary sinus drains most of the cardiac venous return into the right atrium, and its ostium sits near the posteroseptal tricuspid annulus, close to the triangle of Koch, which is why coronary sinus cannulation is performed from the right atrium. It does not open into the left atrium, left ventricle, or right ventricle. This anatomy allows a lead advanced from the right atrium through the coronary sinus to reach epicardial branch veins overlying the left ventricle for resynchronization pacing.
  13. The correct answer is B (The L-type calcium current, ICaL, sustaining inward charge movement.): ICaL activates more slowly than INa and inactivates gradually, providing a sustained inward current that balances outward potassium currents to produce the characteristic plateau of the ventricular action potential. INa has already inactivated by this point and no longer contributes meaningful current. Ito and IK1 are outward potassium currents that oppose, rather than sustain, the plateau. The calcium influx during this phase is also essential for triggering excitation-contraction coupling.
  14. The correct answer is A (Phase 4, driven mainly by the funny current, If.): In pacemaker cells, phase 4 lacks a stable resting potential and instead shows spontaneous diastolic depolarization, driven substantially by If along with contributions from T-type calcium current and declining potassium conductance, carrying the membrane toward threshold. Phase 0 refers to the upstroke itself, which in nodal tissue is calcium-dependent rather than sodium-dependent. Phase 2 is the plateau seen in working myocardium, not present in typical nodal action potentials. Phase 1 represents early rapid repolarization in fast-response tissue, unrelated to diastolic depolarization.
  15. The correct answer is D (The slow delayed rectifier current, IKs, needed for sympathetic-driven repolarization.): KCNQ1 encodes the pore-forming subunit of the channel carrying IKs, which is critical for shortening repolarization during sympathetic stimulation such as swimming or exercise, and its loss produces LQT1, the subtype most associated with exertion-triggered and swimming-triggered cardiac events. INa mutations causing delayed inactivation define LQT3, not LQT1. IKr is encoded by KCNH2 and its loss defines LQT2, often triggered by auditory stimuli. ICaT is a nodal automaticity current unrelated to this repolarization reserve mechanism.
  16. The correct answer is C (IKr, the rapid delayed rectifier potassium current encoded by KCNH2/hERG.): KCNH2 encodes the hERG channel carrying IKr, and LQT2 caused by its loss of function is classically associated with syncope triggered by sudden auditory stimuli such as alarms or telephones ringing. IKs loss defines LQT1, more strongly linked to exercise and swimming triggers. INa gain-of-function mutations define LQT3, associated with events during sleep or rest. If is a pacemaker current unrelated to ventricular repolarization or long QT syndrome.
  17. The correct answer is A (Phase 2, from persistent late sodium current failing to inactivate.): LQT3 results from SCN5A gain-of-function mutations that impair fast inactivation, allowing a persistent late sodium current to continue flowing during the plateau, prolonging the action potential and QT interval, with events characteristically occurring during sleep or bradycardia when the QT is already relatively longer. Phase 0 upstroke velocity is not the mechanism of QT prolongation in this syndrome. Reduced Ito would shorten rather than prolong repolarization. Ventricular myocytes lack meaningful If, so enhanced funny current is not the mechanism.
  18. The correct answer is B (The fast inward sodium current, INa, in right ventricular tissue.): Loss-of-function SCN5A mutations reduce INa, and because the right ventricular outflow tract already has relatively lower sodium channel density and a prominent transient outward current, this reduction unmasks a transmural voltage gradient that produces the coved ST elevation and predisposes to ventricular fibrillation. IKs abnormalities relate to long QT syndrome, not Brugada syndrome. ICaT in the sinoatrial node concerns pacemaking, not the right ventricular repolarization gradient. If in the AV node is unrelated to this ventricular channelopathy.
  19. The correct answer is C (The sarcoplasmic reticulum ryanodine receptor, causing diastolic calcium leak.): RYR2 encodes the cardiac ryanodine receptor, and mutations cause abnormal diastolic calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum during catecholamine surges, triggering delayed afterdepolarizations that produce catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia, classically bidirectional and exercise-induced with a normal baseline QT. This mechanism does not involve sodium channel inactivation kinetics. hERG dysfunction underlies long QT syndrome, which presents with QT prolongation, unlike CPVT. The L-type calcium channel is not the primary mutated structure in this condition.
  20. The correct answer is D (Phase 2 or phase 3, during prolonged repolarization itself.): Early afterdepolarizations occur when repolarization is markedly delayed, allowing L-type calcium channels to recover from inactivation and reopen during the plateau or late repolarization, producing a secondary depolarization that can trigger torsades de pointes in the setting of QT prolongation from drugs such as amiodarone. Phase 4 afterdepolarizations are termed delayed, not early, afterdepolarizations. Phase 0 is too brief and too early for the calcium recovery required. Phase 1's notch is not the site of afterdepolarization generation in this mechanism.
  21. The correct answer is B (A delayed afterdepolarization occurring after full repolarization, phase 4.): Digoxin toxicity causes intracellular calcium overload, leading to spontaneous sarcoplasmic reticulum calcium release after the membrane has already fully repolarized, activating the sodium-calcium exchanger and producing a delayed afterdepolarization during phase 4 that can trigger triggered arrhythmias including bidirectional ventricular tachycardia. Early afterdepolarizations occur before full repolarization is complete, not after it. This mechanism is triggered activity, not fixed-circuit reentry, so it lacks a stable anatomic isthmus. It also does not represent enhanced normal automaticity from the sinus node.
  22. The correct answer is A (Overdrive suppression from increased sodium-potassium pump activity during rapid pacing.): Rapid pacing increases intracellular sodium loading in pacemaker cells, which stimulates the sodium-potassium ATPase pump, hyperpolarizing the cell and transiently suppressing spontaneous phase 4 depolarization once pacing stops, producing overdrive suppression and a delayed return of sinus node automaticity. Concealed conduction describes partial impulse penetration affecting subsequent conduction, not automaticity suppression. This phenomenon is a property of automaticity, not a reentrant circuit. It reflects pump-driven hyperpolarization rather than a triggered afterdepolarization.
  23. The correct answer is D (Concealed conduction, partial penetration that alters subsequent AV node refractoriness.): Concealed conduction occurs when an impulse penetrates part way into the AV node without producing a visible conducted beat, but still resets the node's refractory period and irregularly affects the timing and conduction of subsequent impulses, a key contributor to the irregular ventricular response of atrial fibrillation. Overdrive suppression concerns automaticity, not conduction refractoriness. Delayed afterdepolarizations are a triggered activity mechanism unrelated to this phenomenon. This behavior reflects decremental node physiology rather than a fixed reentrant circuit.
  24. The correct answer is C (The atrioventricular node, which exhibits decremental conduction properties.): The AV node's cells have slow, calcium-dependent conduction with progressively slower recovery at faster rates, producing decremental conduction in which each successive impulse conducts more slowly until one fails entirely, generating the classic Wenckebach pattern. The sinoatrial node generates the pacing impulse but is not the site of this decremental block. The Purkinje network and His bundle are fast-conducting, non-decremental fast-response tissues and do not typically produce Wenckebach-type block under normal physiologic conditions.
  25. The correct answer is A (The A1 adenosine receptor, activating inward rectifier potassium channels.): Adenosine binds A1 receptors on nodal tissue, activating a G-protein coupled inward rectifier potassium current that hyperpolarizes the cell and slows or transiently blocks AV nodal conduction, terminating AV node-dependent reentrant tachycardias. Beta-1 receptor activation would instead increase, not decrease, nodal conduction and automaticity. The M2 muscarinic receptor mediates a similar potassium current but is activated by acetylcholine rather than adenosine. The angiotensin II type 1 receptor has no direct role in acute AV nodal electrophysiology.
  26. The correct answer is B (The M2 muscarinic receptor, coupled to inhibitory G-protein signaling in nodal tissue.): M2 receptors on sinoatrial and AV nodal cells couple to inhibitory G proteins, activating an acetylcholine-sensitive potassium current and inhibiting adenylate cyclase, which slows diastolic depolarization and nodal conduction, producing the sinus slowing and AV block seen with vagal maneuvers. M1 receptors are found primarily in neural tissue, not cardiac nodal tissue. M3 receptors mediate smooth muscle and glandular effects rather than nodal electrophysiology. Nicotinic receptors act at a structurally distinct site and are not the target of vagally mediated cardiac slowing.
  27. The correct answer is D (The beta-1 adrenergic receptor, increasing cyclic AMP and enhancing If.): Beta-1 adrenergic stimulation raises intracellular cyclic AMP, which directly binds HCN channels to enhance If and also increases ICaL, together steepening phase 4 diastolic depolarization and accelerating sinus node discharge during sympathetic activation such as exercise. M2 receptor activation produces the opposite, slowing effect on nodal automaticity. A1 adenosine receptor activation similarly hyperpolarizes and slows nodal tissue. Alpha-1 receptors primarily mediate vascular tone rather than direct sinoatrial pacemaker acceleration.
  28. The correct answer is C (The effective refractory period, the shortest interval producing propagation.): The effective refractory period is defined as the shortest coupling interval at which a premature stimulus produces any propagated response beyond the stimulation site, marking the boundary between complete local block and successful, if slowed, conduction. The relative refractory period describes the broader interval during which conduction occurs but at reduced velocity, distinct from this specific threshold definition. The supernormal period refers to a brief window of enhanced excitability, not reduced conduction. The functional refractory period is measured differently, at the shortest interval between two consecutive responses at a distal site.
  29. The correct answer is B (The ostium of the coronary sinus, forming the triangle's base.): The triangle of Koch is bounded by the tendon of Todaro superiorly, the septal leaflet of the tricuspid valve inferolaterally, and the ostium of the coronary sinus forming its base, with the compact AV node situated near the triangle's apex. The fossa ovalis lies more superiorly on the interatrial septum and is not one of the triangle's three defining borders. The crista terminalis is a separate right atrial ridge unrelated to this landmark. The mitral valve annulus is a left-sided structure and plays no role in defining this right atrial triangle.
  30. The correct answer is A (Bachmann's bundle, a muscular tract connecting the right and left atria.): Bachmann's bundle is a discrete muscular bundle along the anterior interatrial groove that provides the fastest preferential conduction pathway from the right to the left atrium, explaining the short right-to-left activation delay seen in normal sinus rhythm. The crista terminalis and cavotricuspid isthmus are confined to the right atrium and do not connect to the left atrium. The fossa ovalis is a passive anatomic landmark on the septum without a specialized fast-conducting role.
  31. The correct answer is C (The crista terminalis, the sinus venosus junction and a common arrhythmia focus site.): The crista terminalis is a prominent muscular ridge marking the embryologic junction of the sinus venosus with the right atrial appendage, and its anisotropic fiber orientation and automaticity make it a recognized source of focal atrial tachycardia and a substrate for reentry adjacent to the sinus node. The tendon of Todaro is a fibrous, not muscular, landmark used to define the triangle of Koch. Bachmann's bundle connects the two atria rather than marking this junction. The moderator band is a right ventricular, not atrial, structure.
  32. The correct answer is D (The fossa ovalis, a thin membranous depression on the septum.): The fossa ovalis is a thin, often membranous region of the interatrial septum representing the remnant of the foramen ovale, and its relative thinness compared to surrounding muscular septum makes it the preferred and safest site for transseptal needle puncture during left atrial access. The crista terminalis is thick and muscular, unsuitable for puncture. The tendon of Todaro is a fibrous landmark near the AV node, not a septal crossing point. The cavotricuspid isthmus lies in the right atrial floor, entirely unrelated to septal puncture.
  33. The correct answer is A (The left atrial appendage, a trabeculated outpouching prone to thrombus formation.): The left atrial appendage is a highly trabeculated, often multilobed structure with relatively stagnant blood flow during atrial fibrillation, making it the source of the vast majority of atrial fibrillation-related thrombi and the target of percutaneous occlusion devices. The right atrial appendage is smoother and broader based and is rarely implicated in systemic thromboembolism. The coronary sinus is a venous conduit, not an appendage, and is not a thrombus source targeted by these devices. The fossa ovalis is unrelated to appendage occlusion therapy.
  34. The correct answer is B (The moderator band, carrying a distal portion of the right bundle branch.): The moderator band is a prominent muscular trabeculation crossing the right ventricular cavity from the septum toward the anterior papillary muscle, and it typically carries the distal right bundle branch, helping deliver rapid activation to the anterior right ventricular free wall. The crista supraventricularis is a separate right ventricular muscular ridge unrelated to the AV node. Papillary muscles do not carry the left anterior fascicle, which travels within the left ventricular subendocardium. The tricuspid annulus is a valvular structure, not a conduction pathway for the His bundle.
  35. The correct answer is D (The vein and ligament of Marshall, an embryologic venous remnant.): The vein and ligament of Marshall is a remnant of the embryologic left superior vena cava that courses along the lateral left atrium near the appendage and left pulmonary veins, containing muscle fibers and autonomic ganglionated plexi that can contribute to atrial fibrillation and mitral isthmus-related atrial flutters, and it is a recognized adjunctive ablation target. The great cardiac vein is a distinct coronary venous structure without this embryologic origin. The crista terminalis and cavotricuspid isthmus are right atrial structures unrelated to this left atrial venous remnant.
  36. The correct answer is C (The left ventricular summit, a high-risk epicardial region near coronary arteries.): The left ventricular summit is the highest portion of the left ventricular epicardium, bounded by the left anterior descending and circumflex coronary arteries, and accessed via the great cardiac vein and anterior interventricular vein, making it a common but high-risk source of epicardial outflow tract ventricular tachycardia given the proximity of coronary vessels limiting radiofrequency delivery. The cavotricuspid isthmus and tricuspid annulus are right-sided structures unrelated to this left ventricular epicardial region. The moderator band lies within the right ventricular cavity, not the epicardial summit.
  37. The correct answer is B (The right phrenic nerve, which courses near the SVC and right pulmonary veins.): The right phrenic nerve runs in close anatomic proximity to the superior vena cava and the right pulmonary veins, making it vulnerable to thermal injury during right-sided pulmonary vein isolation, and pacing with diaphragmatic monitoring helps detect impending injury before it becomes permanent. The vagus nerve runs posteriorly near the esophagus and is not the nerve monitored by diaphragmatic pacing. The recurrent laryngeal nerve is near the aortic arch and unrelated to pulmonary vein isolation. The left phrenic nerve runs near the left atrial appendage and left-sided structures, not the right pulmonary veins or SVC.
  38. The correct answer is A (The esophagus, which lies immediately posterior to the left atrium.): The esophagus runs directly posterior to the left atrium, often in close or direct contact with the posterior wall targeted during atrial fibrillation ablation, and excessive thermal injury at this site can rarely produce a life-threatening atrioesophageal fistula, which is why esophageal temperature monitoring and power reduction are used posteriorly. The trachea lies well anterior and superior to the heart, away from ablation catheters. The thoracic duct and descending aorta are also nearby mediastinal structures but are not the structure directly adjacent to the posterior left atrial wall responsible for this specific complication.
  39. The correct answer is D (At the membranous septum, just distal to the tricuspid annulus.): The compact His bundle lies at the membranous portion of the interventricular septum, just distal to where it penetrates the central fibrous body near the tricuspid annulus, and this precise location is the anatomic target for His bundle pacing leads to achieve physiologic activation. The coronary sinus ostium is a separate right atrial landmark unrelated to His bundle location. The right ventricular apex is the traditional, non-physiologic pacing site, not the His bundle. The mid left ventricular septum is the target for left bundle branch area pacing, a distinct technique from direct His bundle pacing.
  40. The correct answer is C (The left ventricular septal subendocardium, beyond the right septal surface.): Left bundle branch area pacing involves screwing the lead transseptally from the right ventricular septal surface deep into the left ventricular septal subendocardium to capture the left bundle branch or adjacent conduction fibers, which typically provides a more stable position and lower, more consistent capture thresholds than direct His bundle pacing. The sinoatrial node region and right atrial free wall are unrelated atrial locations. The coronary sinus lumen is used for left ventricular epicardial pacing in resynchronization therapy, a different technique entirely from conduction system pacing.
  41. The correct answer is A (The great cardiac vein, running along the anterior interventricular groove.): The great cardiac vein accompanies the left anterior descending artery in the anterior interventricular groove before curving posteriorly to become the coronary sinus proper, and it is the tributary most consistently identified near the coronary sinus origin on venography. The middle cardiac vein instead runs along the posterior interventricular groove, accompanying the posterior descending artery. The posterolateral vein drains the lateral left ventricular wall and is a common target for resynchronization leads, but it is not the anterior groove tributary. The small cardiac vein runs along the right atrioventricular groove, a separate location.
  42. The correct answer is B (The right coronary artery, in the majority of patients.): In roughly 55 to 60 percent of individuals, the sinoatrial nodal artery arises from the right coronary artery, explaining why proximal right coronary artery occlusion, as in an inferior myocardial infarction, can produce transient or persistent sinus node dysfunction. The left circumflex artery supplies the sinoatrial node in most of the remaining cases, making it the minority supply rather than the majority. The left anterior descending artery does not typically supply the sinoatrial node directly. The posterior descending artery more commonly relates to AV nodal blood supply depending on coronary dominance.
  43. The correct answer is D (The right coronary artery, via its posterior descending branch.): In the roughly 85 to 90 percent of individuals with right coronary dominance, the AV nodal artery arises as a branch near the crux of the heart from the right coronary artery's posterior descending branch, which is why inferior infarctions frequently produce transient AV block. In left-dominant circulations, the left circumflex instead gives rise to this branch, but this is the minority pattern. The left anterior descending artery does not typically supply the AV node. The first septal perforator supplies proximal septal myocardium and the bundle branches, not the compact AV node itself.
  44. The correct answer is C (The T-type calcium channel, activating at more negative potentials than L-type.): T-type calcium channels activate at more hyperpolarized potentials than L-type channels and contribute a modest inward current during the later portion of phase 4 diastolic depolarization in sinoatrial node cells, complementing If and the later-activating ICaL near threshold. The L-type calcium channel activates closer to threshold and is more prominent in phase 0 upstroke and phase 2 plateau of nodal and working myocardium respectively. The ryanodine receptor is an intracellular calcium release channel, not a sarcolemmal current source described here. The sodium-calcium exchanger is an electrogenic transporter, not a voltage-gated channel.
  45. The correct answer is B (IK1, the inward rectifier current that stabilizes resting potential in myocytes.): Sinoatrial node cells have low or absent IK1, removing the strong stabilizing potassium conductance present in working ventricular myocytes, which allows the relatively small inward currents of phase 4, including If and ICaT, to depolarize the membrane spontaneously toward threshold rather than being held at a flat resting potential. IKr and Ito are involved in ventricular repolarization phases and are not the current whose absence explains nodal automaticity. ICaL contributes to the plateau in myocytes and to the nodal upstroke, but its presence, not absence, is relevant to pacemaking.
  46. The correct answer is A (It increases IKr, accelerating repolarization and shortening the action potential.): A gain-of-function KCNH2 mutation increases IKr, the rapid delayed rectifier potassium current, which accelerates phase 3 repolarization and shortens both the action potential duration and the surface QT interval, producing short QT syndrome and predisposing to ventricular fibrillation and atrial fibrillation. A decrease in IKr would instead prolong repolarization, as seen in loss-of-function LQT2. Increased late INa would prolong rather than shorten the plateau, as in LQT3. Decreased ICaL could shorten the action potential but is not the mechanism described for this particular gain-of-function KCNH2 mutation.
  47. The correct answer is C (The open and inactivated states occurring during depolarization and early repolarization.): Class IC agents like flecainide preferentially bind sodium channels in their open and inactivated states during depolarization and dissociate slowly, producing marked use dependence in which conduction slowing becomes more pronounced at faster heart rates, an important property exploited for atrial fibrillation suppression but also a risk during rapid ventricular rates. Binding to the resting closed state would not produce this rate-dependent effect. The deactivated state pertains to potassium channel kinetics, not sodium channel gating relevant here. Nodal hyperpolarized states are not the primary target of these predominantly fast-response tissue-acting drugs.
  48. The correct answer is D (The slope, or rate, of phase 4 diastolic depolarization.): Pacemaker discharge rate is determined by the interplay of maximum diastolic potential, threshold potential, and the slope of phase 4 diastolic depolarization, with a steeper slope reaching threshold sooner and producing a faster rate, as can occur with enhanced automaticity in a junctional focus after cardiac surgery. Phase 0 amplitude reflects upstroke magnitude, not diastolic depolarization rate. Refractory period duration influences how soon a tissue can respond to a subsequent impulse rather than its own spontaneous rate. The resting potential of adjacent myocardium does not directly determine a separate pacemaker focus's intrinsic discharge rate.
  49. The correct answer is B (HCN4, the predominant isoform expressed in sinoatrial pacemaker cells.): HCN4 is the dominant hyperpolarization-activated cyclic nucleotide-gated channel isoform in the sinoatrial node, carrying the majority of If current, and it is both the principal target of ivabradine and a gene implicated in familial sinus node dysfunction and bradycardia when mutated. HCN1 is expressed mainly in neuronal and retinal tissue, not cardiac pacemaker cells. HCN2 has a broader tissue distribution and is not the dominant nodal isoform. HCN3 is expressed at low levels and is not a major contributor to nodal If.
  50. The correct answer is A (Anisotropic conduction, in which velocity differs by direction relative to fiber orientation.): Anisotropic conduction describes propagation velocity that depends on direction relative to myocardial fiber orientation and gap junction distribution, and fibrosis with disorganized, reduced connexin expression exaggerates this anisotropy, slowing conduction transverse to fiber orientation and creating substrate for reentry in longstanding atrial fibrillation. Isotropic conduction would imply uniform velocity in all directions, the opposite of what remodeled, fibrotic tissue produces. Decremental conduction describes rate-dependent slowing typical of the AV node, not this structural fiber-orientation effect. Concealed conduction refers to partial impulse penetration affecting subsequent conduction, a distinct phenomenon.
  51. The correct answer is B (The effective refractory period of the stimulated ventricular tissue.): The effective refractory period is defined as the longest coupling interval at which a premature extrastimulus fails to produce a propagated response beyond the site of stimulation, even though local capture occurs. The functional refractory period instead describes the shortest interval between two consecutive propagated responses measured at a distal site, which is a different concept applied downstream of the stimulation site. The relative refractory period would instead permit a slowed but propagated response, and no supernormal excitability is described in this scenario.
  52. The correct answer is A (Increased cyclic AMP accelerates funny current activation, hastening threshold.): Beta-1 adrenergic stimulation raises intracellular cyclic AMP, which binds directly to the funny current channels and shifts their activation to less negative voltages, steepening phase 4 diastolic depolarization and allowing the membrane to reach threshold sooner. This shortens the time between action potentials and increases sinus rate. Acetylcholine-sensitive potassium current is a parasympathetic, not sympathetic, effector and would slow rather than accelerate the sinus node, and L-type calcium current is actually increased, not decreased, by sympathetic stimulation.
  53. The correct answer is C (Decremental conduction reflecting incomplete recovery of AV nodal cells.): The atrioventricular node is composed of cells with slow calcium-dependent action potentials and long recovery times, so as the pacing interval shortens, node cells are progressively less recovered and conduct more slowly, producing the characteristic AH prolongation seen before Wenckebach block. This decremental property protects the ventricles from excessively rapid atrial rates such as atrial fibrillation. His-Purkinje tissue instead relies on fast sodium channels with rapid recovery, which is why its conduction time stays relatively fixed across a wide range of rates, and concealed conduction refers to a distinct phenomenon of incomplete penetration by a nonconducted impulse.
  54. The correct answer is D (Constant ventriculoatrial conduction time despite incremental ventricular pacing.): An accessory pathway conducts retrogradely with fast sodium channel kinetics and minimal decremental properties, so the ventriculoatrial interval stays essentially fixed even as the ventricular pacing rate increases. Nodal retrograde conduction, in contrast, is decremental and would show progressive lengthening of the ventriculoatrial interval and eventually Wenckebach-type block as pacing accelerates. A shifting retrograde activation sequence would instead suggest a second pathway or fusion, and complete loss of conduction is a nonspecific finding that does not distinguish pathway type.
  55. The correct answer is A (Normal automaticity arising from specialized pacemaker cell membrane currents.): Normal automaticity is the intrinsic ability of specialized pacemaker cells, including the sinoatrial node and latent subsidiary pacemakers, to spontaneously depolarize during phase 4 using currents such as the funny current, T-type calcium current, and the sodium-calcium exchanger, without requiring a preceding triggering beat. Triggered activity, by contrast, only occurs as a consequence of an afterdepolarization following a prior action potential, and reentry requires a physical or functional circuit rather than spontaneous depolarization. Abnormal automaticity arises in non-pacemaker myocardium that has become pathologically depolarized, which is not the case in a normal sinoatrial node cell.
  56. The correct answer is B (Abnormal automaticity from partially depolarized, diseased myocardial tissue.): Abnormal automaticity develops when non-pacemaker myocardial cells are pathologically depolarized to a resting potential less negative than normal, which inactivates fast sodium channels and allows slow calcium-dependent currents to generate spontaneous, rate-dependent impulse formation. This differs from normal automaticity, which occurs in specialized pacemaker tissue at a normal resting potential. Triggered activity requires an afterdepolarization following a preceding action potential rather than spontaneous depolarization from a stable depolarized baseline, and reentry requires a conduction circuit rather than a focal source of automatic firing.
  57. The correct answer is D (Early afterdepolarization from reactivated L-type calcium current during repolarization.): When repolarization is markedly prolonged, as with QT-prolonging drugs, the action potential plateau remains long enough for L-type calcium channels to recover from inactivation and reactivate before full repolarization, producing an early afterdepolarization that arises during phase 2 or phase 3. If this afterdepolarization reaches threshold, it can trigger torsades de pointes, the classic arrhythmia associated with acquired or congenital long QT syndrome. Delayed afterdepolarizations instead occur after repolarization is complete and result from spontaneous diastolic calcium release, which does not match the timing described here.
  58. The correct answer is C (Delayed afterdepolarization caused by spontaneous diastolic calcium release events.): Digoxin toxicity causes intracellular calcium overload, which triggers spontaneous release of calcium from the sarcoplasmic reticulum after the action potential has already fully repolarized. This calcium activates the electrogenic sodium-calcium exchanger, generating a transient inward current that produces a delayed afterdepolarization occurring in phase 4, and if it reaches threshold it can trigger a full action potential and sustained arrhythmia such as bidirectional ventricular tachycardia. Early afterdepolarizations instead occur before repolarization is complete during the plateau phase, which does not match the phase 4 timing described here.
  59. The correct answer is A (Anatomic reentry confined to a fixed, defined conduction pathway.): Typical atrial flutter is the prototypical example of anatomic reentry, in which the circuit is constrained by fixed anatomic barriers, here the tricuspid annulus and crista terminalis, with the cavotricuspid isthmus serving as the obligatory zone of slow, protected conduction that makes the circuit stable and ablatable. Functional reentry instead depends on a continuously refractory, non-anatomically fixed core rather than a defined structural boundary. Triggered activity and abnormal automaticity are focal mechanisms that do not involve a circulating wavefront at all, unlike the reentrant circuit described here.
  60. The correct answer is C (Functional reentry, exemplified by the leading circle mechanism concept.): The leading circle model describes functional reentry in which the circulating wavefront itself maintains a continuously refractory central core through centripetal wavelets, so no fixed anatomic obstacle is required, and the circuit's size is set by the tissue's wavelength, the product of refractory period and conduction velocity. This differs from anatomic reentry, which requires a genuine fixed structural pathway such as scar or a valve annulus. Automaticity and triggered activity are both focal mechanisms of impulse initiation rather than circulating wavefront phenomena, so neither applies to this description.
  61. The correct answer is B (Electrical remodeling, summarized by the phrase atrial fibrillation begets fibrillation.): Sustained rapid atrial rates during fibrillation cause calcium overload that downregulates L-type calcium current and alters potassium currents, producing progressive shortening of the atrial effective refractory period within days, a phenomenon commonly summarized as atrial fibrillation begets fibrillation. This electrical remodeling shortens wavelength and makes reentrant circuits easier to sustain, promoting perpetuation of the arrhythmia. Structural remodeling from fibrosis develops over a longer timescale and involves tissue architecture rather than refractory period shortening, and no single-gene mutation is implied by an acquired change following an arrhythmia episode.
  62. The correct answer is D (Structural remodeling driven by fibroblast-mediated interstitial collagen deposition over time.): Structural remodeling refers to the slower process of atrial tissue architectural change, including fibroblast proliferation and interstitial collagen deposition, that develops over months to years of sustained atrial fibrillation and creates fixed anatomic substrate for reentry that is visible on late gadolinium-enhanced MRI. This differs from electrical remodeling, which develops within days through ion channel current changes rather than structural tissue change, and correlates with reduced ablation success because fibrotic substrate is harder to eliminate. Autonomic and genetic remodeling are distinct concepts not primarily characterized by collagen deposition on imaging.
  63. The correct answer is A (Transmural dispersion of repolarization across the ventricular wall.): Transmural dispersion of repolarization describes the normal physiologic difference in action potential duration among epicardial, M cell, and endocardial layers, driven largely by lower slow delayed rectifier potassium current density in M cells. Drugs or conditions that prolong repolarization exaggerate this dispersion disproportionately, creating a substrate for early afterdepolarization-triggered torsades de pointes because adjacent regions repolarize at markedly different times. Concealed conduction, use-dependent block, and retrograde nodal block are all separate electrophysiologic phenomena unrelated to transmural differences in ventricular repolarization timing.
  64. The correct answer is D (Retrograde conduction block preventing penetration into the reentrant circuit.): When a paced impulse fails to reset a tachycardia despite local capture, it indicates that the impulse encountered tissue that remained refractory and blocked retrograde propagation before it could reach and penetrate the reentrant circuit, a form of retrograde conduction block. This is distinct from true entrainment, in which each paced beat does successfully penetrate and advance the circuit. The scenario described is a supraventricular mechanism being tested by ventricular pacing, so it does not represent ventricular automaticity, an afterdepolarization, or a purely ventricular anatomic circuit.
  65. The correct answer is C (Complex fractionated atrial electrograms with continuous, disorganized activity.): Complex fractionated atrial electrograms are characterized by continuous, low-amplitude, multi-deflection signals with an absent or very short isoelectric interval, thought to represent areas of slow, disorganized conduction, wavefront collision, or rotor activity that may help sustain atrial fibrillation. These have historically been proposed as ablation targets, though their value as an independent target beyond pulmonary vein isolation remains debated in later trials. Discrete unipolar signals, His bundle electrograms, and far-field ventricular signals are all recognizable, organized signal types that do not share this continuously fractionated appearance.
  66. The correct answer is B (Fever-induced unmasking of an underlying Brugada pattern via temperature-sensitive sodium channel dysfunction.): Fever is a well-established provocative trigger in Brugada syndrome, converting a subtle or concealed ECG pattern into the diagnostic type 1 coved pattern by further impairing already-dysfunctional cardiac sodium channels at higher body temperature, which is exactly why febrile Brugada patients are treated aggressively with antipyretics and monitored closely. The pattern reverting toward baseline after the fever resolves does not mean the channelopathy has been cured, since the underlying genetic substrate and arrhythmic risk persist regardless of ECG appearance. Reverse use-dependence describes a drug-specific pharmacologic phenomenon and does not apply here since no sodium channel blocking medication is mentioned, and concealed conduction is an unrelated electrophysiologic concept describing incomplete penetration of an impulse.
  67. The correct answer is A (N-acetylprocainamide, a hepatically formed active class III metabolite.): N-acetylprocainamide, commonly abbreviated NAPA, is formed by hepatic acetylation of procainamide and possesses predominant class III potassium channel blocking activity that prolongs the QT interval, distinct from the parent compound's class Ia sodium channel blocking properties. Patients who are slow acetylators or who have renal impairment, since NAPA is renally cleared, can accumulate this metabolite and develop clinically significant QT prolongation. Desethylamiodarone, nordiltiazem, and norpropafenone are genuine active metabolites of other antiarrhythmic drugs but are not relevant to procainamide metabolism.
  68. The correct answer is B (Use-dependence, with a greater blocking effect at faster heart rates.): Use-dependence, also called frequency-dependent block, describes how a drug's binding affinity for sodium or other channels is greatest when the channel cycles frequently through open and inactivated conformational states, which occurs more often at faster heart rates, so class Ic drugs like flecainide show more pronounced conduction slowing during tachycardia. Reverse use-dependence is the opposite pattern, seen with many class III potassium channel blockers, where the effect is greatest at slow rates. This state-dependent binding also explains why use-dependent conduction block is not simply a fixed, frequency-independent property of the drug.
  69. The correct answer is D (Reverse use-dependence, with greater QT prolongation at slower heart rates.): Most class III potassium channel blocking drugs, including sotalol, exhibit reverse use-dependence, meaning their repolarization-prolonging effect is more pronounced at slow heart rates and less pronounced during tachycardia, which is the opposite pattern from use-dependent sodium channel blockers. This explains why QT prolongation and the risk of torsades de pointes are often greatest during bradycardia, such as during sleep, rather than during exercise-induced tachycardia. Use-dependence describes the pharmacologically opposite, rate-related pattern seen with agents like flecainide, and neither an active metabolite nor autonomic withdrawal is the primary mechanism being tested here.
  70. The correct answer is C (Moderate sodium channel block combined with potassium channel block prolonging repolarization.): Class Ia agents such as quinidine and procainamide produce intermediate-affinity, moderate blockade of fast sodium channels that slows conduction to a moderate degree, and they also block potassium channels enough to prolong action potential duration and the QT interval, distinguishing them from class Ib agents that shorten repolarization. This dual effect explains both their antiarrhythmic efficacy and their proarrhythmic risk of drug-induced torsades de pointes. Class Ia drugs are not calcium channel blockers or beta blockers, which act through entirely different receptor and channel mechanisms.
  71. The correct answer is C (Fast dissociation kinetics with selectivity for ischemic or depolarized tissue.): Class Ib agents such as lidocaine and mexiletine have rapid onset and offset kinetics at the sodium channel, meaning they bind and unbind quickly, which gives them relative selectivity for depolarized, ischemic, or otherwise abnormal tissue where channels spend more time in inactivated states, while producing minimal effect on normal, well-polarized myocardium. This is the opposite kinetic profile from class Ic agents, which dissociate slowly and produce marked conduction slowing even in normal tissue. Class Ib drugs also tend to shorten, not prolong, action potential duration, unlike class Ia and class III agents that block potassium currents.
  72. The correct answer is A (Marked conduction slowing in diseased tissue can promote sustained reentrant ventricular arrhythmia.): Class Ic agents such as flecainide have slow sodium channel dissociation kinetics, producing pronounced use-dependent conduction slowing that, in the presence of structural heart disease or prior infarction with scar-related conduction heterogeneity, can create the substrate for sustained, often incessant and hemodynamically unstable reentrant ventricular arrhythmias, as demonstrated in the CAST trial. This is why class Ic drugs are reserved for patients without significant structural heart disease. These agents have fast, not slow, dissociation would be incorrect, and they are primarily sodium channel blockers rather than acting predominantly on potassium channels or repolarization.
  73. The correct answer is D (Reduced sympathetic drive lowering automaticity and slowing AV nodal conduction.): Beta blockers act by antagonizing beta-1 adrenergic receptors, which reduces cyclic AMP-mediated enhancement of pacemaker currents and calcium currents, thereby lowering automaticity in ectopic foci and slowing conduction through the atrioventricular node, which is particularly useful in reducing sympathetically driven ventricular ectopy and controlling ventricular rate in supraventricular arrhythmias. This mechanism is receptor-mediated rather than a direct membrane channel blocking effect, distinguishing class II agents from classes I, III, and IV. Direct sodium, potassium, or calcium channel blockade describes the mechanisms of other Vaughan Williams classes, not beta blockers.
  74. The correct answer is B (Potassium channel blockade prolonging repolarization and refractory periods.): Class III antiarrhythmic agents such as amiodarone, sotalol, and dofetilide primarily block potassium channels, most notably the rapid component of the delayed rectifier current, which prolongs action potential duration and the effective refractory period throughout the myocardium and increases the wavelength of reentrant circuits, making sustained reentry less likely. Amiodarone also has additional sodium, calcium, and beta-blocking properties, but its dominant and defining mechanism is potassium channel blockade. Pure sodium channel blockade, calcium channel blockade, or isolated beta blockade describe the mechanisms of classes I, IV, and II respectively, not the primary class III effect.
  75. The correct answer is B (Blockade of L-type calcium channels slowing AV nodal conduction.): Class IV agents such as verapamil and diltiazem block L-type calcium channels, which carry the primary depolarizing current in the calcium-dependent, slow-response tissue of the sinoatrial and atrioventricular nodes, slowing conduction through the AV node and prolonging its refractory period. This effect is particularly useful for terminating or preventing atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia, since the AV node forms one limb of the reentrant circuit. Sodium channel blockade governs His-Purkinje and myocardial conduction rather than nodal tissue, potassium channel blockade is the class III mechanism, and beta-1 receptor blockade is the distinct class II mechanism.
  76. The correct answer is C (SCN5A, encoding the cardiac voltage-gated sodium channel alpha subunit.): SCN5A is the most commonly identified disease gene in Brugada syndrome, and loss-of-function mutations reduce cardiac sodium current, most prominently in the right ventricular outflow tract epicardium, producing the characteristic coved ST-segment elevation and predisposing to polymorphic ventricular tachycardia and sudden cardiac arrest. Even so, a causative mutation is identified in only a minority of clinically diagnosed Brugada syndrome patients, reflecting genetic heterogeneity. KCNQ1 and KCNH2 are potassium channel genes associated with long QT syndrome types 1 and 2, and RYR2 causes catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia, none of which are the primary Brugada syndrome gene.
  77. The correct answer is A (Long QT syndrome type 3, caused by SCN5A gain of function.): Long QT syndrome type 3 results from gain-of-function mutations in SCN5A that impair normal fast inactivation of the cardiac sodium channel, producing a persistent late sodium current during the plateau phase that prolongs the action potential and the QT interval. Patients with type 3 characteristically experience cardiac events predominantly during sleep or at rest, in contrast to type 1, which is triggered by exercise, particularly swimming, and type 2, which is often triggered by auditory stimuli or postpartum. Catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia from RYR2 mutations typically presents with a normal QT interval and exercise-induced bidirectional or polymorphic ventricular tachycardia, not QT prolongation.
  78. The correct answer is D (SCN5A cardiac sodium channelopathies show overlapping, pleiotropic phenotypic expression.): SCN5A mutations are recognized to produce a spectrum of overlapping sodium channelopathy phenotypes within the same family and even the same individual, including Brugada syndrome, progressive cardiac conduction disease, sick sinus syndrome, and long QT syndrome type 3, reflecting the sodium channel's diverse roles across the atria, ventricles, and specialized conduction system. This pleiotropy means a single mutation's functional effect on channel gating can manifest differently depending on cell type and modifying factors, without requiring separate mutations. SCN5A is therefore not limited to only long QT syndrome type 3, and purely environmental or coincidental explanations do not account for the recognized genotype-phenotype relationship in these families.
  79. The correct answer is D (Long QT syndrome type 1, classically triggered by swimming and exertion.): Long QT syndrome type 1 results from loss-of-function mutations in KCNQ1, which encodes the alpha subunit of the slow delayed rectifier potassium current responsible for repolarization reserve during increased heart rate, so patients are unable to adequately shorten their QT interval during exercise and are classically at highest risk during swimming and other exertion. Long QT syndrome type 2, from KCNH2 mutations, is more classically triggered by sudden auditory stimuli such as alarm clocks, and type 3 events occur predominantly during sleep or rest. Catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia from RYR2 mutations presents with a normal QT interval, unlike the QT prolongation described here.
  80. The correct answer is B (Jervell and Lange-Nielsen syndrome, with deafness from biallelic KCNQ1 mutations.): Jervell and Lange-Nielsen syndrome is the autosomal recessive form of long QT syndrome caused by biallelic loss-of-function mutations in KCNQ1, inherited from two unaffected heterozygous carrier parents, and it uniquely combines profound congenital sensorineural hearing loss with severe QT prolongation, because KCNQ1 potassium channels are also required for normal inner ear endolymph production. This differs from Romano-Ward syndrome, the far more common autosomal dominant form of long QT syndrome from a single mutant allele, which does not cause hearing loss. Andersen-Tawil and Timothy syndromes are distinct channelopathies caused by different genes and clinical features not described here.
  81. The correct answer is C (Long QT syndrome type 2, from KCNH2 loss-of-function mutation.): Long QT syndrome type 2 results from loss-of-function mutations in KCNH2, which encodes the rapid component of the delayed rectifier potassium current also known as hERG, and is classically associated with low-amplitude, notched T waves and cardiac events triggered by sudden auditory stimuli such as alarm clocks or telephones. KCNH2-encoded channels are also the principal target of many drugs that cause acquired, drug-induced QT prolongation, making carriers particularly sensitive to QT-prolonging medications. KCNQ1 mutations instead cause type 1 with exertional triggers, SCN5A gain-of-function mutations cause type 3, and KCNJ2 mutations cause the distinct Andersen-Tawil syndrome with periodic paralysis and dysmorphic features.
  82. The correct answer is A (Abnormal ryanodine receptor calcium leak causing delayed afterdepolarization-triggered activity.): Catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia results from gain-of-function mutations in RYR2, the cardiac ryanodine receptor, which cause excessive, catecholamine-sensitive calcium leak from the sarcoplasmic reticulum during diastole. This calcium overload activates the sodium-calcium exchanger, producing delayed afterdepolarizations that can trigger the characteristic bidirectional or polymorphic ventricular tachycardia during exercise or emotional stress, typically with a structurally normal heart and normal resting QT interval. This mechanism is distinct from the potassium current reduction of long QT syndrome types 1 and 2, the persistent late sodium current of long QT syndrome type 3, and the sodium current reduction seen in Brugada syndrome.
  83. The correct answer is C (LMNA, encoding the nuclear envelope proteins lamin A and C.): LMNA mutations cause a distinctive cardiac laminopathy characterized by early conduction system disease, often progressing from first-degree block to complete heart block requiring pacemaker implantation, followed by dilated cardiomyopathy and a disproportionately high risk of malignant ventricular arrhythmias and sudden death relative to the degree of ejection fraction reduction, which influences earlier defibrillator consideration in these patients. This is a non-ion-channel genetic cause of arrhythmia, acting through nuclear envelope structural disruption rather than direct channel dysfunction. SCN5A, PKP2, and RYR2 are ion channel or desmosomal genes with distinct phenotypes not matching this combined conduction disease and cardiomyopathy presentation.
  84. The correct answer is D (Arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy from a PKP2 desmosomal mutation.): Arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy is most commonly caused by mutations in genes encoding desmosomal proteins, of which PKP2, encoding plakophilin-2, is the most frequently identified, leading to progressive fibrofatty replacement of right ventricular myocardium, right ventricular dilation, and a substrate for scar-related reentrant ventricular tachycardia, often provoked or accelerated by intensive athletic exercise. This is a non-ion-channel genetic cause of arrhythmia acting through impaired cell-to-cell mechanical coupling rather than direct channel dysfunction. Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, long QT syndrome type 1, and catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia are each caused by different genes with distinct structural and electrocardiographic findings not matching this presentation.
  85. The correct answer is B (PRKAG2, encoding an AMP-activated protein kinase gamma-2 regulatory subunit.): PRKAG2 cardiac syndrome is a glycogen storage cardiomyopathy caused by mutations in the gamma-2 regulatory subunit of AMP-activated protein kinase, leading to excessive myocardial glycogen accumulation that produces a phenocopy of left ventricular hypertrophy on imaging, along with ventricular preexcitation from disrupted annular insulation and progressive conduction system disease including risk of complete heart block. This distinguishes it from true sarcomeric hypertrophic cardiomyopathy caused by genes like MYBPC3 or TTN, which do not typically cause preexcitation, and from Fabry disease caused by GLA mutations, which is a lysosomal storage disorder with a different multisystem phenotype.
  86. The correct answer is A (The acetylcholine-activated inward rectifier potassium current, hyperpolarizing nodal cells.): Vagal stimulation releases acetylcholine, which binds muscarinic receptors on atrioventricular and sinoatrial nodal cells and activates the acetylcholine-sensitive inward rectifier potassium current, hyperpolarizing the resting membrane potential and slowing the rate of phase 4 depolarization and conduction velocity, which can transiently increase atrioventricular nodal refractoriness enough to terminate a reentrant tachycardia that depends on the node for one limb of its circuit. This is the opposite effect from sympathetic stimulation, which enhances the funny current and L-type calcium current to accelerate conduction and automaticity. The late sodium current is not a primary target of vagal signaling in nodal tissue.
  87. The correct answer is D (Reduced wavelength and dispersion of refractoriness favoring multiple reentrant wavelets.): High vagal tone shortens atrial effective refractory period heterogeneously across different atrial regions because acetylcholine-sensitive potassium current density varies across atrial tissue, reducing the wavelength of potential reentrant circuits and increasing spatial dispersion of refractoriness, which together create favorable conditions for the initiation and maintenance of multiple simultaneous reentrant wavelets characteristic of atrial fibrillation. This is why vagally mediated atrial fibrillation classically occurs during periods of high parasympathetic tone such as sleep or after meals. The other options describe effects that would suppress rather than promote atrial fibrillation, which does not match the clinical pattern described.
  88. The correct answer is A (Delayed afterdepolarization-triggered activity from spontaneous diastolic calcium release.): Catecholamine-driven increases in cyclic AMP enhance protein kinase A-mediated phosphorylation of the ryanodine receptor and phospholamban, increasing sarcoplasmic reticulum calcium content and the likelihood of spontaneous calcium release during diastole in susceptible individuals, which activates the sodium-calcium exchanger to produce a delayed afterdepolarization. If this afterdepolarization reaches threshold, it triggers an extra beat or sustained triggered arrhythmia, the mechanism underlying catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia and digoxin toxicity-related arrhythmias. Early afterdepolarizations instead arise during the plateau or repolarization phase rather than during diastole, and anatomic reentry and AV nodal conduction are unrelated to this calcium-handling mechanism.
  89. The correct answer is C (Atrioventricular nodal block from progressive decremental conduction failure.): Mobitz type I, or Wenckebach, second-degree atrioventricular block is characterized by progressive PR interval prolongation before a nonconducted P wave, reflecting the atrioventricular node's decremental conduction property in which each successive impulse finds the node less recovered until one impulse fails to conduct entirely, after which the cycle resets. This nearly always localizes to the atrioventricular node itself, typically producing a narrow QRS complex as seen here, and generally carries a more benign prognosis than infranodal block. Sinoatrial exit block and ventricular automaticity would produce different, distinguishable electrocardiographic patterns that do not match progressive PR prolongation.
  90. The correct answer is B (Infranodal His-Purkinje block with higher risk of progression to complete block.): Mobitz type II second-degree atrioventricular block shows a constant PR interval with sudden, unpredictable nonconducted beats without preceding gradual prolongation, and is typically associated with a wide QRS complex because the diseased conduction tissue usually lies within the His-Purkinje system rather than the atrioventricular node. This infranodal localization carries a substantially higher risk of unpredictable progression to complete heart block and is generally considered an indication for permanent pacing even when asymptomatic. Nodal block tends to be more benign and often narrow-complex, and this pattern is not reliably vagally mediated or atropine-responsive, unlike typical nodal Wenckebach block.
  91. The correct answer is D (Retrograde conduction proceeds over a left free-wall accessory pathway.): Concentric, septal-earliest retrograde atrial activation during ventricular pacing is typical of conduction through the compact atrioventricular node, whereas eccentric retrograde activation that is earliest at a site away from the septum, such as the left lateral mitral annulus, indicates that an accessory pathway located at that same lateral site is conducting retrogradely instead of, or in addition to, the node. This eccentric pattern is a key mapping clue used to localize and target accessory pathways for ablation. A right-sided septal pathway or nodal-only conduction would instead produce earliest activation at or near the septum, and dual nodal pathway physiology is assessed with a different diagnostic maneuver.
  92. The correct answer is C (Dual atrioventricular nodal pathways with differing conduction and refractory properties.): A discontinuous jump in the AH interval during progressively premature atrial extrastimuli, in which a small decrease in coupling interval produces a disproportionately large increase in AH interval, reflects block in a fast-conducting pathway with a longer refractory period and subsequent conduction over a slow-conducting pathway with a shorter refractory period, the electrophysiologic hallmark of dual atrioventricular nodal pathway physiology that provides the substrate for atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia. A single homogeneous pathway would instead show smooth, continuous AH prolongation without a jump. An accessory pathway and isolated ventriculoatrial block are separate findings that do not by themselves produce this antegrade jump phenomenon.
  93. The correct answer is A (Unidirectional conduction block combined with sufficiently slow conduction velocity.): Reentry requires two essential conditions occurring together: unidirectional block, in which a premature impulse fails to conduct in one direction through a region of tissue while still conducting in the other, and sufficiently slow conduction through that alternate pathway to allow enough time for the originally blocked tissue to recover excitability before the impulse arrives back at it, completing the circuit. If block were bidirectional, no impulse could propagate through the zone at all and no circuit could form, and neither enhanced automaticity nor triggered activity alone describes this circuit-based mechanism, which depends on conduction properties rather than a focal source of impulse formation.
  94. The correct answer is B (The wavelength, the product of conduction velocity and refractory period.): The wavelength of a cardiac impulse is defined as the product of conduction velocity and the effective refractory period, and it represents the minimum anatomic path length needed to sustain a reentrant circuit, since the circuit must be at least as long as the wavelength for the tissue at the origin to have recovered excitability by the time the wavefront returns. Antiarrhythmic strategies that either slow conduction further or, more commonly, prolong refractoriness increase the wavelength, making a given anatomic circuit too short to sustain reentry and terminating or preventing the arrhythmia. The excitable gap is a related but distinct concept describing the fully recovered tissue between the head and tail of a reentrant wavefront.
  95. The correct answer is A (Reduced repolarization reserve unmasked by an additional repolarizing current stressor.): Repolarization reserve refers to the combined redundancy of multiple repolarizing potassium currents that normally protects the heart from excessive QT prolongation even when one current is mildly impaired. A patient with a subclinical genetic reduction in one current, such as a silent hERG variant, may have a normal baseline QT interval but has less reserve remaining, so an additional pharmacologic insult that further blocks repolarizing current, such as a QT-prolonging antibiotic, can unmask marked QT prolongation and torsades de pointes that would not occur in a patient with full reserve. This concept explains why drug-induced long QT syndrome often reflects an underlying genetic predisposition rather than pure drug toxicity alone.
  96. The correct answer is D (High lipophilicity producing extensive tissue accumulation and a long half-life.): Amiodarone is highly lipophilic and accumulates extensively in adipose tissue, liver, lung, and myocardium, producing an enormous volume of distribution and an elimination half-life that can extend to many weeks, which explains both the delayed onset of full therapeutic effect, often requiring an oral loading strategy, and the prolonged persistence of drug and its active metabolite desethylamiodarone in the body for months after discontinuation. Amiodarone is hepatically metabolized rather than primarily renally cleared, so renal function has comparatively little effect on its clearance. Its active metabolite is not short-lived, and its extensive tissue distribution is a direct consequence of high, not low, lipophilicity.
  97. The correct answer is C (Sotalol is renally cleared, so accumulation increases the risk of QT prolongation.): Unlike most other antiarrhythmic drugs, sotalol is eliminated almost entirely unchanged by the kidneys with minimal hepatic metabolism, so reduced renal clearance leads to drug accumulation, higher plasma concentrations, and a substantially increased risk of excessive QT prolongation and torsades de pointes. Dosing intervals must therefore be lengthened, and doses reduced, in proportion to the degree of renal impairment, with the drug generally avoided or used with extreme caution at very low creatinine clearance. This makes sotalol dosing considerations meaningfully different from lipophilic, hepatically cleared class III agents such as amiodarone.
  98. The correct answer is B (Use-dependence causing more pronounced sodium channel block at faster rates.): Flecainide and other class Ic agents exhibit pronounced use-dependence, meaning their sodium channel blocking effect and resulting conduction slowing, reflected by QRS widening, become progressively greater as heart rate increases and channels cycle more frequently through the open and inactivated states that the drug preferentially binds. Exercise testing is therefore used to unmask excessive QRS widening or proarrhythmic conduction slowing that might not be apparent on a resting electrocardiogram, helping assess safety margins before the drug is used chronically. This use-dependent pattern is the opposite of reverse use-dependence, and it is not explained by renal clearance or metabolite formation changes during exercise.
  99. The correct answer is D (Multiple wavelet reentry sustaining fibrillation through continuous electrical activity.): The multiple wavelet hypothesis, originally proposed by Moe, describes atrial fibrillation as being maintained by numerous simultaneous, independently propagating reentrant wavelets that continuously collide, extinguish, and regenerate across the atria, producing continuous, disorganized electrical activity without dependence on any single stable circuit or focal driver, a concept later linked to the complex fractionated electrograms recorded at sites of wavefront collision and slow conduction. This contrasts with the single stable rotor or focal driver theories that have also been proposed as alternative or complementary mechanisms in different patients. A single macroreentrant circuit or a single focal source would instead be expected to produce a more organized rhythm such as atrial flutter or focal atrial tachycardia.
  100. The correct answer is A (Cascade genetic testing for the identified familial mutation in relatives.): When a pathogenic mutation is identified through molecular autopsy in a sudden cardiac death victim, cascade genetic testing for that specific familial variant should be offered to first-degree relatives, since a normal resting electrocardiogram does not exclude catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia, which classically presents with exercise-induced arrhythmia and a structurally and electrocardiographically normal heart at rest. Relatives who test positive can then undergo exercise stress testing and be started on appropriate therapy such as beta blockade, while those who test negative can be reassured and spared unnecessary lifelong surveillance. Empiric treatment without testing and coronary angiography are not the appropriate next steps for this inherited arrhythmia syndrome.
  101. The correct answer is C (Patient movement or a loose lead is producing electrical artifact.): A patient who remains awake, conversant, and hemodynamically stable during an apparent ventricular fibrillation tracing cannot actually be in that rhythm, since true VF causes immediate loss of consciousness and pulse. Motion artifact or a loose electrode commonly produces bizarre, irregular baseline changes that mimic malignant arrhythmias on telemetry. The mismatch between the tracing and the patient's clinical stability is the key clue that the recording itself is unreliable. Confirming a rhythm strip against a simultaneous pulse check or repeat 12-lead ECG is standard practice before treating an apparently lethal arrhythmia.
  102. The correct answer is A (The alarm was a false positive; the underlying rhythm is normal sinus.): The rhythm strip shown demonstrates a P wave preceding every QRS complex with a normal rate and a normal PR interval, which is the definition of normal sinus rhythm. Telemetry alarms frequently trigger on artifact, baseline noise, or brief lead disconnection rather than a genuine change in rhythm. Because the captured strip does not show any conduction abnormality or ectopy, no arrhythmia is actually present despite the alarm. Confirming the alarm against the actual recorded tracing before acting prevents unnecessary intervention for a spurious trigger.
  103. The correct answer is D (Depolarization of the His-Purkinje conduction system itself.): A sharp deflection recorded between the atrial and ventricular electrograms at the His bundle position, occurring at a consistent interval before ventricular activation, represents the His potential generated by depolarization of the His-Purkinje system. This signal is used to measure the AH and HV intervals and to localize the level of conduction disease during electrophysiology study. A far field ventricular signal would not appear as a discrete sharp spike positioned between the atrial and ventricular electrograms. Recognizing the His potential correctly is fundamental to interpreting intracardiac recordings during any diagnostic study.
  104. The correct answer is B (It rapidly builds a denser, more accurate map with less operator time.): Automated, AI-assisted mapping algorithms acquire and validate large numbers of points very quickly, producing a denser and more spatially accurate activation and voltage map than sequential manual point collection while shortening procedure time. These systems still rely on the operator to review electrogram morphology and confirm ablation targets rather than acting autonomously. Fluoroscopy use is typically reduced with 3D mapping in general but is not eliminated entirely, since catheter and sheath manipulation may still require imaging. Intracardiac electrograms remain essential, since the automated algorithm is built directly from those same recorded signals.
  105. The correct answer is A (Head-up tilt-table testing to reproduce a vasovagal response.): The prodrome of nausea, warmth, and visual graying while standing, followed by rapid full recovery, is classic for neurally mediated vasovagal syncope, and tilt-table testing is the noninvasive study designed to reproduce that hemodynamic response. Holter monitoring is more useful when episodes are frequent enough to be captured or when an arrhythmic cause is suspected rather than a clear vasovagal prodrome. Exercise testing targets exertional or ischemia-related syncope, which does not match this prolonged-standing presentation. Signal-averaged electrocardiography assesses substrate for ventricular arrhythmia risk and has no role in evaluating classic vasovagal syncope.
  106. The correct answer is C (Ventricular tachycardia originating from prior infarct scar tissue.): Atrioventricular dissociation together with capture beats and fusion complexes are highly specific findings that confirm a ventricular origin for a wide complex tachycardia, since they demonstrate independent atrial and ventricular activity with occasional supraventricular capture of the ventricles. A history of prior myocardial infarction further increases the likelihood of scar-related monomorphic ventricular tachycardia as the underlying mechanism. Supraventricular tachycardia with aberrancy or bundle branch block would typically show a fixed one-to-one atrioventricular relationship rather than dissociation. Antidromic tachycardia also conducts one-to-one and would not produce fusion or capture beats.
  107. The correct answer is D (Obtain a clinical grade ECG or extended monitor to confirm the diagnosis.): Smartwatch-based photoplethysmography and single-lead ECG algorithms have reasonable sensitivity for detecting an irregular rhythm suggestive of atrial fibrillation, but false positives occur, so confirmation with a clinical grade 12-lead ECG or a longer ambulatory monitor is recommended before committing to a diagnosis. Starting anticoagulation on an unconfirmed consumer device alert alone exposes the patient to bleeding risk without a confirmed indication. Dismissing the alert entirely ignores validated data showing these algorithms have clinically useful, though imperfect, accuracy.
