Proceeding directly to ablation is premature without first establishing a confirmed diagnosis and considering less invasive management.
  108. The correct answer is B (Sinus tachycardia driven by an underlying physiologic stressor.): A narrow QRS tachycardia with a normal upright P wave preceding every QRS complex, a rate below 150 beats per minute, and gradual variability with respiration and physiologic stress is characteristic of sinus tachycardia rather than a reentrant arrhythmia. Fever and anxiety are common physiologic drivers that increase sinus rate through increased sympathetic tone. Atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia and focal atrial tachycardia typically start and stop abruptly and lack this gradual rate variability. Atrial flutter with fixed conduction produces a very regular rate, usually near 150 beats per minute, without the variable P wave morphology seen here.
  109. The correct answer is D (The pacing site lies within the flutter circuit's critical isthmus.): Entrainment with concealed fusion and a post-pacing interval close to the tachycardia cycle length is the standard criterion confirming that the pacing site is located within the reentrant circuit, typically its critical isthmus. This finding predicts that ablation delivered at this exact location has a high likelihood of terminating the arrhythmia. A site outside the circuit would instead produce a post-pacing interval substantially longer than the tachycardia cycle length. A focal, automatic mechanism would not demonstrate entrainment with concealed fusion at all, since entrainment is a property specific to reentrant circuits.
  110. The correct answer is A (Skeletal muscle tremor artifact superimposed on normal sinus rhythm.): Parkinsonian tremor commonly produces fine, rapid baseline oscillations on the ECG that can closely mimic atrial fibrillatory waves, but a discernible regular P wave to QRS relationship and a regular pulse confirm that the underlying rhythm is actually normal sinus rhythm. True atrial fibrillation would produce an irregularly irregular pulse and no discrete P waves once the artifact is set aside. Multifocal atrial tachycardia requires at least three distinct P wave morphologies and an irregular rate, which is not described here. Recognizing tremor artifact prevents an unnecessary diagnosis of atrial fibrillation in patients with movement disorders.
  111. The correct answer is B (Implant a loop recorder for long-term continuous monitoring.): When symptomatic episodes are infrequent, occurring roughly every one to two months, a short duration monitor such as a 48-hour Holter is unlikely to capture an event, and an implantable loop recorder providing months to years of continuous monitoring is the appropriate next step. Repeating another short Holter monitor carries the same low yield as the first attempt given the infrequent symptom pattern. A resting ECG performed between episodes will almost certainly be normal and will not establish a diagnosis. Proceeding to an invasive electrophysiology study without first documenting the clinical rhythm during symptoms is premature and unnecessarily invasive.
  112. The correct answer is C (Bipolar recording subtracts far field signal shared by both poles.): Bipolar recording compares the voltage between two closely spaced electrodes, which effectively cancels out far field signals that are recorded similarly by both poles while preserving the sharp local, near-field activity unique to each electrode. Unipolar recording, measured against a distant reference, retains both local and far field components, producing a broader, lower-frequency appearance. Both signals came from the same catheter position, so a location difference does not explain the discrepancy. Unipolar electrograms are actually used specifically because they can detect local activity, including for identifying the earliest activation site during mapping.
  113. The correct answer is A (A mixed vasovagal response with both hypotension and bradycardia.): A tilt test response showing progressive hypotension followed by a marked drop in heart rate, together with reproduction of the patient's typical presyncopal symptoms, describes a mixed vasovagal response involving both vasodepressor and cardioinhibitory components. A pure cardioinhibitory response would show bradycardia as the dominant feature without a preceding significant fall in blood pressure. Postural orthostatic tachycardia syndrome is characterized by an excessive heart rate increase without significant hypotension or bradycardia, the opposite pattern seen here. Chronotropic incompetence refers to an inadequate heart rate rise during exertion and is not assessed by tilt-table testing.
  114. The correct answer is D (Atrial fibrillation with rate-related aberrant ventricular conduction.): An irregularly irregular rhythm with no discrete P waves and variable QRS morphology at a rapid rate is characteristic of atrial fibrillation, and the beat-to-beat QRS variability reflects rate-related aberrant conduction through the His-Purkinje system rather than a separate ventricular arrhythmia. Polymorphic ventricular tachycardia is typically a regular or near-regular rapid rhythm and is far less likely in a hemodynamically described palpitation presentation like this one. Multifocal atrial tachycardia requires visible discrete P waves of at least three morphologies, which are absent here. Atrial flutter classically produces a regularly irregular pattern with sawtooth flutter waves rather than a truly irregularly irregular baseline.
  115. The correct answer is C (Algorithms can misclassify noisy or far field signals as true tissue.): Automated point acquisition algorithms rely on programmed criteria to distinguish genuine local electrogram signals from noise, far field activity, or catheter instability, and these criteria can occasionally misclassify a fractionated or noisy signal as an acceptable mapping point. This is precisely why operator review of individual electrograms remains necessary even when using AI-assisted mapping. The system fully supports bipolar recording, which is central to how it builds voltage and activation maps in any cardiac chamber. Because misclassification can occur, these tools do not eliminate the need for the electrophysiologist to review and validate electrogram quality.
  116. The correct answer is B (A continuous 24 to 48 hour ambulatory Holter monitor.): Because symptoms occur almost daily and are too brief for reliable patient activation, a continuous recording Holter monitor over 24 to 48 hours is likely to capture multiple episodes without depending on the patient triggering a device. A patient-activated event recorder depends on the patient recognizing and responding to the symptom in time, which is unreliable for episodes lasting only seconds. A smartwatch used only during symptoms shares this same activation limitation and is not a continuous recording tool. An implantable loop recorder is reserved for infrequent episodes occurring less often than daily, which does not match this presentation.
  117. The correct answer is A (This precordial pattern strongly favors ventricular tachycardia as the diagnosis.): The absence of an RS complex in all six precordial leads is the first step of the Brugada criteria for wide complex tachycardia and, when present, is highly specific for ventricular tachycardia rather than supraventricular tachycardia with aberrant conduction. Supraventricular rhythms with aberrancy typically preserve at least one precordial lead with a recognizable RS pattern reflecting relatively normal ventricular activation. This finding alone is a validated diagnostic criterion and is not without significance. While an electrophysiology study can further characterize the mechanism, the ECG criterion itself is sufficient to guide an initial clinical diagnosis and management decision.
  118. The correct answer is D (Tilt-table testing is not indicated once a cardiac cause is established.): Tilt-table testing is intended to evaluate suspected neurally mediated or orthostatic causes of syncope and is not indicated once a clear structural cardiac cause, such as severe symptomatic aortic stenosis, has already been identified and explains the presentation. Pursuing tilt-table testing in this setting would not change management and could delay appropriate treatment of the underlying valvular disease. It is not a prerequisite for structural intervention, and no such requirement exists in standard practice. Tilt-table testing evaluates autonomic and hemodynamic response to postural change and cannot substitute for echocardiographic assessment of valvular anatomy and severity.
  119. The correct answer is C (Ventricular preexcitation from an accessory atrioventricular pathway.): A short PR interval combined with a slurred initial QRS upstroke, known as a delta wave, and mild QRS widening is the classic electrocardiographic signature of ventricular preexcitation caused by conduction over an accessory atrioventricular pathway. This pattern is distinct from left ventricular hypertrophy, which produces voltage criteria and repolarization changes without a delta wave or short PR interval. It is a genuine and reproducible electrical finding rather than a lead placement artifact, since the same short PR and delta wave would persist on repeat recording. Early repolarization produces J point elevation rather than a short PR interval and slurred QRS upstroke.
  120. The correct answer is B (Poor tissue contact reduces lesion durability at that site.): Contact force sensing catheters measure the actual mechanical force between the catheter tip and the myocardial wall, and consistently low readings indicate inadequate tissue contact, which is strongly associated with shallow, less durable ablation lesions and a higher risk of arrhythmia recurrence. Stable fluoroscopic position does not guarantee adequate contact, since the catheter can appear stationary while only loosely touching or oscillating against the tissue surface. Low contact force readings do not indicate catheter tip fracture, which would typically present with abnormal impedance or electrical signal loss. Similarly, low force alone does not indicate the catheter has entered a vessel rather than the atrial chamber.
  121. The correct answer is D (Atrioventricular dissociation confirming a ventricular origin for the rhythm.): Independent P waves marching through the tachycardia at their own slower rate, unrelated to the QRS complexes, demonstrates atrioventricular dissociation, which is one of the most specific ECG findings confirming that a wide QRS tachycardia is ventricular in origin. Retrograde one-to-one ventriculoatrial conduction would instead show a fixed relationship between each QRS and a following P wave rather than independent atrial activity. First degree block describes a prolonged but still linked PR relationship, which is not what is described here. Supraventricular tachycardia with bundle branch block would maintain a one-to-one relationship between P waves and QRS complexes rather than dissociating.
  122. The correct answer is A (Electrical interference from nearby powered equipment in the room.): A fine, uniform, high-frequency oscillation that is unrelated to patient movement and superimposed evenly across the tracing is the classic appearance of alternating current electrical interference, commonly from nearby powered equipment, rather than a physiologic signal. Coarse atrial fibrillation would produce irregular, variable amplitude fibrillatory waves rather than a perfectly regular high-frequency oscillation. Diaphragmatic or hiccup-related artifact tends to be intermittent and correlated with respiratory or diaphragmatic movement rather than continuous and uniform. A malfunctioning pacemaker would typically produce discrete pacing spikes rather than a continuous fine oscillation across the entire baseline.
  123. The correct answer is C (Review and reprogram detection algorithm sensitivity to reduce false alarms.): Automated detection algorithms in loop recorders can generate a substantial burden of false positive episodes from noise or oversensing, and the appropriate response is to review stored tracings and reprogram detection thresholds and algorithm parameters to reduce the false alarm rate while preserving sensitivity for true arrhythmias. Disabling automated detection entirely would sacrifice the device's main advantage of continuous monitoring for asymptomatic events. Explanting the device is an excessive response to a programming issue that can typically be corrected. Ignoring all stored data risks missing a true arrhythmic event within the same dataset, since some proportion of episodes may still represent genuine findings.
  124. The correct answer is B (Monomorphic ventricular tachycardia arising from prior infarct scar.): A regular wide complex tachycardia with uniform QRS morphology throughout, occurring in a patient with ischemic cardiomyopathy and a prior myocardial infarction, is highly consistent with monomorphic ventricular tachycardia arising from reentry around infarct-related scar tissue. This clinical context substantially raises the pretest probability of a ventricular mechanism compared with a structurally normal heart. Supraventricular tachycardia with aberrancy is less likely given the strong structural substrate for ventricular arrhythmia in this patient. Atrial fibrillation conducting over an accessory pathway would typically produce an irregularly irregular rhythm rather than the regular tachycardia described here.
  125. The correct answer is D (Dense scar representing the core of the arrhythmogenic substrate.): Bipolar voltage consistently below 0.5 mV is the standard threshold used to define dense scar on electroanatomic voltage maps, and such regions typically represent the core of the arrhythmogenic substrate responsible for reentrant ventricular tachycardia. Border zone tissue is instead defined by an intermediate voltage range, generally between 0.5 and 1.5 mV, reflecting a mixture of viable and scarred myocardium. Normal viable myocardium records bipolar voltages above 1.5 mV, well outside the range described here. While catheter instability can produce artifactually low readings, the description of multiple consistent points supports a true dense scar finding rather than a technical error.
  126. The correct answer is A (Negative precordial concordance strongly suggests a ventricular origin.): Negative precordial concordance, meaning all six precordial leads show a purely negative QRS deflection during a wide QRS tachycardia, is a highly specific finding for a ventricular origin, typically from a site near the cardiac apex activating the ventricles from below upward. This pattern has established diagnostic value and is not a benign or meaningless variant. It is not characteristic of an accessory pathway location, which would produce a different precordial transition pattern based on the pathway's anatomic position. Atrial tachycardia produces a narrow QRS complex under normal conduction, so precordial concordance of the QRS is not applicable to that diagnosis.
  127. The correct answer is B (Pulsed field ablation produces tissue injury through nonthermal electroporation.): Pulsed field ablation delivers brief high voltage electrical pulses that cause cell death through nonthermal electroporation rather than through the heat-based coagulation used by radiofrequency energy, which explains the absence of the gradual impedance rise and thermal-related electrogram changes typically seen with radiofrequency lesions. The observation described does not indicate catheter malfunction, since it reflects the expected mechanistic difference of this energy source rather than an equipment failure. Intracardiac electrograms remain fully usable to confirm loss of local signal amplitude after a pulsed field lesion, so this modality can still be electrically monitored. Absence of the radiofrequency-specific signal pattern does not mean the lesion failed to create durable tissue injury.
  128. The correct answer is C (An adhesive patch monitor worn continuously for two to four weeks.): An adhesive, wearable patch monitor provides continuous noninvasive recording for extended periods, typically two to four weeks, which substantially increases the yield for detecting paroxysmal atrial fibrillation after cryptogenic stroke compared with brief Holter monitoring, without requiring a surgical procedure. Repeating short 24-hour Holter monitors multiple times still provides limited cumulative monitoring time and lower diagnostic yield than one continuous extended recording. An implantable loop recorder offers the longest monitoring duration but requires a minor surgical implantation, which this patient wishes to avoid. A single resting ECG only captures the rhythm at one moment and has essentially no yield for detecting paroxysmal arrhythmia.
  129. The correct answer is D (Antidromic atrioventricular reentrant tachycardia using the accessory pathway.): In a patient with known Wolff-Parkinson-White syndrome, a regular, very rapid wide QRS tachycardia reflects antidromic atrioventricular reentrant tachycardia, in which conduction travels antegrade down the accessory pathway and retrograde back through the atrioventricular node, producing a fully preexcited, bizarre wide QRS complex. Orthodromic reentrant tachycardia conducts antegrade through the atrioventricular node and would therefore produce a narrow QRS complex rather than the wide morphology shown. Atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia with a bystander pathway is a much less common and less likely explanation than straightforward antidromic conduction in a known preexcitation patient. Sinus tachycardia would show a normal P wave and a much slower, more variable rate than the regular 210 beats per minute described.
  130. The correct answer is A (Review additional leads before concluding failure to capture is present.): A pacing spike that appears unaccompanied by a QRS complex on a single monitored lead may simply reflect that the resulting paced QRS complex is isoelectric or poorly visualized in that particular lead, so reviewing additional simultaneous leads is necessary before concluding true failure to capture is present. Reprogramming the pacemaker output before confirming an actual capture problem could expose the patient to unnecessary changes based on an artifact of lead selection. Assuming failure to capture and proceeding to device replacement without adequate confirmation is premature and could lead to an unnecessary invasive procedure. Disabling pacing entirely in a pacemaker-dependent patient without confirming the finding could be dangerous if pacing is actually functioning normally in reality.
  131. The correct answer is A (Proceed to invasive electrophysiology study with programmed ventricular stimulation.): In a patient with ischemic cardiomyopathy, reduced ejection fraction, and unexplained syncope after noninvasive monitoring is unrevealing, invasive EP study with programmed stimulation helps identify inducible monomorphic ventricular tachycardia as the syncope mechanism, guiding ICD decision-making. Guidelines support EP testing in this scenario given the high pretest probability of a ventricular arrhythmic cause. Empiric drug therapy or pacing without establishing a mechanism would leave the patient at ongoing risk of sudden death. Reassurance is inappropriate because structural heart disease substantially raises the likelihood of a life-threatening cause.
  132. The correct answer is C (Typical atrioventricular nodal reentry using dual AV nodal pathways.): A discontinuous jump in the AH interval during programmed atrial stimulation demonstrates dual AV nodal physiology, and tachycardia initiation immediately after the jump with septal atrial activation is the classic signature of typical slow-fast AVNRT. Orthodromic AVRT would instead show eccentric atrial activation over an accessory pathway rather than a septal pattern tied to nodal conduction. Automatic tachycardia would not depend on a critical AH jump for induction. Sinus node reentry produces a P wave morphology matching sinus rhythm, unlike the pattern described here.
  133. The correct answer is B (An accessory pathway is present and participating in the tachycardia circuit.): A His-refractory ventricular extrastimulus can only advance atrial timing if it reaches the atrium via a pathway other than the His-Purkinje-AV node axis, confirming the presence of an accessory pathway participating in the circuit. Pure AVNRT lacks a route for a His-refractory ventricular impulse to preexcite the atrium. Atrioventricular dissociation would show no consistent relationship between the extrastimulus and atrial timing. An automatic ventricular focus would not explain resetting of a supraventricular tachycardia by a properly timed extrastimulus.
  134. The correct answer is D (Aggressive stimulation protocols can nonspecifically induce polymorphic arrhythmia in normal hearts.): Triple extrastimuli, especially from multiple sites at short coupling intervals, are a nonspecific trigger that can provoke polymorphic ventricular tachycardia or fibrillation even in structurally normal hearts, limiting the diagnostic value of this particular result. Only induction of sustained monomorphic ventricular tachycardia with less aggressive protocols is considered a specific finding tied to a reentrant scar substrate. This nonspecific result does not by itself establish the cause of prior syncope. It also does not mandate ablation, since the induced rhythm may not reflect the patient's clinical arrhythmia.
  135. The correct answer is B (The critical isthmus of the reentrant ventricular tachycardia circuit.): Concealed fusion during entrainment combined with a post-pacing interval within 30 ms of the tachycardia cycle length and a stimulus-to-QRS interval matching the local electrogram-to-QRS interval are the classic criteria confirming a site lies within the protected isthmus of a reentrant circuit. A bystander site would produce a longer post-pacing interval that clearly exceeds the tachycardia cycle length. Automatic ectopy would not entrain with concealed fusion at all. Dense unexcitable scar could not be captured to demonstrate these pacing criteria.
  136. The correct answer is A (The cavotricuspid isthmus is a critical component of the flutter circuit.): A post-pacing interval equal to the tachycardia cycle length with concealed entrainment during pacing from the cavotricuspid isthmus confirms this region lies within the reentrant circuit, consistent with typical isthmus-dependent flutter. A focal mechanism would not permit concealed entrainment with a matching post-pacing interval. These findings direct ablation to the cavotricuspid isthmus itself, not the left atrial roof. Because the isthmus is confirmed critical, isthmus ablation is expected to be an effective and durable treatment.
  137. The correct answer is D (The pathway has a short refractory period conferring meaningful sudden death risk.): A shortest pre-excited RR interval of 260 ms during atrial fibrillation indicates rapid antegrade conduction over the accessory pathway, a marker of a higher-risk pathway capable of conducting atrial fibrillation fast enough to precipitate ventricular fibrillation. Pathways with this property do not have a favorable, negligible-risk profile. This measurement is one of the standard invasive methods for estimating antegrade refractory period, not an unusable finding. A short refractory period reflects rapid, not slow, conduction capability, so the pathway is not too slow to support antidromic tachycardia.
  138. The correct answer is C (This infra-Hisian block is an indication for permanent pacing.): Block occurring below the His deflection (HV block) reflects disease in the His-Purkinje system, which carries a risk of progression to complete heart block and is a recognized indication for permanent pacemaker implantation regardless of symptoms in many cases. AV nodal block above the His deflection, not infra-Hisian block, is the pattern generally considered benign and vagally mediated. Atropine enhances AV nodal conduction but does not reliably improve, and can even worsen, infra-Hisian conduction disease. This localization argues against a benign vagal mechanism.
  139. The correct answer is A (Greater contact force increases resistive heating and lesion depth at the tip.): Radiofrequency energy heats tissue directly in a thin rim immediately adjacent to the electrode through resistive heating, and better catheter-tissue contact improves current density and energy delivery, increasing lesion depth and volume. Contact force is a well-established determinant of lesion size, not an irrelevant variable. Radiofrequency ablation always relies on an initial zone of resistive heating with subsequent conductive heating into deeper tissue, and higher force does not eliminate resistive heating. Excessive contact force and power together, not reduced force, raise the risk of rapid overheating and steam pop.
  140. The correct answer is D (Steam pop from explosive tissue vaporization risking cardiac perforation.): A sudden sharp impedance rise with an audible pop indicates rapid intramyocardial steam formation and explosive microvaporization, known as a steam pop, which can cause a crater lesion, tissue disruption, and even perforation with tamponade. Coagulum formation typically produces a gradual, not sudden, impedance rise without an audible pop. This is not a benign generator artifact, since it reflects a real tissue event. It is a recognized complication rather than a sign of normal, safe lesion formation.
  141. The correct answer is B (Saline flow cools the electrode surface, limiting char and coagulum formation.): Continuous saline irrigation cools the electrode-tissue interface, preventing the surface overheating that causes char and coagulum, which allows higher power and longer duration delivery without impedance rise while achieving deeper lesions. Irrigation does not meaningfully change the catheter's electrical resistance as the mechanism of benefit. Stable tissue contact remains essential for effective lesion formation even with irrigation. The therapeutic current still passes through the myocardium via the electrode, not through the irrigant fluid itself.
  142. The correct answer is C (Stop energy delivery immediately at that site to prevent esophageal injury.): The esophagus lies in close proximity to the posterior left atrial wall, and a rapid rise in luminal temperature during radiofrequency delivery signals thermal injury risk, so energy delivery at that site should be stopped immediately to avoid esophageal injury and the rare but catastrophic complication of atrioesophageal fistula. Continuing or increasing power would worsen thermal injury rather than represent expected, benign behavior. Switching modalities mid-procedure without first stopping the current delivery does not address the immediate thermal risk.
  143. The correct answer is D (The impedance drop pattern shown indicates effective lesion formation.): A steady, gradual impedance decline of roughly 15 to 20 ohms over the course of a stable power application, as depicted, is the expected and desired pattern reflecting progressive tissue heating and effective lesion formation. An unchanged impedance would instead suggest poor contact or ineffective energy delivery, which is not what the chart shows. The chart shows continuous power delivery at a stable 30 watts rather than an interruption. A sudden loss of tissue contact typically produces an abrupt impedance rise rather than the smooth downward trend shown here.
  144. The correct answer is A (To confirm the desired electrophysiologic effect before permanent tissue destruction.): Cryomapping at a moderately cold temperature produces a reversible functional block that allows the operator to confirm loss of pathway conduction or arrhythmia termination without permanent injury, and if AV conduction is affected the application can be stopped before permanent damage occurs. This step is deliberately not intended to create a permanent lesion, unlike full cryoablation at colder temperatures. It does not measure contact force, which is assessed separately. The cooling itself does not warm tissue; it is testing for a reversible effect near a critical structure.
  145. The correct answer is C (Ice formation causes catheter tip adherence, improving positional stability.): As tissue freezes, ice crystal formation causes the catheter tip to adhere firmly to the endocardial surface, markedly improving catheter stability during energy delivery near a critical structure like the AV node, which reduces the risk of catheter displacement during the lesion. Cryoablation lesions are generally not deeper than radiofrequency lesions of comparable duration. Cryoablation reduces but does not eliminate all risk of AV nodal injury, since reversible or even permanent block can still occur. Fluoroscopic guidance is still routinely used during cryoablation catheter positioning.
  146. The correct answer is B (Terminate the freeze cycle immediately to prevent permanent phrenic nerve injury.): The right phrenic nerve runs in close proximity to the right pulmonary veins, and a weakening diaphragmatic response during phrenic nerve pacing is an early warning sign of impending nerve injury, so the freeze cycle should be terminated immediately to allow the nerve to recover before permanent palsy develops. Continuing the freeze despite this warning sign risks a permanent, not transient, injury. Increasing cooling would worsen rather than expedite safe completion of the procedure. Switching ablation modality at the same site does not address the urgent need to stop the ongoing thermal injury first.
  147. The correct answer is D (High-voltage electrical fields creating irreversible nanoscale pores in cell membranes.): Pulsed field ablation delivers brief high-voltage electrical pulses that create irreversible nanoscale pores in the cardiomyocyte cell membrane, a nonthermal mechanism called irreversible electroporation that disrupts cellular homeostasis and leads to cell death. This is fundamentally different from radiofrequency's resistive heating mechanism. It also does not rely on freezing or ice crystal formation like cryoablation. Pulsed field ablation is not a microwave-based heating technology, which distinguishes it from other energy sources entirely.
  148. The correct answer is A (Reduced risk of collateral thermal injury to the adjacent esophagus.): Myocardial tissue has a lower threshold for electroporation than surrounding structures such as the esophagus and vagal fibers, so pulsed field ablation can create effective lesions in the myocardium while substantially reducing the risk of collateral thermal injury to the nearby esophagus compared with radiofrequency or cryoenergy. This selectivity reduces but does not entirely eliminate the risk of pulmonary vein stenosis, since some cases have still been reported. Transmurality still depends on adequate energy delivery and contact, not on selectivity alone. Many operators still use esophageal monitoring as an added safety measure despite the improved safety profile.
  149. The correct answer is B (Transient coronary artery vasospasm requiring intracoronary nitroglycerin.): New inferior ST-segment elevation with hypotension immediately after energy delivery near the right inferior pulmonary vein, which resolves with intracoronary nitroglycerin, is characteristic of coronary artery vasospasm, a recognized complication of pulsed field ablation related to its electrical effect on vascular smooth muscle near the right coronary artery. Phrenic nerve palsy would present with diaphragmatic weakness, not ST elevation. Esophageal injury would not typically cause ST elevation or respond to nitroglycerin. Tamponade would present with pulsus paradoxus and echocardiographic effusion rather than resolving promptly with a vasodilator.
  150. The correct answer is C (Delivery outside the vulnerable period reduces the risk of inducing ventricular arrhythmia.): Because pulsed field ablation delivers strong electrical fields directly to cardiac tissue, energy is synchronized to deliver during the absolute refractory period, avoiding the vulnerable window of the cardiac cycle when an electrical stimulus could otherwise induce ventricular fibrillation. This synchronization does not change the power delivered per pulse train. It has no bearing on anesthesia requirements for the procedure. This is a genuine physiologic safety measure rather than merely a labeling formality.
  151. The correct answer is D (Electroporation achieves cell death within seconds rather than requiring sustained heating.): Because irreversible electroporation disrupts the cell membrane almost instantaneously with each pulse train, effective lesions can be created in a few seconds per application, unlike thermal energy sources that require sustained heating or freezing over many seconds to achieve lethal tissue temperatures. Catheter repositioning is still required between applications at different anatomic sites. Confirmation of pulmonary vein isolation with entrance and exit block testing remains standard regardless of energy source. Anesthesia requirements depend on institutional and patient factors rather than being strictly tied to energy modality.
  152. The correct answer is A (Deep general anesthesia with a neuromuscular blocking agent during energy delivery.): The high-voltage pulses used in pulsed field ablation stimulate skeletal and diaphragmatic muscle, producing significant patient movement, so general anesthesia with neuromuscular blockade is typically used to keep the patient still and prevent catheter displacement during energy delivery. Conscious sedation alone is generally inadequate because muscle contractions are common, not rare, with this energy source. Topical anesthesia has no effect on the deep skeletal muscle stimulation produced by the electrical field. Beta-blockade addresses cardiac rate and conduction, not skeletal muscle contraction from direct electrical stimulation.
  153. The correct answer is C (Successful crossing of the fossa ovalis into the left atrium.): Visible tenting of the fossa ovalis followed by sudden loss of tenting as the needle advances, combined with a pressure waveform transitioning to a typical left atrial contour, is the standard confirmation that the needle and sheath have successfully crossed into the left atrium. Free wall perforation would typically show loss of tenting with a pericardial rather than left atrial pressure waveform, along with echocardiographic evidence of effusion. Persistent right atrial position would show no change in pressure waveform at all. Aortic puncture produces a distinctly different, higher-pressure arterial waveform, not a left atrial contour.
  154. The correct answer is B (Inadvertent puncture of the aortic root instead of the interatrial septum.): Aspiration of pulsatile, bright red blood with a high-pressure arterial waveform during an anteriorly directed transseptal attempt is characteristic of inadvertent aortic root puncture, a recognized and potentially serious complication requiring immediate needle withdrawal and hemodynamic monitoring. Entry into the left atrial appendage would not produce a systemic arterial pressure waveform. Blood from a correctly crossed left atrium would show a left atrial, not high-pressure arterial, pressure contour despite being oxygenated. Coronary sinus puncture occurs with a posterior, not anterior, needle trajectory and produces a venous, not arterial, waveform.
  155. The correct answer is D (The needle has inadvertently entered a cardiac chamber rather than the pericardial space.): Aspiration of frank, pulsatile blood rather than the small clear or slightly serosanguineous volume expected from the pericardial space strongly suggests inadvertent puncture of a cardiac chamber, most often the right ventricle, requiring immediate recognition and careful management to avoid tamponade. Normal pericardial fluid aspiration would be minimal in volume and typically not frankly bloody in this context. This finding does not confirm correct pericardial placement, since chamber puncture is a distinct and more dangerous outcome. Proceeding as though access were successful without addressing this finding risks a serious, potentially fatal complication.
  156. The correct answer is C (Perform emergent pericardiocentesis to relieve cardiac tamponade.): Hypotension, pulsus paradoxus, elevated jugular venous pressure, and echocardiographic evidence of a new pericardial effusion with right ventricular diastolic collapse together confirm cardiac tamponade, which requires emergent pericardiocentesis to relieve pericardial pressure and restore hemodynamics. Diuretics would worsen hemodynamics by further reducing preload in a preload-dependent state. Vasopressors alone do not address the underlying mechanical compression and would only temporize while the patient deteriorates. These findings represent a genuine emergency, not an expected or benign occurrence during epicardial mapping.
  157. The correct answer is A (Early anticoagulation reduces the risk of thromboembolism from left atrial catheter manipulation.): Once catheters and sheaths are within the left atrium, the risk of thrombus formation on catheter surfaces and subsequent systemic thromboembolism, including stroke, rises significantly, so prompt therapeutic anticoagulation after transseptal crossing and maintenance of an adequate activated clotting time throughout the left atrial portion of the procedure is standard practice. Anticoagulation timing is directly tied to stroke risk, not irrelevant to it. Its primary purpose here is thromboembolic prevention, not femoral access site hemostasis. Delaying heparin until the procedure ends would leave the patient unprotected during the entire period of left atrial catheter manipulation.
  158. The correct answer is B (It provides real-time visualization of catheter-tissue contact and early complications.): Intracardiac echocardiography allows real-time visualization of catheter position relative to cardiac structures, confirms adequate tissue contact, and can detect early complications such as thrombus formation, pericardial effusion, or steam pop microbubbles well before they become clinically apparent. It does not remove the need for periprocedural anticoagulation, which is managed separately. Preprocedural imaging such as cardiac CT or transesophageal echocardiography still plays an important complementary role and is not replaced by intracardiac imaging. Intracardiac echocardiography visualizes anatomy and tissue effects rather than measuring electrical pacing thresholds.
  159. The correct answer is D (Image update frequency decreases, requiring reliance on mapping system data.): Lowering the fluoroscopic frame rate reduces the frequency of live image updates, meaning the operator sees catheter motion less continuously and must rely more heavily on electroanatomic mapping system data for real-time catheter position and cardiac geometry. Electroanatomic mapping accuracy itself is independent of fluoroscopic frame rate and is not degraded by this change. Reducing frame rate lowers, rather than eliminates, radiation exposure to both patient and staff. Lower frame rates reduce, not increase, cumulative patient radiation dose during a procedure.
  160. The correct answer is C (It identifies scar location and border zone tissue that may harbor arrhythmia substrate.): Late gadolinium enhancement on cardiac MRI identifies the location, extent, and transmurality of myocardial scar along with heterogeneous border zone tissue, which often harbors the slow conducting channels responsible for reentrant ventricular tachycardia, allowing this information to be integrated into procedural planning and image-guided mapping. It does not measure tachycardia cycle length, which requires actual induction and recording during electrophysiology study. It complements rather than replaces invasive voltage mapping, since the two provide correlated but distinct information. Cardiac MRI is performed before the procedure and does not provide real-time intraprocedural catheter guidance.
  161. The correct answer is A (Intrinsic sinoatrial node fibrosis reducing pacemaker cell automaticity and discharge rate.): Age-related fibrotic degeneration of the sinoatrial node is the most common cause of sinus node dysfunction and produces a slow, but normally conducted, sinus rhythm exactly as shown. Thyroid dysfunction and structural heart disease were excluded, narrowing the differential to intrinsic nodal disease. An ectopic atrial focus would produce an abnormal P wave morphology rather than the normal sinus P waves seen here, and there is no evidence of digoxin use or junctional escape.
  162. The correct answer is B (Discontinue the combined beta blocker and calcium channel blocker and reassess.): Combining a beta blocker with a nondihydropyridine calcium channel blocker produces additive suppression of sinoatrial automaticity and can mimic or unmask intrinsic sinus node dysfunction. Withdrawing the offending agents and reassessing rhythm before committing to permanent pacing avoids an unnecessary device for a reversible drug effect. Proceeding directly to pacing skips this reversible step, and atropine or isoproterenol are only appropriate as short-term measures, not maintenance therapy.
  163. The correct answer is D (Type II sinoatrial exit block with intermittent failure to conduct impulses.): When a pause equals an exact multiple of the baseline P-P interval, the sinus node is still firing on schedule but the impulse intermittently fails to exit and depolarize the atrium, defining type II sinoatrial exit block. A wandering pacemaker would show varying P wave morphology rather than dropped, but otherwise regular, beats. Complete sinus arrest produces pauses that are not simple multiples of the baseline cycle length, and an isolated atrioventricular block would not affect the sinus P-P timing itself.
  164. The correct answer is A (Tachycardia-bradycardia syndrome with a post-conversion sinus node recovery pause.): In tachycardia-bradycardia syndrome, rapid atrial rates overdrive-suppress the diseased sinus node, so termination of the tachyarrhythmia is followed by a prolonged pause before sinus activity resumes, producing the symptomatic dizziness described. This pattern is a form of sinus node dysfunction rather than an isolated arrhythmia occurring without any underlying conduction disease. Vasovagal syncope would not be temporally linked so consistently to arrhythmia termination, and there is no mention of digoxin use or atrioventricular block on the strip.
  165. The correct answer is B (Sinus arrest reflecting failure of sinus node impulse generation itself.): A pause that bears no fixed mathematical relationship to the baseline P-P interval indicates the sinus node itself failed to generate an impulse, defining sinus arrest, rather than a generated impulse simply failing to exit the node. Type II exit block instead produces pauses that are exact multiples of the sinus cycle length. First-degree block affects the PR interval, not P wave generation, and a nonconducted premature atrial contraction would typically show an early, often hidden, P wave altering the timing.
  166. The correct answer is C (Implant a permanent pacemaker to allow safe pharmacologic rate control.): Permanent pacing protects against symptomatic bradycardia and pauses, which then allows beta blockers or other rate-controlling agents to be safely uptitrated to manage the tachycardia component without causing presyncope. Discontinuing rate control leaves the patient symptomatic from uncontrolled atrial fibrillation, and simply increasing the beta blocker dose without pacing support would worsen the bradycardic episodes. Sinoatrial node ablation is not a standard first-line approach and would not address the atrial fibrillation itself.
  167. The correct answer is B (Long-term oral anticoagulation based on his calculated stroke risk score.): Stroke risk in atrial fibrillation is determined by the CHA2DS2-VASc score rather than by symptom control or arrhythmia burden, so anticoagulation remains indicated even after successful pacing resolves bradycardic symptoms. Stopping all therapy or assuming the pacemaker itself protects against stroke ignores that pacing addresses rhythm, not thromboembolic risk. Rate control medications may still be needed to manage residual atrial fibrillation, and antiarrhythmic therapy should be guided by symptoms and recurrence, not applied empirically regardless of outcome.
  168. The correct answer is A (Chronotropic incompetence from an inadequate heart rate response to exertion.): Chronotropic incompetence is defined by the failure to achieve an adequate heart rate increase during exercise, commonly measured as failing to reach 80% of the age-predicted maximum, and it is a recognized manifestation of sinus node dysfunction that can cause exertional fatigue with a normal resting rate. Atrial fibrillation would produce an irregular, not simply blunted, rate response, and there is no mention of a vasovagal trigger or beta blocker use to explain the finding.
  169. The correct answer is C (Rate-responsive sensor programming to increase pacing rate with exertion.): A rate-responsive sensor, most often accelerometer- or minute-ventilation-based, allows the pacemaker to increase paced rate in proportion to physical activity, directly compensating for chronotropic incompetence and preserving functional exercise capacity. Fixed-rate pacing would leave her unable to increase heart rate with exertion, exactly reproducing her presenting symptom. Magnet mode is used for temporary asynchronous pacing during device interrogation, not daily activity, and biventricular pacing addresses dyssynchrony rather than chronotropic response.
  170. The correct answer is C (It reflects delayed conduction, most often within the atrioventricular node.): First-degree atrioventricular block is defined by a prolonged PR interval with preserved one-to-one conduction and a normal QRS duration, and the delay most commonly localizes to the atrioventricular node itself rather than the infranodal conducting system. It does not represent any failure of conduction, since every atrial impulse still reaches the ventricle. A normal QRS duration argues against infranodal His-Purkinje disease, and an isolated prolonged PR interval in an asymptomatic patient is not diagnostic of acute infarction.
  171. The correct answer is A (Decremental conduction properties recover during the pause before conducting again.): Mobitz I, or Wenckebach, block occurs because the atrioventricular node exhibits decremental conduction that progressively fatigues with each successive impulse, and the pause following the dropped beat allows partial recovery of nodal conduction, producing a shorter PR interval on the first conducted beat afterward. This is a functional, often reversible, property of the atrioventricular node rather than permanent structural damage. There is no accessory pathway or alternating His-Purkinje pathway physiology involved in this classic pattern.
  172. The correct answer is B (Observation alone, since this pattern rarely progresses to complete block.): Mobitz I second-degree atrioventricular block typically localizes to the atrioventricular node, carries a benign prognosis, and rarely progresses to complete heart block, so asymptomatic hemodynamically stable patients can generally be observed without pacing. Immediate permanent pacing is not warranted for an asymptomatic, benign conduction pattern. Emergency temporary pacing and prophylactic high-dose atropine are reserved for symptomatic bradycardia or hemodynamic compromise, neither of which is present in this stable patient.
  173. The correct answer is D (Mobitz II block, since the dropped beat occurs without warning.): Mobitz II is characterized by a constant PR interval on all conducted beats with sudden, unpredictable failure of conduction, reflecting an all-or-none infranodal conduction defect rather than the progressive decremental fatigue seen in Mobitz I. Mobitz I instead shows progressively lengthening PR intervals that warn of the impending dropped beat. Complete heart block would show no relationship whatsoever between P waves and QRS complexes, and sinus arrhythmia does not cause dropped ventricular beats.
  174. The correct answer is A (Below the atrioventricular node within the infranodal His-Purkinje system.): Mobitz II block reflects an all-or-none conduction defect that localizes to the infranodal His-Purkinje system, and this pattern is especially concerning after anterior myocardial infarction because it suggests extensive septal conduction system damage with a high risk of progression to complete heart block. Sinoatrial exit block would affect P wave generation and timing, not the PR interval and QRS relationship. Atrioventricular nodal or atrial-level disease typically produces the decremental Wenckebach pattern rather than sudden, fixed-interval dropped beats.
  175. The correct answer is C (Permanent pacemaker implantation is indicated regardless of symptom presence.): Mobitz II block carries a substantial risk of sudden progression to complete heart block and asystole because of its infranodal, all-or-none conduction mechanism, so permanent pacing is recommended even in asymptomatic patients, unlike the generally benign Mobitz I pattern. Observation alone underestimates the real risk of sudden complete block. Beta blockers would further impair conduction and worsen the block, and there is no antiarrhythmic drug therapy that reverses an infranodal structural conduction defect.
  176. The correct answer is B (The block is likely infranodal, consistent with underlying bundle branch disease.): A wide baseline QRS complex in the setting of second-degree block with fixed PR intervals suggests underlying diffuse conducting system disease and supports an infranodal, Mobitz II-type mechanism, since Mobitz I typically occurs with a normal QRS and isolated atrioventricular nodal disease. It is not correct to attribute a wide QRS to Mobitz I, and QRS width is in fact a well-recognized clue to block localization. A regular dropped beat pattern with organized P waves does not represent ventricular tachycardia.
  177. The correct answer is D (Improvement in conduction ratio with vagolytic maneuvers such as atropine.): Because 2:1 block can occur at either the atrioventricular node or the infranodal system, dynamic maneuvers help localize it, and improved conduction with atropine, which reduces vagal tone and enhances nodal conduction, points toward a nodal level of block. A narrow QRS with normal conducted PR interval already favors a nodal site, but the response to atropine provides direct functional confirmation. Worsening with atropine, or a wide QRS with prolonged PR, would instead suggest infranodal disease.
  178. The correct answer is A (A nodal level of block, since increased sympathetic tone improves conduction.): Exercise increases sympathetic tone and reduces vagal tone, which enhances atrioventricular nodal conduction and can improve a nodal 2:1 block to 1:1 conduction; infranodal block instead typically worsens with exercise because the increased atrial rate further stresses diseased His-Purkinje tissue. It is incorrect to claim exercise always worsens infranodal disease as a universal rule while ignoring that improvement with exercise is a well-established localizing clue. This exercise response is directly informative, and there is no indication of separate sinus node disease here.
  179. The correct answer is C (A nodal block, since increased vagal tone worsens atrioventricular nodal conduction.): Carotid sinus massage increases vagal tone, which slows conduction specifically through the atrioventricular node, so worsening block ratios in response to this maneuver localize the block to the node rather than the infranodal system, which is relatively vagally insensitive. It is incorrect to attribute vagally mediated worsening to infranodal disease. Vagal maneuvers are a well-established bedside tool for localizing block level, and this response pattern is a classic and reliable finding.
  180. The correct answer is D (Third-degree atrioventricular block with an escape rhythm driving the ventricle.): Complete atrioventricular dissociation, with independent regular atrial and slower regular ventricular rates and no fixed PR relationship, defines third-degree atrioventricular block, in which a subsidiary escape pacemaker below the block sustains the ventricular rhythm. First-degree block still shows one-to-one conduction with a prolonged but consistent PR interval, which is not present here. Mobitz I would show progressively lengthening, related PR intervals before a dropped beat, and sinus arrhythmia does not cause dissociation between atrial and ventricular rates.
  181. The correct answer is A (A junctional escape focus located above the bifurcation of the bundle branches.): A narrow QRS escape rhythm with a relatively faster rate, typically 40 to 60 beats per minute, indicates the escape focus arises from the atrioventricular junction above the bundle branch bifurcation, allowing rapid conduction through the intact His-Purkinje system. A ventricular myocardial focus produces a wide QRS at a slower, less reliable rate, the opposite of what is described. The sinus node cannot be driving the ventricle across a complete block, and accessory pathways are not a source of escape rhythms.
  182. The correct answer is D (Pacing may still be warranted based on specific risk criteria over time.): Congenital complete heart block can remain asymptomatic for years, but pacing is generally recommended when specific risk features develop, including wide QRS escape, ventricular ectopy, prolonged QT interval, a daytime rate below roughly 50 beats per minute, or exercise intolerance, so ongoing monitoring rather than a permanent decision either way is required. Claiming a pacemaker is never required ignores these established risk criteria. Pacing is not contraindicated by a currently narrow, stable escape rhythm, and deferring the decision indefinitely without reassessment is inappropriate.
  183. The correct answer is B (A nodal block, since atropine acts by reducing vagal tone there.): Atropine works by blocking vagal parasympathetic input to the atrioventricular node, so improved conduction after its administration localizes the block to the node, which is rich in vagal innervation, rather than the infranodal system, which is largely vagally independent. Infranodal block typically shows no meaningful improvement, or can even worsen, with atropine because the increased atrial rate stresses diseased distal tissue. Atropine does not reliably restore conduction in complete heart block, and this response is in fact a well-established localizing tool.
  184. The correct answer is C (An infranodal block, since exercise increases stress on diseased distal tissue.): Exercise raises the atrial rate and sympathetic tone, which improves nodal conduction but paradoxically stresses diseased infranodal, His-Purkinje tissue by demanding faster conduction than it can sustain, so worsening block with exercise localizes the defect below the atrioventricular node. It is incorrect to claim exercise improves nodal block while ignoring that the opposite response occurred here. There is no indication of sinus node disease, since the atrial rate rose appropriately, and this exercise response is a well-validated localizing clue, not a nonspecific finding.
  185. The correct answer is B (The block is likely infranodal, since increased vagal tone typically impairs conduction distal to the AV node.): This is a classic teaching point read the OPPOSITE way from how it is commonly misremembered. Actually, carotid sinus massage slows the sinus rate through increased vagal tone, and a slower atrial rate reduces the rate-dependent conduction demand on diseased, fatigued infranodal (His-Purkinje) tissue that cannot keep up at faster rates, allowing 1:1 conduction to reappear. Direct vagal effects on the AV node itself would instead be expected to worsen, not improve, AV nodal block. Atrioventricular dissociation is not present here since conduction is intermittently normalizing, and carotid sinus massage is an established bedside diagnostic maneuver for this exact purpose.
  186. The correct answer is D (The block localizes to the atrioventricular node above the His bundle.): Prolongation of the atrial-to-His interval reflects delay proximal to the His bundle, within the atrioventricular node itself, while a constant, normal His-to-ventricular interval confirms that infranodal conduction is intact, together localizing the block definitively to the node. This directly contradicts localizing the block to the infranodal system, since the His-to-ventricular interval is unaffected. The recording is in fact diagnostic and does not require repetition, and ventricular myocardial tissue plays no role in the atrioventricular conduction delay described.
  187. The correct answer is B (Suppressed sinus node activity allowing a junctional escape rhythm to emerge.): Beta blockade can suppress sinus node automaticity sufficiently that the sinus node no longer captures the atria, allowing the atrioventricular junction to emerge as a subsidiary pacemaker, producing a regular narrow QRS rhythm around the typical junctional escape rate of 40 to 60 beats per minute. Third-degree block would require evidence of atrioventricular dissociation with visible, dissociated P waves, which are absent here. An accelerated idioventricular rhythm typically produces a wide QRS at a faster rate, and atrial fibrillation would be irregularly irregular, not regular.
  188. The correct answer is C (Accelerated junctional rhythm exceeding the intrinsic junctional escape rate.): A junctional rhythm faster than the intrinsic escape range of roughly 40 to 60 beats per minute, such as 75 beats per minute here, is termed an accelerated junctional rhythm, reflecting enhanced junctional automaticity rather than a true passive escape mechanism. Calling this a junctional escape rhythm mislabels the rate, since it clearly exceeds the escape range. Complete heart block requires atrioventricular dissociation, but retrograde P waves here indicate active retrograde conduction linking the junction to the atria, and sinus rhythm requires antegrade sinus P waves, which are absent.
  189. The correct answer is D (It arises from ventricular myocardium and is inherently unstable and slow.): A wide QRS escape rhythm at a slow rate below roughly 30 beats per minute typically originates from ventricular myocardial tissue distal to the His-Purkinje system, and such ventricular escape rhythms are inherently unreliable, slow, and prone to sudden failure, making them a medical emergency requiring urgent pacing. A junctional origin above the bundle branches would produce a narrow QRS at a faster rate, the opposite of this finding. This rhythm reflects failed, not intact, His-Purkinje conduction, and it is not a stable long-term solution.
  190. The correct answer is B (Accelerated idioventricular rhythm, a benign reperfusion arrhythmia marker.): Accelerated idioventricular rhythm is a regular wide-complex rhythm typically between 60 and 110 beats per minute that commonly appears transiently after successful reperfusion in acute myocardial infarction and generally resolves spontaneously without treatment or hemodynamic consequence. Sustained monomorphic ventricular tachycardia is usually faster and does not self-terminate as reliably or benignly. This rhythm is not related to atrioventricular block, since it reflects enhanced ventricular automaticity rather than an escape mechanism, and there is no polymorphic morphology or QT prolongation described to suggest torsades de pointes.
  191. The correct answer is B (Implant a permanent pacemaker for symptomatic sinus node dysfunction.): Documented sinus pauses that correlate with symptoms of presyncope and fatigue, in the absence of a reversible cause, meet a class I indication for permanent pacing in sinus node dysfunction. Electrophysiology study has poor sensitivity and specificity for sinus node dysfunction and adds little once symptom-rhythm correlation is already established. Starting a beta-blocker would risk worsening bradycardia rather than clarifying its cause. Simple reassurance is inappropriate given the clear correlation between a substantial pause and her symptoms.
  192. The correct answer is A (Chronotropic incompetence causing an inadequate heart rate response to exertion.): Failure to reach at least 80% of the age-predicted maximum heart rate during symptom-limited exercise, in the setting of exertional intolerance, defines chronotropic incompetence and is an accepted indication for rate-adaptive permanent pacing. Coronary disease and atrial fibrillation are excluded by the clinical evaluation and rhythm during testing. Beta-blocker toxicity would require the patient to be taking such a medication, which is not stated. This pattern falls well outside expected age-related change and reflects true sinus node impairment.
  193. The correct answer is D (Permanent pacemaker implantation for documented asystolic reflex syncope.): Recurrent reflex syncope with a documented spontaneous asystolic pause of 6 seconds or longer on loop recorder monitoring is a class IIa indication for pacing in patients over 40 years old. Counterpressure maneuvers and lifestyle measures remain useful adjuncts but are insufficient once significant asystole has been objectively documented. Midodrine primarily targets the vasodepressor rather than the cardioinhibitory component and would not address this pause. Fluid and salt loading alone does not treat a documented cardioinhibitory asystolic mechanism.
  194. The correct answer is C (Recommend permanent pacemaker implantation regardless of symptom status.): Mobitz type II second-degree atrioventricular block carries a substantial risk of progression to complete heart block and is a class I pacing indication even in asymptomatic patients, unlike Mobitz type I. Observation alone would leave the patient at risk for sudden high-grade block. Atropine acts primarily above the atrioventricular node and is unreliable and potentially harmful in infranodal Mobitz II block. Deferring for repeat monitoring delays definitive therapy for a condition already meeting an established indication.
  195. The correct answer is A (Alternating bifascicular block signifying advanced and unstable conduction disease.): Alternating bundle branch block, in which conduction shifts between different combinations of bundle and fascicular block, reflects diffuse and unstable infranodal disease and is a class I indication for permanent pacing regardless of symptoms. This pattern combined with near-syncope raises concern for impending complete heart block. It is not a benign variant and warrants pacing rather than observation. Neither isolated hypertrophy nor digoxin effect produces this specific alternating fascicular pattern.
  196. The correct answer is D (The lead is screwed into the septal tissue overlying the recorded His signal.): Permanent His bundle pacing uses a fixed-screw lead delivered through a specialized sheath and actively fixated into the septal tissue at the site where the largest His bundle electrogram is recorded, allowing direct capture of the conduction system. The right ventricular apex is the target for conventional myocardial pacing, not His bundle pacing. A retrograde transaortic approach and coronary sinus placement are techniques used for left ventricular access in other pacing strategies, not for His bundle lead delivery.
  197. The correct answer is B (A transition from nonselective to selective His bundle capture.): Nonselective His capture simultaneously depolarizes local ventricular myocardium and the His bundle, producing a fused, near-normal QRS with no isoelectric interval, while selective capture engages only the conduction system and shows a discrete isoelectric segment before the QRS as the impulse propagates through the His-Purkinje system. This is an expected output-dependent transition, not loss of capture, which would instead widen or change the QRS to a myocardial pattern. It does not represent a new bundle branch block or anodal capture.
  198. The correct answer is C (His bundle leads have a known risk of late threshold rise.): Compared with conventional myocardial or left bundle branch area leads, His bundle pacing leads carry a higher documented incidence of both acute and late threshold rise, sometimes severe enough to require lead revision or a backup ventricular lead, and this pattern should prompt close monitoring rather than being dismissed. Perforation typically presents with acute pain, hemodynamic change, or pericardial effusion rather than an isolated gradual threshold rise. Infective endocarditis would present with systemic signs of infection, not an isolated pacing parameter change.
  199. The correct answer is D (Lead micro-dislodgement away from the His bundle recording site.): A combination of a new bundle branch block pattern on paced beats, rising threshold, and falling sensed amplitude after His bundle pacing implantation is the classic signature of lead micro-dislodgement away from the conduction system, requiring lead repositioning. Interatrial septal perforation and coronary sinus stenosis are not relevant to a right ventricular septal His bundle lead. New atrial fibrillation would affect atrial sensing and rhythm regularity but would not independently produce this specific ventricular pacing parameter pattern.
  200. The correct answer is A (Recording of a discrete left bundle branch potential preceding the QRS.): During left bundle branch area pacing, recording a sharp, discrete potential preceding the local ventricular electrogram, along with a paced QRS showing a right bundle branch block pattern and a short and stable left ventricular activation time, confirms capture of the left conduction system. A persistent right bundle branch block pattern alone is expected during transseptal passage but does not by itself confirm conduction system capture. Failure to capture or a progressively widening QRS would instead suggest the lead has not reached the target and needs repositioning.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (It offers lower and more stable long-term pacing thresholds.): Left bundle branch area pacing has consistently shown lower and more stable acute and chronic capture thresholds and greater sensed R-wave amplitudes than His bundle pacing, along with a lower rate of lead revision, which has made it the preferred conduction system pacing strategy at most centers. The delivery systems and procedural complexity are broadly comparable rather than simpler for left bundle branch area pacing. Neither technique eliminates the risk of distal conduction disease below the pacing site, and both still require fluoroscopic guidance.
  2. The correct answer is B (Septal perforation with the lead tip protruding into the left ventricle.): Advancing a left bundle branch area pacing lead too deeply through the interventricular septum can result in septal perforation with the lead tip crossing into the left ventricular cavity, a recognized complication requiring lead repositioning or extraction to avoid thromboembolism and valve interference. This is distinct from coronary sinus dissection, which relates to left ventricular lead delivery for cardiac resynchronization rather than transseptal pacing leads. Right ventricular free wall perforation and tamponade present with pericardial fluid and hemodynamic compromise rather than septal crossing into the left ventricle.
  3. The correct answer is D (Conduction system pacing using the His bundle or left bundle branch area.): In patients anticipated to require a high burden of ventricular pacing, conduction system pacing preserves near-physiologic ventricular activation and has been shown to reduce the risk of pacing-induced cardiomyopathy compared with conventional right ventricular pacing. A longer atrioventricular delay may reduce pacing burden somewhat but cannot eliminate the need for pacing in complete heart block. Right ventricular septal pacing still produces non-physiologic activation and does not reliably prevent pacing-induced dysfunction. Raising the lower rate limit would increase, not decrease, the frequency of paced beats.
  4. The correct answer is A (It cannot reliably capture the conduction system distal to infra-Hisian block.): When conduction disease is located distal to the His bundle recording site, pacing at that site cannot bypass the block and fails to restore reliable conduction, which is a key practical limitation compared with left bundle branch area pacing, whose more distal capture site can bypass many proximal blocks. His bundle pacing does not necessarily produce a wider QRS than left bundle branch pacing when capture is successful, and it is typically performed under conscious sedation rather than general anesthesia. A backup right ventricular lead is commonly placed alongside a His bundle lead precisely because of this risk.
  5. The correct answer is C (A right bundle branch block pattern with a terminal R wave.): Because the lead tip lies within the left-sided conduction system after crossing the septum, activation proceeds from left to right, producing a right bundle branch block pattern with a terminal R wave in lead V1, which is a hallmark used to support correct left bundle branch area lead placement. A qR pattern resembling native left bundle branch block would instead suggest the lead has not reached the left conduction system. A dominant R wave in V1 without this pattern is more typical of right ventricular apical pacing. An isoelectric tracing throughout QRS would indicate failure to capture.
  6. The correct answer is B (Normal atrioventricular sequential pacing in a dual-chamber pacemaker.): A pacing spike immediately preceding a paced P wave, followed after a programmed atrioventricular delay by a second pacing spike preceding a paced QRS, is the expected appearance of normal atrioventricular sequential pacing when both intrinsic atrial and ventricular activity are absent or suppressed. Failure to sense would instead show pacing spikes falling on native P waves or QRS complexes rather than a consistent sequential pattern. Ventricular oversensing would cause inappropriate pauses or inhibition rather than this regular sequence. Pacemaker-mediated tachycardia produces a progressively rapid rhythm at the upper tracking rate, not this steady rate.
  7. The correct answer is A (Conduction system pacing targeting the His bundle or left bundle branch area.): In a patient with a narrow native QRS and an anticipated high pacing burden, conduction system pacing engages the native His-Purkinje system and preserves near-normal ventricular activation, avoiding the electrical and mechanical dyssynchrony produced by right ventricular apical or outflow tract pacing. Right ventricular apical and outflow tract pacing both bypass the conduction system and produce a wide, dyssynchronous paced QRS. Upfront biventricular pacing is generally reserved for patients with reduced ejection fraction or an existing indication for cardiac resynchronization rather than routine bradycardia pacing with normal baseline function.
  8. The correct answer is D (A single-chamber leadless pacemaker implanted directly within the right ventricle.): Given permanent atrial fibrillation with no need for atrial pacing, along with a history of recurrent transvenous device infection, a leadless pacemaker placed entirely within the right ventricle avoids the subcutaneous pocket and transvenous lead that are the principal infection risk factors in a conventional system. A conventional transvenous device on either side would carry the same infection risk factors that led to his prior extractions. Epicardial pacing is more invasive and reserved for special circumstances rather than routine reimplantation. A subcutaneous defibrillator does not provide bradycardia pacing support and would not address his syncope.
  9. The correct answer is C (Stable engagement of the self-expanding nitinol tines in myocardium.): Most leadless pacemakers are secured using self-expanding nitinol tines that engage the trabeculated right ventricular myocardium, and gentle tug testing before release confirms that these tines have achieved stable mechanical fixation. This differs from lead-based systems, which more commonly use an active helical screw mechanism rather than tines. Tricuspid annular position is assessed separately by fluoroscopic and electrogram criteria before deployment, not by the tug test itself. Air within the delivery system is managed by de-airing steps earlier in the procedure, not by mechanical tug testing.
  10. The correct answer is B (Elimination of lead fracture, venous occlusion, and pocket-related complications.): Because a leadless pacemaker has no transvenous lead and no subcutaneous pocket, it eliminates the lead fracture, lead-related venous occlusion, pocket infection, and pocket erosion complications that occur with conventional transvenous systems. Battery longevity for single-chamber leadless devices is generally comparable to, not clearly longer than, similar transvenous single-chamber devices. A single-chamber leadless pacemaker without an atrial component does not by itself provide atrioventricular synchrony. Cardiac perforation remains a recognized, though uncommon, risk during leadless device implantation rather than being eliminated.
  11. The correct answer is D (A single-chamber leadless device alone cannot provide atrioventricular synchronous pacing.): A traditional single-chamber ventricular leadless pacemaker paces only the right ventricle and cannot restore atrioventricular synchrony in a patient with an intact, functioning atrium who needs coordinated atrial and ventricular pacing, which historically limited leadless therapy to patients without this need or those with permanent atrial fibrillation. Femoral venous access is in fact the standard delivery route for leadless pacemakers, not a contraindication. Leadless pacemakers are actually well suited to, not contraindicated in, permanent atrial fibrillation when only ventricular pacing is required. All commercially available leadless pacemakers use catheter-based delivery rather than open chest surgery.
  12. The correct answer is A (They exchange implant-to-implant communication signals through the cardiac tissue itself.): Dual-chamber leadless pacemaker systems use beat-to-beat implant-to-implant, or i2i, communication in which electrical pulses are conducted through the cardiac tissue between the atrial and ventricular devices, allowing the ventricular device to track sensed atrial events and deliver atrioventricular synchronous pacing without a physical connecting lead. There is no shared external subcutaneous antenna or chest-worn relay device required for this beat-to-beat coordination. Neither device senses the surface electrocardiogram directly, since each device only senses local intracardiac electrograms at its own implant site.
  13. The correct answer is C (Retrieval remains feasible in many patients but grows technically harder over time.): Percutaneous retrieval of leadless pacemakers using a snare-based catheter technique has been demonstrated to be feasible even years after implantation, although the tissue encapsulation that develops around the fixation tines over time can make retrieval technically more challenging, so many centers choose to leave a depleted device in place and implant a second device alongside it. Retrieval is not simply impossible after one year, though difficulty does increase. It is generally accomplished percutaneously rather than requiring open surgery in most cases. Leadless pacemakers are metal and electronic devices that do not dissolve within the body.
  14. The correct answer is B (Proceed with the scan following the manufacturer's specific conditional protocol.): Most currently available leadless pacemakers carry an MR conditional label, meaning the scan can be performed safely as long as the manufacturer's specific scanning parameters and device settings are followed, similar to conditions used for MR conditional transvenous systems. Refusing the scan is unnecessarily restrictive when the device is confirmed MR conditional and appropriate protocols are followed. Surgical device removal is far more invasive than needed and is not standard practice before an MR conditional scan. A standard MRI suite is appropriate rather than an operating room, since general anesthesia is not required solely because of the device.
  15. The correct answer is A (Multiple leadless devices can safely coexist within the right ventricle.): Clinical experience and device labeling support leaving a depleted leadless pacemaker in place and implanting a second device at a separate right ventricular site, since multiple leadless devices can coexist safely without significant interaction, avoiding the added risk of an extraction procedure. There is no requirement or evidence that two coexisting devices achieve lower thresholds than a single functioning device. The original depleted device is simply left inactive rather than reprogrammed into an active backup role. Leaving devices in place does reduce certain procedural risks, but this is a general consideration rather than an absolute avoidance of all retrieval-related risk.
  16. The correct answer is D (Effective right ventricular pacing with the expected wide paced morphology.): A pacing spike consistently followed by a wide QRS with a left bundle branch block pattern, at a regular rate, is the expected appearance of effective conventional right ventricular pacing, since myocardial activation spreads slowly from the right ventricle to the left, producing this characteristic wide morphology. Failure to capture would instead show a pacing spike with no following QRS at all. Undersensing would produce spikes falling inappropriately close to native beats rather than a clean, regular paced rhythm. Pacemaker-mediated tachycardia produces a progressively accelerating rate rather than this steady baseline paced rate.
  17. The correct answer is C (Cardiac perforation with resulting pericardial effusion and tamponade physiology.): New hypotension, distended neck veins, and an acute pericardial effusion with signs of chamber compression shortly after leadless device deployment are classic findings of cardiac perforation progressing to tamponade, a recognized procedural complication that requires urgent pericardiocentesis and hemodynamic support. A vasovagal reaction would not produce a new pericardial effusion on imaging. Pulmonary embolism typically presents with hypoxia and right heart strain rather than a pericardial effusion. Contrast anaphylaxis would present with allergic or anaphylactic features such as urticaria or bronchospasm, not tamponade physiology.
  18.
