The correct answer is B (Appropriately functioning atrioventricular sequential pacing without any timing abnormality.): A regular, consistent sequence of an atrial pacing spike followed at a fixed appropriate interval by a ventricular pacing spike, with no dropped beats, extra spikes, or irregular timing, reflects normally functioning atrioventricular sequential pacing rather than a device problem, so her palpitations likely have another explanation that should be investigated separately. Device malfunction would typically show inappropriate pacing, failure to capture, or failure to pace on this same tracing, none of which are present. An undersensed premature contraction would appear as an irregularity disrupting the regular paced sequence. Cross-talk produces inappropriate inhibition of ventricular pacing by a sensed atrial signal, not this clean regular pattern.
  19. The correct answer is A (It can achieve resynchronization without depending on coronary venous anatomy.): Conduction system pacing corrects left bundle branch block by engaging the native conduction system directly and does not require cannulation of a coronary vein or depend on favorable coronary sinus branch anatomy, which can limit or prevent successful lead placement with conventional biventricular pacing. Randomized outcome data comparing conduction system pacing with biventricular pacing on hard mortality endpoints remain limited, so a guaranteed mortality advantage cannot be assumed. A backup right ventricular lead is still commonly used with His bundle pacing in particular. Postimplant optimization of pacing parameters is still generally performed with conduction system pacing, not eliminated.
  20. The correct answer is D (Proceed with permanent pacemaker implantation for documented asystolic syncope.): A documented spontaneous asystolic pause of 4 seconds or longer correlating with a syncopal episode, following the pattern established by the ISSUE trials, supports proceeding to permanent pacemaker implantation as a class IIa indication for reflex-mediated asystolic syncope. An antiarrhythmic drug would not address a bradyarrhythmic rather than tachyarrhythmic mechanism and carries unnecessary risk. Sinoatrial node ablation would eliminate rather than treat the sinus node and is not an accepted therapy for this condition. Continued monitoring alone is inadequate once a clear symptom-rhythm correlation with significant asystole has already been captured.
  21. The correct answer is A (The muscular sleeves of the pulmonary veins near the ostia.): Ectopic triggers for paroxysmal atrial fibrillation arise most often from myocardial sleeves that extend a few centimeters into the pulmonary veins, particularly the superior veins. Rapid firing from these sleeves initiates fibrillatory conduction across the atria, which is why pulmonary vein isolation is the cornerstone ablation strategy. The coronary sinus, superior vena cava, and ligament of Marshall are recognized but far less common trigger sites.
  22. The correct answer is B (Structural and electrical remodeling perpetuates substrate for reentry over time.): Sustained atrial fibrillation drives progressive atrial fibrosis, chamber dilation, and shortening of the effective refractory period, a process often summarized as atrial fibrillation begets atrial fibrillation. This structural and electrical remodeling creates a self-sustaining substrate that makes both spontaneous and pharmacologic termination progressively less likely. Vagal tone changes and AV nodal conduction do not drive the atrial substrate itself, and reduced automaticity would not explain persistence of a reentrant arrhythmia.
  23. The correct answer is C (Untreated obstructive sleep apnea causing recurrent nocturnal atrial stretch.): Obstructive sleep apnea causes repeated episodes of negative intrathoracic pressure, hypoxemia, and autonomic surges that stretch and remodel the atria, and untreated apnea is one of the strongest independent predictors of atrial fibrillation recurrence after ablation. Treating sleep apnea, typically with positive airway pressure, meaningfully improves ablation success rates. Hyperlipidemia, mild vitamin D deficiency, and well-controlled diabetes are not established drivers of atrial fibrillation recurrence to the same degree.
  24. The correct answer is D (They act as localized reentrant sources sustaining fibrillatory wavefronts.): In persistent atrial fibrillation, localized rotors or focal reentrant sources in remodeled atrial tissue can continuously generate fibrillatory wavefronts even after the original pulmonary vein triggers are removed. Targeting these sources is the rationale behind rotor-guided or substrate-based ablation strategies beyond pulmonary vein isolation. They are reproducible electrophysiologic phenomena rather than artifact, and while important they are not the sole mechanism in every patient.
  25. The correct answer is B (Atrial fibrillation with an irregularly irregular ventricular response.): The absence of discrete, organized P waves along with a chaotic undulating baseline and an irregularly irregular ventricular rate is the classic electrocardiographic signature of atrial fibrillation. Multifocal atrial tachycardia would show at least three distinct P wave morphologies with an isoelectric baseline between beats, and atrial flutter with fixed conduction would produce a regular ventricular rate. Frequent premature atrial contractions alone would not produce a continuously irregular baseline.
  26. Assuming the finding is artifact without review is inappropriate given the stroke risk implications. A subcutaneous defibrillator addresses ventricular arrhythmias, not atrial ones, and raising the pacing rate does not treat the underlying arrhythmia.
  27. The correct answer is D (Paroxysmal atrial fibrillation that has since self-terminated.): Absent P waves with an irregularly irregular RR interval that spontaneously converts back to sinus rhythm is characteristic of a self-terminating episode of paroxysmal atrial fibrillation, a common finding on implantable loop recorders placed to investigate cryptogenic stroke. Ventricular tachycardia would show a wide, typically regular QRS morphology rather than an irregular narrow rhythm. Artifact usually shows an implausibly erratic baseline without organized sinus beats framing it, and sinus arrhythmia preserves normal P waves before every QRS.
  28. The correct answer is C (A beta blocker or non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blocker.): In atrial fibrillation with preserved ejection fraction and no decompensated heart failure, beta blockers or non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers such as diltiazem or verapamil are the preferred first-line agents for rate control because they reliably slow atrioventricular nodal conduction at rest and with exertion. Amiodarone carries greater long-term toxicity and is reserved for refractory cases, flecainide is a rhythm-control agent that does not primarily slow the AV node, and digoxin alone is less effective during exertion.
  29. The correct answer is A (Flecainide or propafenone as a first-line class IC agent.): In patients without structural or ischemic heart disease, class IC agents such as flecainide or propafenone are preferred first-line rhythm-control drugs because they are effective and avoid the organ toxicity associated with amiodarone. Amiodarone is generally reserved for patients with structural heart disease or when other agents fail. Sotalol requires baseline and follow-up QT monitoring, and dofetilide must be initiated as an inpatient with continuous ECG monitoring because of torsades de pointes risk.
  30. The correct answer is C (Overall mortality is generally similar between the two strategies.): Landmark trials comparing rate control and rhythm control strategies in atrial fibrillation have generally shown similar overall mortality and stroke outcomes between the two approaches, particularly in older or minimally symptomatic patients, although early rhythm control has shown benefit in some higher-risk subgroups. Neither strategy removes the need for anticoagulation based on stroke risk score, since restoring sinus rhythm does not reliably eliminate thromboembolic risk. Rate control has not been shown to increase mortality compared with rhythm control.
  31. The correct answer is B (Rate control with a beta blocker and correction of triggers.): Postoperative atrial fibrillation after cardiac surgery is common and usually managed initially with rate control, typically a beta blocker, along with correction of reversible contributors such as electrolyte abnormalities, volume shifts, and pain or catecholamine surges. Most episodes resolve within the initial postoperative weeks as inflammation subsides. Immediate cardioversion is reserved for hemodynamic instability, permanent pacing is not indicated for isolated postoperative atrial fibrillation, and ablation is not performed emergently in this setting.
  32. The correct answer is D (It shows postoperative atrial fibrillation with an irregular response.): An irregularly irregular ventricular rate without discernible organized P waves in the early postoperative period after valve surgery is characteristic of postoperative atrial fibrillation, which occurs in a substantial proportion of cardiac surgery patients due to inflammation, atrial stretch, and autonomic changes. Sinus tachycardia would show a consistent PR interval, complete heart block would show a regular escape rhythm dissociated from atrial activity, and atrial flutter typically produces a more regular ventricular response when conduction is fixed.
  33. The correct answer is D (Perioperative beta blockade continued through the surgical period.): Continuing beta blocker therapy through the perioperative period is the most well-established and widely recommended strategy for reducing the incidence of postoperative atrial fibrillation after cardiac surgery, largely by blunting the catecholamine surge associated with surgery. Amiodarone can be used prophylactically in higher-risk patients but is typically reserved for those who cannot tolerate beta blockade, not started only after fibrillation occurs. High-dose diuretics and preinduction digoxin loading are not established prophylactic strategies.
  34. The correct answer is B (Anticoagulation should be considered based on his stroke risk score.): Postoperative atrial fibrillation, even when it converts back to sinus rhythm before discharge, carries an elevated risk of stroke and recurrence, and current guidance recommends basing anticoagulation decisions on the patient's calculated stroke risk score rather than assuming the episode was benign because it resolved. A score of 3 indicates a meaningful annual stroke risk that generally favors anticoagulation. Withholding anticoagulation solely because sinus rhythm resumed, relying on aspirin alone, or considering anticoagulation contraindicated are all inconsistent with current risk-based practice.

This allows most patients to eventually discontinue systemic anticoagulation after a short transitional period. Mitral valve replacement, subcutaneous defibrillator implantation, and inferior vena cava filters do not address left atrial appendage thrombus formation and are not indicated here.

  42. The correct answer is A (It shortens atrial refractoriness and promotes reentrant wavelet formation.): Heightened vagal tone shortens the atrial effective refractory period and increases dispersion of refractoriness, creating favorable conditions for multiple reentrant wavelets to form and sustain atrial fibrillation, which explains why vagally mediated atrial fibrillation often occurs during sleep or rest in athletes with high vagal tone. This is the opposite effect of what would stabilize sinus rhythm. Vagal tone primarily slows atrioventricular nodal conduction rather than increasing its automaticity, and it clearly does have measurable atrial electrophysiologic effects.

Wolff-Parkinson-White would show evidence of an accessory pathway rather than a purely toxin-triggered episode. Lone atrial fibrillation refers to fibrillation without an identifiable cause or structural disease rather than one with a clear alcohol trigger, and this episode was not permanent since it resolved.

Simply repeating the identical energy and pad configuration is unlikely to succeed a third time, and switching to lower-energy monophasic shocks would move in the wrong direction since biphasic waveforms are already more efficient. Reassurance without further evaluation ignores a clinically significant early recurrence, and there is no implanted device in this scenario to suggest malfunction.
  81. The correct answer is C (A single oral dose of flecainide or propafenone taken at onset.): The pill-in-pocket strategy uses a single supervised oral dose of a class IC agent such as flecainide or propafenone taken by the patient at the onset of symptoms, reserved for patients without significant structural heart disease who have been shown to tolerate the drug safely under observation. Daily maintenance amiodarone is a continuous therapy, not an as-needed self-administered approach. Intravenous ibutilide is not appropriate for unsupervised home use, and a continuous digoxin infusion is neither practical nor an effective conversion strategy.
  82. The correct answer is D (Cardioversion may proceed without first completing 3 weeks of anticoagulation.): A negative transesophageal echocardiogram excluding left atrial appendage thrombus allows cardioversion to proceed without waiting to complete the standard 3 weeks of pre-procedure anticoagulation, since the imaging directly confirms the absence of a thrombus source. This TEE-guided approach does not eliminate the need for periprocedural and post-cardioversion anticoagulation, so stopping anticoagulation entirely would be incorrect. The TEE result clearly does affect timing, since it is precisely what allows earlier cardioversion.
  83. The correct answer is D (Delivery of high-voltage electrical pulses causing nonthermal irreversible electroporation.): Pulsed field ablation delivers brief high-voltage electrical pulses that create nonthermal irreversible electroporation, disrupting cell membrane integrity and causing cell death without relying on heat or cold. This mechanism is fundamentally different from radiofrequency energy, which relies on sustained thermal heating to cause coagulation necrosis, or cryoballoon energy, which relies on prolonged freezing. Focused ultrasound is not the mechanism used by any of the standard clinically available ablation catheters described here.
  84. The correct answer is B (Cardiomyocytes have a markedly lower electroporation threshold than esophageal tissue.): Cardiomyocytes have a substantially lower electroporation threshold than esophageal smooth muscle and other surrounding tissues, so pulsed field energy delivered at doses sufficient to ablate atrial myocardium is largely below the threshold needed to injure the adjacent esophagus. This tissue selectivity is the key biophysical reason atrioesophageal fistula, a devastating complication of thermal ablation, is markedly less common with pulsed field energy. The esophagus is not surgically retracted during standard pulmonary vein isolation, and pulsed field energy is in fact routinely delivered near the posterior left atrium.
  85. The correct answer is A (The right phrenic nerve, which runs close to the right pulmonary veins.): The right phrenic nerve courses in close proximity to the right pulmonary veins, making it vulnerable to thermal injury during cryoballoon or radiofrequency ablation at that site, so pulsed field ablation's relative sparing of nerve tissue is particularly beneficial there. The mitral valve annulus, sinus node artery, and coronary sinus ostium are not the structures typically placed at risk during right-sided pulmonary vein isolation. This selectivity is the specific advantage that reduces phrenic nerve palsy rates during right pulmonary vein ablation.
  86. The correct answer is C (Total procedure time, since pulsed field lesions are delivered very rapidly.): Pulsed field ablation delivers each lesion in a fraction of a second per application, which can meaningfully shorten total procedure and ablation time compared with point-by-point radiofrequency delivery. It does not change the mandatory duration of post-procedure monitoring, the total duration of anticoagulation required, which is governed by stroke risk rather than ablation energy, or the timing of follow-up echocardiography. The efficiency benefit of pulsed field ablation is specifically procedural, not related to these downstream management decisions.
  87. The correct answer is B (The risk of phrenic nerve injury would be substantially lower overall.): Pulsed field ablation's relative tissue selectivity for myocardium over nerve tissue means the risk of phrenic nerve injury during right-sided pulmonary vein ablation is substantially lower than with cryoballoon or radiofrequency energy. Claiming the risk would be identical or somehow certain misrepresents the well-documented safety advantage of this energy source for nerve-adjacent structures. Pulsed field ablation can in fact be delivered safely near the phrenic nerve, which is precisely why this risk reduction is clinically relevant.
  88. The correct answer is D (Demonstration of both entrance block and exit block at that vein.): Durable electrical isolation of a pulmonary vein is confirmed by demonstrating both entrance block, meaning atrial signals no longer conduct into the vein, and exit block, meaning pacing from within the vein no longer captures the atrium. A reduction in fluoroscopy time is a procedural efficiency measure, not evidence of electrical isolation, and the patient's comfort during energy delivery says nothing about whether isolation was achieved. A drop in blood pressure during ablation is a potential complication, not an endpoint confirming successful isolation.
  89. The correct answer is C (Transient coronary artery vasospasm during energy delivery near a vein.): Pulsed field ablation delivered close to coronary arteries, particularly near the right inferior pulmonary vein adjacent to the right coronary artery, has been specifically reported to cause transient coronary vasospasm during energy delivery, a complication distinct from those typically seen with radiofrequency ablation. Describing permanent coronary occlusion or a guaranteed fistula as inevitable outcomes uses absolutist language that does not reflect the actual reported rarity and reversibility of this complication. Complete avoidance of coronary proximity is also not accurate, since some pulmonary vein ablation sites are inherently close to coronary structures.
  90. The correct answer is A (Anticoagulation should continue based on his baseline stroke risk score.): A normal sinus rhythm strip the morning after ablation does not establish that atrial fibrillation has been permanently eliminated, so anticoagulation decisions continue to be based on the patient's underlying stroke risk score rather than on the immediate post-procedure rhythm. Stopping anticoagulation immediately because sinus rhythm is present ignores both the risk of early recurrence and residual atrial myopathy. Anticoagulation was clearly indicated before ablation based on his risk profile, and switching to daily aspirin does not provide adequate stroke protection in atrial fibrillation.
  91. The correct answer is D (Mechanical hemolysis from the delivered high-voltage electrical pulse trains.): Pulsed field ablation has been specifically associated with mechanical hemolysis from the high-voltage electrical pulse trains themselves, producing elevated free hemoglobin and lactate dehydrogenase in the days following the procedure, and this laboratory pattern should prompt monitoring for downstream effects such as acute kidney injury. An unrelated coincidental autoimmune process is far less likely given the clear temporal relationship and the known mechanism specific to this energy source. Thermal red cell injury is a cryoballoon or radiofrequency phenomenon, not a pulsed field one, and there is no mention of a transfusion in this scenario.
  92. The correct answer is B (Pulsed field energy spares the esophagus due to its electroporation threshold.): Because cardiomyocytes have a markedly lower electroporation threshold than esophageal tissue, pulsed field ablation delivered at typical energies is far less likely to cause thermal esophageal injury, making continuous luminal temperature monitoring less critical than it is during thermal ablation modalities. Esophageal temperature probes are compatible with catheter-based ablation and are in fact standard practice during radiofrequency procedures. The esophagus does lie close to the posterior left atrial wall, and radiofrequency energy does pose a genuine thermal risk to it, which is exactly why this monitoring exists for that energy source.
  93. The correct answer is A (Recovery of conduction with reconnection of one or more pulmonary veins.): The most common finding on repeat electroanatomic mapping in patients with recurrent atrial fibrillation after an initial pulmonary vein isolation is recovery of conduction, meaning one or more previously isolated veins have electrically reconnected over time. Complete absence of atrial tissue at prior ablation sites is not a real physiologic finding, and a newly formed accessory pathway unrelated to the pulmonary veins is a rare and unrelated cause of recurrent arrhythmia. Spontaneous resolution of the original substrate would not explain ongoing symptomatic recurrence requiring redo ablation.
  94. The correct answer is C (Improved lesion durability by confirming adequate tissue contact at each point.): Real-time contact force sensing allows the operator to confirm adequate catheter-tissue contact throughout each radiofrequency application, which improves lesion durability and reduces the likelihood of gaps that can lead to conduction recovery and arrhythmia recurrence. It does not eliminate the need for post-procedure anticoagulation, which remains based on stroke risk, and it does not automatically convert atrial fibrillation to sinus rhythm during mapping. Contact force feedback also does not guarantee avoidance of phrenic nerve injury, since that risk depends on catheter location relative to the nerve rather than contact force alone.
  95. The correct answer is D (The posterior wall commonly harbors additional non-pulmonary-vein atrial fibrillation triggers.): The left atrial posterior wall shares embryologic origin with the pulmonary veins and commonly harbors additional non-pulmonary-vein triggers and substrate for atrial fibrillation, particularly in patients with more advanced or persistent disease, which is why posterior wall isolation is sometimes added to standard pulmonary vein isolation. Posterior wall lesions are not limited to isolated typical atrial flutter cases, and the posterior wall clearly does have embryologic and electrophysiologic relevance to fibrillation. Isolating the posterior wall is an addition to, not a replacement for, isolating the pulmonary veins themselves.
  96. The correct answer is C (The high-voltage electrical pulses used directly and nonselectively stimulate skeletal muscle, causing pronounced contractions with each application.): Pulsed field ablation delivers high-voltage electrical pulses that directly depolarize skeletal muscle throughout the body, producing pronounced, nonspecific muscle contractions with every application, which is exactly why most pulsed field ablation protocols require deep general anesthesia with reliable neuromuscular blockade for the entire case, more so than most radiofrequency cases. Pulsed field ablation is not performed under lighter sedation than thermal ablation; if anything, more reliable paralysis is typically needed. Radiofrequency does cause localized stimulation near nerves such as the phrenic nerve, but this differs from the widespread, pulse-synchronized muscle contraction seen with pulsed field delivery, and paralytics are used with either energy source when needed, not thermal energy exclusively.
  97. The correct answer is B (Pulsed field ablation is noninferior to thermal energy for freedom from recurrence.): Randomized trials comparing pulsed field ablation with conventional thermal energy sources for pulmonary vein isolation have generally demonstrated noninferiority for freedom from atrial arrhythmia recurrence, along with a favorable safety profile. This evidence does not show pulsed field ablation to be markedly less effective, and thermal ablation has not been entirely abandoned, since it remains a widely used and effective option at many centers. Substantial comparative trial data does exist and has informed the growing adoption of pulsed field technology.
  98. The correct answer is A (Optimize rate or rhythm control medically and observe through the blanking period.): Recurrences within the standard three-month blanking period after ablation are common and often reflect transient post-procedural inflammation rather than true long-term treatment failure, so the appropriate approach is to optimize rate or rhythm control medically and continue observation through the blanking period. Proceeding immediately to a repeat ablation this same week is premature before the blanking period has elapsed. Concluding the ablation has failed permanently and stopping anticoagulation, or referring emergently for surgical Maze, both overreact to an early and often self-limited recurrence.
  99. The correct answer is D (Early catheter ablation may reduce cardiovascular outcomes better than drugs alone.): Contemporary randomized trial evidence supports considering early catheter ablation as a first-line rhythm control strategy in appropriately selected patients with recent-onset atrial fibrillation, with some data suggesting improved outcomes compared with antiarrhythmic drug therapy alone. Ablation is not contraindicated until years of drug failure have occurred, and rhythm control strategies have demonstrated benefit in appropriately selected populations, contrary to the claim that they offer no benefit at all. Ablation is also not reserved solely for permanent atrial fibrillation, since early intervention in paroxysmal disease is an active area of favorable evidence.
  100. The correct answer is B (Continue anticoagulation based on his stroke risk score, not ablation success.): Even with documented freedom from atrial fibrillation recurrence on continuous monitoring, anticoagulation decisions continue to be based on the patient's calculated stroke risk score rather than on ablation success, because subclinical recurrences and residual atrial myopathy can still confer thromboembolic risk. Discontinuing anticoagulation entirely based on apparent ablation success is not supported by current evidence in a patient with an elevated risk score. Replacing anticoagulation with aspirin does not provide adequate stroke protection, and battery life of a monitoring device has no bearing on this clinical decision.
  101. The correct answer is C (Automated identification of anatomic structures to guide accurate catheter positioning.): AI-assisted electroanatomic mapping platforms are primarily intended to automate identification of cardiac anatomic structures and landmarks, helping the operator achieve more accurate and efficient catheter positioning during the procedure. These platforms do not replace the ablation catheter itself with an autonomous device, nor do they eliminate the need for operator supervision, since the physician remains responsible for procedural decisions and energy delivery. Automated administration of anticoagulation medications is not a function of electroanatomic mapping software.
  102. The correct answer is A (Relative tissue selectivity sparing vascular structures like the pulmonary vein wall.): Pulsed field ablation's relative tissue selectivity spares vascular structures such as the pulmonary vein wall itself, which is a key reason pulmonary vein stenosis, a known complication of excessive thermal injury to the vein wall, is considerably rarer with this energy source. Pulsed field catheters can absolutely reach and ablate at the pulmonary veins, so an inability to reach them is incorrect. Routine stent placement and mandatory avoidance of the vein ostia are not standard components of pulmonary vein isolation with any energy source.
  103. The correct answer is D (Reduced need for deep paralysis, allowing lighter sedation in many cases.): Because pulsed field ablation causes far less skeletal muscle stimulation than radiofrequency energy, it reduces the need for deep neuromuscular paralysis, which in many cases allows for lighter sedation rather than mandatory general anesthesia. General anesthesia is not required in one hundred percent of pulsed field ablation cases, and some form of anesthesia or sedation is still required, so the procedure is not performed without any anesthesia at all. There is no requirement that anesthesia be administered exclusively by the electrophysiologist rather than an anesthesia provider.
  104. The correct answer is A (Tissue selectivity sparing nearby structures combined with a shorter procedure time.): The combination of tissue selectivity, which reduces injury to structures such as the phrenic nerve and esophagus, and a shorter overall procedure time from rapid lesion delivery explains why pulsed field ablation has become a preferred first-line energy source at many centers. Describing it as having higher cost with no demonstrated advantage contradicts the accumulating safety and efficiency data supporting its adoption. Pulsed field ablation is delivered through standard catheter-based access rather than requiring open surgery, and it has demonstrated the ability to achieve durable pulmonary vein isolation in clinical trials.
  105. The correct answer is B (Creating a defined pattern of conduction block to eliminate reentrant circuits.): The Cox-Maze lesion set is designed to create a defined geometric pattern of conduction block throughout the atria, compartmentalizing the tissue so that reentrant circuits sustaining atrial fibrillation cannot be maintained. It does not involve removing the atria or replacing them with prosthetic material, nor does it consist of a single lesion confined to the aortic root, which is not even atrial tissue. The Maze lesion set extends well beyond the atrioventricular node alone and involves a comprehensive pattern across both atria.
  106. The correct answer is C (It addresses the arrhythmia during an already planned cardiac operation.): Performing a concomitant Maze procedure during coronary artery bypass grafting takes advantage of an already planned cardiac operation and existing surgical access, avoiding the need for a separate future procedure and its associated risks. It does not eliminate the need for postoperative anticoagulation, which continues to be guided by stroke risk assessment. There is no regulatory requirement mandating a Maze procedure whenever bypass surgery is performed, and the Maze procedure does not replace the coronary revascularization that is the primary indication for the surgery.
  107. The correct answer is D (Reduction of long-term ischemic stroke risk by removing the dominant thrombus source.): The left atrial appendage is the dominant source of thrombus formation in patients with atrial fibrillation, so surgical excision during a Maze procedure reduces long-term ischemic stroke risk by eliminating this primary thromboembolic source. It does not restore normal sinus node automaticity, which depends on the sinoatrial node rather than the appendage, and it does not eliminate the surgical need to isolate the pulmonary veins, which remains a separate component of the lesion set. Appendage excision also does not guarantee prevention of ventricular arrhythmias, which arise from an entirely different mechanism.
  108. The correct answer is C (Comparable lesion transmurality with substantially shorter operative time required.): Energy-based lesion creation using radiofrequency or cryothermal sources can achieve lesion transmurality comparable to the original cut-and-sew technique while substantially shortening operative time, which is the primary reason it has largely replaced cut-and-sew in contemporary surgical practice. Cut-and-sew is not the only technique ever proven effective, since it remains the historical gold standard, and cut-and-sew lesions are fully capable of achieving conduction block when performed correctly. Energy-based lesion sets still typically require cardiopulmonary bypass support in a full Cox-Maze procedure.
  109. The correct answer is A (Epicardial lesions are delivered thoracoscopically without opening the chest widely.): A minimally invasive thoracoscopic Maze approach delivers epicardial ablation lesions through small port incisions without the wide chest opening required for a full sternotomy Cox-Maze IV procedure. This standalone approach does not require simultaneous open mitral valve replacement, since it is typically reserved for patients without other structural indications for open surgery. It is not restricted to the original cut-and-sew technique and generally does not require general cardiopulmonary bypass support for its entire duration.
  110. The correct answer is B (Epicardial and endocardial approaches together can achieve more complete transmural lesions.): Combining a surgical epicardial lesion set with subsequent catheter-based endocardial mapping and touch-up ablation allows the two approaches to complement each other, achieving more complete transmural lesion sets than either technique alone, which is particularly valuable after a failed catheter-only attempt. Hybrid ablation does not eliminate the need for anticoagulation afterward, which continues to be based on stroke risk. The surgical portion alone is often not sufficient in patients with long-standing persistent disease, and this strategy is used for more advanced disease rather than exclusively for new-onset paroxysmal atrial fibrillation.
  111. The correct answer is D (Gaps in the lesion set allow residual conduction that can sustain reentry.): Incomplete transmurality leaves gaps in the lesion set through which residual myocardial conduction can persist, and these gaps can sustain or reinitiate the reentrant circuits responsible for atrial fibrillation recurrence after Maze surgery. Incomplete lesions do not reliably convert to complete transmural block over time on their own. Transmurality has a direct and well-documented relationship with long-term freedom from recurrence, and this issue specifically concerns atrial rather than ventricular conduction.
  112. The correct answer is C (Permanent pacemaker implantation to treat the resulting sinus node dysfunction.): Extensive atrial lesion sets created during a Maze procedure can injure the sinus node or its blood supply, producing symptomatic sinus node dysfunction with pauses, and this recognized complication is appropriately managed with permanent pacemaker implantation when symptomatic. Repeating the Maze surgery to remove the lesions would undo the intended therapeutic benefit and is not appropriate. High-dose antiarrhythmic therapy does not treat sinus node dysfunction, and observation alone is inadequate since this complication does not reliably resolve spontaneously.
  113. The correct answer is B (A regularized ventricular rate achieved through pacemaker-driven rhythm control.): After atrioventricular junction ablation, the native atrioventricular connection is permanently interrupted, so the pacemaker becomes the sole source of ventricular activation, and the rhythm strip demonstrates a regularized ventricular rate driven entirely by the device. This procedure does not restore native atrioventricular nodal conduction or sinus rhythm, since the junction has been intentionally and permanently ablated. There is no recurrence of the original uncontrolled rapid ventricular response, and the regular paced rhythm confirms the procedure succeeded rather than failed.
  114. The correct answer is A (Complete heart block is created intentionally, making the patient pacemaker dependent.): The ablate-and-pace strategy intentionally creates complete atrioventricular heart block by ablating the atrioventricular junction, permanently making the patient dependent on the previously implanted pacemaker for ventricular rate control. Only a partial slowing of conduction is not the intended endpoint, since a durable and complete block is required for reliable rate control. The atrial fibrillation itself is not eliminated by this procedure, since it addresses ventricular rate rather than the atrial arrhythmia, and normal atrioventricular conduction is not preserved.
  115. The correct answer is C (Conduction system pacing reduces the risk of pacing-induced cardiomyopathy over time.): Conduction system pacing, which activates the heart through the native His-Purkinje pathway, produces a more physiologic ventricular activation sequence than conventional right ventricular apical pacing and reduces the long-term risk of pacing-induced cardiomyopathy, which is particularly important in a patient who will be paced essentially continuously after junction ablation. Right ventricular apical pacing remains technically possible in adults and is not obsolete, but its dyssynchronous activation pattern is the concern here. Conduction system pacing does not eliminate the ongoing need for device programming and follow-up, and apical leads can still be placed safely when indicated.
  116. The correct answer is D (The permanent pacemaker should be implanted before the junction is ablated.): In a planned atrioventricular junction ablation for rate control, the permanent pacemaker is implanted first, and its function is confirmed, before the junction is deliberately ablated, ensuring the patient has reliable ventricular pacing support the moment complete heart block is created. Waiting until spontaneous complete heart block develops does not apply here, since the block is being intentionally induced rather than awaited. A pacemaker is required in this strategy because permanent heart block is the intended outcome, and a temporary external pacemaker cannot permanently substitute for an implanted device.
  117. The correct answer is B (Bradycardia-dependent polymorphic ventricular tachycardia related to sudden rate slowing.): Programming a higher base pacing rate for the first several weeks after atrioventricular junction ablation is specifically intended to prevent bradycardia-dependent polymorphic ventricular tachycardia, a recognized early complication related to sudden slowing of the ventricular rate and associated repolarization changes immediately after ablation. This programming strategy does not affect the underlying atrial fibrillation burden, since the atria are untouched by junction ablation. It does not prevent recurrence of native conduction through the ablated pathway, and it has no meaningful effect on battery longevity.
  118. The correct answer is A (Anticoagulation should continue based on her stroke risk score, unaffected by the ablation.): Atrioventricular junction ablation controls ventricular rate but does not eliminate the underlying atrial fibrillation, so her thromboembolic risk and the corresponding need for anticoagulation remain governed by her stroke risk score rather than by whether her rate is now well controlled. Anticoagulation should not be stopped simply because symptoms have improved, and switching to aspirin does not provide adequate stroke protection in atrial fibrillation. Anticoagulation was already indicated based on her risk profile independent of whether a pacemaker was implanted.
  119. The correct answer is D (Persistent symptomatic rapid rates despite optimized drugs, with rhythm control not pursued.): An ablate-and-pace strategy is most appropriate when a patient has persistent symptomatic rapid ventricular rates despite optimized pharmacologic rate control and rhythm control is either not desired or not being pursued, since this permanently and reliably resolves the rate problem. A first episode in an otherwise healthy young adult, atrial fibrillation that is already well controlled on a single low-dose medication, or a patient who has never even attempted rate-control therapy would all warrant less invasive management first rather than proceeding directly to junction ablation.
  120. The correct answer is C (Pacing thresholds and lead integrity, since she is now fully pacemaker dependent.): Because atrioventricular junction ablation has rendered her completely pacemaker dependent with no native ventricular escape rhythm, ongoing surveillance of pacing thresholds and lead integrity at every follow-up visit is essential, since any lead failure or loss of capture could be catastrophic. Junction ablation does not guarantee permanently normal device function, so device parameters must continue to be checked rather than assumed adequate. Anticoagulation status and heart failure medications remain important but do not substitute for the device-specific monitoring uniquely critical in a pacemaker-dependent patient.
  121. The correct answer is B (Macroreentry around the tricuspid annulus through the cavotricuspid isthmus.): The sawtooth flutter waves at approximately 300 beats per minute with 2:1 conduction are classic for typical, cavotricuspid isthmus-dependent atrial flutter, a macroreentrant circuit rotating around the tricuspid annulus. Option A describes focal atrial tachycardia, which shows discrete P waves with an isoelectric baseline rather than a continuous sawtooth pattern. Multiple wavelet reentry (option C) describes atrial fibrillation, producing an irregularly irregular rhythm without organized flutter waves. Pulmonary vein automaticity (option D) drives certain atrial tachycardias, not the macroreentrant circuit responsible for typical flutter.
  122. The correct answer is D (Typical cavotricuspid isthmus-dependent atrial flutter.): A regular sawtooth atrial deflection at 300 beats per minute with 2:1 AV block is the classic ECG signature of typical, cavotricuspid isthmus-dependent atrial flutter. Multifocal atrial tachycardia (option A) shows multiple distinct P-wave morphologies with variable PR intervals, not a fixed sawtooth pattern. Sinus tachycardia with a premature beat (option B) would show discrete sinus P waves rather than continuous flutter waves. Focal atrial tachycardia from the crista terminalis (option C) produces discrete P waves separated by an isoelectric baseline, unlike the continuous undulation seen here.
  123. The correct answer is A (A beta-blocker or nondihydropyridine calcium channel blocker for rate control.): When immediate cardioversion or ablation is not planned, initial management of atrial flutter focuses on rate control using an AV nodal blocking agent such as a beta-blocker or nondihydropyridine calcium channel blocker. Ibutilide (option B) is a rhythm-control agent for cardioversion, not a rate-control strategy, and should not be used without prior anticoagulation planning. High-dose digoxin alone (option C) is often insufficient for flutter rate control, especially with elevated sympathetic tone. Flecainide without AV nodal blockade (option D) risks dangerous 1:1 conduction by slowing the flutter rate.
  124. The correct answer is C (The cavotricuspid isthmus between the tricuspid annulus and inferior vena cava.): The cavotricuspid isthmus, bounded by the tricuspid annulus and the inferior vena cava, is the standard ablation target for typical atrial flutter because it forms the critical narrow conduction pathway in the macroreentrant circuit. The superior vena cava to right atrial junction (option A) is not part of the typical flutter circuit. The coronary sinus ostium (option B) lies near but is not itself the isthmus target. The fossa ovalis (option D) is a septal structure relevant to transseptal access, not typical flutter ablation.
  125. The correct answer is B (Anticoagulation guided by the CHA2DS2-VASc score, as in atrial fibrillation.): Atrial flutter carries a thromboembolic risk similar to atrial fibrillation, so stroke prevention decisions use the same CHA2DS2-VASc scoring system and thresholds. Claiming flutter carries negligible stroke risk (option A) is incorrect and could lead to preventable strokes. Aspirin monotherapy (option C) is not an adequate substitute for guideline-based anticoagulation in at-risk patients. Restricting anticoagulation only to episodes over 48 hours (option D) ignores the established risk-based approach used for flutter.
  126. The correct answer is A (Proceed with synchronized electrical cardioversion given the short, well-defined onset.): When atrial flutter has a clearly documented onset within the safe window and the patient is hemodynamically stable but symptomatic, prompt cardioversion is appropriate without the delay required for uncertain-duration arrhythmias. Deferring for 6 weeks (option B) is unnecessary when onset is well established and recent. Starting long-term amiodarone without attempting cardioversion (option C) delays restoration of sinus rhythm without added benefit. Proceeding directly to isthmus ablation before addressing the acute presentation (option D) is not the appropriate immediate step.
  127. The correct answer is A (It is generally more complex, often requiring detailed electroanatomic mapping of scar borders.): Atypical, scar-related atrial flutters, such as those seen after congenital heart surgery, arise from reentry around surgical scar rather than the cavotricuspid isthmus and typically require detailed electroanatomic mapping to define the circuit. Claiming it is identical to typical flutter ablation (option B) ignores the different, scar-dependent substrate. The scar does not simplify ablation by acting as a natural block (option C); rather, it complicates circuit identification. Ablation can often be performed safely in selected congenital heart disease patients at experienced centers (option D is false as stated).
  128. The correct answer is C (Focal atrial tachycardia arising from a discrete atrial site.): Discrete P waves separated by an isoelectric baseline, rather than a continuous undulating pattern, are characteristic of focal atrial tachycardia originating from a single atrial site. Atrial fibrillation (option A) would show an irregularly irregular ventricular response without organized discrete P waves. Typical flutter with variable block (option B) would still show continuous sawtooth flutter waves between conducted beats, not an isoelectric baseline. Complete heart block with an escape rhythm (option D) would show dissociated P waves and a slow, regular escape rate, not this pattern.
  129. The correct answer is A (An automatic focal atrial tachycardia with warm-up and cool-down.): Gradual acceleration at onset and gradual deceleration before termination, known as warm-up and cool-down, is a hallmark of automatic focal atrial tachycardia rather than a reentrant mechanism. An accessory pathway reentrant circuit (option B) typically starts and stops abruptly rather than gradually. Typical isthmus-dependent flutter (option C) maintains a fixed atrial rate rather than showing gradual rate change. Atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia (option D) is also characterized by abrupt onset, not gradual warm-up.
  130. The correct answer is B (A superior location, such as the crista terminalis or right superior pulmonary vein.): P waves that are positive in the inferior leads and negative in aVL suggest a superiorly located focus, consistent with sites such as the crista terminalis or right superior pulmonary vein region. An inferior left atrial site near the coronary sinus os (option A) would typically produce negative inferior lead P waves, opposite to this pattern. The left atrial appendage base (option C) and the inferior vena cava to right atrial junction (option D) are also inferior locations that would not produce this positive inferior lead morphology.
  131. The correct answer is C (A beta-blocker or nondihydropyridine calcium channel blocker as initial therapy.): First-line pharmacologic therapy for focal atrial tachycardia in a patient with preserved ejection fraction is a beta-blocker or nondihydropyridine calcium channel blocker before escalating to more potent antiarrhythmic agents. Empiric intravenous amiodarone as first-line outpatient therapy (option A) is excessive for initial management and carries greater toxicity risk. High-dose sotalol without trying simpler agents first (option B) skips the appropriate stepwise approach. Class IC agents without background rate control (option D) risk organizing the tachycardia into a slower, potentially 1:1 conducted rhythm.
  132. The correct answer is D (Deliver radiofrequency energy at the site of earliest activation.): Once activation mapping identifies the earliest atrial site preceding the surface P wave, radiofrequency energy should be delivered there since this typically represents the focal tachycardia's site of origin. Withdrawing the catheter to remap the coronary sinus (option A) abandons a validated finding without justification. Entrainment mapping for a protected isthmus (option B) applies to macroreentrant circuits like flutter, not to a focal automatic or triggered focus. Assuming artifact and searching only the left atrium (option C) ignores a clinically meaningful mapping result.
  133. The correct answer is D (Clinically correlate the finding and evaluate for anticoagulation and rhythm management.): A device-detected rapid, regular atrial electrogram with 2:1 ventricular conduction is consistent with atrial flutter and warrants clinical correlation, stroke risk assessment, and consideration of rate or rhythm control. Leadless systems with an atrial sensing component can detect such rhythms, so disregarding the finding (option A) is inappropriate. Reprogramming the device to ignore future atrial-rate alerts (option B) removes a valuable monitoring safeguard. Assuming artifact and waiting a full year to repeat the transmission (option C) risks delayed diagnosis and stroke prevention.
  134. The correct answer is A (An AV nodal blocking agent such as a beta-blocker.): Flecainide can slow the atrial flutter rate enough to permit 1:1 AV conduction, so a concurrent AV nodal blocking agent such as a beta-blocker is essential to prevent this dangerous acceleration in ventricular rate. Adding a second class IC agent (option B) increases proarrhythmic risk rather than addressing the 1:1 conduction concern. A high-dose loop diuretic (option C) has no role in preventing this specific conduction risk. An inhaled bronchodilator with no cardiac electrophysiologic activity (option D) would not protect against 1:1 conduction.
  135. The correct answer is B (Percutaneous left atrial appendage occlusion as an anticoagulation alternative.): For patients with a high stroke risk but recurrent major bleeding on anticoagulation, percutaneous left atrial appendage occlusion offers an alternative approach to reduce stroke risk without lifelong anticoagulation. Simply discontinuing anticoagulation with no alternative therapy (option A) leaves the patient unprotected against stroke. Switching to aspirin alone (option C) is not an adequate substitute for effective stroke prevention in a high-risk patient. Doubling the anticoagulant dose despite recurrent bleeding (option D) would worsen his bleeding risk rather than address it.
  136. The correct answer is C (Transesophageal echocardiography to exclude thrombus, or 3 weeks of anticoagulation, before cardioversion.): When atrial flutter duration is uncertain or exceeds 48 hours without prior anticoagulation, cardioversion should be preceded by transesophageal echocardiography to exclude thrombus or by at least 3 weeks of therapeutic anticoagulation, mirroring atrial fibrillation practice. Cardioverting immediately with no anticoagulation (option A) risks dislodging an atrial thrombus and causing stroke. Starting anticoagulation only after cardioversion (option B) does not address pre-procedural thromboembolic risk. A single aspirin dose immediately before the procedure (option D) provides inadequate stroke protection.
  137. The correct answer is A (Activation proceeds up the septum and down the free wall of the right atrium.): In counterclockwise, typical cavotricuspid isthmus-dependent flutter, the wavefront ascends the interatrial septum and descends the right atrial free wall as it circles the tricuspid annulus, viewed in the standard left anterior oblique projection. Descending the septum and ascending the lateral wall exclusively (option B) describes the reverse, clockwise pattern. Rotation entirely within the left atrium around the mitral annulus (option C) describes a different, atypical left-sided circuit. Confinement to the superior vena cava without annular involvement (option D) does not describe a macroreentrant flutter circuit.
  138. The correct answer is D (Vagal maneuvers followed by intravenous adenosine if vagal maneuvers fail.): Orthodromic AVRT depends on antegrade conduction through the AV node, so vagal maneuvers and adenosine, which transiently block the AV node, are appropriate and effective first-line therapies for terminating this regular narrow-complex tachycardia in a stable patient. Procainamide is more appropriate for irregular, pre-excited wide-complex tachycardia rather than this narrow-complex rhythm. Cardioversion is unnecessary in a stable patient who can be managed pharmacologically first. Amiodarone is not the preferred first-line agent when vagal maneuvers and adenosine are readily available and effective.

Typical AVNRT usually has a very short RP interval with the P wave often buried within or just after the QRS. He does not remain classified as pattern alone once a life-threatening arrhythmia has occurred. Isoproterenol is used here to stress conduction properties, not simply to measure blood pressure response. This testing has no role in assessing coronary artery disease. Pacemaker candidacy is not the purpose of confirmatory pathway testing after ablation.
  195. The correct answer is C (Disagree, since beta-blockade can preferentially slow the AV node and worsen conduction over the pathway.): Beta-blockers slow AV nodal conduction while having little effect on the accessory pathway, which can shift a greater proportion of the very rapid atrial fibrillation impulses down the pathway and increase the risk of ventricular fibrillation, making them inappropriate for pre-excited atrial fibrillation. Beta-blockers are not universally the preferred agent for every tachycardia, particularly this one. Simply increasing the dose does not address the underlying mechanism of harm. Beta-blockers clearly have measurable electrophysiologic effects on the AV node.
  196. The correct answer is D (Return of normal sinus conduction with the accessory pathway simply not manifesting now.): Cardioversion terminates the arrhythmia and restores normal sinus rhythm, but it does not eliminate the anatomic accessory pathway itself, and depending on relative conduction properties the pathway may or may not manifest a visible delta wave during normal sinus rhythm, so the underlying pathway is very likely still present and requires further evaluation. Electrical cardioversion does not ablate or permanently destroy accessory pathway tissue. There is no indication of new heart block on this description. A narrow QRS after cardioversion indicates restored sinus rhythm, not persistent atrial fibrillation.
  197. The correct answer is A (Conduction system pacing targeting the His bundle or left bundle branch area.): Conduction system pacing, which captures the His bundle or left bundle branch area, produces a more physiologic activation pattern and is increasingly preferred over traditional right ventricular apical pacing, especially in patients who will be fully pacemaker-dependent after AV node ablation, to reduce the risk of pacing-induced cardiomyopathy. Right ventricular apical pacing is no longer considered the universally preferred approach given this risk. Surgical epicardial pacing is reserved for specific circumstances, not routine AV node ablation cases. Temporary pacing is not an appropriate permanent solution after AV node ablation.
  198. The correct answer is B (Continued clinical observation without ablation, given the low-risk study results.): A long accessory pathway effective refractory period combined with non-inducibility of AVRT or atrial fibrillation during aggressive stimulation suggests a low-risk pathway, and continued observation without proceeding to ablation is a reasonable, guideline-supported option for such patients, particularly if they prefer to avoid an invasive procedure. Mandatory ablation is not required when the risk profile is reassuring. A subcutaneous defibrillator is not indicated for primary prevention in this low-risk pre-excitation scenario. Chronic AV nodal blocking therapy is not the definitive management strategy for an accessory pathway.
  199. The correct answer is D (It depolarizes the myocardium directly, terminating reentry regardless of the circuit involved.): Synchronized cardioversion works by simultaneously depolarizing a critical mass of myocardium, interrupting any reentrant circuit regardless of whether the AV node, accessory pathway, or both are involved, which is why it remains safe and effective across the different arrhythmia mechanisms seen in WPW. It is an electrical, not pharmacologic, intervention and does not act through sodium channel blockade. It does not act through the same selective AV nodal blocking mechanism as calcium channel blockers. Vagal stimulation is not the mechanism by which cardioversion terminates arrhythmias.
  200. The correct answer is C (Pre-excited atrial fibrillation, which can closely mimic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia.): Pre-excited atrial fibrillation can appear grossly irregular with varying QRS width and morphology because the degree of fusion between AV nodal and accessory pathway conduction changes from beat to beat, and this appearance can be mistaken for polymorphic ventricular tachycardia unless the underlying discrete QRS complexes and irregular atrial mechanism are recognized. True polymorphic VT would not typically show this same fusion-related variability tied to an atrial arrhythmia. Monomorphic VT has a single, not changing, morphology at a given axis. Torsades occurs with QT prolongation, which is not described here.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Yes, because a symptomatic tachyarrhythmia has now occurred with her pre-excitation.): WPW syndrome requires the combination of ventricular pre-excitation with any documented symptomatic tachyarrhythmia, and presyncope during a documented episode of pre-excited atrial fibrillation qualifies as a symptomatic event even without frank syncope. Complete loss of consciousness is not a required criterion for the diagnosis. Atrial fibrillation is directly relevant to accessory pathway classification because it can conduct rapidly over the pathway. A single documented symptomatic episode is sufficient, and recurrence is not required to establish the diagnosis.
  2. The correct answer is B (It is not reassuring, and further invasive risk stratification should be considered.): Persistence of the delta wave through peak exercise heart rate fails to demonstrate the reassuring abrupt block seen with a long pathway refractory period, so this result is not reassuring and typically prompts consideration of invasive electrophysiology study for more definitive risk stratification. It does not by itself definitively place the patient in the highest possible risk category, since exercise testing is an imperfect surrogate. It does not prove anything about the pathway's behavior during actual atrial fibrillation. It cannot substitute for the more definitive information an electrophysiology study can provide.
  3. The correct answer is C (Intravenous procainamide, since it slows conduction within the accessory pathway itself.): Procainamide is generally considered a first-line pharmacologic agent for stable pre-excited atrial fibrillation because it slows conduction and increases refractoriness within the accessory pathway itself, reducing the ventricular rate without the risk of preferentially blocking the AV node. Diltiazem and digoxin are both AV nodal blockers that are contraindicated in this setting. Adenosine has an extremely short half-life and does not reliably terminate atrial fibrillation, and it also acts on the AV node rather than the pathway.
  4. The correct answer is B (Orthodromic reentry using the AV node antegrade and an accessory pathway retrograde.): A VA interval longer than 70 ms and a V-A-V response to ventricular overdrive pacing, rather than V-A-A-V, confirm that an accessory pathway, not the AV node alone, forms the retrograde limb of the circuit. This narrow-QRS pattern with a long VA timing is classic for orthodromic AVRT, where the AV node conducts antegrade and the pathway conducts retrograde. AVNRT typically produces a VA interval under 70 ms and a V-A-A-V response because atrial activation depends on the AV node itself. An automatic focus would not entrain with a fixed V-A-V response.
  5. The correct answer is D (Atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia or an atrial tachycardia.): A V-A-A-V response after ventricular entrainment indicates that atrial activation is not obligately linked one-to-one to the paced ventricular beats, meaning the tachycardia circuit does not require the ventricle, which excludes an accessory pathway as the retrograde limb. This pattern is typical of AVNRT, where the AV node itself generates atrial activation, or atrial tachycardia, which is entirely independent of ventricular timing. Orthodromic AVRT instead produces a V-A-V response because the accessory pathway obligately links each paced ventricular beat to one atrial response. Confirming this distinction is essential before selecting an ablation target.
  6. The correct answer is A (Antegrade conduction over the accessory pathway with retrograde conduction elsewhere.): A regular wide-complex tachycardia whose QRS morphology matches the maximally pre-excited pattern indicates the ventricle is activated entirely through the accessory pathway, which defines antidromic AVRT. The retrograde limb completing the circuit is usually the AV node or, less commonly, a second accessory pathway. A bystander pathway conducting antegrade through the AV node would not produce a fully pre-excited QRS, and scar-related VT would not typically match the paced pre-excited morphology this closely. Recognizing this mechanism is essential because antidromic AVRT is easily mistaken for VT.
  7. The correct answer is C (Inadvertent complete heart block requiring permanent pacemaker implantation.): Anteroseptal and midseptal accessory pathways lie close to the compact AV node and His bundle, so radiofrequency energy delivered nearby carries a meaningfully higher risk of damaging normal conduction tissue and producing complete heart block. This risk is much lower for free wall pathways, which are farther from the conduction axis. Phrenic nerve injury is a concern for right free wall and epicardial pathways near the phrenic course, not septal sites. Many operators use lower power, shorter durations, or cryoablation at septal sites specifically to reduce this risk.
  8. The correct answer is D (The accessory pathway is located ipsilateral to the bundle branch block.): Coumel's sign describes tachycardia cycle length prolongation when bundle branch block develops on the same side as the accessory pathway, because ventricular activation must travel farther across the septum before reaching the pathway's ventricular insertion, lengthening the circuit. If the block were contralateral to the pathway, cycle length would remain essentially unchanged since conduction to the pathway is not delayed. This maneuver is a classic bedside tool for localizing an accessory pathway without invasive mapping. It does not indicate a mechanism change or a second pathway.
  9. The correct answer is B (Higher acute success rate, generally 95 percent or greater, with low recurrence.): Left free wall accessory pathways are the most favorable ablation targets, with acute success rates typically reported at 95 percent or higher and comparatively low recurrence, largely because stable catheter contact on the mitral annulus is readily achievable and the pathway lies well away from the AV conduction axis. This favorable profile contrasts with septal pathways, which carry a higher AV block risk despite generally still achieving good success. Catheter stability on the mitral annulus is actually a strength of this approach, not a limitation. Left free wall pathways are also well suited to a retrograde aortic or transseptal technique.
  10. The correct answer is A (A fasciculoventricular pathway connecting the conduction system to ventricle.): A fasciculoventricular pathway inserts distal to the AV node, connecting the His bundle or a bundle branch fascicle directly to ventricular myocardium, so its degree of pre-excitation and the HV interval remain fixed regardless of atrial pacing rate or AV nodal blocking agents like adenosine. A typical atrioventricular accessory pathway would show increasing pre-excitation with faster atrial pacing as AV nodal conduction slows relatively. These pathways do not participate in reentrant tachycardia because they cannot function as a retrograde limb from the ventricle back to atrium. They are generally considered benign and do not require ablation.
  11. The correct answer is C (Multiple accessory pathways, a finding strongly associated with Ebstein anomaly.): Patients with Ebstein anomaly have a markedly increased prevalence of multiple accessory pathways, often located along the abnormally displaced tricuspid annulus, and two distinct earliest activation sites with different delta wave morphologies are a reliable sign of two separate pathways rather than one variable pathway. Catheter artifact would not reproducibly generate two consistent, distinct activation patterns across the pacing protocol. A fasciculoventricular pathway has a single fixed insertion and would not produce two separate morphologies. Recognizing multiple pathways is important because ablating only one may leave the patient with persistent pre-excitation or recurrent tachycardia.
  12. The correct answer is B (An accessory pathway connects the ventricle to the atrium outside the AV node.): A His-refractory premature ventricular beat cannot conduct retrogradely through the AV conduction system to the atrium, since the His bundle is refractory at that moment. If it still advances the atrial electrogram, the impulse must have traveled to the atrium through an accessory pathway, definitively confirming its presence and its participation in the tachycardia circuit. This maneuver is one of the most reliable diagnostic tools for distinguishing AVRT from AVNRT during electrophysiology study. It does not by itself indicate whether the tachycardia will terminate.
  13. The correct answer is D (Because AV nodal blocking drugs can be harmful if this is pre-excited atrial fibrillation.): A regular wide-complex tachycardia in a patient with known pre-excitation could represent antidromic AVRT, but the differential must also include pre-excited atrial fibrillation, and AV nodal blocking agents such as adenosine, calcium channel blockers, digoxin, or beta-blockers can preferentially block the AV node while leaving the accessory pathway free to conduct rapidly, potentially accelerating ventricular response and precipitating ventricular fibrillation. Procainamide or synchronized cardioversion is generally safer when the rhythm cannot be confidently distinguished. Digoxin in particular can shorten accessory pathway refractoriness and is specifically avoided in pre-excited rhythms.
  14. The correct answer is A (Orthodromic AVRT terminating on the atrial side of the circuit.): Termination of a narrow-QRS tachycardia by adenosine with the atrial electrogram as the last event indicates the AV node, which adenosine transiently blocks, was the last component of the circuit to conduct, consistent with orthodromic AVRT in which the AV node forms the antegrade limb and the accessory pathway forms the retrograde limb. Ventricular tachycardia would not typically terminate with adenosine acting on the AV node, and the regular narrow QRS with a distinct retrograde P wave argues against a purely sinus mechanism. Multifocal atrial tachycardia produces an irregular rhythm with variable P wave morphologies, not this pattern.
  15. The correct answer is D (Cryoablation allows reversible cryomapping before committing to a permanent lesion.): Cryoablation permits a brief cryomapping application that can be reversed if AV conduction is affected, allowing the operator to confirm efficacy and safety at a septal site before delivering a full, permanent cryolesion, which reduces the risk of causing irreversible complete heart block near the compact AV node. Radiofrequency lesions form quickly and are generally not reversible once transmural injury occurs. Cryoablation lesions are actually often shallower than radiofrequency lesions, and procedure times can be longer due to the mapping and freeze-thaw cycles involved.
  16. The correct answer is C (Permanent junctional reciprocating tachycardia from an atypical pathway.): Permanent junctional reciprocating tachycardia, or PJRT, is caused by a slowly and decrementally conducting concealed posteroseptal accessory pathway, producing an incessant long RP tachycardia with negative P waves inferiorly and frequently leading to tachycardia-induced cardiomyopathy if unrecognized. Typical AVNRT produces a short RP tachycardia rather than a long RP pattern. Wolff-Parkinson-White syndrome requires a manifest, antegradely conducting pathway producing a delta wave, which is absent here since the pathway is concealed. Ablation of the pathway is curative and typically reverses the cardiomyopathy.
  17. The correct answer is B (Antidromic AVRT using two accessory pathways for antegrade and retrograde limbs.): When two accessory pathways are present, antidromic AVRT can use one pathway for antegrade conduction into the ventricle and the other for retrograde conduction back to the atrium, rather than relying on the AV node for the retrograde limb, and the atrial activation site matching pathway two's known location confirms this circuit. A single pathway cannot provide both limbs of an orthodromic circuit and antegrade conduction here is clearly pre-excited, ruling out orthodromic AVRT. AV nodal reentry would not depend on either accessory pathway for its circuit. Identifying both pathways before ablation prevents leaving a substrate for recurrent tachycardia.
  18. The correct answer is A (No ablation is needed because fasciculoventricular pathways rarely cause tachycardia.): Fasciculoventricular pathways insert distal to the AV node and cannot serve as a retrograde limb from ventricle to atrium, so they essentially never sustain reentrant tachycardia, and the inability to induce any tachycardia despite thorough programmed stimulation supports observation rather than ablation. Unlike typical atrioventricular accessory pathways associated with WPW syndrome, fasciculoventricular pathways are not linked to an increased risk of sudden cardiac death from rapid conduction during atrial fibrillation. Pacemaker implantation and anticoagulation are not indicated based on this finding alone. Reassurance and clinical follow-up are appropriate.
  19. The correct answer is C (It avoids crossing the mechanical aortic valve and diseased peripheral arteries.): A retrograde aortic approach requires advancing a catheter through the femoral artery, aorta, and across the aortic valve, which is unsafe in a patient with a mechanical aortic valve because of the risk of valve damage or thromboembolism, and is also more difficult with significant peripheral arterial disease. A transseptal approach reaches the left atrium and mitral annulus directly without crossing the aortic valve, avoiding both problems. Transseptal puncture actually addresses the mitral annulus, not the tricuspid annulus, and it still carries its own perforation risk, just a different one than the retrograde approach.
  20. The correct answer is D (Antidromic AVRT with the accessory pathway conducting antegrade.): A regular wide-complex tachycardia whose morphology exactly matches the patient's known maximally pre-excited QRS pattern strongly supports antidromic AVRT, in which the accessory pathway conducts the impulse antegrade into the ventricle while the AV node or a second pathway completes the circuit retrograde to the atrium. Termination with adenosine, which blocks the AV node, supports the AV node's participation as the retrograde limb in this case. Scar-related VT would not typically match a previously characterized pre-excited morphology this precisely, and torsades produces an irregular, polymorphic pattern rather than a monomorphic regular tachycardia.
  21. The correct answer is B (A second, previously unrecognized accessory pathway remains present.): A change in retrograde atrial activation sequence after successful elimination of one pathway's antegrade conduction, with tachycardia still inducible, indicates that a separate accessory pathway with a different atrial insertion site is now completing the circuit, since the originally targeted pathway's location would no longer match the new activation pattern. Coincidental new-onset AVNRT is possible in general but would not explain a change specifically tied to atrial activation sequence following ablation at a known site. Recovered conduction of the original pathway would reproduce the original activation sequence, not a different one. Careful remapping is needed to locate and ablate the second pathway.
  22. The correct answer is A (A VA interval this long favors AVRT over typical AVNRT.): A VA interval greater than about 70 ms measured from the onset of ventricular activation to the earliest atrial electrogram is a well-established criterion favoring orthodromic AVRT over typical AVNRT, since retrograde conduction through an accessory pathway to the atrium takes measurably longer to record than the near-simultaneous atrionodal conduction seen in typical AVNRT. Atypical fast-slow AVNRT can also produce a long VA interval, so this single measurement alone is not fully diagnostic of one specific mechanism, but it does clearly argue against typical AVNRT. It provides no direct information about a fasciculoventricular pathway.
  23. The correct answer is D (Ablate from within or adjacent to the coronary sinus diverticulum itself.): Some posteroseptal and left posterior accessory pathways have an epicardial course associated with a coronary sinus diverticulum, and ablation delivered from within or immediately adjacent to the diverticulum, sometimes using an irrigated catheter for better lesion depth, is often required to reach the pathway's actual course when standard endocardial sites fail. Simply increasing power at an endocardial site that has already failed is unlikely to succeed if the pathway's course is truly epicardial. Surgical division is reserved for rare cases where catheter ablation, including a coronary sinus approach, is unsuccessful, not as a first-line alternative.
  24. The correct answer is C (The ventricle is activated entirely through the accessory pathway alone.): During antidromic AVRT, the entire ventricular myocardium is depolarized through the accessory pathway with essentially no fusion contribution from AV nodal-His-Purkinje conduction, which is described as maximally pre-excited and produces the widest possible QRS morphology attributable to that pathway. In sinus rhythm, some degree of fusion between pathway conduction and normal AV node conduction usually occurs, producing a narrower, partially fused delta wave complex. A partially fused QRS would not be described as maximally pre-excited. This maximally pre-excited morphology is also the reference pattern used to identify antidromic AVRT on a 12-lead tracing.
  25. The correct answer is A (A left-sided accessory pathway, likely along the mitral annulus or free wall.): Earliest retrograde atrial activation recorded at the distal coronary sinus electrodes, which lie along the left atrioventricular groove, indicates an eccentric activation pattern originating from the left side rather than the septum, pointing to a left free wall or posterolateral accessory pathway near the mitral annulus. Septal pathways would produce earliest activation near the His bundle catheter instead, close to the true septal AV conduction axis. Typical AVNRT produces concentric activation that is earliest near the His bundle region, not eccentric activation from the coronary sinus. This localization guides catheter approach for subsequent ablation.
  26. The correct answer is B (These pathways are generally benign and typically do not require ablation.): Because a fasciculoventricular pathway inserts distal to the AV node and cannot form the retrograde limb of a reentrant circuit, it is not associated with an increased risk of tachyarrhythmia or sudden cardiac death in the way that a typical atrioventricular accessory pathway can be, and observation without ablation is the standard approach when no tachycardia is inducible. There is no established association between fasciculoventricular pathways and progression to atrial fibrillation. Pacemaker implantation has no role in the management of an isolated fasciculoventricular pathway. Reassurance is appropriate counseling in this scenario.
  27. The correct answer is D (Recovery of conduction across the originally targeted accessory pathway.): Recurrence of tachycardia mapping back to the same anatomic site previously ablated most commonly reflects recovery of conduction across the original accessory pathway, which occurs in a meaningful minority of cases, particularly if the initial lesion was not fully transmural or durable. Development of a completely new accessory pathway at the same location would be an unusual coincidence rather than the most likely explanation. A conversion to AVNRT would produce a different activation pattern and mechanism entirely, not one localizing to the same accessory pathway region. Repeat ablation targeting the same site is typically curative in this situation.
  28. The correct answer is C (Orthodromic AVRT using a concealed accessory pathway retrograde.): A narrow-QRS tachycardia with a short RP interval where a His-refractory ventricular extrastimulus still advances the next atrial electrogram confirms that the atrium is being activated through a pathway outside the normal AV conduction system, since the His bundle's refractoriness at that moment would have blocked any retrograde AV nodal conduction. This is the classic finding of orthodromic AVRT with a concealed accessory pathway. Typical AVNRT would not show atrial advancement from a His-refractory ventricular extrastimulus because its circuit does not include an extranodal pathway.
