Focal atrial tachycardia would not be affected at all by a ventricular extrastimulus timed to the His bundle.
  29. The correct answer is A (Antidromic AVRT incorporating two distinct accessory pathways in the circuit.): When retrograde atrial activation matches the known location of a second accessory pathway rather than the concentric septal pattern expected from AV nodal conduction, the circuit is using that second pathway as the retrograde limb while pathway A conducts antegrade, defining antidromic AVRT using two accessory pathways rather than the AV node. A single pathway cannot conduct in two directions simultaneously to form this pattern for orthodromic AVRT. If the AV node were the retrograde limb, activation would be concentric and earliest near the His region, not matching pathway B's site. This dual-pathway mechanism is particularly relevant in Ebstein anomaly given its association with multiple pathways.
  30. The correct answer is D (An accessory pathway is a necessary component of the reentrant circuit.): A His-refractory ventricular extrastimulus that advances atrial activation must have reached the atrium through a pathway outside the His-Purkinje system, and its ability to also terminate the tachycardia when it collides with and interrupts the circuit demonstrates that the ventricle, and specifically the accessory pathway connecting it to the atrium, is an obligatory part of the reentrant loop. If the tachycardia did not involve the ventricle, a ventricular extrastimulus could not terminate it this way. This maneuver is one of the most definitive diagnostic and mechanistic confirmations available during electrophysiology study for AVRT.
  31. The correct answer is B (Use lower power, shorter duration lesions with continuous His bundle monitoring.): When an accessory pathway lies very close to a prominent His bundle potential, the risk of collateral injury to the compact AV node and His bundle is high, so operators typically use reduced power and shorter application durations while continuously monitoring the His bundle signal and PR interval, stopping immediately if any AV conduction change occurs. Using maximum power in this location substantially increases the risk of causing complete heart block. Ignoring the His bundle signal removes the very safeguard needed to detect impending injury. Free wall pathway technique is not appropriate here given the fundamentally different anatomic risk.
  32. The correct answer is C (QRS morphology identical to the patient's known maximally pre-excited pattern.): When the tachycardia's QRS morphology exactly matches a previously documented maximally pre-excited pattern from that patient's own accessory pathway, this strongly favors antidromic AVRT because it demonstrates the ventricle is being activated through the known pathway rather than from an independent ventricular focus. AV dissociation, fusion beats, extreme axis deviation with precordial concordance, and a history of infarction with reduced ejection fraction are all classic findings that favor ventricular tachycardia instead. Absence of structural heart disease also makes VT statistically less likely, though it alone is not the strongest discriminating finding listed here.
  33. The correct answer is A (The pathway inserts beyond the point where AV nodal delay would apply.): A fixed, unchanging HV interval during both sinus rhythm and rapid atrial pacing indicates that conduction to the ventricle is not passing through the AV node's rate-dependent decremental delay, which happens when a fasciculoventricular pathway inserts distal to the AV node directly from the His bundle or a fascicle into ventricular myocardium. A typical manifest accessory pathway connecting atrium to ventricle would instead show a progressively shortening HV interval, or an unmeasurable one, as pre-excitation increases with faster atrial pacing. This finding does not indicate AV block or a need for pacing; it simply reflects the pathway's distal insertion point.
  34. The correct answer is D (Antidromic AVRT with the accessory pathway conducting antegrade.): A regular wide-complex tachycardia that precisely reproduces the maximally pre-excited QRS morphology recorded during pacing at the known accessory pathway site, without AV dissociation, is the hallmark presentation of antidromic AVRT, in which the pathway conducts antegrade into the ventricle and the AV node or a second pathway completes the retrograde limb. AV dissociation would instead point toward ventricular tachycardia as an independent ventricular rhythm. Idiopathic fascicular VT produces a characteristic right bundle branch block pattern with left axis deviation unrelated to a known accessory pathway morphology. Polymorphic VT would not produce a stable, reproducible regular morphology like this one.
  35. The correct answer is B (Success rates remain good, but the AV block risk is meaningfully higher.): Midseptal accessory pathways can generally be ablated successfully, but because of their close proximity to the compact AV node and His bundle, the risk of inadvertent AV block is meaningfully higher than for free wall pathways, and this elevated risk should be specifically discussed during informed consent. It is inaccurate to describe the risk as identical to free wall pathways, since the anatomic proximity genuinely differs. Midseptal pathways are not abandoned as unablatable; experienced operators routinely treat them successfully with careful technique. The AV block risk here is higher, not lower, than at other pathway locations away from the conduction axis.
  36. The correct answer is C (The finding confirms the pathway is ipsilateral to the right bundle branch block.): Cycle length prolongation when bundle branch block develops on the same side as the accessory pathway, known as Coumel's sign, occurs because ventricular activation must now travel across the septum before reaching the pathway's insertion, adding time to the reentrant circuit, and this finding is consistent with the previously identified right free wall pathway location. If the pathway were on the contralateral left side, right bundle branch block would not meaningfully affect cycle length. This maneuver does not suggest a second pathway; it is a well-established localization tool for a single known pathway. The result should not cause reconsideration of the pathway's side.
  37. The correct answer is D (Perform full activation mapping to identify a second, concealed pathway.): Persistent inducibility of a narrow-QRS tachycardia after successful elimination of a manifest pathway's antegrade conduction raises strong suspicion for a second, concealed accessory pathway that was not apparent on the baseline ECG since it never conducted antegrade, and full activation mapping is needed to identify and target it before ending the procedure. Concluding the case at this point would leave the patient with an unaddressed arrhythmia substrate. The rhythm is unlikely to be benign sinus tachycardia given that it was reproducibly induced with programmed stimulation. Repeating ablation at the original site is unlikely to help since that pathway's conduction has already been confirmed eliminated.
  38. The correct answer is A (Antidromic AVRT is uncommon, accounting for roughly 5 to 10 percent of AVRT cases.): Antidromic AVRT is genuinely uncommon, representing roughly 5 to 10 percent of AVRT cases in patients with WPW, while orthodromic AVRT using the AV node antegrade and the accessory pathway retrograde accounts for the large majority of AVRT presentations. It is not the most common form, and the two mechanisms do not occur with equal frequency. Antidromic AVRT can occur with a single accessory pathway, using the AV node as the retrograde limb, so multiple pathways are not strictly required for this mechanism, though a second pathway is an alternative retrograde route when present.
  39. The correct answer is B (Femoral venous access advancing the catheter to the right atrium.): Right free wall accessory pathways along the tricuspid annulus are readily reached through standard femoral venous access, advancing the ablation catheter through the inferior vena cava into the right atrium and then positioning it along the tricuspid annulus without needing to cross any valve or enter the left heart. Retrograde aortic and transseptal approaches are used specifically for left-sided pathways along the mitral annulus, not right-sided pathways. Epicardial access is reserved for pathways proven to have a true epicardial course that cannot be reached endocardially, which is not the typical situation for a standard right free wall pathway.
  40. The correct answer is C (The pathway conducts only retrograde and is therefore concealed on ECG.): A concealed accessory pathway is capable of conducting only in the retrograde, ventricle-to-atrium direction and has no antegrade conducting capability, so it never produces a delta wave or any visible pre-excitation on the surface ECG during sinus rhythm, even though it fully participates as the retrograde limb of orthodromic AVRT. This is distinct from a manifest pathway, which conducts antegrade and produces the classic WPW pattern. There is no indication of AV nodal damage or anesthesia dependence here. A fasciculoventricular pathway would not be confirmed by the His-refractory extrastimulus maneuver, which specifically demonstrates an atrioventricular, not fasciculoventricular, connection.
  41. The correct answer is D (Orthodromic AVRT requiring an accessory pathway as the retrograde limb.): A V-A-V response after ventricular overdrive entrainment, where each paced ventricular beat is obligately linked to exactly one atrial response, indicates the tachycardia circuit requires the ventricle and an accessory pathway connecting it back to the atrium, which defines orthodromic AVRT. Atrial tachycardia would show atrial activity independent of the ventricular pacing pattern, producing a V-A-A-V response instead. Typical AVNRT also typically produces a V-A-A-V response because the AV node itself, not an obligate ventricular link, generates the atrial activation. Ventricular tachycardia with variable conduction would not show this consistent one-to-one V-A-V linkage.
  42. The correct answer is A (A fasciculoventricular pathway from a standard atrioventricular accessory pathway.): A standard atrioventricular accessory pathway shows progressively increasing pre-excitation as atrial pacing rate increases and AV nodal conduction slows relatively more than pathway conduction, whereas a fasciculoventricular pathway inserts beyond the AV node's decremental delay and therefore shows no change in the degree of pre-excitation regardless of pacing rate. This behavior is specifically used to differentiate these two pathway types on electrophysiology study rather than to distinguish tachycardia mechanisms or pathway side. Neither orthodromic versus antidromic AVRT nor right versus left free wall pathway location is determined by this particular pacing maneuver.
  43. The correct answer is C (Low power titration, continuous PR interval monitoring, and cryomapping when available.): When ablating near the compact AV node, standard risk-reduction strategies include titrating radiofrequency power upward slowly from a low starting level, continuously monitoring the PR interval and junctional rhythm during energy delivery to detect early signs of AV nodal injury, and using reversible cryomapping when the equipment and pathway location make it feasible. Using maximum power without monitoring markedly increases the risk of causing irreversible complete heart block. Anesthesia type does not meaningfully influence this specific procedural risk. Avoiding all septal catheter contact would make ablating a septal pathway impossible, defeating the purpose of the procedure.
  44. The correct answer is B (Antidromic AVRT is more often associated with right free wall pathways.): Antidromic AVRT is disproportionately associated with right free wall and, to a lesser extent, right-sided accessory pathways compared with orthodromic AVRT, likely related to how antegrade conduction over these pathways interacts with AV nodal conduction timing during programmed stimulation. Posteroseptal concealed pathways, which cannot conduct antegrade, cannot participate in antidromic AVRT at all since that mechanism requires antegrade pathway conduction. Left free wall pathways can still cause antidromic AVRT, just less commonly than right-sided ones. There is in fact a recognized relationship between pathway location and the relative frequency of this mechanism.
  45. The correct answer is D (Because multiple pathways are common in Ebstein anomaly and surgery may alter access.): Ebstein anomaly carries a substantially increased prevalence of multiple accessory pathways along the malformed and often displaced tricuspid annulus, so thorough mapping to identify all pathways before planned tricuspid valve surgery is important, since surgical repair can distort anatomy and make later catheter access to any missed pathway more difficult. Ebstein anomaly is strongly associated with, not protective against, accessory pathways. Pacemaker implantation is not a routine requirement based on pre-excitation findings alone. Multiple pathways are well documented as common, not rare, in this specific population, making careful pre-surgical screening especially valuable.
  46. The correct answer is A (The tachycardia circuit requires the accessory pathway as the antegrade limb.): Progressive QRS fusion that increases with faster atrial entrainment pacing rates during a maximally pre-excited wide-complex tachycardia demonstrates that antegrade conduction is competing between the accessory pathway and the paced wavefront, confirming the pathway is an obligatory antegrade component of the tachycardia circuit, consistent with antidromic AVRT. A purely ventricular tachycardia unrelated to any pathway would not show this specific fusion behavior tied to pathway capture. AV nodal reentry with a bystander pathway would not require the pathway for antegrade conduction, so entrainment would not produce progressive pathway-related fusion in this way. A fasciculoventricular pathway does not participate in tachycardia circuits at all, so it could not underlie this entrainment response.
  47. The correct answer is B (Typical atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia with retrograde atrial activation.): A pseudo r-prime deflection appearing in V1 only during tachycardia represents the terminal portion of retrograde atrial depolarization superimposed on the QRS, a classic finding in typical slow-fast AVNRT because the very short VA interval places the retrograde P wave almost simultaneously with the QRS. Atrial flutter typically shows sawtooth flutter waves rather than a pseudo r-prime. Orthodromic AVRT usually produces a longer VA interval with the P wave visible after the QRS, not buried within it. Focal atrial tachycardia would show a distinct P wave morphology preceding each QRS with a variable PR interval.
  48. The correct answer is A (Atypical fast-slow AVNRT conducting antegrade over the fast pathway.): In fast-slow AVNRT, antegrade conduction travels down the fast pathway while retrograde conduction returns over the slow pathway, producing a long VA interval that exceeds the AV interval and earliest retrograde activation near the coronary sinus os where the slow pathway inserts. Typical slow-fast AVNRT produces the opposite pattern, a very short VA interval with fast pathway retrograde conduction. A posteroseptal accessory pathway would typically show eccentric left-sided activation rather than activation confined to the coronary sinus os region. Sinus tachycardia would show a P wave preceding, not following, the QRS with normal PR variability.
  49. The correct answer is D (Slow-slow atypical AVNRT using a second, left-sided slow pathway.): Slow-slow AVNRT uses a second slow pathway for retrograde conduction that often inserts near the mid-septal or left inferior extension of the AV node, producing earliest retrograde activation away from the classic coronary sinus os site and explaining failure of an ablation limited to the standard slow pathway target. Typical slow-fast AVNRT retrogradely activates the fast pathway near the His bundle region, not the mid-septum. A right free-wall accessory pathway would show eccentric activation on that lateral wall. Focal atrial tachycardia would demonstrate a P wave morphology and activation sequence independent of any ventricular pacing maneuver.
  50. The correct answer is C (Atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia with a short RP interval.): The rhythm strip shows a regular narrow-complex tachycardia with a very short RP interval consistent with typical AVNRT, where near-simultaneous atrial and ventricular activation buries the retrograde P wave within or immediately after the QRS. Atrial fibrillation would show an irregularly irregular ventricular response with no discrete P waves, not the regular pattern seen here. Multifocal atrial tachycardia requires at least three distinct P wave morphologies with an irregular rate. Ventricular tachycardia produces a wide QRS complex, which is absent in this narrow-complex strip.
  51. The correct answer is B (Transient block of antegrade conduction through the slow pathway.): Adenosine activates potassium channels and hyperpolarizes AV nodal tissue, transiently blocking antegrade conduction through the slow pathway, which interrupts the reentrant circuit at its most vulnerable point and terminates typical AVNRT. It does not suppress automaticity in atrial tissue, so it is not the primary mechanism for terminating true ectopic atrial tachycardia, which often persists or only transiently slows. Adenosine has minimal direct effect on ventricular myocardial refractoriness at standard doses. While adenosine can also block accessory pathway conduction in some cases, the dominant site of action relevant to AVNRT termination is the AV node itself, not a bypass tract.
  52. The correct answer is D (Increased parasympathetic tone slowing conduction and prolonging AV nodal refractoriness.): Vagal maneuvers such as the modified Valsalva increase parasympathetic outflow to the AV node, slowing conduction velocity and prolonging refractoriness in the nodal tissue that forms both limbs of the AVNRT circuit, which can interrupt reentry and terminate the tachycardia. Increased sympathetic tone would instead facilitate conduction and could sustain or accelerate the arrhythmia rather than terminate it. There is no direct mechanical compression of intracardiac tissue produced by these maneuvers. Coronary vasodilation is not the mechanism by which vagal maneuvers interrupt AV nodal reentry.
  53. The correct answer is A (Dual atrioventricular nodal physiology with a discrete slow pathway.): An abrupt jump in the AH interval of 50 ms or more with only a small decrement in the atrial extrastimulus coupling interval demonstrates a sudden switch from fast to slow pathway antegrade conduction, the electrophysiologic hallmark of dual AV nodal physiology that underlies AVNRT. Infra-Hisian block would prolong the HV interval or produce block below the His bundle, not an AH jump. Accessory pathways conduct independently of the AV node and would not produce this specific AH interval discontinuity. Humans have a single AV node, not a second independent node, so this description is physiologically inaccurate.
  54. The correct answer is C (A single reentrant beat through the dual AV nodal pathways.): An echo beat occurs when the impulse conducts antegrade down the slow pathway and returns retrograde up the fast pathway, producing one extra atrial depolarization before the circuit fails to sustain, confirming the anatomic substrate for AVNRT without inducing clinical tachycardia. This is not a premature ventricular contraction, since the sequence originates from atrial pacing and returns to the atrium through the reentrant nodal pathways, not from ventricular ectopy. Because tachycardia was not sustained, this does not represent an arrhythmia requiring cardioversion. Sinus node recovery is unrelated to this finding, which specifically reflects AV nodal reentry rather than sinus node function.
  55. The correct answer is D (Anatomic approach ablation of the posteroseptal slow pathway region.): The anatomic approach targets the slow pathway based on its typical location along the tricuspid annulus near the coronary sinus os and the tendon of Todaro, using fluoroscopic and anatomic landmarks rather than a specific electrogram signature, and is the standard technique for AVNRT ablation because it avoids the compact node and reduces AV block risk. Fast pathway ablation targets tissue closer to the compact node and carries a substantially higher risk of complete heart block, so it is rarely performed as first-line therapy. Cavotricuspid isthmus ablation treats typical atrial flutter, an unrelated arrhythmia substrate. Pulmonary vein isolation is the standard ablation strategy for atrial fibrillation, not AVNRT.
  56. The correct answer is B (Loss of retrograde ventriculoatrial conduction during junctional rhythm warns of impending AV block.): Accelerated junctional rhythm is expected and generally desirable during successful slow pathway ablation, but if retrograde ventriculoatrial conduction is lost during that junctional rhythm, it signals that energy delivery may be affecting the compact node or fast pathway, warning of impending complete heart block and prompting immediate cessation of ablation. Junctional rhythm reflects heating near the AV nodal region, not confirmed elimination of the fast pathway specifically. It indicates the catheter is near the compact node area, not that it has migrated into the atrium. Junctional rhythm during ablation is a procedural safety marker, not a predictor of long-term arrhythmia recurrence.
  57. The correct answer is A (Atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia rather than an accessory pathway circuit.): A ventriculoatrial-ventricular, or VAV, response after ventricular overdrive pacing indicates the atrium is not an obligatory part of the tachycardia circuit, favoring AVNRT since the reentry loop can continue confined to the AV node and ventricle without requiring the atrium. Orthodromic AVRT, which requires the atrium as part of the circuit through the accessory pathway, characteristically produces a ventriculoatrial-atrial-ventricular, or VAAV, response instead. Antidromic AVRT similarly requires atrial participation and would also produce a VAAV pattern. True atrial tachycardia does not depend on the ventricle at all and typically continues independent of ventricular pacing entrainment.
  58. The correct answer is A (Orthodromic tachycardia using a right posteroseptal accessory pathway.): During para-Hisian pacing, an unchanged retrograde atrial activation sequence and VA timing regardless of His bundle capture indicates that retrograde conduction is occurring over a septal accessory pathway near the pacing site, independent of the His-Purkinje system and AV node. If retrograde conduction instead traveled through the AV node alone, losing His capture would force the impulse to travel further through ventricular myocardium before reaching the AV node, changing the VA timing or activation sequence. A left lateral pathway would produce eccentric retrograde atrial activation earliest at the lateral left atrium, not a pattern unaffected by His capture. Atrial tachycardia from the crista terminalis would show a fixed P wave morphology independent of ventricular pacing maneuvers entirely.
  59. The correct answer is B (Atrioventricular nodal blocking agents such as beta-blockers or calcium channel blockers.): Beta-blockers or non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers slow conduction and prolong refractoriness within the AV node, the tissue that forms both limbs of the AVNRT reentrant circuit, making them reasonable first-line chronic pharmacologic therapy for patients who decline ablation. Loop diuretics have no direct antiarrhythmic effect on AV nodal reentry and are not indicated for this purpose. Class IC agents can be effective but are typically reserved as second-line therapy given their proarrhythmic risk, particularly without prior structural evaluation for ischemic disease. Digoxin has a narrow therapeutic window and is not preferred as first-line monotherapy for this indication in current practice.
  60. The correct answer is D (Intravenous adenosine, which is considered safe during pregnancy.): Adenosine has an extremely short half-life of only a few seconds and does not cross into significant fetal circulation exposure, making it the preferred first-line pharmacologic agent for terminating AVNRT during pregnancy when vagal maneuvers fail. Amiodarone carries significant fetal risks including thyroid dysfunction and is avoided except in life-threatening situations without safer alternatives. Flecainide is not first-line for acute termination and requires prior evaluation for structural heart disease before use. Procainamide is not a preferred first-line agent for acute AVNRT termination given the availability of a safer, faster-acting option.
  61. The correct answer is A (Approximately 1% or less in experienced electrophysiology centers.): Slow pathway ablation using the anatomic approach carries a low but real risk of complete AV block requiring permanent pacemaker implantation, generally cited around 1% or less in experienced centers, which is why careful monitoring for junctional rhythm and loss of ventriculoatrial conduction during ablation is essential. A 25% or 50% risk would be far higher than reported outcomes and would make the procedure unacceptable given its otherwise high success rate. While the risk is low, it is not zero, because the slow pathway lies in close anatomic proximity to the compact AV node and fast pathway.
  62. The correct answer is C (Its tissue-selective mechanism may reduce collateral injury to nearby conduction tissue.): Pulsed field ablation uses nonthermal electroporation that preferentially affects cardiomyocytes over surrounding structures such as nerves, esophagus, and potentially conduction tissue, offering a theoretical advantage of reduced collateral injury when ablating near the compact AV node and fast pathway during slow pathway modification. It does not eliminate the need for fluoroscopic or electroanatomic guidance, which remains important for catheter positioning. The goal of ablation is to eliminate the slow pathway alone while preserving the fast pathway and normal AV conduction, not to disable both pathways. Pulsed field ablation catheters still require endocardial contact to deliver effective energy to the target tissue.
  63. The correct answer is D (Recurrence is uncommon, occurring in a small minority of patients.): Long-term follow-up studies after successful slow pathway ablation with confirmed noninducibility show high durable success rates, with arrhythmia recurrence occurring in only a small minority of patients, generally cited in the low single digits to low double digits depending on the series. Recurrence in the majority of patients would contradict the well-established high long-term success of this procedure. Stating recurrence is essentially universal is inaccurate and would misrepresent the procedure's excellent outcomes. While rare, recurrence is not impossible even after confirmed noninducibility, so telling a patient it never occurs would be an overstatement.
  64. The correct answer is B (Automatic focal atrial tachycardia arising from an ectopic focus.): Automatic arrhythmias such as focal atrial tachycardia characteristically show a gradual warm-up in rate at onset and gradual cool-down before termination because they depend on progressive changes in an ectopic focus's automaticity rather than abrupt reentrant circuit initiation. Reentrant arrhythmias such as AVNRT typically begin and end abruptly, often triggered by a critically timed premature beat, rather than showing this gradual rate change. Orthodromic AVRT is also a reentrant mechanism and would similarly show abrupt onset and termination rather than gradual warm-up. Atypical fast-slow AVNRT remains a reentrant circuit and would still be expected to start and stop abruptly despite its different pathway configuration.
  65. The correct answer is C (Perform synchronized direct current cardioversion without further delay.): A hemodynamically unstable patient with hypotension and altered mental status during a sustained narrow-complex tachycardia requires immediate synchronized direct current cardioversion regardless of the underlying mechanism, since further pharmacologic attempts risk delaying definitive termination. A lower repeat dose of adenosine would not be appropriate management for an unstable patient and could further delay effective treatment. Long-term oral beta-blocker therapy has no role in acute unstable tachycardia and would not terminate the current episode. Elective ablation scheduling is entirely inappropriate for an actively unstable patient who requires emergent intervention.
  66. The correct answer is A (Fast pathway ablation carries a substantially higher risk of complete heart block.): The fast pathway lies in close proximity to the compact AV node, so ablation targeting it carries a substantially higher risk of inadvertently damaging the compact node and causing complete heart block compared with the more anatomically separated slow pathway, which is why slow pathway ablation is the preferred first-line strategy. Fast pathway ablation is technically feasible and has been performed historically, so it is not impossible with current technology. Both fast and slow pathway ablation can theoretically interrupt either typical or atypical AVNRT circuits, since eliminating either limb disrupts the reentrant loop. The choice between targets is based on safety profile, not anesthesia requirements, and both are typically performed under similar sedation protocols.
  67. The correct answer is D (Observe with continuous monitoring and reassess AV conduction before further action.): After transient complete heart block resolves with restored normal AV conduction, the appropriate next step is careful observation with continuous monitoring and reassessment of conduction over time, since immediate pacemaker implantation is not automatically required if conduction has genuinely recovered, and further ablation attempts should not resume until conduction stability is confirmed. Resuming ablation at the same site immediately after a heart block scare would risk causing permanent, irreversible AV block. Switching to fast pathway ablation as an alternative would place the catheter even closer to the compact node, increasing rather than decreasing the risk of further conduction injury. Immediate pacemaker implantation before allowing time to confirm recovery is premature if conduction has returned to normal.
  68. The correct answer is B (Adenosine can precipitate atrial fibrillation with rapid conduction over the pathway.): Adenosine can precipitate atrial fibrillation in patients with ventricular preexcitation, and because the accessory pathway may conduct rapidly and without the protective decrement of the AV node, this can result in a dangerously fast ventricular response, which is why caution and preparedness for cardioversion are warranted in these patients. Adenosine does not act through QT prolongation and is not a recognized cause of torsades de pointes. Adenosine has a well-established direct effect of transiently blocking AV nodal conduction, so the statement that it has no effect is incorrect. Adenosine is a pharmacologic agent with a transient effect and does not permanently ablate any cardiac tissue.
  69. The correct answer is A (Preexisting baseline conduction system disease such as first-degree AV block.): Preexisting conduction system disease, including baseline first-degree AV block, indicates underlying compromise of AV nodal or His-Purkinje tissue and is associated with increased risk of complete heart block during slow pathway ablation, warranting extra caution and lower power settings in such patients. Younger patients with entirely normal baseline conduction generally have healthier AV nodal tissue and lower procedural risk, not higher. A structurally normal heart without comorbidities is generally a favorable, lower-risk profile rather than a risk factor. Sex alone, without other clinical conduction abnormalities, is not established as an independent major risk factor for AV block during this procedure.
  70. The correct answer is C (Typical atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia with near-simultaneous activation.): A VA interval of 70 ms or less measured during tachycardia strongly favors typical AVNRT, since the atrium and ventricle are activated in rapid, near-simultaneous succession when retrograde conduction travels quickly up the fast pathway. Orthodromic AVRT using an accessory pathway typically produces a longer VA interval, generally well above 70 ms, because retrograde conduction must first pass through the AV node before reaching the bypass tract. Focal atrial tachycardia with incidental 1:1 conduction would not reliably produce such a short, fixed VA relationship tied to a reentrant mechanism. Antidromic AVRT conducts antegrade over the bypass tract with a different activation pattern and is not characterized by this short VA interval finding.
  71. The correct answer is D (He may typically resume normal activity within a short recovery period.): After successful, uncomplicated slow pathway ablation with confirmed noninducibility, most patients can resume normal activity, including driving, within a short recovery period, often within days, since the arrhythmia substrate has been eliminated and there is no ongoing high risk of sudden incapacitation. Permanent driving restriction is not warranted after a successful ablation without ongoing symptoms or complications. Extended hospitalization of two additional weeks is unnecessary for an uncomplicated outpatient-appropriate ablation procedure. Lifelong beta-blocker therapy is not routinely required after a successful ablation that has eliminated the arrhythmia substrate.
  72. The correct answer is C (Retrograde P wave activity superimposed on the terminal QRS complex.): A pseudo S wave appearing in the inferior leads only during tachycardia, and absent during baseline sinus rhythm, represents retrograde atrial activation from the fast pathway superimposed on the terminal portion of the QRS complex, a classic finding in typical AVNRT due to its very short VA interval. A new rate-related bundle branch block would typically alter QRS morphology across multiple leads and would not specifically produce this inferior lead pattern tied to retrograde atrial timing. This finding reflects electrical activation timing rather than ischemic repolarization changes, which would appear as ST segment abnormalities, not a QRS morphology change. Because this pseudo S wave is reproducible and rate-dependent, it is a genuine electrophysiologic finding rather than movement artifact.
  73. The correct answer is B (Flecainide carries increased proarrhythmic and mortality risk in structural heart disease.): Class IC agents such as flecainide are associated with increased proarrhythmic risk and worse mortality outcomes in patients with structural heart disease, particularly after myocardial infarction, based on landmark clinical trial evidence, which is why they are avoided in this population even though they can be effective in structurally normal hearts. Flecainide does have documented efficacy against AV nodal dependent arrhythmias in appropriate patients, so lack of efficacy is not the concern here. Flecainide is available in oral formulations for outpatient chronic use, so administration route is not the limiting issue. The relevant contraindication relates to underlying structural heart disease, not a specific age cutoff.
  74. The correct answer is A (To detect prolongation of the AH or HV interval suggesting conduction injury.): Continuous His bundle electrogram monitoring during slow pathway ablation allows the operator to detect early prolongation of the AH interval or, more concerningly, the HV interval, which can signal thermal injury spreading toward the compact node or His-Purkinje system, prompting immediate cessation of energy delivery before permanent block develops. The His bundle catheter does not provide information about left ventricular ejection fraction, which is assessed by imaging modalities such as echocardiography. Anticoagulation status is not assessed through His bundle electrogram recordings. Pulmonary vein anatomy is relevant to atrial fibrillation ablation procedures, not to AVNRT slow pathway ablation, which does not involve transseptal access or pulmonary vein mapping.
  75. The correct answer is D (A leadless transcatheter pacemaker implanted directly within the right ventricle.): A leadless transcatheter pacemaker delivers single-chamber ventricular pacing without a transvenous lead or subcutaneous generator pocket, making it a reasonable modern option for a patient who develops permanent AV block after ablation and wishes to avoid a conventional lead-based pacing system. A subcutaneous ICD without pacing capability cannot provide bradycardia pacing support and would not address her complete heart block. A wearable external defibrillator vest is intended as a temporary bridge therapy, not definitive long-term pacing treatment for chronic AV block. An implantable loop recorder only monitors rhythm and provides no pacing therapy whatsoever.
  76. The correct answer is B (Junctional ectopic tachycardia arising from an automatic focus near the AV node.): Junctional ectopic tachycardia is an automatic arrhythmia arising near the AV node that can show a gradual warm-up, intermittent AV dissociation, and resistance to reproducible induction or termination by programmed extrastimuli, features that distinguish it from a reentrant mechanism such as AVNRT. True typical AVNRT is a reentrant circuit that should be reproducibly inducible and terminable with programmed stimulation and would not typically show persistent AV dissociation, since the atrium and ventricle are usually linked in a fixed relationship during the tachycardia. Orthodromic AVRT is also a reentrant mechanism requiring the atrium as part of its circuit and would not produce AV dissociation. Atypical slow-slow AVNRT remains reentrant in mechanism and would similarly be expected to be reproducibly induced and terminated by programmed stimulation.
  77. The correct answer is C (Individual variability in adenosine metabolism and receptor sensitivity requires titration.): Adenosine has an extremely short half-life and individual patients vary in adenosine deaminase activity, receptor sensitivity, and factors such as caffeine or theophylline use, which is why standard protocols call for stepwise dose escalation from 6 mg to 12 mg if the initial dose fails to terminate the tachycardia. Adenosine does have a well-established dose-dependent effect on AV nodal conduction, so the statement that it has none is incorrect. As long as the medication is given as a rapid intravenous bolus followed by a saline flush, the injection technique itself is not typically the explanation for dose escalation needs. There is no indication the underlying tachycardia mechanism changed between doses given in rapid succession for the same episode.
  78. The correct answer is A (An extended monitoring strategy such as a longer-term event or loop recorder.): For infrequent symptoms not captured on a standard 24-hour Holter, an extended monitoring strategy such as a longer-duration event monitor, mobile cardiac telemetry, or an implantable loop recorder increases the likelihood of capturing the arrhythmia and confirming or excluding recurrence before committing to further invasive therapy. Simply repeating an identical short-duration Holter study is unlikely to meaningfully increase diagnostic yield if the episodes are genuinely infrequent. Relying solely on self-reported pulse checks lacks the diagnostic precision needed to confirm arrhythmia recurrence and its mechanism. Proceeding directly to repeat ablation without objective documentation of recurrent arrhythmia risks an unnecessary invasive procedure if symptoms are unrelated to AVNRT recurrence.
  79. The correct answer is D (A regular narrow-complex rhythm with the P wave obscured or absent.): The rhythm strip shows a regular, narrow-complex tachycardia in which the retrograde P wave is buried within or immediately follows the QRS complex, distinguishing it from her baseline sinus rhythm where a normal upright P wave clearly precedes each QRS, a pattern consistent with typical AVNRT rather than sinus tachycardia. Progressive PR prolongation with a dropped beat describes Mobitz type I AV block, which is not present on this tracing of a regular tachycardia. There is no irregularly irregular pattern here, which would instead suggest atrial fibrillation. The QRS complex remains narrow on this strip, so a wide-complex bundle branch pattern is not present.
  80. The correct answer is C (The small risk of pacemaker need is outweighed by high long-term success rates.): Appropriate informed consent should convey that slow pathway ablation carries a small, well-documented risk of complete AV block requiring pacemaker implantation, generally around 1% or less, but that this risk is outweighed for most patients by the procedure's high long-term arrhythmia-free success rates. Stating pacemaker implantation is virtually guaranteed would misrepresent the actual low incidence of this complication. Claiming there is no risk of conduction injury at all would be inaccurate and could constitute inadequate informed consent. This risk is not age-restricted to patients younger than eighteen and applies across the adult population undergoing this procedure as well.
  81. The correct answer is B (Reassurance and observation, since these effects are expected and self-limited.): Transient chest tightness, flushing, and a brief sinus pause are well-recognized, expected, and self-limited effects of adenosine due to its very short half-life, and the appropriate management is reassurance with continued observation rather than aggressive intervention. Emergent temporary pacing is not warranted for a brief, self-resolving pause of only a few seconds that is a known pharmacologic effect. Epinephrine is not routinely required to counteract adenosine's effects, which resolve spontaneously within seconds given the drug's extremely short half-life. There is no indication of coronary artery dissection from this expected pharmacologic response, and urgent surgical consultation would be inappropriate.
  82. The correct answer is A (The target lies on the left atrial side, requiring transseptal access.): When the second slow pathway of atypical slow-slow AVNRT inserts near the left inferior extension of the AV node, reaching this target may require transseptal puncture to access the left atrial side, a technical step not typically needed for standard right-sided anatomic slow pathway ablation in typical AVNRT. Coronary artery ligation is not part of any AVNRT ablation procedure and would be inappropriate and unnecessary. General anesthesia is not mandatory for all AVNRT ablations, which are commonly performed under conscious sedation. Catheter ablation is technically feasible and has been successfully performed for atypical AVNRT forms, including those requiring left-sided access.
  83. The correct answer is D (Cryoablation allows reversible cryomapping before permanent lesion formation.): Cryoablation permits a reversible cryomapping phase in which tissue is transiently cooled and its electrophysiologic effect assessed before committing to a permanent freeze, allowing the operator to abort if unwanted AV conduction changes occur, an advantage particularly valued when ablating near the compact AV node in AVNRT. Cryoablation does not eliminate arrhythmia recurrence entirely and, in fact, several studies have shown slightly higher recurrence rates compared with radiofrequency for AVNRT. Cryoablation procedures are generally longer, not faster, than radiofrequency ablation due to the freeze-thaw cycle times involved. Cryoablation catheters still require direct endocardial contact to achieve an effective freeze at the target tissue.
  84. The correct answer is B (Standard AVNRT management, including possible ablation, remains appropriate alongside her device.): The presence of a dual-chamber leadless pacemaker system does not preclude standard evaluation and management of newly diagnosed AVNRT, including pharmacologic therapy or catheter ablation of the slow pathway if indicated, since the pacemaker addresses her separate sinus node dysfunction rather than the AV nodal reentrant circuit. Pacemakers provide pacing support but do not have any ablative capability and cannot treat an arrhythmia on their own. There is no indication to explant a functioning, appropriately indicated pacemaker system simply because a new arrhythmia has been diagnosed. AVNRT can occur in patients with implanted devices, since the underlying dual AV nodal physiology is unrelated to pacemaker presence.
  85. The correct answer is C (Surface ECG P wave morphology alone cannot always reliably differentiate the two mechanisms.): Atypical AVNRT and atrial tachycardia can produce similar long RP intervals and overlapping P wave morphologies on surface ECG, so an invasive electrophysiology study using maneuvers such as entrainment, para-Hisian pacing, and analysis of atrial activation sequence during ventricular pacing is often required to definitively establish the true mechanism before pursuing targeted ablation. Atrial tachycardia can, in fact, present with a long RP interval, particularly with slower AV node conduction, so this option misstates its typical presentation. Atypical AVNRT is a supraventricular arrhythmia with a narrow QRS complex under normal conduction conditions, not a visibly abnormal QRS. The invasive study serves a genuine diagnostic and therapeutic purpose, not merely an administrative one.
  86. The correct answer is A (Absence of continuous sawtooth atrial activity between QRS complexes.): Typical atrial flutter characteristically shows continuous sawtooth flutter waves, most visible in the inferior leads, between QRS complexes at an atrial rate near 300 beats per minute, and the absence of this sawtooth pattern on the rhythm strip argues against flutter and supports AVNRT instead, where retrograde P waves are typically hidden within or just after the QRS. A regular ventricular rate can occur in both AVNRT and atrial flutter with fixed conduction ratios, so regularity alone does not distinguish between them. A narrow QRS complex is common to both AVNRT and flutter with normal conduction, so this finding alone does not favor one diagnosis over the other. Atrial flutter's atrial rate, not its ventricular rate, is classically near 300 beats per minute, so ventricular rate alone is not the distinguishing feature described here.
  87. The correct answer is D (Atypical AVNRT's variable slow pathway location can make precise mapping more difficult.): Atypical AVNRT, particularly the slow-slow form, can involve a second slow pathway with a more variable anatomic location, sometimes requiring left-sided or mid-septal access, which increases mapping complexity and can result in somewhat lower acute success rates compared with the well-characterized, consistently located slow pathway target in typical AVNRT. Atypical AVNRT still fundamentally involves dual AV nodal physiology and reentry through nodal tissue, so it is inaccurate to say it does not involve the AV node at all. Patient age and health status are not consistently different between typical and atypical AVNRT populations based on the underlying mechanism alone. There is no established evidence that atypical AVNRT responds preferentially better to medication such that ablation is rarely pursued for it.
  88. The correct answer is C (To provoke residual inducibility that baseline conditions might have masked.): Isoproterenol increases sympathetic tone and catecholamine-mediated conduction, which can unmask residual dual AV nodal physiology or inducible AVNRT that might not be apparent under baseline resting conditions, so repeating programmed stimulation during isoproterenol infusion helps confirm a durable ablation result before ending the procedure. While isoproterenol does increase heart rate, its use here is diagnostic to test for residual inducibility rather than specifically to treat bradycardia. The goal of slow pathway ablation is to preserve the fast pathway and normal AV conduction, not eliminate it, so this option misstates the ablation target. Isoproterenol has no role in reversing sedative medications used during the procedure.
  89. The correct answer is B (Ablation is an option but not mandatory given her mild, infrequent symptoms.): For patients with infrequent, mild AVNRT episodes that respond well to vagal maneuvers and cause minimal quality-of-life disruption, catheter ablation remains a reasonable option to discuss but is not mandatory, and a shared decision-making approach weighing symptom burden against procedural risks and benefits is appropriate. Stating ablation is mandatory for every patient regardless of symptom severity does not reflect a shared decision-making, guideline-consistent approach to a low-burden arrhythmia. Long-term amiodarone carries significant end-organ toxicity risks and would not be an appropriate first recommendation for infrequent, mild, self-terminating episodes. AVNRT does not reliably resolve spontaneously with age, so this statement would be medically inaccurate reassurance.
  90. The correct answer is C (Accelerated junctional rhythm reflecting enhanced automaticity of the AV junction): A junctional rate only modestly faster than the patient's sinus rate, without P waves preceding the QRS, is typical of accelerated junctional rhythm from enhanced automaticity, often seen after inferior infarction affecting the AV nodal artery territory. This rhythm is usually benign and transient rather than a true reentrant tachycardia. AVNRT typically presents at a much faster, more abrupt rate with a discrete onset, and atrial flutter would show flutter waves on the surface ECG rather than an absent P wave pattern.
  91. The correct answer is A (Isorhythmic AV dissociation from a junctional pacemaker competing with sinus node): When the sinus and junctional rates are nearly equal, P waves drift in and out of a fixed relationship to the QRS without true AV block, producing isorhythmic dissociation rather than complete heart block. Complete heart block would typically show a slower, more fixed escape rate unrelated to atrial timing. Wenckebach requires progressive PR prolongation ending in a dropped beat, which is not described here, and atrial fibrillation would show an irregularly irregular rhythm rather than organized P waves.
  92. The correct answer is D (Junctional tachycardia arising from enhanced AV junctional automaticity): A narrow complex rhythm without preceding P waves at a rate faster than typical sinus rates, occurring soon after cardiac surgery near the AV node, is characteristic of postoperative junctional tachycardia from enhanced automaticity. Sinus tachycardia would show a normal P wave preceding each QRS, which is absent here. Atrial fibrillation would appear irregularly irregular rather than regular, and atrial flutter would demonstrate characteristic flutter waves rather than an absent atrial signal.
  93. The correct answer is B (Gradual onset and offset without an abrupt initiating premature beat): Junctional tachycardia from automaticity typically warms up and cools down gradually rather than starting and stopping abruptly with a premature beat, which is the hallmark distinguishing it from reentrant AVNRT. A delta wave would suggest ventricular preexcitation rather than a junctional mechanism, and a markedly long RP interval is more typical of atypical AVNRT or atrial tachycardia. Response to a specific antiarrhythmic does not reliably distinguish the two mechanisms.
  94. The correct answer is A (Junctional ectopic tachycardia related to surgical trauma near the AV node): Postoperative congenital heart surgery near the AV node commonly produces junctional ectopic tachycardia, presenting with a fast narrow complex rhythm, AV dissociation, and fusion beats reflecting competing junctional and sinus activity. Reentrant SVT typically shows a fixed 1:1 relationship rather than AV dissociation. Sinus tachycardia would demonstrate a normal P wave preceding each QRS rather than dissociation, and a crista terminalis focus would present as atrial tachycardia with a distinct P wave morphology rather than junctional dissociation.
  95. The correct answer is C (A warm-up and cool-down pattern typical of enhanced junctional automaticity): Gradual acceleration and deceleration without an abrupt onset or offset is the classic warm-up and cool-down behavior of automatic rhythms arising from the AV junction, distinguishing them from reentrant tachycardias. Reentrant SVT instead begins and ends abruptly with a triggering premature beat, which is not described here. This warm-up pattern is a well-recognized and expected feature of junctional automaticity rather than something that cannot occur. Concealed conduction refers to incomplete penetration of an impulse into conducting tissue, not to rate behavior.
  96. The correct answer is D (Digoxin toxicity causing enhanced automaticity in the AV junction): A regular junctional rhythm replacing atrial fibrillation in the setting of an elevated digoxin level is the classic presentation of digoxin toxicity producing regularization through enhanced junctional automaticity. Accessory pathways do not spontaneously form in adulthood, and this scenario has no evidence of a reentrant pathway. Sinus node reentry would show a normal P wave morphology preceding the QRS, and atrial flutter degenerating into a junctional escape would present as a much slower rate than described here.
  97. The correct answer is B (Isorhythmic dissociation between competing sinus and junctional pacemakers): Nearly identical atrial and ventricular rates with a slowly drifting PR relationship, rather than true AV block, indicates isorhythmic dissociation between an independent sinus rhythm and a junctional pacemaker of similar rate. Complete heart block requires atrial and ventricular rates that are clearly independent, typically with the ventricular rate much slower. Multifocal atrial tachycardia requires at least three distinct P wave morphologies with an irregular rhythm, and Mobitz II block shows a fixed PR interval with intermittently nonconducted beats, neither of which matches this description.
  98. The correct answer is C (Controlled surface cooling to mild hypothermia to slow the ectopic focus): Mild controlled hypothermia is a first-line supportive strategy for postoperative junctional ectopic tachycardia because lowering core temperature slows the rate of the automatic focus. Cardioversion is ineffective against an automatic rhythm since there is no reentrant circuit to interrupt. Adenosine transiently blocks the AV node but does not terminate an automatic junctional focus and can be dangerous in this setting. Epinephrine would accelerate rather than suppress the junctional focus, worsening the arrhythmia.
  99. The correct answer is A (Discontinue digoxin and administer digoxin immune Fab if toxicity is severe): Nonparoxysmal junctional tachycardia with an elevated digoxin level and classic toxicity symptoms requires immediate discontinuation of digoxin, with digoxin immune Fab reserved for severe or life-threatening toxicity. Increasing the dose would worsen toxicity and is never appropriate. Intravenous calcium is contraindicated in digoxin toxicity because it can precipitate fatal arrhythmias by further raising intracellular calcium. Catheter ablation is not an appropriate acute treatment for a reversible, drug-induced arrhythmia.
  100. The correct answer is B (Partial suppression of automaticity, with ongoing junctional tachycardia at a lower rate): A slower but still elevated rate with persistent AV dissociation shows that beta blockade has partially suppressed the automatic junctional focus without eliminating it, so the arrhythmia continues at a reduced rate. Complete resolution would restore normal sinus rhythm with a P wave preceding each QRS, which has not occurred. Retrograde conduction producing sinus rhythm is not consistent with continued AV dissociation. New complete heart block would show a bradycardic escape rhythm rather than a persistently rapid rate.
  101. The correct answer is D (Its combined slowing of automaticity and AV nodal conduction with low proarrhythmia risk): Amiodarone slows both the automatic junctional focus and AV nodal conduction while carrying a relatively low risk of proarrhythmia, making it well suited for refractory postoperative junctional ectopic tachycardia in children. Its predominant electrophysiologic effects are on potassium and multiple channel types rather than isolated fast sodium channels in atrial tissue. Amiodarone typically requires a loading period rather than acting within minutes, and it has broad effects on cardiac conduction tissue rather than being selective for accessory pathways.
  102. The correct answer is A (Wean catecholamine infusions as tolerated since they accelerate the junctional focus): Catecholamines directly accelerate automatic junctional foci, so weaning dopamine and epinephrine as hemodynamically tolerated is an important adjunct to reduce arrhythmia burden while other therapies take effect. Increasing epinephrine would worsen the tachycardia because higher catecholamine exposure accelerates automaticity rather than overdriving it away. Atropine increases sinus and junctional rates and would worsen the arrhythmia. Switching to another catecholamine such as dobutamine would similarly continue to stimulate the ectopic focus.
  103. The correct answer is C (Avoid intravenous calcium since it can worsen digoxin-induced toxicity in hyperkalemia): In digoxin toxicity with hyperkalemia, intravenous calcium is relatively contraindicated because it can precipitate fatal arrhythmias by further increasing intracellular calcium in already digoxin-poisoned myocytes. Calcium should never be given reflexively without recognizing this interaction. Further raising the potassium level would be dangerous and is never appropriate management. Emergent hemodialysis is not routinely required for hemodynamically stable digoxin toxicity unless renal failure or severe refractory hyperkalemia is present.
  104. The correct answer is B (Improve AV synchrony and cardiac output despite the persistent junctional rate): Atrial overdrive pacing in junctional ectopic tachycardia is used to restore AV synchrony and improve cardiac output rather than to terminate the automatic focus itself, since pacing cannot capture and reset an automatic rhythm the way it can a reentrant circuit. It is not intended to permanently replace the conduction system, only to support hemodynamics temporarily. Inducing a faster arrhythmia would worsen rather than help the clinical situation, and there is no automatic cardioversion mechanism triggered by pacing.
  105. The correct answer is D (Hold digoxin and monitor, since a slow junctional escape reflects sinus node suppression): A slow junctional escape rhythm in a digoxin-treated patient reflects digoxin-related suppression of higher pacemakers, and the appropriate management is to hold digoxin and monitor for recovery rather than adding further AV nodal blocking agents. A beta blocker would further slow conduction and is inappropriate in a bradycardic patient. Catheter ablation of the AV junction is not indicated for a reversible drug effect. Adenosine would risk further AV nodal suppression and provides no useful diagnostic information in this bradycardic scenario.
  106. The correct answer is C (A junctional tachycardia arising below the His bundle recording site's atrial input): A His deflection preceding each QRS with a normal HV interval, combined with AV dissociation and a ventricular rate exceeding the atrial rate, indicates that the tachycardia originates from the AV junction independent of atrial activity. A concealed accessory pathway would require a fixed 1:1 ventriculoatrial relationship rather than dissociation. Atrial tachycardia with variable block would show the atrial rate exceeding the ventricular rate, the opposite of this finding. True ventricular tachycardia typically originates below the His recording site without a preceding His deflection of normal timing.
  107. The correct answer is A (Retrograde conduction depends on the AV node rather than an accessory pathway): When loss of His capture changes the retrograde atrial activation pattern and timing, this indicates that retrograde conduction is occurring through the AV node and His-Purkinje system rather than through an independent accessory pathway. If an accessory pathway were present, retrograde activation would remain unchanged regardless of His capture. This finding does not support a purely atrial mechanism, since it specifically addresses the retrograde limb of conduction. The response shows the His bundle is directly involved in retrograde conduction, contrary to that option.
  108. The correct answer is D (The tachycardia originates at or below the His bundle, consistent with a junctional focus): A consistent His potential immediately preceding each ventricular electrogram with a fixed short HV interval, without a preceding atrial signal driving the rhythm, localizes the origin to the AV junction rather than the atrium. If the atrium were driving the rhythm with 1:1 conduction, a consistent atrial signal would precede the His deflection. True ventricular tachycardia usually shows a His deflection that is dissociated from or follows the ventricular electrogram rather than consistently preceding it. There is no evidence here implicating an accessory pathway as a bystander.
  109. The correct answer is B (The atrium is not a required component of the tachycardia circuit, favoring a junctional mechanism): When atrial pacing fails to entrain or reset the tachycardia despite confirmed atrial capture, this indicates the atrium is a bystander rather than part of the circuit, supporting a junctional focus that does not require atrial participation. If the pacing site were part of the critical isthmus, entrainment with resetting would typically be demonstrated. This finding argues against atrial fibrillation, which is disorganized rather than a discrete entrainable rhythm. Confirmed atrial capture during pacing rules out catheter dislodgement as the explanation.
  110. The correct answer is A (A septal mechanism such as AV nodal reentry or a junctional focus): Earliest retrograde atrial activation recorded at the His bundle catheter, rather than eccentrically at the coronary sinus, points toward a septal mechanism such as AV nodal reentry or an AV junctional focus rather than a laterally located accessory pathway. A left lateral pathway would produce eccentric activation earliest at the coronary sinus catheter. A right free wall pathway would show earliest activation along the tricuspid annulus rather than centrally at the His position. An atrial tachycardia from the left atrial appendage would show earliest activation at a left atrial site, not the His catheter.
  111. The correct answer is C (Junctional tachycardia with independent atrial and junctional pacemaker activity): A consistent His deflection preceding each QRS with a stable HV interval but no fixed relationship to atrial activity confirms an independent junctional pacemaker producing AV dissociation, consistent with junctional tachycardia. Atrial flutter with fixed conduction would show a constant relationship between flutter waves and QRS complexes rather than dissociation. Orthodromic reentrant tachycardia requires a fixed 1:1 ventriculoatrial relationship through an accessory pathway, which contradicts the dissociation seen here. Sinus tachycardia would show a consistent PR relationship rather than independent atrial and junctional activity.
  112. The correct answer is B (A retrograde pathway distinct from the AV node connects the ventricle to the circuit): A premature ventricular stimulus delivered during His refractoriness that still resets the tachycardia demonstrates that ventricular impulses can reach the circuit without passing through the His-Purkinje system, implying a separate retrograde connection. This finding argues against the atrium being essential, since atrial extrastimuli did not affect timing. The result does not exclude a component below the AV node, since the ventricular resetting itself implicates tissue near or below that level. A junctional or nodal contribution is not excluded, since the circuit can still involve junctional tissue reached through this alternate connection.
  113. The correct answer is D (A junctional tachycardia driving both the atrium and ventricle through fixed pathways): A fixed, unvarying AH interval with exactly matched atrial and ventricular rates is most consistent with a single junctional pacemaker driving both chambers through stable anterograde and retrograde pathways. Atrial tachycardia with decremental AV nodal conduction would typically show a variable or progressively changing AH interval rather than a fixed one. Ventricular tachycardia with variable retrograde conduction would produce beat-to-beat variability in the VA relationship, which is not described. Multifocal atrial tachycardia produces an irregular rhythm with multiple P wave morphologies, inconsistent with this stable finding.
  114. The correct answer is A (A favorable sign suggesting effective lesion formation near the slow pathway): Junctional ectopy with preserved 1:1 retrograde atrial activation during slow pathway ablation is a well-recognized favorable marker indicating that the lesion is being delivered in an appropriate location, and it does not require stopping energy delivery. Impending heart block is a concern specifically when retrograde VA conduction is lost during junctional ectopy, which is not the case here. This finding does not indicate catheter movement into the fast pathway, since junctional ectopy near the slow pathway is expected there. The rate and context described are consistent with junctional ectopy rather than ventricular tachycardia.
  115. The correct answer is C (Stop energy delivery promptly, since loss of VA conduction may signal impending AV block): Loss of retrograde ventriculoatrial conduction during junctional ectopy near the compact AV node is a warning sign of impending AV nodal injury, and energy delivery should be stopped immediately to avoid permanent AV block. Increasing power at the same site would raise the risk of causing permanent heart block. Junctional ectopy alone is reassuring only when VA conduction remains intact throughout, which is not the case here. Switching to a larger catheter without addressing the underlying safety signal does not resolve the immediate risk.
  116. The correct answer is D (Cryoablation allows a reversible test lesion and cryoadhesion that reduces catheter movement): Cryoablation permits a reversible cryomapping lesion before permanent freezing, and cryoadhesion stabilizes catheter position during energy delivery, both of which reduce the risk of permanent AV block near the compact AV node. Early cryolesions are not irreversible within seconds, which is the opposite of the actual advantage of a reversible test lesion. Junctional ectopy can still occur with cryoablation, so risk is reduced but not eliminated entirely. A test lesion is a key safety feature of cryoablation, not something it avoids using.
  117. The correct answer is B (Use cryoablation or low-power titrated radiofrequency with continuous His bundle monitoring): A focal automatic junctional tachycardia mapping adjacent to the His bundle warrants a cautious approach, using cryoablation or carefully titrated low-power radiofrequency with continuous His bundle monitoring to minimize the risk of AV block. Delivering maximal power near the His signal significantly increases the risk of permanent AV block and is inappropriate. Junctional foci near the AV node can often be treated safely with appropriate technique, so abandoning the procedure is not the best answer. Targeting an unrelated ventricular site would not address the actual focus responsible for the arrhythmia.
  118. The correct answer is C (The lesion may be ineffective, since junctional ectopy is an expected marker of slow pathway ablation): Junctional ectopy is an expected and reassuring finding during effective slow pathway ablation, so its complete absence suggests the lesion may not be adequately affecting the target tissue and further evaluation or repositioning may be needed. The absence of ectopy does not confirm effectiveness, contrary to that option. It also does not by itself indicate the catheter is in the fast pathway, since that would be suggested by other findings such as PR prolongation. This scenario involves slow pathway ablation for AVNRT, not accessory pathway ablation, so elimination of an accessory pathway is not relevant here.
  119. The correct answer is A (Pause ablation, reassess conduction, and consider repositioning before further energy delivery): New PR prolongation after a lesion near the slow pathway is a warning sign of AV nodal effect, and the appropriate response is to pause, reassess conduction, and consider repositioning before continuing rather than proceeding immediately. Continuing at the same site risks progression to complete heart block. PR prolongation can indeed reflect AV nodal injury and should never be disregarded. Surgical AV node ablation with permanent pacing is a drastic and unnecessary step for a transient finding that can often be managed by adjusting the ablation approach.
  120. The correct answer is D (Use low-power titration with a His bundle electrogram displayed continuously during each application): Ablating an anteroseptal accessory pathway near the compact AV node requires low-power titration with continuous His bundle electrogram monitoring so that any early sign of AV nodal injury can be detected and energy stopped promptly. Ablating at maximum power at the first pathway signal significantly increases the risk of permanent AV block in this location. Avoiding His bundle recording removes the very safety signal needed to detect impending injury. Continuous, uninterrupted energy delivery for several minutes without monitoring increases rather than reduces the risk of AV block.
  121. The correct answer is B (Pre-existing conduction system disease such as baseline first-degree AV block): Baseline conduction system disease, such as pre-existing first-degree AV block, increases the risk of progression to complete heart block during slow pathway ablation because the AV node has less reserve to tolerate additional injury. A structurally normal heart without prior surgery is generally a lower-risk substrate rather than a higher-risk one. A short baseline PR interval indicates better, not worse, baseline conduction reserve. Younger patient age is generally associated with lower procedural risk of AV block rather than higher risk.
  122. The correct answer is A (Assess for recovery over the following days while providing temporary pacing support if needed): Complete heart block occurring immediately after slow pathway ablation often reflects transient AV nodal injury or edema, so the appropriate approach is to provide temporary pacing support as needed while observing for spontaneous recovery over the following days before committing to permanent therapy. Proceeding immediately to a permanent pacemaker without any observation period would be premature if conduction is likely to recover. Repeating ablation at the same site would risk worsening the injury rather than restoring conduction. Atropine is not a definitive long-term therapy for complete heart block from AV nodal injury.
  123. The correct answer is C (A previously masked concealed accessory pathway now revealed as the second mechanism): Eccentric retrograde atrial activation earliest over the coronary sinus, appearing after successful slow pathway ablation confirmed by noninducibility, indicates a distinct concealed left-sided accessory pathway that had been masked by the dominant AVNRT circuit. Recurrence of the original AVNRT would be expected to show the same typical septal retrograde activation pattern as before, not eccentric activation. Sinus tachycardia would show a normal P wave morphology rather than a distinct reentrant tachycardia with retrograde atrial activation. Atrial flutter would demonstrate characteristic flutter wave activity rather than a discrete reentrant SVT pattern.
  124. The correct answer is D (Map and ablate both substrates in the same procedure when feasible, confirming noninducibility of each): When two clinically relevant SVT substrates are confirmed on the same study, the appropriate approach is to map and ablate both in the same procedure when feasible, confirming noninducibility of each mechanism before concluding. Choosing to ablate only the easier target would leave a clinically relevant arrhythmia untreated. The order of discovery during the study has no bearing on which substrate should be treated. Both mechanisms can typically be safely ablated in experienced hands, so medical therapy alone is not the best answer when curative ablation is achievable.
  125. The correct answer is B (An accessory pathway and dual AV nodal physiology can coexist independently in the same patient): Manifest accessory pathways and dual AV nodal physiology are separate anatomic and electrophysiologic substrates that can coexist in the same patient, each capable of producing its own distinct tachycardia. Different cycle lengths and retrograde activation patterns confirm these are genuinely separate mechanisms rather than the same one. The presence of an accessory pathway does not exclude AVNRT, since both substrates are common in the general population and can occur together. WPW preexcitation does not convert into AVNRT over time, since they represent distinct anatomic substrates.
  126. The correct answer is A (Perform differential pacing maneuvers such as the VA interval response to ventricular overdrive pacing): Differential pacing maneuvers, including comparing the VA interval response during ventricular overdrive pacing at different sites, help distinguish atypical AVNRT, a slowly conducting posteroseptal accessory pathway, or an atrial tachycardia as the cause of a residual long RP tachycardia. Assuming artifact without further testing risks leaving a clinically relevant arrhythmia untreated. Repeating ablation at the original site is inappropriate since that pathway has already been successfully eliminated. Attributing a reproducibly inducible tachycardia to catheter-induced ectopy without further evaluation would miss a genuine second mechanism.
  127. The correct answer is C (Address the mechanism causing greater symptom burden first while confirming noninducibility of both before ending): When two distinct SVT mechanisms are confirmed, a reasonable sequencing strategy is to address the arrhythmia causing greater symptom burden first, then confirm noninducibility of both mechanisms before ending the procedure. There is no fixed rule requiring the atrial tachycardia to always be treated first, since sequencing should be guided by clinical impact and anatomic considerations. The two mechanisms arise from different anatomic sites and cannot be ablated simultaneously at one catheter position. A third EP study is unnecessary when both mechanisms are already well characterized from prior studies.
  128. The correct answer is D (Map and ablate the newly revealed atrial tachycardia focus if clinically appropriate): A newly revealed focal atrial tachycardia with a distinct P wave morphology and separate earliest activation site represents a genuine second mechanism that should be mapped and ablated if it is clinically appropriate, rather than being ignored. Concluding the study prematurely could leave the patient with an unaddressed arrhythmia responsible for symptoms. Assuming the finding is nonclinical without further correlation to the patient's history risks missing a real second SVT. Repeating slow pathway ablation would not address a focus that originates from atrial tissue at a distinct anatomic site.
  129. The correct answer is B (Conclude the procedure, since noninducible dual physiology alone does not require ablation): Dual AV nodal physiology without inducible tachycardia, even after aggressive stimulation, does not itself require ablation, since it is a common and often clinically silent electrophysiologic finding. Proceeding with empiric slow pathway ablation despite noninducibility exposes the patient to unnecessary AV block risk without proven benefit. Repeating isoproterenol infusion indefinitely offers diminishing returns once standard aggressive protocols have already failed to induce tachycardia. Surgical AV node ablation is a drastic, unwarranted intervention for asymptomatic dual physiology with no demonstrated clinical arrhythmia.
  130. The correct answer is C (Multiple coexisting or overlapping SVT mechanisms can be missed without thorough remapping): This case illustrates that rare or atypical substrates such as a nodofascicular accessory pathway can coexist with more common mechanisms and be missed unless a thorough repeat electrophysiologic evaluation is performed after recurrent symptoms. Nodofascicular pathways are actually uncommon rather than something that should always be assumed first. Repeat EP studies clearly remain valuable, as demonstrated by the successful identification of the missed mechanism in this case. Ablation of one SVT mechanism, such as AVNRT, does not guarantee elimination of every other possible coexisting arrhythmia substrate.
  131. The correct answer is A (The left ventricular inferior wall near the prior infarct scar.): A right bundle branch block pattern during ventricular tachycardia indicates a left ventricular origin, because the right ventricle must be activated late across the septum. A superior axis with a QS complex in lead I localizes that origin to the inferior wall, matching the region of prior infarct scar. Outflow tract tachycardias instead produce a left bundle branch block pattern with an inferior axis, which is not seen here.
  132. The correct answer is B (The right ventricular outflow tract just beneath the pulmonic valve.): A left bundle branch block pattern with an inferior axis and precordial transition around V3 is the classic signature of right ventricular outflow tract tachycardia. Left ventricular outflow tract foci usually transition earlier, by V1 or V2, and often show a taller R wave in lead I. The structurally normal heart also favors an idiopathic outflow tract mechanism rather than scar-related reentry.
  133.
