The correct answer is C (The inferior left ventricular wall at the healed infarct border.): A superior axis with a QS pattern in the inferior leads points directly to an inferior wall origin, consistent with reentry around the border zone of a healed inferior infarct. The right bundle branch block morphology confirms a left ventricular rather than right ventricular exit site. An anteroseptal or lateral origin would instead be expected to produce an inferior rather than superior axis.
  134. The correct answer is D (The exit site lies in the lateral left ventricular wall.): A late precordial transition, with the R wave not becoming dominant until V5, reflects activation moving away from the anterior chest wall for longer than usual, which localizes the exit site to the lateral left ventricular wall. An apical origin typically produces a rapid loss of R wave amplitude across the precordium instead. An outflow tract origin would transition much earlier, well before V4.
  135. The correct answer is B (The exit site is epicardial rather than endocardial in location.): A pseudo-delta wave and prolonged intrinsicoid deflection time reflect slow initial ventricular activation, which occurs when the tachycardia exits from the epicardium and must conduct slowly through myocardium before reaching the rapidly conducting endocardial Purkinje network. Endocardial exits, including those near the His bundle, activate the ventricle more quickly and do not produce this pattern. These ECG criteria are commonly used to plan epicardial access before ablation.
  136. The correct answer is A (The right ventricular outflow tract near the pulmonic valve.): A left bundle branch block pattern with an inferior axis and an early precordial transition is the classic morphology of premature ventricular contractions arising from the right ventricular outflow tract. Papillary muscle and left ventricular summit foci typically produce a different axis and later precordial transition. Targeting the outflow tract first with pace-mapping is the standard initial approach for this morphology.
  137. The correct answer is D (Bundle branch reentry using the right and left bundle branches.): A His deflection preceding each QRS with an HV interval matching the baseline sinus HV interval, combined with underlying conduction system disease and a dilated cardiomyopathy, is characteristic of bundle branch reentrant tachycardia. The circuit uses the right bundle for antegrade conduction and the left bundle for retrograde conduction, producing a left bundle branch block QRS pattern. This mechanism is treated definitively with ablation of the right bundle branch.
  138. The correct answer is C (Unexplained syncope with structural heart disease and reduced ejection fraction.): Unexplained syncope in a patient with significant structural heart disease and a markedly reduced ejection fraction raises concern for occult ventricular tachycardia as the cause, and guidelines support invasive electrophysiology study in this setting when noninvasive monitoring has failed to capture the rhythm. A family history alone, self-limited vasovagal symptoms, or vagally responsive palpitations do not carry the same risk of a life-threatening ventricular arrhythmia. The reduced ejection fraction substantially raises the pretest probability of inducible VT.
  139. The correct answer is B (To risk-stratify for sudden death and guide defibrillator decisions.): In a patient with a reduced ejection fraction and nonsustained ventricular tachycardia after myocardial infarction, programmed ventricular stimulation helps identify those at highest risk for sudden cardiac death by testing for inducible sustained monomorphic VT. Inducibility strengthens the case for implantable defibrillator therapy beyond ejection fraction alone. This use has nothing to do with atrial fibrillation, sinus node function, or atrioventricular nodal assessment.
  140. The correct answer is D (The tachycardia is ventricular in origin and mechanism.): Atrioventricular dissociation with more ventricular than atrial activations, together with fusion and capture beats, are classic markers of ventriculoatrial dissociation that confirm a ventricular origin for a wide complex tachycardia. Supraventricular tachycardias with aberrancy maintain a fixed one-to-one atrioventricular relationship rather than dissociation. Neither atrioventricular nodal reentry nor antegrade accessory pathway conduction would produce independent ventricular activity exceeding atrial activity.
  141. The correct answer is A (Idiopathic fascicular ventricular tachycardia from the left posterior fascicle.): A relatively narrow, right bundle branch block pattern tachycardia with a superior axis that terminates with verapamil is the classic presentation of idiopathic fascicular ventricular tachycardia, which uses Purkinje fascicular tissue as part of its reentry circuit. This verapamil sensitivity is a distinguishing diagnostic and therapeutic feature not shared by scar-related VT. A structurally normal heart also argues against infarct-related scar VT as the cause.
  142. The correct answer is C (To attempt induction of clinically relevant ventricular tachycardia.): Programmed ventricular stimulation with a drive train followed by extrastimuli of decreasing coupling interval is designed to provoke reentry and induce sustained monomorphic ventricular tachycardia in patients at risk, which supports both diagnosis and risk stratification. Measuring atrioventricular nodal refractoriness, assessing sinus node recovery, or identifying an accessory pathway are goals of other parts of an electrophysiology study, not this specific ventricular stimulation protocol. Induced VT that matches the clinical arrhythmia is considered a positive study.
  143. The correct answer is D (It is within the normal range for His to ventricle conduction.): The normal HV interval, measured from the onset of the His deflection to the earliest ventricular activation, is typically 35 to 55 ms, so a value of 45 ms reflects normal infra-Hisian conduction. Delayed infra-Hisian conduction would instead produce a prolonged HV interval, usually greater than 55 to 70 ms. An accessory pathway would shorten rather than lengthen this interval, and complete heart block would produce AV dissociation rather than a measurable HV interval at all.
  144. The correct answer is B (It identifies significant infra-Hisian disease warranting pacemaker consideration.): A markedly prolonged HV interval, generally greater than 70 to 100 ms, identifies substantial disease below the His bundle even when the surface QRS is narrow, and this finding is an accepted indication for permanent pacing in a patient with unexplained syncope. This does not exclude conduction disease, and it does not confirm a ventricular tachycardia mechanism since no arrhythmia was induced. An accessory pathway would shorten, not prolong, the HV interval.
  145. The correct answer is A (The tachycardia originates in the ventricle rather than above it.): Independent, dissociated atrial activity that is slower than the ventricular rate during a wide complex tachycardia demonstrates atrioventricular dissociation, which confirms that the ventricle is driving the rhythm independently of the atrium and therefore that the tachycardia is ventricular in origin. Atrioventricular nodal reentry and accessory pathway mediated tachycardias require a fixed relationship between atrial and ventricular activation, not independent dissociation. The atrial rhythm here is simply the underlying sinus rhythm continuing unaffected.
  146. The correct answer is C (It identifies a burden threshold linked to reversible cardiomyopathy.): A premature ventricular contraction burden approaching or exceeding roughly 10 to 15% of total beats, and especially a burden as high as 24%, is associated with premature ventricular contraction-induced cardiomyopathy that can improve or resolve with successful suppression or ablation. This burden calculation has no relationship to stroke risk or anticoagulation decisions. Quantifying burden actually strengthens rather than replaces the case for further imaging, such as echocardiography, to assess ventricular function.
  147. The correct answer is A (Implantation of an insertable cardiac loop recorder for long-term monitoring.): When syncope remains unexplained after a negative electrophysiology study and noninvasive monitoring, an insertable cardiac loop recorder provides extended monitoring capable of capturing an infrequent arrhythmia over months to years. Empiric pacemaker implantation or antiarrhythmic therapy without a confirmed diagnosis is not appropriate, and simply repeating the same negative study within days is unlikely to yield new information. Long-term monitoring is the recommended next step in this diagnostic pathway.
  148. The correct answer is D (Entrainment with concealed fusion of the tachycardia's reentry circuit.): When pacing faster than the tachycardia produces a paced QRS morphology identical to the tachycardia QRS with no visible fusion on the surface ECG, this is entrainment with concealed fusion, meaning the pacing site lies within the tachycardia's reentry circuit itself. Manifest fusion would instead show a QRS morphology intermediate between the paced and tachycardia complexes. Failure to capture or unrelated oversensing would not produce a consistent paced morphology matching the tachycardia at all.
  149. The correct answer is B (The pacing site lies within the reentry circuit's isthmus.): A post-pacing interval that closely matches the tachycardia cycle length, typically within 20 to 30 ms, together with concealed fusion, confirms that the pacing site sits within the protected isthmus of the reentrant circuit. This combination is a core criterion for selecting an effective ablation target. A bystander site distant from the circuit would produce a post-pacing interval substantially longer than the tachycardia cycle length, and an automatic focus would not entrain with concealed fusion at all.
  150. The correct answer is C (The site lies outside the reentrant circuit itself.): Manifest fusion, where the paced QRS is a blend of the paced and tachycardia morphologies rather than an identical match, indicates that the pacing wavefront is colliding with the tachycardia wavefront outside the protected circuit, meaning the site lies outside the true reentrant pathway. A site within the central isthmus would instead produce concealed fusion with an identical paced morphology. This finding argues against, rather than for, selecting this location as the primary ablation target.
  151. The correct answer is B (This site lies within the critical isthmus and is a good ablation target.): Concealed fusion, a post-pacing interval closely matching the tachycardia cycle length, and mid-diastolic electrical activity together satisfy the classic criteria identifying a site within the reentrant circuit's critical isthmus, making it an excellent ablation target. A true bystander site would show a longer post-pacing interval despite possibly showing diastolic activity. Triggered activity would not entrain with concealed fusion, and there is no basis here to expect spontaneous termination.
  152. The correct answer is A (The site is outside the circuit despite the concealed fusion.): Concealed fusion alone is not sufficient to identify an isthmus site; a post-pacing interval that substantially exceeds the tachycardia cycle length, generally by more than 30 ms, indicates that conduction time to reach the true circuit is prolonged, meaning the site lies outside or at the periphery of the reentrant pathway. Both concealed fusion and a matching post-pacing interval are required together to confirm an isthmus location. This finding argues against selecting this site as a primary ablation target.
  153. The correct answer is C (Amiodarone loaded orally or intravenously to suppress recurrent episodes.): Amiodarone is the most effective antiarrhythmic for suppressing recurrent monomorphic ventricular tachycardia in patients with structural heart disease and is commonly used as a bridge to catheter ablation. Digoxin has no meaningful role in suppressing ventricular tachycardia and can be proarrhythmic in this setting. Adenosine is used to terminate certain supraventricular arrhythmias, not to prevent VT, and verapamil can be harmful in structural heart disease-related VT by causing hemodynamic collapse.
  154. The correct answer is D (Procainamide infused intravenously with continuous blood pressure monitoring.): Intravenous procainamide is an appropriate first-line agent for hemodynamically stable sustained monomorphic ventricular tachycardia and can also help distinguish VT from supraventricular tachycardia with aberrancy. Adenosine can be diagnostic in some regular wide complex tachycardias but is not a reliable therapy for confirmed VT and can be harmful in some contexts. Digoxin and diltiazem are not effective and can be dangerous in ventricular tachycardia associated with structural heart disease.
  155. The correct answer is A (Lidocaine given as an intravenous bolus and infusion.): Lidocaine remains a preferred agent for suppressing ventricular arrhythmias arising specifically in the setting of acute myocardial ischemia, given its favorable safety profile and mechanism targeting ischemic, depolarized tissue. Flecainide is contraindicated in structural or ischemic heart disease due to proarrhythmic risk. Sotalol requires careful QT monitoring before use and is not first-line in the acute post-infarction period, and digoxin has no role in suppressing ventricular tachycardia.
  156. The correct answer is B (The left ventricular outflow tract just above the aortic valve.): An early precordial transition by V2, together with a dominant R wave in lead I, favors a left ventricular outflow tract origin rather than a right ventricular outflow tract origin. Right ventricular outflow tract tachycardias typically transition later, around V3 or V4, and usually show a smaller or negative R wave in lead I. The tricuspid annulus and moderator band produce distinctly different QRS morphologies not matching this description.
  157. The correct answer is D (Intensification of beta-blockade to reduce sympathetic drive on the heart.): Intensifying beta-blockade, often combined with sedation, is a first-line strategy in electrical storm because it reduces the sympathetic surge that perpetuates recurrent ventricular tachycardia episodes. Discontinuing beta-blockade would remove this protective effect and worsen the storm. Calcium channel blockers have little role in ischemic or scar-related VT storm, and flecainide is contraindicated in structural heart disease due to proarrhythmic risk.
  158. The correct answer is C (Inpatient monitoring of the QT interval and renal function.): Sotalol carries a dose-dependent risk of QT prolongation and torsades de pointes, so initiation typically requires inpatient monitoring of the QT interval along with renal function, since the drug is renally eliminated and requires dose adjustment for reduced clearance. Digoxin levels and chest radiographs are unrelated to sotalol safety monitoring. Potassium monitoring is relevant but is not sufficient on its own without concurrent ECG assessment of the QT interval.
  159. The correct answer is B (Quinidine started to suppress the arrhythmogenic substrate directly.): Quinidine, through its transient outward potassium current blocking effect, has the strongest evidence for suppressing electrical storm related to Brugada syndrome and is considered a preferred pharmacologic therapy alongside isoproterenol in this setting. Amiodarone has limited efficacy against Brugada-related arrhythmias specifically. Flecainide and other pure sodium channel blockers can worsen the Brugada pattern and are contraindicated, and sotalol is not established therapy for this condition.
  160. The correct answer is C (It preferentially affects myocardial tissue while sparing nearby structures.): Pulsed field ablation uses electroporation rather than thermal energy, which produces relative tissue selectivity for myocardium while reducing injury to adjacent structures such as the esophagus, phrenic nerve, and coronary arteries compared with radiofrequency energy. It still requires electroanatomic mapping to guide catheter positioning, so it does not eliminate that need. Procedure times with pulsed field ablation are generally comparable to or shorter than radiofrequency, and its relative safety near coronary vessels is actually an advantage, not a limitation.
  161. The correct answer is A (Substantial improvement or normalization of ejection fraction over months.): Premature ventricular contraction-induced cardiomyopathy is a reversible process, and successful elimination of a high-burden PVC focus typically leads to substantial recovery, and often normalization, of left ventricular ejection fraction over the following months as the myocardium remodels favorably. This improvement is not immediate and continues to develop over time rather than lasting only days. A durable reduction in PVC burden after ablation is expected to improve rather than worsen ventricular function.
  162. The correct answer is D (It accelerates identification of substrate targets and channel corridors.): AI-assisted mapping platforms analyze large volumes of voltage and activation data to rapidly highlight low-voltage scar, conduction channels, and potential ablation targets, which speeds up substrate characterization compared with manual point-by-point review. The operator still must review and validate the annotated electrograms rather than relying on the software exclusively. This technology does not replace programmed stimulation for induction and confirmation of noninducibility, and it does not guarantee elimination of every inducible arrhythmia.
  163. The correct answer is C (The device lacks antitachycardia pacing to painlessly terminate the VT.): Subcutaneous implantable defibrillators do not have ventricular pacing capability and therefore cannot deliver antitachycardia pacing, which means a slower monomorphic ventricular tachycardia that might otherwise be painlessly pace-terminated will instead require a shock for termination. The device can and does deliver defibrillation shocks, which is its core function. It uses a subcutaneous rather than transvenous lead system by design, and most current systems do support remote interrogation and monitoring.
  164. The correct answer is B (The lead captures conduction system tissue producing a paced RBBB pattern.): Left bundle branch area pacing captures conduction system fibers on the left septal side, which activates the left ventricle rapidly via the native conduction system while right ventricular activation lags slightly, producing a paced QRS with a right bundle branch block-like appearance on the surface ECG. This is unrelated to coronary sinus pacing, which is used for conventional biventricular pacing instead. The lead does actively pace the ventricle, and it is positioned in the septum rather than the right atrial appendage.
  165. The correct answer is A (Intracardiac electrogram-based algorithms analyzing local ventricular signal timing.): Because a single-chamber leadless pacemaker has no atrial lead, it relies on sophisticated intracardiac electrogram-based sensing algorithms that analyze local ventricular signal characteristics and timing patterns to differentiate native and ectopic ventricular activity from paced or expected beats. It has no separate atrial lead to provide a reference signal, since the entire device sits within the ventricle. There is no ultrasound imaging component, and ignoring sensing entirely would risk inappropriate competitive pacing.
  166. The correct answer is D (Urgent catheter ablation targeting the ventricular tachycardia substrate.): When ventricular tachycardia storm remains refractory despite optimized beta-blockade, antiarrhythmic drug therapy, and sedation, urgent catheter ablation targeting the underlying arrhythmogenic substrate is the recommended next step to achieve durable control. Discontinuing antiarrhythmic therapy would likely worsen the storm rather than help. Discharge home is unsafe given ongoing recurrent life-threatening arrhythmia, and adding a calcium channel blocker has little proven benefit in scar-related VT storm and can cause hemodynamic compromise.
  167. The correct answer is B (The mechanism is consistent with verapamil-sensitive fascicular tachycardia.): Prompt and reproducible termination with verapamil in a young patient with a structurally normal heart and this characteristic ECG morphology is a diagnostic hallmark of idiopathic fascicular ventricular tachycardia, which involves a calcium-dependent reentrant circuit within Purkinje fascicular tissue. Automatic foci are typically suppressed by different mechanisms and do not respond this specifically to verapamil. This response has no relationship to an accessory pathway or to coronary artery disease, which was not suggested by the clinical presentation.
  168. The correct answer is C (Bidirectional ventricular tachycardia caused by underlying digoxin toxicity itself.): Beat-to-beat alternation of the QRS axis by approximately 180 degrees is the characteristic appearance of bidirectional ventricular tachycardia, a rhythm classically associated with severe digoxin toxicity due to delayed afterdepolarizations arising from alternating Purkinje fascicles. Torsades de pointes instead shows a twisting pattern of QRS amplitude around the baseline rather than fixed beat-to-beat alternation. Fixed monomorphic VT and aberrantly conducted atrial fibrillation do not produce this specific alternating morphology.
  169. The correct answer is D (Inducible sustained polymorphic ventricular tachycardia or fibrillation on stimulation.): In an asymptomatic patient with a spontaneous type 1 Brugada pattern, inducibility of sustained polymorphic ventricular tachycardia or ventricular fibrillation during programmed stimulation is used by some centers as supportive evidence for elevated risk and a rationale to consider defibrillator implantation, since risk stratification in this population otherwise remains difficult. A normal HV interval and normal inducibility would not support this decision. Atrioventricular nodal reentry and sinus node recovery time are unrelated to Brugada-related sudden death risk.
  170. The correct answer is A (Dense myocardial scar capable of harboring reentrant circuits.): Bipolar voltage below 0.5 mV during sinus rhythm defines dense electrical scar, while the surrounding intermediate zone between 0.5 and 1.5 mV represents border zone tissue containing surviving myocyte bundles capable of slow, fractionated conduction that can support reentry. Normal myocardium typically shows bipolar voltage above 1.5 mV. While poor catheter contact can occasionally cause artifactually low voltage, a well-defined scar region with a border zone on a validated map is consistent with true fibrotic substrate rather than artifact, and this pattern does not represent active ischemia.
  171. The correct answer is C (Substrate-based mapping performed during stable sinus or paced rhythm.): When induced ventricular tachycardia is hemodynamically unstable, substrate-based mapping performed during sinus rhythm or ventricular pacing allows identification of scar, border zone, and likely conduction channels without requiring the patient to tolerate sustained tachycardia, making it the appropriate strategy in this situation. Continuing to induce unstable tachycardia repeatedly for activation or entrainment mapping risks further hemodynamic collapse and is unsafe. Abandoning the procedure entirely forgoes an achievable and safer mapping approach.
  172. The correct answer is B (A paced QRS morphology matching in all twelve of twelve leads.): A near-perfect or exact twelve out of twelve lead match between the paced QRS morphology and the clinical premature ventricular contraction is considered the gold standard pace-mapping criterion for identifying the true site of origin. A match in only six of twelve leads is considered a poor match and suggests the site is not the true origin. A mirror-image or unrelated narrow morphology would not support this site as the ectopic focus at all.
  173. The correct answer is D (Intermittent loss of ventricular capture is producing unreliable data.): Beat-to-beat variability in paced QRS morphology and inconsistent post-pacing interval measurements at a fixed pacing rate most commonly reflect intermittent failure to reliably capture the ventricular myocardium, which invalidates entrainment criteria obtained during that attempt. True isthmus or bystander site classification requires consistent, reproducible capture and measurements, which are absent here. The findings do not reliably support either conclusion about the circuit, and there is no indication of a rhythm change to atrial fibrillation.
  174. The correct answer is B (Reentry through a protected isthmus of surviving myocardium within dense scar.): Post-infarct monomorphic VT is classically caused by macroreentry through channels of surviving myocytes running within dense fibrotic scar, forming a protected isthmus with slow conduction. These channels are identified on voltage mapping as corridors of intermediate voltage bordered by scar. Automaticity and triggered activity are less common mechanisms in chronic ischemic scar and typically produce non-sustained or polymorphic rhythms rather than stable monomorphic VT. Reflection is a rare experimental mechanism not typical of clinical post-infarct VT.
  175. The correct answer is C (Late potentials reflecting delayed, fractionated conduction through scar.): Late potentials are low-amplitude, fractionated electrograms that extend past the surface QRS, representing slow, delayed conduction through diseased scar tissue that can sustain reentry. Their presence identifies critical substrate for ablation, and their elimination is a common procedural endpoint. Far-field signals from viable myocardium occur earlier and merge with the local electrogram rather than extending beyond it. Poor contact would produce inconsistent, non-reproducible signals rather than the characteristic fractionated morphology described.
  176. The correct answer is A (Macroreentry through a fixed scar-related circuit with a stable exit.): A regular wide complex tachycardia with a uniform QRS morphology in a patient with ischemic cardiomyopathy is monomorphic ventricular tachycardia, most often driven by macroreentry through a fixed scar-related circuit with a consistent exit site. Multifocal atrial tachycardia and sinus tachycardia with bundle branch block would show narrow or variable P waves and beat-to-beat variability inconsistent with this regular monomorphic pattern. Atrial fibrillation conducting over an accessory pathway produces an irregularly irregular wide complex rhythm, not the regular uniform morphology described.
  177. The correct answer is D (Scar homogenization targeting all abnormal electrograms within the substrate.): Scar homogenization is a substrate-based strategy that ablates all abnormal, fractionated, and late electrograms throughout the low-voltage scar region, not just the single clinical VT isthmus, aiming to eliminate multiple potential reentry pathways. This approach is particularly useful when VT is hemodynamically unstable or multiple morphologies are inducible. Activation and entrainment mapping require the patient to tolerate sustained tachycardia, which is often not feasible in this population. Pace mapping alone cannot substitute for systematic elimination of the abnormal substrate.
  178. The correct answer is B (Percutaneous subxiphoid puncture into the pericardial space.): Percutaneous epicardial access is most commonly obtained through a subxiphoid puncture into the pericardial space using a needle and guidewire technique, allowing catheter mapping and ablation without surgery. Retrograde aortic and transseptal approaches access the left ventricular or left atrial endocardium, not the pericardial space. Surgical thoracotomy is reserved for cases with dense pericardial adhesions or prior cardiac surgery that prevent percutaneous access, not a routine first approach.
  179. The correct answer is A (An epicardial or midmyocardial scar not reached by endocardial mapping.): A discordant pattern of normal endocardial voltage with extensive epicardial low voltage, particularly in the basal inferolateral region, is a recognized signature of nonischemic cardiomyopathy where scar is often epicardial or midmyocardial rather than subendocardial. Poor contact force typically produces patchy, inconsistent voltage rather than a reproducible regional pattern. Epicardial fat can lower unipolar voltage but usually preserves bipolar voltage, and respiratory motion causes point instability rather than a consistent regional low-voltage zone.
  180. The correct answer is C (The site is likely near the tachycardia exit from the epicardial scar.): A twelve-lead paced QRS morphology that closely matches the clinical tachycardia, especially with a delayed intrinsicoid deflection typical of epicardial exit, strongly suggests the pacing site lies near the actual tachycardia exit within the epicardial scar. A remote or unrelated site would produce a discordant paced morphology. This pattern is not simply a marker of right ventricular capture or isolated right bundle branch block conduction, since those would not reproduce the full clinical QRS morphology in every lead.
  181. The correct answer is D (Recognizing that VT can still cause right ventricular failure despite LVAD support.): Although a continuous-flow LVAD unloads the left ventricle and can maintain systemic perfusion during VT, sustained tachyarrhythmia can still precipitate right ventricular failure and hemodynamic compromise, so it should never be dismissed as benign. Emergent device removal is not indicated for VT alone, and anticoagulation should not be stopped without specific evidence of pump thrombosis. Careful hemodynamic monitoring and consideration of antiarrhythmic therapy or ablation are appropriate rather than assuming the arrhythmia is inconsequential.
  182. The correct answer is A (Suction events from cannula contact with the interventricular septum.): Hypovolemia or excessive pump speed can cause the LVAD inflow cannula to abut and mechanically irritate the interventricular septum, triggering suction events that provoke ventricular ectopy and VT, often heralded by low flow alarms. This mechanical trigger is distinct from progression of the underlying scar substrate, which would not correlate tightly with volume status. Accessory pathway conduction and sinus node dysfunction are not typical consequences of LVAD suction events and do not explain this specific temporal pattern.
  183. The correct answer is B (Using fluoroscopy and electroanatomic mapping to avoid cannula contact.): Catheter ablation in LVAD patients requires careful integration of fluoroscopic guidance and electroanatomic mapping to safely navigate around the inflow cannula and avoid dislodging thrombus or damaging the device. Removing the device or disabling the pump during ablation would cause hemodynamic collapse in a patient who depends on continuous support. A purely surgical epicardial approach is reserved for cases where percutaneous endocardial access is not feasible, not a routine requirement for every LVAD ablation.
  184. The correct answer is D (Percutaneous left ventricular assist device providing hemodynamic support during mapping.): A percutaneous left ventricular assist device such as Impella provides active hemodynamic support that allows prolonged activation and entrainment mapping during induced, hemodynamically unstable VT without the operator needing to terminate tachycardia prematurely. An intra-aortic balloon pump provides only modest augmentation and cannot fully support cardiac output during sustained VT. Venovenous ECMO addresses oxygenation, not circulatory support, and a temporary right ventricular device would not address the left-sided hemodynamic collapse typical of VT in this population.
  185. The correct answer is C (Mechanical circulatory support enables mapping of otherwise unstable VT.): This scenario illustrates the core benefit of percutaneous mechanical circulatory support during ablation, allowing hemodynamically unstable VT to be mapped in detail because the device maintains adequate perfusion pressure despite the tachycardia. The device supports the circulation but does not eliminate the arrhythmogenic scar substrate itself, so ablation is still required. Support remains necessary throughout tachycardia induction, not only before it starts, and the sustained monomorphic pattern shown is consistent with true VT rather than artifact.
  186. The correct answer is B (To provide full cardiopulmonary support allowing safe prolonged ablation.): Venoarterial ECMO provides comprehensive cardiopulmonary support in patients with refractory VT storm and cardiogenic shock, allowing the electrophysiologist to safely perform prolonged mapping and ablation despite ongoing hemodynamic instability. ECMO itself does not terminate the tachycardia, which still requires cardioversion, ablation, or antiarrhythmic therapy directed at the substrate. It does not eliminate the need for antiarrhythmic drugs during the acute period, and long-term arrhythmia monitoring remains essential after the patient recovers.
  187. The correct answer is A (Electrical storm, defined as three or more separate VT episodes within 24 hours.): Electrical storm is defined as three or more separate episodes of sustained ventricular tachycardia or fibrillation occurring within a 24-hour period, often requiring multiple defibrillator therapies or cardioversions. It is strongly associated with underlying ischemic or structural scar and often prompts consideration of ablation or mechanical support. Torsades de pointes is a polymorphic VT tied to QT prolongation, accelerated idioventricular rhythm is a slow benign reperfusion rhythm, and bidirectional VT is a distinct pattern classically linked to digoxin toxicity, none of which match this presentation.
  188. The correct answer is D (It marks a likely critical isthmus site for future VT ablation.): Deceleration zones, where conduction velocity progressively slows within a scar channel during sinus or paced rhythm, correspond closely to the critical isthmus sites that sustain reentrant VT and are increasingly used as ablation targets even without inducing tachycardia. This finding reflects diseased, arrhythmogenic tissue rather than healthy myocardium. It is a reproducible electrophysiologic phenomenon rather than a mapping artifact, and it is unrelated to the presence of epicardial fat, which does not produce this activation pattern.
  189. The correct answer is C (The PVC originates from the same critical isthmus that sustains the VT.): When a spontaneous premature ventricular contraction matches the clinical VT morphology and its ablation eliminates both the ectopy and inducibility of sustained tachycardia, this strongly indicates the PVC arises from the same critical isthmus that sustains the reentrant circuit. This relationship is often exploited clinically to guide ablation using spontaneous ectopy when sustained VT cannot be safely induced or tolerated. The PVC is not an unrelated automatic focus, since its elimination directly abolished the tachycardia substrate rather than having no effect.
  190. The correct answer is A (Noninducibility supports likely elimination of the targeted arrhythmogenic substrate.): Failure to reinduce a previously reproducible VT using an adequate stimulation protocol after ablation is a favorable procedural endpoint suggesting the arrhythmogenic substrate has been effectively modified or eliminated, though it does not guarantee long-term freedom from recurrence. Noninducibility does not by itself justify discontinuing defibrillator therapy, since other VT morphologies or future scar progression can still occur. The original tachycardia was a genuine clinical arrhythmia, not an artifact, given its documented reproducible induction before ablation.
  191. The correct answer is B (The central isthmus, since it is the narrowest, most vulnerable segment.): The central protected isthmus is the narrowest and most electrically vulnerable segment of a reentrant circuit, and successful ablation there is generally the most effective way to terminate and prevent recurrence of the tachycardia, since it interrupts the sole conduction pathway sustaining reentry. Targeting only the exit site can leave the isthmus intact and allow a new exit to form. The entrance zone alone is similarly insufficient, and ablating bystander tissue outside the actual circuit has no meaningful effect on tachycardia recurrence.
  192. The correct answer is C (Avoid ablation at that site or use modified technique to protect the vessel.): When an epicardial ablation target lies directly over a major coronary artery, the operator should avoid ablating at that precise site or use techniques such as reduced power, irrigation adjustment, or alternative energy sources to minimize the risk of coronary injury or stenosis. Proceeding with full-power ablation risks acute vessel occlusion or delayed stenosis. Abandoning the entire procedure is usually unnecessary since other targets may still be safely reached, and injecting contrast directly into the artery during ablation is not a recognized protective technique.
  193. The correct answer is D (Continue close monitoring with imaging and be prepared to drain if it enlarges.): A small, hemodynamically insignificant pericardial effusion during epicardial access or ablation should be closely monitored with repeat imaging, with the team prepared to perform pericardiocentesis promptly if the effusion enlarges or causes tamponade physiology. Emergency surgery is reserved for uncontrolled bleeding or tamponade that cannot be managed percutaneously. Effusions during epicardial procedures are common and clinically relevant enough to require monitoring, not dismissal, and anticoagulation decisions should be individualized rather than permanently discontinued based on a small stable effusion.
  194. The correct answer is B (Localized inflammation near the device can create a new arrhythmogenic focus.): Device-related infection with local inflammation near the LVAD pocket or cannula can create adjacent myocardial irritation and scar, generating a new arrhythmogenic focus or altering the existing substrate, so new VT in this setting should prompt evaluation for infection as a contributing cause. This relationship makes the temporal association clinically meaningful rather than coincidental. Staphylococcal infection does not cause accessory pathway formation, which is a congenital finding, and new arrhythmia alone does not confirm mechanical device failure without additional pump parameter abnormalities.
  195. The correct answer is D (True VT can occur with preserved pressure because the LVAD maintains flow.): In a patient with a continuous-flow LVAD, true sustained VT can occur while arterial pressure and pump flow remain relatively preserved, because the device continues to actively unload and eject blood from the left ventricle independent of the native rhythm. This does not mean the electrocardiographic finding is artifact, since the underlying arrhythmia is genuinely present and can still risk right ventricular failure or later decompensation. Preserved pressure does not exclude VT in this population, and the tracing alone does not indicate mechanical device failure.
  196. The correct answer is A (Functional substrate mapping performed without relying on tachycardia induction.): Functional substrate mapping uses signals recorded during stable sinus or paced rhythm, such as late potentials, deceleration zones, and pace-mapped morphology matches, to identify likely critical VT sites without requiring the patient to tolerate induced sustained tachycardia. This approach is valuable when hemodynamic intolerance precludes activation or entrainment mapping. It is not purely anatomic, since it relies heavily on detailed electrogram analysis, and unlike entrainment or activation mapping it does not require tachycardia to be induced or sustained.
  197. The correct answer is C (The phrenic nerve is at risk, and the site should be avoided or protected.): Diaphragmatic capture during high-output pacing from an epicardial catheter indicates proximity to the phrenic nerve, most often along the lateral and posterolateral left ventricular epicardium, and the operator should avoid ablating at that exact site or use techniques such as pericardial saline or balloon separation to protect the nerve. The vagus nerve, coronary sinus, and esophagus are not identified by diaphragmatic capture on pacing, so this finding specifically implicates phrenic nerve proximity rather than those other structures.
  198. The correct answer is D (It provides biventricular and respiratory support beyond isolated left ventricular unloading.): Venoarterial ECMO provides combined biventricular circulatory support along with gas exchange, making it advantageous over an isolated percutaneous left ventricular assist device in patients with significant right ventricular dysfunction or respiratory compromise in addition to VT. Vascular access complications remain a real risk with ECMO cannulation, and systemic anticoagulation is still required to prevent circuit thrombosis. Patients supported with ECMO for ablation still require intensive postprocedural monitoring for hemodynamic and mechanical complications, not less monitoring than other support strategies.
  199. The correct answer is B (The faster tachycardia, since a shorter cycle length limits diastolic filling.): A faster VT with a shorter cycle length, such as 260 ms compared with 450 ms, generally produces greater hemodynamic compromise because the abbreviated diastolic filling time and reduced cardiac output are more pronounced at higher rates, especially in a patient with underlying cardiomyopathy. Cycle length is a reasonably reliable predictor of tolerance in this context, so tolerance is not unpredictable. The slower tachycardia does not inherently cause more ischemia, and the two rhythms would not be expected to be equally well tolerated given their different rates.
  200. The correct answer is A (Extended duration or higher power ablation with careful temperature monitoring.): For deep intramural substrate that is not reached by standard lesions, extending ablation duration or using higher power with irrigation and careful temperature and impedance monitoring can help achieve deeper lesion formation, sometimes combined with techniques such as simultaneous unipolar ablation or needle ablation in refractory cases. Switching entirely to cryoballoon ablation is not standard for focal VT substrate ablation. Reducing irrigation flow to zero increases the risk of char formation and steam pop rather than improving lesion depth, and external beam radiation is reserved for select refractory cases under specialized protocols, not routine deep substrate ablation.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (A surgical or hybrid approach to obtain epicardial access safely.): When dense pericardial adhesions from prior cardiac surgery prevent safe percutaneous subxiphoid access, a surgical approach, such as a limited subxiphoid window or hybrid procedure with a cardiac surgeon, is the appropriate alternative to obtain epicardial access safely. Repeated blind percutaneous attempts risk cardiac perforation in the setting of adhesions. Relying solely on endocardial ablation may leave critical epicardial substrate untreated, and transseptal puncture accesses the left atrium, not the pericardial space, so it cannot substitute for epicardial access.
  2. The correct answer is D (Defibrillator therapies should be temporarily reprogrammed or disabled during ablation.): In patients undergoing VT ablation, implantable defibrillator therapies are typically reprogrammed to monitor-only or have detection and therapy temporarily disabled during the procedure to prevent inappropriate shocks during programmed stimulation and induced tachycardia, with therapies restored afterward. The defibrillator remains clinically important even with an LVAD in place, since it still treats sustained ventricular arrhythmias. Permanent LVAD explantation is not required for device reprogramming, and having both an LVAD and defibrillator does not itself preclude a well-planned ablation procedure.
  3. The correct answer is B (Border zone tissue with intermediate voltage bridging scar and healthy muscle.): Hibernating but viable myocardium typically shows intermediate bipolar voltage, classifying it as border zone tissue that bridges normal myocardium and dense scar, and this region often harbors the diseased conducting channels responsible for VT. It is not equivalent to fully normal tissue, which would show preserved voltage without abnormal electrogram characteristics. It is also distinct from dense scar, which shows very low or absent voltage, and this intermediate finding is a genuine electrophysiologic pattern rather than a mapping artifact requiring repeat acquisition.
  4. The correct answer is A (The esophagus, given its close posterior proximity to the left ventricle.): The esophagus runs in close proximity to the posterior left atrium and can also lie near the posterior and basal left ventricular epicardium, making thermal esophageal injury an important risk during ablation at this location, sometimes managed with esophageal temperature monitoring or mechanical deviation. The trachea and thymus are located in the anterior mediastinum and are not adjacent to the posterior left ventricle. The thoracic duct courses through the posterior mediastinum but is not the structure of primary concern during posterior left ventricular epicardial ablation.
  5. The correct answer is C (The tachycardia likely arises from the same preexisting scar-related substrate.): When VT morphology after LVAD implantation is unchanged from episodes documented before surgery, this strongly suggests the arrhythmia continues to arise from the same preexisting scar-related substrate, since LVAD implantation unloads the ventricle but does not remove or heal the underlying fibrotic scar. The device itself is not the primary cause of an arrhythmia with an identical preoperative morphology. LVAD implantation is not expected to eliminate preexisting arrhythmic substrate, which is why many patients continue to require antiarrhythmic therapy or ablation after implantation.
  6. The correct answer is D (Gradually wean support while closely monitoring hemodynamic stability.): After successful ablation with noninducibility confirmed and no ongoing arrhythmia, mechanical circulatory support should typically be gradually weaned while closely monitoring hemodynamic parameters, allowing the team to confirm the patient tolerates reduced support before device removal. Leaving support running indefinitely without reassessment ignores the risks of continued vascular access and device-related complications. Removing the device abruptly without first confirming stability risks hemodynamic collapse, and increasing support to maximum output is unnecessary once the procedural goals have already been achieved.
  7. The correct answer is A (Ventricular tachycardia, since AV dissociation and fusion beats indicate a ventricular origin.): Atrioventricular dissociation with more ventricular complexes than atrial complexes, along with fusion beats representing partial capture of the ventricle by a sinus impulse, are classic and highly specific electrocardiographic findings for ventricular tachycardia rather than a supraventricular rhythm with aberrant conduction. Supraventricular tachycardia with aberrancy typically maintains a fixed relationship between P waves and QRS complexes. Atrial fibrillation produces an irregularly irregular rhythm rather than a regular tachycardia, and sinus tachycardia with bundle branch block would show a one-to-one relationship between P waves and QRS complexes, not dissociation.
  8. The correct answer is B (Local abnormal ventricular activities distinct from far-field signals.): Local abnormal ventricular activities, often abbreviated LAVA, are sharp, distinct near-field electrogram components clearly separated from the far-field ventricular signal, and they can occur during or after the QRS complex, marking diseased tissue capable of sustaining reentry. Their elimination is a recognized ablation endpoint associated with improved outcomes. These signals are not characteristic of healthy myocardium, and unipolar electrograms by definition reflect a broader far-field view rather than isolating a sharp local component. Sinus node electrograms are unrelated and would not be recorded during ventricular substrate mapping.
  9. The correct answer is C (Hold or reverse anticoagulation to a safe level before epicardial puncture.): Percutaneous epicardial puncture carries a meaningful risk of bleeding into the pericardial space, so anticoagulation should be held, bridged, or reversed to a safe level before the procedure, then carefully resumed afterward, balancing bleeding risk against the thrombotic risk from the mechanical valve. Continuing full therapeutic anticoagulation or increasing the dose further would substantially increase the risk of hemopericardium and tamponade during needle access. Anticoagulation status is highly relevant to procedural safety and must be actively managed, not ignored, before attempting epicardial puncture.
  10. The correct answer is D (Prolonged arrhythmia burden or volume shifts have stressed the right ventricle.): In LVAD patients, prolonged arrhythmia burden, fluid administration, or increased venous return during a long ablation procedure can precipitate or worsen right ventricular dysfunction, which is a well-recognized vulnerability in this population even when left-sided device flow remains stable. This is not evidence of primary LVAD mechanical failure, since the left-sided parameters remain preserved. While pulmonary embolism is a differential consideration, it is not the most likely explanation given the clear procedural context, and right ventricular function is very much affected by prolonged arrhythmia and volume shifts in this setting.
  11. The correct answer is A (Assisting rapid, consistent identification of arrhythmogenic substrate during mapping.): AI-assisted electroanatomic mapping tools use algorithms trained on large electrogram datasets to help rapidly and consistently flag abnormal, scar-related signals such as late potentials and fractionated electrograms, supporting the operator during substrate mapping and potentially reducing procedure time. These tools are intended to assist, not replace, physician interpretation and clinical decision making during ablation. They do not eliminate the role of programmed stimulation for confirming ablation endpoints, and no mapping technology can guarantee permanent freedom from VT recurrence given the progressive nature of underlying cardiomyopathy.
  12. The correct answer is B (Pulsed field ablation using nonthermal electroporation to injure myocardial tissue.): Pulsed field ablation delivers high-voltage electrical pulses that cause nonthermal irreversible electroporation of myocardial cell membranes, and because it is largely tissue-selective and nonthermal, early data suggest a potential safety advantage when ablating near structures such as coronary arteries and the esophagus compared with thermal energy sources. Standard irrigated radiofrequency ablation relies on continuous thermal injury, which carries greater risk to adjacent structures. Cryoballoon and laser balloon ablation are also thermal-based techniques used primarily for pulmonary vein isolation rather than ventricular substrate ablation, not the nonthermal mechanism described.
  13. The correct answer is D (Epicardial fat overlying relatively preserved deeper myocardium.): A pattern of reduced bipolar voltage with relatively preserved unipolar voltage on epicardial mapping is a recognized signature of epicardial fat overlying deeper myocardium that remains relatively viable, since fat attenuates the closely spaced bipolar signal more than the farther-reaching unipolar signal. In contrast, dense transmural scar or true epicardial fibrosis typically reduces both bipolar and unipolar voltage concordantly. This discordant pattern is a genuine, clinically useful mapping finding rather than an artifact to be dismissed, helping distinguish fat from true arrhythmogenic scar.
  14. The correct answer is C (Corrected QT interval, given sotalol's risk of QT prolongation and torsades.): Sotalol is a class III antiarrhythmic with well-recognized risk of dose-dependent QT prolongation and torsades de pointes, so the corrected QT interval must be monitored closely, particularly at initiation and with any change in renal function or electrolyte status, regardless of LVAD support. Sotalol does not directly raise digoxin levels, and stating it has no cardiac electrical effects is incorrect given its core mechanism. Potassium should be monitored throughout therapy, not only with the very first dose, since ongoing hypokalemia increases torsades risk at any point during treatment.
  15. The correct answer is A (It differs, since ischemic scar usually follows a subendocardial, coronary territory pattern.): Ischemic cardiomyopathy scar typically follows a subendocardial to transmural distribution corresponding to a specific coronary artery territory, reflecting the vascular origin of the infarct, whereas nonischemic cardiomyopathy scar is more often patchy and located in midmyocardial or epicardial layers without respecting coronary boundaries. These patterns are not identical between the two conditions, and a nonischemic distribution does not indicate undiagnosed coronary disease. Recognizing this distinction provides genuinely useful diagnostic and procedural planning information, including anticipating the likely need for epicardial access in nonischemic disease.
  16. The correct answer is B (Promptly evaluate for limb ischemia and consider distal perfusion catheter placement.): A cool, pulseless limb distal to a large-bore arterial access site during mechanical circulatory support is concerning for limb ischemia, a well-recognized complication of percutaneous ventricular assist devices, and requires prompt vascular assessment with consideration of a distal perfusion catheter or other intervention to restore flow. Continuing the procedure without addressing this finding risks irreversible limb damage. Simply increasing anticoagulation without vascular assessment does not address a mechanical obstruction to flow, and this finding is a recognized complication that must be treated promptly, not assumed to be benign or expected.
  17. The correct answer is A (Combined endocardial and epicardial mapping and ablation of the transmural substrate): ARVC substrate is typically transmural with predominant epicardial involvement of the right ventricular free wall, so endocardial-only ablation frequently leaves arrhythmogenic tissue untouched and the clinical VT recurs. A combined endocardial and epicardial approach allows mapping and ablation of the deeper, often epicardially predominant, fibrofatty scar. Endocardial-only strategies have documented lower long-term freedom from VT in ARVC than combined approaches. Options C and D target anatomic regions unrelated to the described ARVC substrate.
  18. The correct answer is C (Located deeper within the myocardial wall than typical ischemic scar): Nonischemic dilated cardiomyopathy characteristically produces patchy, mid-myocardial or subepicardial fibrosis that spares the endocardium, in contrast to postinfarction scar, which begins subendocardially and extends outward following a coronary territory. This deeper, often mid-wall location explains why endocardial voltage mapping can underestimate scar extent and why epicardial access is more often required for successful ablation. A single coronary territory distribution and a subendocardial pattern both describe ischemic, not nonischemic, substrate.
  19. The correct answer is B (Subxiphoid needle puncture with fluoroscopic guidance into the pericardial space): Percutaneous epicardial access for VT ablation is most commonly obtained by a subxiphoid puncture, advancing a needle under fluoroscopic guidance into the pericardial space using a syringe with continuous or intermittent negative pressure, followed by guidewire and sheath placement. Transseptal and retrograde aortic access reach endocardial chambers, not the pericardial space. Direct transthoracic ventricular puncture is not a standard epicardial access technique and carries unacceptable risk of injury.
  20. The correct answer is D (Ejection fraction usually improves substantially over weeks to months after ablation): PVC-induced cardiomyopathy is a reversible process once the causative arrhythmia burden is eliminated. After successful catheter ablation, left ventricular ejection fraction typically improves gradually over several weeks to a few months as adverse remodeling regresses, rather than recovering instantly or remaining fixed. Some patients do not achieve full normalization, particularly with longer-standing dysfunction, but meaningful improvement is the expected pattern. Ablation itself does not cause further depression of ventricular function when the PVC focus is the underlying cause.
  21. The correct answer is B (Idiopathic right ventricular outflow tract ventricular tachycardia or ectopy): A left bundle branch block morphology with an inferior axis and early precordial transition, mapping to earliest activation in the right ventricular outflow tract, is the classic presentation of idiopathic RVOT VT or PVCs, which typically occurs in structurally normal hearts. Fascicular VT instead produces a right bundle branch block morphology. ARVC-related VT can mimic RVOT morphology but is accompanied by structural abnormalities on imaging, which are absent here. Bundle branch reentry requires underlying conduction system disease, not present in this patient.
  22. The correct answer is A (Reentry involving abnormal Purkinje tissue within the left posterior fascicle): Verapamil-sensitive fascicular VT, also called Belhassen tachycardia, produces a right bundle branch block morphology with left axis deviation and results from a reentrant circuit incorporating abnormal, slowly conducting Purkinje fibers near the left posterior fascicle. Its prompt response to verapamil reflects calcium-dependent slow conduction within that circuit, distinguishing it from automatic or catecholamine-triggered mechanisms. An outflow tract focus would not typically respond to verapamil in this fashion, and there is no structural scar in this patient.
  23. The correct answer is D (The left or right coronary cusp region of the aortic sinuses): A right bundle branch block morphology with an inferior axis, an early precordial transition, and a tall R wave in V1 suggests a left-sided outflow origin, and mapping earliest activation above the aortic valve confirms an aortic cusp source, most often the left or right coronary cusp. RVOT foci instead usually produce a left bundle branch block pattern with a later precordial transition. Papillary muscle and moderator band foci have distinctly different activation patterns and are not located above the aortic valve.
  24. The correct answer is C (Deep or epicardial scar exists despite a normal endocardial bipolar map): Endocardial bipolar voltage can appear normal even when deeper mid-myocardial or epicardial scar is present, because bipolar recordings preferentially reflect superficial tissue. A reduced unipolar voltage over the same region, which has a wider field of view extending deeper into and beyond the wall, raises suspicion for deep or epicardial disease that bipolar mapping alone would miss. This finding supports pursuing epicardial mapping rather than concluding the catheter is faulty or reclassifying the arrhythmia mechanism.
  25. The correct answer is A (Disproportionately high, warranting earlier consideration of defibrillator therapy): Lamin A/C cardiomyopathy carries a disproportionately high risk of malignant ventricular arrhythmia and sudden death relative to its degree of left ventricular dysfunction, and current risk models recommend considering defibrillator implantation at higher ejection fraction thresholds than for typical nonischemic cardiomyopathy. Progressive conduction disease and a family history of sudden death further elevate this risk. It is inaccurate to treat this risk as equivalent to other nonischemic cardiomyopathies or as limited to conduction abnormalities alone.
  26. The correct answer is D (Immunosuppressive therapy specifically targeted at the areas of active inflammation): Active myocardial inflammation identified on PET imaging in cardiac sarcoidosis represents a potentially modifiable arrhythmogenic driver, and guideline-directed immunosuppressive therapy, typically corticosteroids with or without steroid-sparing agents, is recommended to reduce ongoing inflammation before or alongside ablation. Revascularization is not indicated since sarcoidosis is not a coronary disease. Antiarrhythmic loading alone or waiting for spontaneous resolution does not address the underlying inflammatory substrate driving VT.
  27. The correct answer is B (The left phrenic nerve, risking permanent diaphragmatic paralysis if ablated): The left phrenic nerve courses along the lateral and anterolateral epicardial surface of the left ventricle, and high-output pacing that captures the diaphragm at a short pacing threshold indicates the nerve lies close to the ablation site, creating a real risk of permanent phrenic nerve injury and diaphragmatic paralysis if energy is delivered there. The vagus, recurrent laryngeal nerve, and sympathetic chain are not located along this epicardial surface and are not the structures at risk in this scenario.
  28. The correct answer is C (Targeting an abnormal Purkinje potential that forms part of the reentrant circuit): Fascicular VT ablation is typically guided by identifying discrete, high-frequency Purkinje potentials that precede the QRS in both sinus rhythm and tachycardia, since these represent the abnormal, slowly conducting Purkinje tissue forming part of the reentrant circuit. This differs fundamentally from AV node modification or slow pathway ablation used for supraventricular arrhythmias, and it does not rely on voltage-defined scar, since these patients have structurally normal hearts.
  29. The correct answer is D (Intracardiac echocardiography to guide precise, stable catheter-tissue contact): Papillary muscle PVCs and VT are notoriously difficult to ablate because catheter instability on the mobile, trabeculated papillary muscle surface leads to inconsistent lesion delivery, and this contributes to their lower reported ablation success rates compared with outflow tract arrhythmias. Intracardiac echocardiography allows real time visualization of catheter-tissue contact and papillary muscle motion, improving lesion stability and success. Cryoballoon application to the mitral annulus and empiric ablation without mapping are not appropriate strategies for this substrate, and access route alone does not solve the stability problem.
  30. The correct answer is A (A left ventricular outflow tract or aortic sinus origin rather than the RVOT): When outflow tract ectopy shows a left bundle branch block inferior axis pattern but the precordial transition is delayed to V3 or V4 rather than occurring by V2 as in typical RVOT foci, this pattern favors a left ventricular outflow tract or aortic sinus origin, since activation must travel further to reach the anterior precordial leads. A right ventricular apical or tricuspid annular origin would not produce this specific transition pattern, and fascicular VT produces a right, not left, bundle branch block morphology.
  31. The correct answer is C (Above the burden commonly associated with PVC-induced cardiomyopathy risk): PVC-induced cardiomyopathy becomes substantially more likely once PVC burden exceeds roughly 15 to 20% of total beats on ambulatory monitoring, and a burden of 22% with concurrent mild ejection fraction reduction is consistent with this threshold being clinically relevant in this patient. While additional factors such as QRS duration, focus location, and interpolation also influence risk, burden remains an important and well-validated predictor, contrary to the claim that it never predicts risk or matters only for a single focus.
  32. The correct answer is D (Ventricular bigeminy, alternating one sinus beat with one ectopic beat): Ventricular bigeminy is defined by a strict alternating pattern of one normal sinus-conducted beat followed by one premature ventricular complex, repeating throughout the strip, which is exactly the pattern described. Trigeminy would instead show two sinus beats between each ectopic beat, and this rhythm shows no runs of consecutive wide complexes as would be seen in nonsustained VT. There is also no evidence of atrioventricular dissociation, which would be required to diagnose complete heart block.
  33. The correct answer is A (An epsilon wave recorded in the right precordial leads): An epsilon wave, a small terminal deflection after the QRS complex best seen in the right precordial leads, reflects delayed activation of diseased right ventricular myocardium and is a recognized major criterion in the diagnostic task force criteria for ARVC. Sinus bradycardia and a short PR interval with delta wave are unrelated findings seen in other conditions. Diffuse ST elevation is not a described ARVC criterion and would instead raise concern for pericarditis or another acute process.
  34. The correct answer is D (Delayed afterdepolarizations mediated by cAMP, producing catecholamine-sensitive triggered activity): Idiopathic outflow tract PVCs and VT arise from cyclic AMP-mediated triggered activity in the form of delayed afterdepolarizations, a mechanism that is catecholamine-dependent and characteristically suppressed by adenosine, which lowers intracellular cAMP through its effect on adenylate cyclase. This mechanism is distinct from macroreentry, which requires structural scar, from Purkinje-dependent fascicular reentry, and from catecholamine independent automaticity, none of which explain the adenosine sensitivity described here.
  35. The correct answer is B (The tachycardia is triggered activity dependent on cyclic AMP signaling): Prompt termination of a wide complex tachycardia with adenosine strongly supports a triggered activity mechanism dependent on cyclic AMP-mediated delayed afterdepolarizations, which is the typical mechanism for idiopathic RVOT VT, since adenosine reduces cAMP and abolishes the trigger. Macroreentry around fixed scar is generally not adenosine sensitive in this fashion. An AV nodal origin or an accessory bypass tract would not produce a ventricular tachycardia with this outflow tract morphology and response pattern.
  36. The correct answer is C (The right ventricular outflow tract, a common idiopathic VT location): A wide complex tachycardia with a left bundle branch block morphology and inferior axis is the classic pattern produced by ventricular activation beginning in the right ventricular outflow tract, the most common site of idiopathic VT in patients without structural heart disease. Fascicular VT instead produces a right bundle branch block pattern, and this morphology is not simply aberrant conduction of a supraventricular rhythm. Diffuse left ventricular scar would typically produce a different, often less organized, activation pattern.
  37. The correct answer is D (The scar border extends predominantly along the epicardial aspect): Chagasic cardiomyopathy classically produces an apical left ventricular aneurysm whose arrhythmogenic border zone extends predominantly along the epicardial surface, similar in distribution to some nonischemic substrates, which often necessitates epicardial mapping and ablation for procedural success. The aneurysm in Chagas disease is left, not right, ventricular, and the disease does not reliably respond to beta-blockers alone. A substrate confined purely to the endocardium would not explain the need for epicardial access.
  38. The correct answer is A (The mitral annulus itself, a recognized idiopathic ventricular ectopy site): The mitral annulus is a recognized, though less common, site of origin for idiopathic ventricular ectopy, typically producing a right bundle branch block morphology with variable axis depending on the exact annular location, consistent with mapping to the posteroseptal mitral annulus in this case. An RVOT origin would instead produce a left bundle branch block pattern, and the noncoronary cusp does not participate in ventricular activation since it lies above the level of ventricular myocardium. The moderator band produces a distinctly different activation sequence.
  39. The correct answer is B (A postmyocarditis scar predominantly located in the subepicardium): Postmyocarditis scar characteristically produces patchy, subepicardial to mid-myocardial late gadolinium enhancement, often in the inferolateral wall, which frequently requires epicardial mapping for successful ablation since the arrhythmogenic tissue lies predominantly beneath the epicardial surface rather than the endocardium. A purely endocardial or fully transmural, uniform pattern would not match this imaging description, and the presence of clear late gadolinium enhancement argues against a purely functional, nonstructural mechanism.
  40. The correct answer is C (Generally lower, related to deeper or epicardial substrate location): Ablation outcomes for VT in nonischemic cardiomyopathy are generally less favorable than for postinfarction VT after a single procedure, largely because nonischemic substrate is more often deep, mid-myocardial, or epicardial and therefore harder to fully address with an endocardial-first strategy alone. This is the opposite of nonischemic scar being easier to reach, and outcomes clearly do differ by substrate location rather than being identical. Nonischemic VT is frequently referred for ablation, making the comparison clinically relevant.
  41. The correct answer is A (Approximately 90% or greater freedom from the targeted arrhythmia): Catheter ablation of idiopathic right ventricular outflow tract PVCs and VT at experienced centers achieves long term success rates of approximately 90% or greater, making it a highly effective and commonly recommended option for symptomatic, drug-refractory patients. This is substantially higher than the reported success for papillary muscle ablation, which is limited by catheter instability. Success is high but not guaranteed at 100%, since some recurrence and repeat procedures do occur.
  42. The correct answer is D (A high PVC burden consistent with PVC-induced cardiomyopathy): A consistent one-to-one alternation of sinus beats with premature ventricular complexes, as shown here, demonstrates ventricular bigeminy, and when this pattern is frequent enough to represent a very high overall ectopy burden in a patient with otherwise unexplained new cardiomyopathy, it strongly supports PVC-induced cardiomyopathy as the diagnosis. This tracing is not atrial fibrillation, since the ventricular complexes follow a regular sinus beat rather than an irregular atrial rhythm, and there is no evidence of atrioventricular dissociation required for complete heart block. This finding is not benign and warrants further evaluation.
  43. The correct answer is C (The pulmonary artery, superior to the level of the pulmonic valve): A minority of outflow tract arrhythmias with a left bundle branch block, inferior axis morphology arise from within the pulmonary artery itself, superior to the pulmonic valve, rather than from the right ventricular outflow tract beneath it, and these can often be identified by an unusually tall R wave in V1 and confirmed by activation mapping above the valve plane. The tricuspid annulus and moderator band produce different morphologies, and a left coronary cusp origin would arise from the aortic, not pulmonary, root.
  44. The correct answer is B (Replacement fibrosis within hypertrophied myocardium forms the reentrant substrate): In hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, areas of myocardial disarray and replacement fibrosis, often identified as late gadolinium enhancement on cardiac MRI, correspond to regions of reduced voltage on electroanatomic mapping and can serve as the anatomic substrate for reentrant monomorphic VT. This directly contradicts the idea that hypertrophic cardiomyopathy lacks a true fibrotic substrate. Dynamic outflow obstruction is a separate hemodynamic phenomenon and does not itself explain a mappable, scar-based reentrant tachycardia, and voltage mapping remains a useful tool in this disease.
  45. The correct answer is D (Left posterior fascicular ventricular tachycardia, a verapamil-sensitive VT): A relatively narrow QRS ventricular tachycardia with a right bundle branch block morphology, left axis deviation, and prompt termination with verapamil is the classic presentation of left posterior fascicular VT, which uses rapidly conducting Purkinje tissue and therefore produces a narrower QRS than typical VT. RVOT VT instead produces a left bundle branch block pattern and does not reliably terminate with verapamil. This tracing shows an organized, monomorphic rhythm, not the irregular pattern of atrial fibrillation or the beat-to-beat variation of polymorphic VT.
  46. The correct answer is A (Avoid ablation at that exact site or reduce power to prevent coronary injury): Coronary arteries can run in close proximity to epicardial ablation targets, and radiofrequency energy delivered directly over a coronary vessel risks thermal injury, dissection, or acute occlusion, so coronary angiography is routinely performed before epicardial ablation and sites overlying major vessels are avoided or treated with reduced power. Proceeding at full power disregards a real and serious complication risk. Switching to an endocardial approach does not address the epicardial substrate, and increasing contact force would worsen, not reduce, the risk of vessel injury.
  47. The correct answer is C (It helps identify likely critical isthmus sites more efficiently within complex scar): AI-assisted electroanatomic mapping analyzes complex voltage and electrogram data to help highlight regions that are more likely to represent critical conducting channels within scar, potentially improving efficiency and consistency in identifying ablation targets in complex nonischemic substrates. It is intended to augment, not eliminate, conventional mapping and operator judgment, and it does not guarantee complete arrhythmia elimination, since procedural success still depends on substrate complexity and lesion delivery.
  48. The correct answer is B (The left anterior fascicle, producing a right axis deviation pattern): Left anterior fascicular VT is a less common variant of fascicular VT that produces a right bundle branch block morphology with right axis deviation, in contrast to the more frequent left posterior fascicular VT, which produces left axis deviation, since the exit site and resulting activation sequence differ between the two fascicles. Bundle branch reentry requires structural conduction disease, which is absent here, and a junctional mechanism would not produce a wide QRS with this specific axis pattern.
  49. The correct answer is A (Meaningful improvement in ejection fraction usually takes weeks to a few months): After successful elimination of the causative PVC burden, left ventricular ejection fraction in PVC-induced cardiomyopathy typically improves gradually over weeks to a few months as reverse remodeling occurs, rather than normalizing within a single day. Not all patients recover completely, particularly with more longstanding dysfunction, but meaningful improvement is the expected pattern following successful ablation alone, without any requirement to continue antiarrhythmic medication indefinitely.
  50. The correct answer is D (It offers relative tissue selectivity that may reduce injury to nearby structures): Pulsed field ablation uses nonthermal electroporation and has shown relative tissue selectivity in early studies, with less injury to nontarget structures such as coronary vessels, the esophagus, and phrenic nerve compared with thermal energy sources, making it an area of active investigation for anatomically challenging epicardial VT substrates. It does not eliminate the need for angiographic assessment near coronaries, its lesion depth is not uniformly deeper than radiofrequency, and it still requires epicardial access to treat epicardial substrate.
  51. The correct answer is B (Reentry within an abnormal Purkinje network near the left posterior fascicle): Verapamil-sensitive fascicular VT is a reentrant arrhythmia involving abnormal, slowly conducting Purkinje tissue near the left posterior fascicle, and reentrant mechanisms are characteristically reproducibly inducible and terminable with programmed electrical stimulation during electrophysiology study, unlike purely automatic or triggered rhythms, which are typically not reliably induced this way. Diffuse scar-related macroreentry would be expected in a structurally abnormal heart, which is not present in this patient.
  52. The correct answer is C (The mobile, trabeculated papillary muscle surface limits stable catheter contact): Papillary muscles are mobile, trabeculated structures that move throughout the cardiac cycle, making it difficult to achieve stable, consistent catheter-tissue contact during ablation, which is the primary reason papillary muscle VT and PVCs have lower reported long-term ablation success than outflow tract arrhythmias. This substrate is not inherently resistant to all energy sources, and standard electroanatomic mapping systems can still localize papillary muscle foci. Lower success reflects genuine recurrence, not an artifact of the reported statistic.
  53. The correct answer is A (The triangle of dysplasia, involving inflow, outflow, and apex): The classic pathologic distribution of ARVC involves the right ventricular inflow tract, outflow tract, and apex, a pattern historically termed the triangle of dysplasia, reflecting the regions most consistently affected by fibrofatty replacement. The Bachmann bundle and Koch triangle are atrial anatomic structures unrelated to ventricular cardiomyopathy, and the circle of Willis is a cerebrovascular structure entirely unrelated to cardiac anatomy.
  54. The correct answer is D (An idiopathic arrhythmia arising from a structurally normal heart): The absence of late gadolinium enhancement on a technically adequate cardiac MRI, combined with normal echocardiography, supports a diagnosis of idiopathic ventricular ectopy arising from a structurally normal heart rather than an underlying scar-based cardiomyopathy such as ARVC, which would typically show fibrofatty or fibrotic changes on imaging.
