This normal imaging also argues against occult coronary disease as the cause. Cardiac MRI remains a valuable and appropriate test in this evaluation, not an unreliable one.
  55. The correct answer is C (Residual epicardial or deep substrate likely was not fully addressed): Early recurrence of the identical clinical VT after an endocardial-only procedure in nonischemic cardiomyopathy strongly suggests that residual arrhythmogenic substrate located deeper within the myocardial wall or on the epicardial surface was not addressed, consistent with the well-documented lower single-procedure success of endocardial-only ablation in this population. This pattern does not indicate an incorrect original diagnosis, and endocardial ablation clearly does not always achieve permanent elimination of nonischemic VT, contrary to option D.
  56. The correct answer is B (The tricuspid annulus is a recognized alternative idiopathic ectopy site): The tricuspid annulus, particularly its septal and anteroseptal aspects, is a recognized, though less common, site of origin for idiopathic ventricular ectopy that can produce a morphology resembling an outflow tract focus, and successful endocardial ablation at the annulus itself is generally achievable. This location does not indicate a mapping error, nor does it prove underlying structural disease in an otherwise normal heart, and it does not inherently require an epicardial approach.
  57. The correct answer is A (It reflects abnormal calcium handling causing polymorphic, not monomorphic, VT): Catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia results from RyR2 or CASQ2-related abnormal intracellular calcium handling that produces delayed afterdepolarizations triggering bidirectional and polymorphic VT under catecholamine surge, which is mechanistically and electrocardiographically distinct from the monomorphic, cAMP mediated but structurally focal arrhythmia seen in idiopathic outflow tract VT. There is no fixed anatomic scar in either condition here, and accessory pathway conduction is unrelated to either arrhythmia.
  58. The correct answer is D (A subcutaneous implantable cardioverter-defibrillator with no transvenous leads at all): A subcutaneous implantable cardioverter-defibrillator avoids transvenous leads entirely while still providing defibrillation for secondary prevention, making it well suited for a young patient without a pacing indication who wishes to minimize long-term intravascular hardware and its associated risks of lead failure and vascular complications. A leadless pacemaker cannot defibrillate, and a dual-chamber transvenous defibrillator or resynchronization device would add unnecessary transvenous leads this patient does not need.
  59. The correct answer is C (A leadless pacemaker implanted directly within the right ventricle): A leadless pacemaker is implanted directly within the right ventricle without any transvenous lead crossing the tricuspid valve, avoiding the long-term risks of lead-related tricuspid regurgitation and lead failure that accompany conventional transvenous systems, making it an appropriate option if permanent AV block develops after ablation near the conduction system. Conventional dual- or single-chamber transvenous pacemakers both still require a transvenous ventricular lead, and epicardial pacing requires a separate surgical procedure rather than the percutaneous approach used here.
  60. The correct answer is A (Spatially dispersed refractory periods create continuous wavebreak that regenerates new reentrant wavelets.): Ventricular fibrillation is sustained by functional reentry in which regions of heterogeneous refractoriness cause propagating wavefronts to fragment and reform continuously, a process termed wavebreak. This self-perpetuating cycle of multiple meandering wavelets, rather than a single dominant driver, explains why VF rarely terminates spontaneously without external defibrillation. A single repetitive focus would instead produce a more organized rhythm such as monomorphic VT, and complete conduction block would simply produce asystole rather than chaotic electrical activity.
  61. The correct answer is B (Polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with a continuously changing QRS axis and shape.): By definition, polymorphic ventricular tachycardia shows a QRS morphology and axis that vary continuously from beat to beat, reflecting a shifting or multiple sites of origin rather than one fixed reentrant circuit. Monomorphic VT, by contrast, maintains a consistent, repeatable QRS shape throughout the tachycardia because it arises from a single stable circuit. Atrial fibrillation with fixed aberrancy or SVT with rate-related block would also show a consistent, unchanging QRS morphology rather than beat-to-beat variability.
  62. The correct answer is A (Deliver an unsynchronized defibrillation shock immediately and begin chest compressions.): A chaotic rhythm with no discernible P waves, QRS complexes, or organized electrical activity in a pulseless patient is ventricular fibrillation, which requires immediate unsynchronized defibrillation combined with high-quality chest compressions per resuscitation guidelines. Synchronized cardioversion cannot be delivered because there is no organized R wave to synchronize to. Adenosine and vagal maneuvers are used for supraventricular arrhythmias with a pulse and have no role in pulseless ventricular fibrillation.
  63. The correct answer is D (Epinephrine 1 milligram intravenously, repeated every three to five minutes.): Advanced cardiac life support protocols call for epinephrine 1 mg intravenously as soon as feasible after the first shock fails, repeated every three to five minutes throughout the resuscitation. Dopamine and phenylephrine are not part of the standard cardiac arrest vasopressor algorithm, and a single fixed-dose norepinephrine bolus without repeat dosing does not match guideline-based cardiac arrest management. Epinephrine improves coronary and cerebral perfusion pressure during ongoing compressions.
  64. The correct answer is B (Amiodarone 300 milligrams given as an intravenous bolus.): For shock-refractory ventricular fibrillation after epinephrine and continued defibrillation attempts, current resuscitation guidelines recommend amiodarone 300 mg as an intravenous bolus, with a second smaller dose available if fibrillation persists. Sotalol and flecainide are not part of the cardiac arrest algorithm and carry their own proarrhythmic risks. Digoxin has no role in acute VF resuscitation and would not be expected to improve return of spontaneous circulation.
  65. The correct answer is A (Lidocaine is an acceptable alternative antiarrhythmic when amiodarone cannot be used.): Current resuscitation guidelines list lidocaine as an acceptable alternative to amiodarone for shock-refractory ventricular fibrillation when amiodarone is unavailable or has already failed, though it has not been shown to replace amiodarone as the preferred first-line agent. It is not mandatory before the first shock, and lidocaine is not contraindicated in cardiac arrest; rather it has a defined, secondary role specifically in refractory VF or pulseless VT.
  66. The correct answer is C (The pause further prolongs repolarization, favoring early afterdepolarizations that trigger torsades.): In patients with an already prolonged QT interval, a preceding pause lengthens the subsequent action potential duration even further, increasing the window for early afterdepolarizations that can trigger torsades de pointes, classically after a short-long-short coupling sequence. Bradycardia does not shorten the QT interval in this setting, vagal tone alone does not directly generate an automatic focus, and coronary perfusion changes are not the driving mechanism of bradycardia-dependent torsades.
  67. The correct answer is D (Torsades de pointes, meaning a twisting of the QRS points.): A polymorphic ventricular tachycardia whose QRS axis progressively twists around the isoelectric baseline, occurring in the setting of QT prolongation, is the classic description of torsades de pointes. Bidirectional VT shows an alternating, not progressively twisting, fixed pair of QRS axes and is more typical of catecholaminergic polymorphic VT or digoxin toxicity. Accelerated idioventricular rhythm and fascicular VT both produce a relatively stable, monomorphic QRS morphology rather than this twisting pattern.
  68. The correct answer is A (Sotalol, which prolongs repolarization in a dose-dependent manner.): Sotalol is a class III antiarrhythmic whose potassium channel blockade prolongs the QT interval in a dose-dependent fashion, making torsades de pointes a well-recognized risk, especially with dose escalation or renal impairment. Lidocaine and mexiletine are class IB agents that shorten rather than prolong repolarization. Verapamil primarily affects atrioventricular nodal conduction and calcium channels and does not typically cause significant QT prolongation.
  69. The correct answer is C (Intravenous magnesium sulfate, regardless of the measured serum magnesium level.): Intravenous magnesium sulfate is the first-line acute therapy for torsades de pointes associated with QT prolongation, and it is given empirically even when the serum magnesium level is normal because it suppresses early afterdepolarizations independent of measured magnesium status. Procainamide is a QT-prolonging agent and would worsen this arrhythmia. Digoxin and verapamil do not address the underlying repolarization abnormality and are not appropriate acute therapies for torsades.
  70. The correct answer is B (Temporary transvenous overdrive pacing to shorten the QT interval and prevent pauses.): When magnesium and drug discontinuation are insufficient and the intrinsic rate remains slow, temporary overdrive pacing shortens the QT interval by increasing the heart rate and eliminates the pauses that precipitate further torsades episodes. Quinidine would worsen QT prolongation, additional beta blockade would further slow the rate and promote more pauses, and elective cardioversion does not prevent recurrent pause-dependent episodes going forward.
  71. The correct answer is A (Acute transmural ischemia creating regional dispersion of refractoriness and reentry.): During acute ST-elevation myocardial infarction, transmural ischemia produces marked regional heterogeneity in refractory periods and conduction velocity, creating the substrate for reentrant ventricular fibrillation early in the ischemic event. This is distinct from the fixed scar-related reentry seen in chronic cardiomyopathy, and it is not explained by an inherited channelopathy given the clear acute ischemic trigger, nor by vagally mediated bradycardia, which does not typically produce fibrillation.
  72. The correct answer is C (Routine defibrillator implantation is not required for VF occurring within 48 hours of infarction with successful revascularization.): Ventricular fibrillation occurring within 48 hours of an acute myocardial infarction, when followed by prompt and complete revascularization, is generally considered a reversible, ischemia-driven event that does not by itself mandate long-term implantable defibrillator therapy, particularly with a recovered ejection fraction. Immediate pre-discharge implantation is not indicated in this setting, and a wearable defibrillator vest may reasonably be considered during recovery rather than being contraindicated.
  73. The correct answer is A (Prompt, complete revascularization substantially reduces the risk of recurrent ischemia-mediated ventricular fibrillation.): When ventricular fibrillation is directly triggered by acute coronary occlusion, restoring normal coronary flow through prompt, complete revascularization removes the ischemic substrate responsible for the arrhythmia and meaningfully lowers the risk of recurrence from that same mechanism. This differs from the persistent scar-based risk seen after established infarction. Revascularization does not increase arrhythmic risk, and recurrence risk here is driven primarily by ongoing ischemia rather than genetic channelopathy testing.
  74. The correct answer is D (Unexplained syncope in the setting of structural heart disease and reduced ejection fraction.): Unexplained syncope in a patient with structural heart disease and reduced ejection fraction, especially after a nondiagnostic noninvasive workup, is a recognized indication for invasive electrophysiology study to assess for inducible ventricular arrhythmia as the cause. A structurally normal heart with a normal ECG, a syncope episode clearly explained by a vasovagal tilt-table result, or a normal ejection fraction without coronary disease would all argue against needing invasive risk stratification.
  75. The correct answer is C (Implantable cardioverter-defibrillator placement for secondary prevention of sudden cardiac death.): A cardiac arrest survivor with documented ventricular fibrillation and no identifiable or reversible cause meets criteria for secondary prevention implantable defibrillator therapy, since her risk of recurrent life-threatening arrhythmia remains substantial. Reassurance alone or amiodarone without a device would leave her unprotected from recurrence. A wearable defibrillator vest is intended as a temporary bridge in reversible or recovering situations, not as a long-term substitute for a permanent device here.
  76. The correct answer is A (Catheter ablation targeting the specific premature ventricular contraction that triggers each episode.): When electrical storm is driven by a reproducible triggering premature ventricular contraction despite antiarrhythmic therapy and sedation, catheter ablation targeting that specific trigger is the most effective definitive therapy and can dramatically reduce further episodes. Discontinuing the defibrillator would leave him unprotected from future life-threatening arrhythmia, simply raising the detection cutoff does not address the underlying trigger, and bilateral renal denervation is not an established first-line therapy for this presentation.
  77. The correct answer is B (A discrete, sharp Purkinje potential preceding the onset of the triggering premature beat.): A successful Purkinje-related trigger ablation target is characterized by a discrete, sharp Purkinje potential that precedes the onset of the triggering premature ventricular contraction, reflecting the specialized conduction tissue's role in initiating the arrhythmia. A late, broad myocardial electrogram or an isolated late potential recorded only in sinus rhythm does not identify the specific trigger site, and a far-field atrial signal is unrelated to a ventricular trigger.
  78. The correct answer is B (Intravenous amiodarone or a beta blocker, with deep sedation for refractory cases.): Acute management of electrical storm centers on intravenous antiarrhythmic therapy, most commonly amiodarone, combined with beta blockade and deep sedation to reduce sympathetic drive, alongside correction of any reversible triggers and device optimization. Withholding all antiarrhythmic drugs until elective ablation delays needed acute therapy, high-dose flecainide alone is not first-line and carries proarrhythmic risk, and immediate surgical epicardial mapping before any medical therapy is not standard initial management.
  79. The correct answer is A (Percutaneous stellate ganglion blockade or surgical cardiac sympathetic denervation.): For refractory, catecholamine-driven electrical storm that persists despite maximal drug therapy and an initial ablation attempt, percutaneous stellate ganglion blockade or surgical cardiac sympathetic denervation has evidence supporting reduced sympathetic outflow to the heart and suppression of further episodes. Bilateral vagus nerve stimulation, repeated carotid sinus massage, and renal sympathetic denervation are not established neuromodulatory therapies for this specific presentation.
  80. The correct answer is D (Certain tricyclic antidepressants and specific antipsychotic medications that prolong repolarization.): Several psychiatric medications, including certain tricyclic antidepressants and specific antipsychotics, are well-recognized causes of acquired QT prolongation and can precipitate torsades de pointes, matching the markedly prolonged corrected QT interval seen here after a dose increase. Beta blockers, angiotensin receptor blockers, and dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers are not typically associated with clinically significant QT prolongation or this presentation.
  81. The correct answer is A (Nonthermal electroporation preferentially affects myocardial tissue while relatively sparing nearby structures.): Pulsed field ablation uses nonthermal electroporation, which has demonstrated relative tissue selectivity for myocardium compared with adjacent structures such as the phrenic nerve, esophagus, and coronary vasculature, making it attractive near the mitral annulus. It does not rely on the same heating mechanism as radiofrequency energy, it is not inherently limited to only very deep lesions, and it has been studied specifically for use near coronary vessels rather than being universally excluded from that location.
  82. The correct answer is B (A lower reported risk of coronary artery and phrenic nerve injury due to tissue selectivity.): Because pulsed field ablation relies on nonthermal electroporation with relative tissue selectivity, it has been associated with a lower reported risk of injury to the coronary arteries and phrenic nerve compared with conventional thermal radiofrequency energy delivered near these structures. It does not eliminate all collateral injury risk entirely, procedure time is not guaranteed to be shorter in every case, and general anesthesia is still commonly used for these procedures regardless of energy source.
  83. The correct answer is C (It reduces operator variability and time spent manually identifying substrate abnormalities.): An AI-assisted mapping algorithm that automatically annotates fractionated and late potentials primarily helps by reducing the time and operator-to-operator variability involved in manually identifying abnormal substrate across a large point cloud, supporting more consistent ablation strategy. It does not replace pacing maneuvers used to confirm specific ablation targets, it cannot guarantee complete elimination of inducible ventricular tachycardia, and it does not eliminate the value of pre-procedural cardiac imaging.
  84. The correct answer is A (More reproducible substrate identification and potentially shorter overall mapping time.): When an AI-assisted mapping platform produces more consistent substrate characterization across operators of varying experience, the most plausible benefit is more reproducible identification of abnormal substrate and potentially reduced time spent on manual annotation during mapping. It does not guarantee fluoroscopy use drops to zero, does not eliminate the role of programmed ventricular stimulation in assessing inducibility, and does not replace intracardiac echocardiography for anatomic guidance.
  85. The correct answer is D (Begin chest compressions immediately and call for the defibrillator without delay.): A completely disorganized, chaotic waveform with no identifiable QRS complexes in an unresponsive, pulseless patient is ventricular fibrillation, and the correct immediate action is to begin high-quality chest compressions and call for the defibrillator without delay, since minimizing time to defibrillation improves survival. Waiting for the code team, spending a full sixty seconds checking a pulse, or giving oral aspirin all inappropriately delay the time-critical interventions this rhythm requires.
  86. The correct answer is B (Stereotactic body radiotherapy directed at the arrhythmogenic myocardial substrate.): For a patient with recurrent, destabilizing ventricular tachycardia who has already failed multiple catheter ablations and is a poor candidate for further invasive procedures due to frailty and comorbid lung disease, stereotactic body radiotherapy, also called cardiac radioablation, offers a noninvasive option directed at the previously mapped arrhythmogenic substrate. Repeating the identical prior endocardial approach is unlikely to add benefit, immediate transplantation is not the next appropriate step, and simply increasing shock energy does not treat the underlying arrhythmia.
  87. The correct answer is C (The full antiarrhythmic effect typically develops gradually over weeks to months afterward.): Unlike catheter ablation, which produces an immediate lesion effect, cardiac radioablation with stereotactic body radiotherapy induces a delayed biological remodeling process in the targeted myocardium, so its full antiarrhythmic benefit typically develops gradually over weeks to months rather than immediately. The treatment is delivered as a single session rather than daily repeated doses, and describing the effect as immediate within minutes or reversing after one year does not reflect its actual delayed and durable time course.
  88. The correct answer is C (Radioablation avoids the added procedural risk of a third invasive catheter-based attempt.): For a patient who has already undergone two prior invasive ablation attempts with significant comorbidity-related procedural risk, single-fraction cardiac radioablation is attractive because it avoids the additional vascular access, anesthesia, and procedural risk of a third invasive catheter-based attempt. It does not carry a substantially higher immediate procedural mortality than repeat catheter ablation, it does not require the identical invasive approach as catheter ablation, and it has not been shown to be broadly superior for routine first-time ventricular tachycardia cases.
  89. The correct answer is A (Sotalol's QT-prolonging effect and torsades risk increase in a dose-dependent fashion.): Sotalol's class III potassium channel blocking effect prolongs the QT interval in a dose-dependent manner, meaning the risk of torsades de pointes rises with dose escalation, making careful QT monitoring during titration essential. Sotalol clearly does affect the QT interval at therapeutic doses, its proarrhythmic risk is not determined solely by baseline heart rate, and it can prolong the QT interval on its own without requiring concomitant digoxin.
  90. The correct answer is C (Hypokalemia and hypomagnesemia prolonging repolarization and precipitating torsades.): Chronic diuretic use commonly causes combined hypokalemia and hypomagnesemia, both of which prolong myocardial repolarization and lower the threshold for early afterdepolarizations, precipitating torsades de pointes as seen here with her low serum potassium and magnesium. Hypercalcemia shortens rather than prolongs the QT interval, hyperkalemia classically produces peaked T waves and eventual widened QRS or sine-wave patterns rather than torsades, and hypernatremia is not a recognized cause of this specific arrhythmia.
  91. The correct answer is A (Beta blockade should be used cautiously, since bradycardia can worsen her arrhythmia risk.): In long QT syndrome type 3, arrhythmic events are often bradycardia- or pause-dependent rather than catecholamine-triggered, so beta blockade must be used cautiously since it can worsen bradycardia and paradoxically increase her arrhythmic risk, sometimes requiring pacing support alongside therapy. Beta blockade is not absolutely contraindicated across all long QT subtypes, exercise restriction remains relevant counseling regardless of subtype, and pacemaker therapy can be appropriate in bradycardia-dependent presentations rather than never indicated.
  92. The correct answer is A (A short-coupled variant of idiopathic VF, often arising from a Purkinje-related trigger.): A very short-coupled premature ventricular contraction consistently initiating episodes of idiopathic ventricular fibrillation in a structurally and genetically normal heart describes the short-coupled variant of idiopathic VF, which frequently originates from Purkinje-related tissue. This differs from the long-QT-dependent mechanism of acquired torsades, does not reflect a slowly conducting scar-based circuit since there is no structural disease, and is not consistent with a sinus node discharge given the documented ventricular fibrillation.
  93. The correct answer is D (Quinidine, which shortens the coupling interval and suppresses the triggering ectopy.): Quinidine has specific evidence supporting its use in short-coupled idiopathic ventricular fibrillation, where it reduces the transient outward current, lengthens the effective refractory period, and suppresses the very short-coupled triggering ectopy responsible for recurrent episodes. Digoxin and amlodipine are not established therapies for this phenotype, and while beta blockade may be used, it is not considered the primary specific pharmacologic therapy for this particular short-coupled VF variant.
  94. The correct answer is C (Catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia, classically producing bidirectional ventricular tachycardia with exertion.): Exercise-induced syncope in a young patient with a structurally normal heart, reproducibly provoking a bidirectional ventricular tachycardia with a beat-to-beat alternating QRS axis, is the classic presentation of catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia. Brugada syndrome is typically fever-provoked rather than exercise-provoked, arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy classically produces monomorphic rather than bidirectional VT, and early repolarization syndrome is more associated with nocturnal than exertional arrhythmic events.
  95. The correct answer is B (A beat-to-beat alternating, rather than continuously varying, QRS axis.): The bidirectional pattern classically seen in catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia is distinguished from more generic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia by a QRS axis that alternates between two fixed positions on a beat-to-beat basis, rather than varying continuously and unpredictably as in torsades-type polymorphic VT. Heart rate alone, episode duration, and the presence of ST-segment elevation do not differentiate these two morphologic patterns.
  96. The correct answer is A (Quinidine, which reduces the transient outward current and suppresses arrhythmic episodes.): Quinidine has the strongest evidence among antiarrhythmic agents for reducing arrhythmic burden in Brugada syndrome by blocking the transient outward current responsible for the characteristic ECG pattern and arrhythmic substrate, making it particularly useful for recurrent electrical storm not fully controlled by fever management alone. Amlodipine, digoxin, and furosemide have no established role in suppressing Brugada-related ventricular fibrillation.
  97. The correct answer is A (Early repolarization syndrome, an increasingly recognized cause of idiopathic ventricular fibrillation.): Prominent J-point elevation with a notched terminal QRS in the inferolateral leads, in the absence of structural heart disease, is the electrocardiographic signature of early repolarization syndrome, which has become an increasingly recognized cause of otherwise unexplained ventricular fibrillation and sudden cardiac arrest. Acute pericarditis produces a different, more diffuse ST elevation pattern without this specific arrhythmic association, left bundle branch block has a distinct QRS morphology, and this specific inferolateral pattern is not simply a benign, risk-free variant here given his arrest.
  98. The correct answer is C (Inducibility of sustained monomorphic ventricular tachycardia on programmed stimulation helps identify higher-risk ischemic patients.): In patients with prior myocardial infarction and reduced ejection fraction, inducibility of sustained monomorphic ventricular tachycardia during programmed ventricular stimulation provides incremental risk stratification beyond ejection fraction alone, helping identify patients at higher risk for future spontaneous ventricular arrhythmia. Programmed stimulation is most informative in structural heart disease rather than normal hearts, and prior myocardial infarction is not a contraindication to performing the study.
  99. The correct answer is B (Intravenous amiodarone, which is less likely to cause additional QT-related proarrhythmia here.): In a patient with recurrent polymorphic ventricular tachycardia storm but a normal baseline and drug-corrected QT interval, intravenous amiodarone is generally preferred for acute suppression because it is less likely than pure QT-prolonging agents to worsen repolarization-related proarrhythmia in a patient without an underlying long QT substrate. Sotalol, dofetilide, and ibutilide are all significant QT-prolonging agents that could paradoxically increase her risk of a torsades-type event rather than help control her storm.
  100. The correct answer is D (A subcutaneous implantable defibrillator, avoiding intravascular leads given his infection history.): For a patient with recurrent ventricular fibrillation, no need for pacing or antitachycardia pacing, and a history of two prior transvenous device infections requiring extraction, a subcutaneous implantable defibrillator provides effective defibrillation while avoiding further intravascular lead placement and its associated infection risk. Repeating the identical transvenous approach risks another infection, relying solely on oral antiarrhythmic therapy without any device leaves him unprotected, and a leadless pacemaker alone provides no defibrillation capability whatsoever.
  101. The correct answer is B (Use short bursts of bipolar cautery and reprogram the device to asynchronous pacing.): Short, intermittent bipolar cautery bursts kept away from the pulse generator minimize the electrical noise reaching the device, and reprogramming to an asynchronous mode prevents that noise from being misinterpreted as intrinsic cardiac activity, which would otherwise inhibit needed pacing output. Unipolar cautery and prolonged continuous bursts carry a much higher interference risk. Placing the grounding pad over the pocket, or manipulating only the rate-responsive sensor while leaving the sensing configuration unprotected, does not address the real risk of oversensing-induced pacing inhibition.
  102. The correct answer is D (Interrogate the device to review stored electrograms and confirm noise oversensing.): Stored intracardiac electrograms allow the team to distinguish a true ventricular arrhythmia from oversensing of nonphysiologic electromagnetic noise before deciding on any further management. Assuming the shock was appropriate without review risks missing a correctable oversensing problem, while permanently disabling ventricular fibrillation detection would leave the patient unprotected against a genuine future arrhythmia. Resuming chainsaw use without addressing the underlying cause risks a recurrent inappropriate shock.
  103. The correct answer is A (Most subcutaneous ICD systems now carry MRI-conditional labeling, so eligibility should be confirmed with the manufacturer.): Current-generation subcutaneous defibrillator systems have received MRI-conditional approval under specific scanning conditions, so the correct next step is verifying the specific implanted model and following the manufacturer's conditions rather than assuming a blanket contraindication. Battery longevity alone does not determine MRI eligibility. Transvenous systems were not the only devices to eventually receive MRI-conditional labeling, since subcutaneous systems achieved this as well.
  104. The correct answer is C (Handheld antitheft security wands should not be held directly over the pulse generator.): These handheld wands generate a stronger, more localized electromagnetic field than walk-through detectors and can transiently interfere with device function if held directly over the pocket, so patients are advised to ask for an alternative screening method or keep the wand moving quickly past the area. Standard cell phones and microwave ovens pose minimal risk with normal use in modern devices, and walk-through metal detectors do not reprogram implanted devices.
  105. The correct answer is B (The proximity of the stimulation electrodes to the pulse generator and leads.): Electromagnetic interference risk from transcutaneous electrical nerve stimulation scales with how close the stimulating electrodes are placed to the device and its leads, since current can be conducted along the lead system toward the sensing circuitry. Placing electrodes away from the device and leads substantially reduces this risk. Resting heart rate, device housing color, and patient handedness have no bearing on the interference mechanism.
  106. The correct answer is A (Rising internal battery impedance reduces the voltage delivered under a given current load.): As a lithium iodine cell discharges, its internal impedance rises, which lowers the effective voltage the battery can deliver to the pacing circuit under load, so pulse width is increased to maintain adequate delivered energy for reliable capture. Lead insulation integrity is unrelated to battery chemistry. Pacing threshold does not automatically fall with battery depletion, and threshold testing remains essential as the battery ages, not unnecessary.
  107. The correct answer is D (A conductor fracture causing a partial or complete lead wire break.): A marked rise in impedance from a normal baseline, combined with a rising pacing threshold and intermittent loss of capture, is the classic signature of a conductor fracture interrupting the electrical pathway within the lead. Normal fibrosis alone would not produce such a dramatic impedance change or intermittent capture loss. Battery depletion and far-field atrial oversensing would not directly explain this specific impedance and threshold pattern.
  108. The correct answer is C (An insulation breach allowing current to leak outside the intended pathway.): A sharp drop in measured impedance, without a corresponding change in sensed amplitude or capture threshold, is characteristic of an insulation defect that creates a lower-resistance leakage pathway for current. A complete conductor fracture typically raises impedance rather than lowering it. Battery depletion affects voltage delivery rather than lead impedance directly, and normal fibrous capsule maturation does not produce this pattern.
  109. The correct answer is B (The shorter distance between bipolar electrodes reduces the antenna effect that picks up extracardiac signals.): Because the two bipolar electrodes sit close together, the sensing circuit captures a smaller loop area and is therefore less susceptible to acting as an antenna for distant myopotentials and environmental electromagnetic noise compared with the larger sensing loop used in unipolar configurations. Bipolar sensing does not inherently require higher pacing output. Bipolar leads are widely compatible with modern defibrillators, and unipolar sensing does not eliminate myopotential oversensing.
  110. The correct answer is D (Active fixation allows stable placement at sites lacking trabeculated tissue for tine engagement.): A screw-in mechanism can achieve reliable fixation directly into thin or smooth tissue that lacks the trabeculations a passive tined lead needs to catch onto, making active fixation the safer choice in a friable or atypical appendage. Acute thresholds are not consistently lower with active fixation. Passive leads can still be extracted, and fluoroscopic guidance remains necessary regardless of fixation type.
  111. The correct answer is A (A compact battery volume within the capsule limits total stored energy capacity.): The small physical footprint required to fit a leadless pacemaker entirely within the heart constrains the volume available for the battery, directly limiting total stored energy and therefore expected longevity compared with a larger conventional generator housed in a pocket. These devices do not pace at a fixed maximal output regardless of threshold, and single-chamber leadless devices do not require continuous bidirectional telemetry consuming most current. A battery is still required for pacing function despite the absence of a lead.
  112. The correct answer is C (Fibrotic tissue formation around the electrode tip increasing the effective threshold.): Over the weeks following implant, fibrous tissue forms around the electrode, increasing the effective distance and resistance current must overcome to depolarize viable myocardium, which is why chronic thresholds are typically higher than acute intraoperative values. This is an expected physiologic process rather than a device malfunction. Battery depletion this early is unlikely, a silent fracture would usually also affect impedance, and automatic reprogramming to a lower output does not occur spontaneously.
  113. The correct answer is D (Capturing the left conduction system fibers requires the electrode to reach beyond the septal myocardium.): The left bundle branch fibers run within the deep left-sided subendocardium, so the pacing electrode must be screwed through the septal myocardium to reach a depth close to that fiber layer in order to achieve reliable conduction system capture. Deeper placement does not automatically guarantee lower impedance. Threshold and impedance testing remain essential at implant regardless of depth, and superficial septal placement typically achieves only septal myocardial capture rather than true conduction system capture.
  114. The correct answer is B (To provide temporary protection against sudden arrhythmic death during the myocardial recovery period.): A wearable cardioverter defibrillator bridges patients through a window when ejection fraction may still improve, protecting against sudden arrhythmic death without committing them to a permanent implanted device until reassessment confirms whether an implantable defibrillator is ultimately indicated. It does not replace future implantable defibrillator evaluation, nor does it provide continuous fixed-rate pacing or deliver shocks regardless of the detected rhythm.
  115. The correct answer is A (Pressing and holding the response buttons to abort a shock if conscious.): The wearable cardioverter defibrillator is designed so a conscious patient can abort an impending shock by pressing and holding the response buttons, preventing an unnecessary shock if the patient is actually alert and the detected rhythm is not truly life-threatening. If the buttons are not pressed, the device proceeds to deliver therapy. Dialing emergency services, removing the vest, or wetting the sensors are not part of the device's built-in shock-abort mechanism.
  116. The correct answer is C (They automatically analyze the cardiac rhythm and advise whether a shock is needed.): Automated external defibrillators are specifically engineered to analyze the rhythm themselves and only advise or deliver a shock when a shockable rhythm is detected, which is what makes them safe and effective for use by bystanders without advanced medical training. They do not require the operator to manually select energy levels, they do not provide continuous pacing, and they are intended for lay use rather than being restricted to advanced-certified physicians.
  117. The correct answer is B (Poor electrode-skin contact or motion artifact is triggering nuisance alarms.): Frequent alarms without any shock delivery in a wearable cardioverter defibrillator most commonly reflect a technical issue such as loose electrode contact, dry gel, or motion artifact generating a signal the device flags for review, and troubleshooting the fit and contact typically resolves it. A permanently failed device would not be expected to alarm frequently in this pattern, ejection fraction recovery cannot be assumed without formal reassessment, and modern wearable device batteries are rechargeable rather than single-use.
  118. Reassurance would leave a documented symptomatic conduction abnormality untreated. A beta-blocker would worsen the underlying bradycardia, and sinus node ablation has no role in treating sinus node dysfunction.
  119. Observation alone would leave a high-risk conduction abnormality unaddressed. Antiarrhythmic drugs and prolonged monitoring do not treat the underlying structural conduction disease.
  120. The correct answer is C (Permanent pacemaker implantation, since persistent block after this procedure rarely resolves.): Complete heart block persisting for several days after transcatheter aortic valve replacement is unlikely to recover spontaneously, so permanent pacemaker implantation is the appropriate definitive management rather than prolonging temporary pacing or discharging the patient without a permanent device. Isoproterenol is used only as a temporizing measure and is not appropriate as definitive long-term therapy for persistent complete heart block.
  121. The correct answer is D (Dual-chamber pacing with a short atrioventricular delay to alter septal contraction.): Pacing the right ventricular apex with a short atrioventricular delay changes the sequence of ventricular activation so the septum contracts earlier, which can reduce dynamic left ventricular outflow tract obstruction in selected patients who are not candidates for septal reduction therapy. A fixed low backup rate alone would not achieve the needed altered activation sequence. A loop recorder only monitors rhythm, and a wearable defibrillator addresses arrhythmic risk rather than mechanical obstruction.
  122. The correct answer is A (A permanent pacemaker, since complete heart block is intentionally created.): Atrioventricular node ablation deliberately produces permanent complete heart block to control the ventricular rate in atrial fibrillation, so a permanent pacemaker must already be in place before the ablation is performed to ensure the patient has reliable ventricular pacing afterward. A subcutaneous defibrillator provides shock therapy but no bradycardia pacing support. A wearable defibrillator is not appropriate for this planned, permanent rhythm change, and no device would leave the patient without any ventricular rhythm.
  123.   124.   125. Oxygen saturation, a prior day's chest radiograph, and auscultation of heart sounds do not provide direct confirmation of mechanical fixation adequacy.
  126. Correcting the metabolic abnormality should be addressed before assuming a primary device or lead problem. Empirically maximizing output, replacing the generator, or disabling bradycardia therapies would not treat the actual reversible cause.
  128.   129. It does not eliminate the need for periodic in-person interrogation entirely, it is typically used far more frequently than once every five years, and it cannot guarantee the complete elimination of inappropriate shocks.

  132.   133. Lead dislodgement would present with pacing or sensing abnormalities rather than respiratory symptoms. A pocket hematoma remains localized to the subcutaneous tissue, and pacemaker syndrome produces hemodynamic symptoms related to loss of atrioventricular synchrony rather than a unilateral lung finding.
  134. The correct answer is A (Lead dislodgement requiring repositioning or revision of the atrial lead.): A lead tip that has visibly moved position on radiograph, accompanied by new failure to sense or pace appropriately, confirms macrodislodgement of the atrial lead, which requires prompt repositioning or revision since the lead is no longer in stable contact with functional atrial tissue.   135.   136. The correct answer is D (Loss of atrioventricular synchrony causing atrial contraction against a closed valve.): Fatigue, neck pulsations, and a sensation of fullness that occur predominantly during ventricular pacing describe pacemaker syndrome, caused by the atrium contracting against a closed atrioventricular valve when ventriculoatrial conduction is present without coordinated atrial pacing.
