  137. The correct answer is C (Inappropriate shock caused by oversensing of nonphysiologic lead noise.): A shock delivered because intermittent, high-frequency noise from a fractured lead was misclassified by the device as ventricular fibrillation is, by definition, an inappropriate shock caused by oversensing rather than a response to a true arrhythmia. This is not appropriate therapy, since no true ventricular fibrillation occurred. It is not pacemaker-mediated tachycardia, which involves a reentrant pacing loop, and it is the opposite of undersensing, which would mean a real arrhythmia was missed.
  138. The correct answer is A (Superior vena cava or myocardial tear causing acute hemopericardium or hemothorax.): Acute hypotension with jugular venous distension occurring at the moment a chronically implanted lead is withdrawn during extraction is a well-recognized presentation of a vascular or myocardial tear, which can rapidly produce hemopericardium with tamponade or hemothorax and requires immediate recognition and intervention. A simple vasovagal reaction or an anesthesia-related allergic reaction would not typically produce jugular venous distension, and prolonged positioning alone would not explain this acute change during lead withdrawal.
  139. The correct answer is D (An electrical storm requiring urgent evaluation and antiarrhythmic or ablation therapy.): Multiple episodes of sustained ventricular arrhythmia requiring device therapy in rapid succession within a short period defines an electrical storm, a medical emergency that requires urgent hospitalization, correction of any reversible triggers, antiarrhythmic drug therapy, and often catheter ablation to control the arrhythmia substrate. This is not pacemaker syndrome, which relates to atrioventricular synchrony rather than tachyarrhythmia. It is neither benign oversensing nor normal expected device behavior.
  140. The correct answer is B (VVI, providing ventricular pacing without reliance on atrial sensing.): In permanent atrial fibrillation there is no organized atrial electrical activity to sense or track, so a single-chamber ventricular pacing mode that senses and paces the ventricle independently, such as VVI, is the appropriate choice. DDD and DDI both require a functioning atrial lead and meaningful atrial signal for their tracking or sensing functions, which is unavailable here, and AAI would fail to address the complete heart block since it only paces the atrium.
  141. The correct answer is C (DDD, tracking sensed atrial activity to trigger synchronized ventricular pacing.): DDD mode senses the patient's own intrinsic sinus activity in the atrium and uses that signal to trigger an appropriately timed, synchronized ventricular paced beat, preserving atrioventricular synchrony while allowing the native sinus node to continue driving the overall heart rate response. VVI would ignore atrial activity entirely and sacrifice synchrony. AAI provides no ventricular backup, which is dangerous in complete block, and VOO is an asynchronous mode reserved for specific situations like electrocautery exposure.
  142. The correct answer is A (Mode switching, which reverts a tracking mode to a nontracking mode during atrial arrhythmia.): Mode switching automatically detects a sustained atrial tachyarrhythmia being tracked by the device and temporarily reverts the pacing mode to a nontracking mode, preventing the ventricle from being paced rapidly in response to the abnormal atrial rate until normal rhythm resumes. Rate-adaptive pacing relates to activity-sensor-driven rate response, hysteresis relates to allowing a lower intrinsic rate before pacing resumes, and capture management relates to automatic threshold-based output adjustment, none of which address tracking of atrial arrhythmia.
  143. The correct answer is D (It paces asynchronously at a fixed rate regardless of intrinsic cardiac activity.): VOO mode delivers ventricular pacing at a fixed programmed rate without sensing any intrinsic cardiac activity at all, which prevents electrocautery-related electromagnetic noise from being misinterpreted as intrinsic activity and inappropriately inhibiting pacing output during surgery. This is the opposite of the patient's usual demand mode, which senses intrinsic activity and inhibits or triggers pacing accordingly. VOO does not track atrial activity, since it is a purely ventricular asynchronous mode.
  144. The correct answer is B (VDD-like accelerometer-based atrioventricular synchronous single-chamber ventricular pacing.): This approach uses a mechanical accelerometer within the single ventricular leadless capsule to detect an atrial contraction signal and time ventricular pacing accordingly, achieving atrioventricular synchrony that resembles a VDD mode without requiring an actual separate atrial lead. True DDD mode requires two physical leads for full atrial and ventricular pacing and sensing, AAI paces only the atrium with no ventricular support, and DOO would pace asynchronously in both chambers rather than sensing atrial activity.
  145. The correct answer is C (AAIR, pacing the atrium with rate-adaptive sensor-driven rate increases during activity.): Because atrioventricular and interventricular conduction are both intact and only the sinus node is diseased, pacing the atrium alone with a rate-adaptive sensor in AAIR mode allows the heart rate to increase appropriately with exertion while preserving the patient's own normal conduction pathway to the ventricles. VVI would unnecessarily pace the ventricle and disrupt normal conduction. DDD with a maximal tracking rate fixed at the lower rate limit would not allow rate increase with activity, and VOO is an asynchronous mode not intended for chronic use.
  146. The correct answer is A (A leadless transcatheter pacemaker implanted directly into the right ventricle): A leadless pacemaker eliminates both the subcutaneous pocket and the transvenous lead, the two components most often responsible for device-related bacteremia and endocarditis, making it the preferred option for a patient with recurrent bloodstream infections. A subclavian pocket device carries substantial pocket infection risk in this setting. A subcutaneous ICD provides no bradycardia pacing support for a patient who needs rate support. An epicardial system requires an open surgical procedure and still carries generator pocket infection risk.
  147. The correct answer is B (Leave the depleted device in place and implant a second nearby): When a leadless pacemaker reaches end of battery life, the standard approach is to leave the deactivated device in place and implant a second leadless pacemaker at an adjacent right ventricular site if anatomically feasible, since chronic fibrous encapsulation makes extraction progressively riskier over time. Emergency thoracotomy is not indicated for a routine battery depletion. Percutaneous extraction after years of dwell time carries meaningfully higher tricuspid valve and myocardial injury risk and is not first-line. Reprogramming cannot restore a depleted battery.
  148. The correct answer is C (Femoral venous access with a steerable catheter to the right ventricle): Leadless pacemakers are delivered through a large-bore femoral vein sheath using a steerable delivery catheter advanced to the right ventricular septum, where the device is deployed and actively fixed. Subxiphoid pericardial access is used for epicardial lead placement, not leadless pacemaker delivery. Subclavian access with a stylet-driven lead describes conventional transvenous pacing, the exact approach this device avoids. Surgical mini-thoracotomy is unnecessary since the entire procedure is performed percutaneously.
  149. The correct answer is D (Self-expanding nitinol tines or an active-fixation helix): Depending on the specific model, leadless pacemakers anchor either through self-expanding nitinol tines that engage trabeculae or through an active-fixation helix that screws into the septal myocardium, and fixation is verified with a tug test before release. Suction-cup adherence and balloon wedging are not fixation mechanisms used by any approved leadless pacemaker. Epicardial suturing applies to surgically placed leads, not a percutaneously delivered leadless device.
  150. The correct answer is B (Ventricular pacing spikes followed by wide paced QRS complexes): A leadless pacemaker programmed to VVI mode paces only the right ventricle, producing a pacing artifact immediately followed by a wide, left-bundle-branch-block-like QRS complex consistent with right ventricular activation. Native sinus rhythm would show a normal narrow QRS without any pacing spike. Atrial fibrillation would appear irregularly irregular without a fixed paced rate, and an unpaced complete heart block escape rhythm would lack the leading pacing artifact seen here.
  151. The correct answer is D (Cardiac perforation with tamponade from the fixation mechanism): New hypotension, tachycardia, and a rapidly accumulating pericardial effusion with right ventricular diastolic collapse shortly after leadless pacemaker implantation is the classic presentation of myocardial perforation with cardiac tamponade, a recognized though uncommon complication of the active fixation process. A femoral arteriovenous fistula would present with a local bruit and limb findings, not tamponade physiology. Device migration to the pulmonary artery would not itself cause a pericardial effusion. Atrial fibrillation alone does not explain tamponade on echocardiography.
  152. The correct answer is A (Current leadless pacemakers are MRI conditional and can be safely scanned): Currently available leadless pacemakers are labeled MRI conditional and can undergo full-body 1.5 or 3 Tesla MRI, including brain imaging, when the manufacturer's specific scanning conditions are followed, making urgent stroke imaging appropriate rather than delayed. MRI is not absolutely contraindicated with these devices, unlike some older transvenous systems. There is no region-specific restriction limiting scanning to the heart alone. Surgical device removal is unnecessary and would delay time-sensitive stroke evaluation.
  153. The correct answer is C (Presence of a mechanical, rather than bioprosthetic, tricuspid valve): A mechanical tricuspid valve is a contraindication to leadless pacemaker implantation because the delivery catheter and device must cross the tricuspid annulus, risking valve leaflet or mechanism damage, whereas a bioprosthetic tricuspid valve can often still be crossed safely. Age alone is not an exclusion criterion for this device. Leadless implantation is typically performed under conscious sedation, not general anesthesia, in most centers. Prior unrelated cardiac surgery does not automatically preclude femoral venous access.
  154. The correct answer is B (An internal accelerometer that detects body motion and vibration): Rate-responsive leadless pacemakers use a built-in accelerometer that senses motion and vibration associated with physical activity to appropriately increase the paced rate, similar to the motion sensors used in many conventional rate-adaptive pacemakers. Minute-ventilation impedance sensing requires a second electrode configuration not available in a single leadless capsule. The device does not measure invasive blood pressure or mixed venous oxygen saturation, neither of which is a feature of current leadless pacemaker technology.
  155. The correct answer is A (Detection of atrial mechanical contraction using the accelerometer signal): A single right ventricular leadless pacemaker with atrioventricular synchrony capability detects the mechanical vibration produced by atrial contraction using its onboard accelerometer, then times ventricular pacing to follow that signal, achieving synchrony without a true atrial lead or second implant. There is no external Bluetooth-linked atrial sensor in this design. Far-field electrical P wave sensing from a right ventricular electrode is not reliable enough for this purpose. No implanted atrial pressure sensor exists in this configuration.
  156. The correct answer is B (Percutaneous snare retrieval from a peripheral vessel is generally feasible and preferred): Device embolization is a rare but recognized complication of leadless pacemaker implantation, and most embolized devices, including those that migrate to the pulmonary artery, can be successfully retrieved percutaneously using a snare catheter, avoiding open surgery. Lifelong anticoagulation alone does not address a malpositioned functioning device. Emergency surgery is reserved for cases where percutaneous retrieval fails or the patient is unstable. Leaving a device embolized in the pulmonary circulation is not standard management when retrieval is feasible.
  157. The correct answer is C (Pacemaker syndrome from loss of atrioventricular synchrony): A single-chamber ventricular leadless pacemaker without atrioventricular synchrony can produce pacemaker syndrome, in which ventricular pacing that occurs out of sequence with atrial contraction causes atrial contraction against a closed atrioventricular valve, producing neck pulsations, fatigue, and dyspnea, a recognized limitation of non-synchronous leadless pacing. Battery depletion would cause loss of capture or pacing, not this symptom pattern. Pericarditis would present with positional chest pain rather than these symptoms. New atrial fibrillation would not typically cause cannon A waves from AV dissociation in this pattern.
  158. The correct answer is A (Remote transmitters and programmed alerts function similarly to transvenous systems): Leadless pacemakers use the same remote monitoring infrastructure as conventional pacemakers, with a home transmitter that periodically uploads battery status, pacing thresholds, and sensing data so that elective replacement indicators and device problems are identified without requiring routine in-person visits. In-person fluoroscopy is not required for routine follow-up. Battery status is readily assessed noninvasively through interrogation, not only via catheterization. Leadless devices can still experience electronic malfunction and require ongoing surveillance.
  159. The correct answer is C (A dual-chamber leadless pacemaker system with two communicating implants): Dual-chamber leadless pacemaker systems consist of two separate leadless capsules, one in the right atrium and one in the right ventricle, that communicate with each other to coordinate atrioventricular synchronous pacing while entirely avoiding transvenous leads and a surgical pocket. A single VVI leadless device cannot restore atrioventricular synchrony on its own. A subcutaneous ICD does not provide chronic bradycardia pacing support. A conventional dual-chamber transvenous system still requires leads and a pocket, the exact drawbacks this technology is designed to eliminate.
  160. The correct answer is B (Conducted electrical impulses transmitted through cardiac tissue and blood): Dual-chamber leadless pacemaker systems communicate through implant-to-implant conducted communication, in which one device emits a rapid electrical impulse that is detected by the other device as it conducts through the surrounding cardiac tissue and blood pool, rather than using radiofrequency or Bluetooth signaling. No subcutaneous antenna or external chest-worn relay is required for beat-to-beat coordination. There is no satellite uplink involved in real-time device-to-device timing.
  161. The correct answer is D (The right atrial appendage or low right atrial septal region): The atrial component of a dual-chamber leadless pacemaker system is typically fixed within the right atrial appendage or a low right atrial septal site, chosen for trabeculated tissue that provides stable active fixation and reliable atrial sensing and pacing. The left atrium is not accessed for this right-heart device and would require transseptal puncture with added risk. The coronary sinus ostium does not provide a stable site for capsule fixation. A patent foramen ovale is not a standard fixation target for atrial leadless pacing.
  162. The correct answer is C (Restoration of atrioventricular synchrony, reducing pacemaker syndrome risk): The key clinical advantage of a dual-chamber leadless system over a single-chamber ventricular leadless pacemaker is its ability to sense atrial activity and deliver atrioventricular synchronous ventricular pacing, which reduces the risk of pacemaker syndrome that can occur with isolated VVI pacing once significant atrioventricular block develops. Battery longevity is not inherently longer with two implants and may be comparable or shorter overall. Device replacement is still eventually required. Dual-chamber leadless pacing does not provide biventricular or cardiac resynchronization capability.
  163. The correct answer is A (Reliable mode switching despite implant-to-implant communication constraints): During atrial fibrillation, a dual-chamber leadless system must reliably detect the irregular, rapid atrial signal and appropriately mode-switch the ventricular device to a non-tracking mode to avoid pacing at inappropriately fast rates, a recognized engineering and clinical challenge given the beat-to-beat implant-to-implant communication this technology depends on. Ventricular pacing capability is not lost during atrial fibrillation, only the tracking behavior changes. The atrial component is not permanently deactivated by a temporary arrhythmia episode. These devices do not provide defibrillation therapy.
  164. The correct answer is D (Both capsules are delivered via femoral venous access in one session): Dual-chamber leadless pacemaker implantation is typically performed as a single procedure with both the atrial and ventricular capsules delivered through femoral venous access, often using separate delivery catheters advanced sequentially, without requiring separate operative sessions. A single femoral vein access site is common but the statement that only one vein is ever used oversimplifies actual technique variations across systems. There is no standard requirement to stagger implantation by weeks. No surgical thoracotomy is needed since the entire procedure is percutaneous.
  165. The correct answer is B (Sequential atrial and ventricular pacing spikes with a fixed AV interval): A functioning dual-chamber leadless pacemaker operating in atrioventricular synchronous mode produces a paced atrial spike followed after a programmed atrioventricular interval by a paced ventricular spike and wide QRS complex, reflecting coordinated implant-to-implant timing. Isolated ventricular pacing spikes alone would indicate the device is functioning like a single-chamber system, not the intended dual-chamber synchrony. Native sinus rhythm would show no pacing spikes at all. Atrial fibrillation would appear irregular and would not show organized sequential pacing artifacts.
  166. The correct answer is A (Dual-chamber leadless systems currently cannot provide biventricular or CRT-level pacing therapy): Current dual-chamber leadless pacemaker technology restores atrioventricular synchrony between one atrial and one ventricular capsule but has no left ventricular lead component and cannot deliver biventricular pacing, so a patient who develops new left bundle branch block and reduced ejection fraction would need a separate transvenous or conduction system pacing approach for resynchronization. Reprogramming cannot add a physical left ventricular pacing site that does not exist. Atrioventricular synchrony alone does not resynchronize ventricular activation. Replacing only the atrial capsule would not add left ventricular capture.
  167. The correct answer is D (Prior pocket infections favor a device avoiding a subcutaneous pocket): A history of recurrent device pocket infections is a strong indication to choose a pocket-free, lead-free pacing strategy, and a dual-chamber leadless system allows this patient to receive atrioventricular synchronous pacing appropriate for his sinus node disease and intermittent AV conduction issues without recreating the infection risk of a conventional pocket. There is no strict upper age limit that excludes leadless pacing candidacy. He has no defibrillation indication described. A normal ejection fraction with no arrhythmia history is reassuring but does not itself justify device selection here.
  168. The correct answer is C (A brief but measurable conduction communication delay must be accounted for): Because implant-to-implant communication relies on an electrical impulse conducting through tissue and blood rather than instantaneous wired signaling, there is a small but measurable communication latency that must be factored into the programmed atrioventricular delay to preserve appropriate timing, unlike a hard-wired conventional dual-chamber system. This latency is real, not eliminated. The AV delay remains programmable within limits, not permanently fixed. Ventricular pacing is triggered automatically by sensed atrial activity, not by manual physician action beat to beat.
  169. The correct answer is B (His bundle pacing using a fixation lead placed at the His bundle): His bundle pacing directly captures the native His-Purkinje conduction system, producing a physiologic, narrow paced QRS in a patient with normal baseline conduction, which preserves synchronous ventricular activation far better than right ventricular apical pacing. Conventional apical pacing bypasses the conduction system and produces a wide, dyssynchronous QRS. Biventricular pacing is intended for patients with existing dyssynchrony or reduced ejection fraction, not routine physiologic pacing in a patient with a narrow QRS. A subcutaneous ICD does not provide chronic physiologic pacing.
  170. The correct answer is C (The tricuspid annulus overlying the membranous interventricular septum): His bundle pacing leads are fixed at the tricuspid annulus directly over the membranous interventricular septum, the location where the compact His bundle penetrates from the atrioventricular node into the ventricular conduction system, allowing the active-fixation helix to engage and capture it. The coronary sinus ostium is used for left ventricular lead access in biventricular pacing, not His bundle targeting. The right ventricular free wall and right atrial appendage are not sites containing the His bundle.
  171. The correct answer is D (Transition between selective and nonselective His bundle capture): At low output, selective His bundle capture activates only the conduction system, reproducing a QRS nearly identical to the native complex, while higher output additionally captures adjacent local myocardium, producing nonselective capture with a fusion-like morphology and a shorter stimulus-to-QRS interval, a well-recognized and expected finding rather than a problem. This pattern indicates successful capture at both outputs, not a failure to capture. It does not indicate lead dislodgement, which would typically show rising thresholds or loss of capture altogether. Atrial sensing is unrelated to this QRS morphology change.
  172. The correct answer is B (Restoration of a normal, narrow paced QRS complex): Because this patient's atrioventricular block is located proximal to the His bundle, at the level of the AV node, pacing distal to the block at the His bundle can restore normal conduction through the native His-Purkinje system, producing a narrow paced QRS. Wide QRS pacing resembling right ventricular apical pacing would suggest the lead failed to engage the conduction system. Improvement is expected precisely because the block is proximal to, not at, the pacing site. There is no mechanism by which appropriately placed His bundle pacing worsens conduction.
  173. The correct answer is C (Distal conduction disease beyond the pacing site limits correction of the block): When conduction disease is located distal to the His bundle, infra-Hisian in the bundle branches or Purkinje system, pacing proximal to that disease at the His bundle cannot reliably bypass or correct the block, often resulting in high, unstable thresholds or an inability to normalize the QRS, a recognized limitation of His bundle pacing in this population. His bundle leads do use active fixation successfully in most patients. Sheath incompatibility and anesthesia availability are not the primary limitations described in this scenario.
  174. The correct answer is A (Threshold rise over time is more common with His bundle than left bundle branch area pacing): Chronic capture threshold rise is a well-documented limitation of His bundle pacing, occurring more frequently than with left bundle branch area pacing, likely related to the small target area and fibrotic changes around the His bundle region, and is an important reason many operators now favor left bundle branch area pacing. Threshold rise is not unique to His bundle pacing since some rise can occur with any chronic lead. A stable, functioning lead with a manageable threshold rise does not automatically require extraction. This is a known biological phenomenon, not a manufacturing defect.
  175. The correct answer is D (Small, sometimes unstable local electrogram amplitude at the His bundle): The His bundle region often generates a relatively small and sometimes variable local electrogram signal, which can predispose to undersensing of native ventricular activity and inappropriate pacing shortly after an intrinsic beat, a recognized programming and follow-up challenge specific to this lead location. Oversensing from excessively large R waves would cause the opposite problem, inappropriate inhibition, not failure to sense. There is no subcutaneous defibrillator described in this scenario. His bundle leads are compatible with standard pacemaker generators when appropriately programmed.
  176. The correct answer is B (A patient with atrioventricular block proximal to the His bundle and normal QRS): His bundle pacing works best in patients whose atrioventricular block is located proximal to the His bundle with a normal baseline QRS, since pacing distal to the block can reliably restore native, narrow QRS conduction through an otherwise healthy distal conduction system. Severe infra-Hisian disease with a very wide QRS often produces high, unstable thresholds and unreliable correction. A patient needing only occasional single-chamber pacing does not require the added complexity and threshold instability risk of His bundle pacing. A mechanical tricuspid valve is a relative contraindication given the crossing required for lead placement.
  177. The correct answer is C (Lead dislodgement given the smaller, less trabeculated fixation target): Because the membranous septal region near the tricuspid annulus offers a smaller and less trabeculated fixation target than the right ventricular apex, His bundle pacing leads are generally considered to carry a comparatively higher risk of dislodgement, one reason many operators have shifted toward the more stable septal fixation achieved with left bundle branch area pacing. Acute tamponade within minutes is not a specifically elevated risk unique to this lead position. Pocket infection risk relates to the generator pocket, not lead tip location. Tricuspid valve dysfunction is a described risk but does not occur in every patient.
  178. The correct answer is D (Shorter generator battery longevity compared with lower-output pacing sites): Because His bundle pacing frequently requires higher voltage or pulse width outputs to maintain reliable chronic capture compared with conventional or left bundle branch area pacing sites, the generator battery is depleted more quickly, an important practical consideration when counseling patients about expected device longevity and future replacement timing. This does not confer immunity from battery depletion, nor does it upgrade the device to a defibrillator. Higher output requirements make ongoing interrogation more, not less, important.
  179. The correct answer is A (Left bundle branch area pacing with a deep septal lumenless lead): Left bundle branch area pacing involves advancing a lumenless, active-fixation lead deep into the interventricular septum from the right ventricular side until it captures the left bundle branch or an adjacent conduction system fascicle, which can bypass proximal conduction disease and achieve physiologic, near-native QRS activation. Standard apical pacing does not target the conduction system and produces a wide paced QRS. Coronary sinus lead placement targets the left ventricular epicardium for biventricular pacing, a different technique entirely. Epicardial subxiphoid placement is a surgical approach unrelated to septal conduction system pacing.
  180. The correct answer is A (A dominant, notched R wave pattern resembling right bundle branch block): Successful left bundle branch area capture typically produces a right bundle branch block-like pattern in lead V1, since activation proceeds from the left side of the septum toward the right ventricle, distinguishing it from right ventricular septal or apical pacing, which produces a left bundle branch block-like pattern instead. A deep QS pattern is more typical of right ventricular apical pacing, not left bundle branch capture. The paced QRS is narrower than apical pacing but is not identical to the native QRS in every lead. Diffuse ST elevation is not an expected finding of appropriately positioned conduction system pacing.
  181. The correct answer is B (Transition from nonselective to selective left bundle branch capture): As output is reduced during left bundle branch area pacing, an abrupt change in QRS morphology and a lengthening stimulus-to-QRS interval typically represents the transition from nonselective capture, in which both local septal myocardium and the left bundle branch are captured, to purely selective capture of the conduction system fascicle alone, a well-recognized and expected finding used to confirm true conduction system engagement. This is not loss of capture, since a paced QRS is still present. Phrenic nerve stimulation would cause diaphragmatic twitching, not a QRS morphology change alone. Perforation would not present as a smooth, reproducible morphology transition with output titration.
  182. The correct answer is A (It captures the conduction system beyond the site of distal block itself): Left bundle branch area pacing advances the lead deep into the septum to a point distal to many sites of left bundle branch block, allowing it to capture healthy conduction tissue beyond the block and achieve near-physiologic activation, an advantage over His bundle pacing in patients whose disease is more distal. It does not restore native sinus rhythm, which is a separate issue from ventricular activation pattern. A programmed atrioventricular delay is still required for appropriate timing. Left bundle branch area pacing leads do use active fixation, just like His bundle pacing leads.
  183. The correct answer is D (Septal perforation with the lead entering the left ventricular cavity and pericardial space): New hypotension and a pericardial effusion after deep septal lead advancement most likely indicates that the lumenless lead perforated through the septum, potentially entering the left ventricular cavity and injuring the pericardium or a nearby vessel, a recognized mechanical complication of left bundle branch area pacing that requires immediate lead repositioning and hemodynamic monitoring. A simple impedance drop during normal advancement would not cause hypotension or an effusion. Phrenic nerve stimulation causes diaphragmatic twitching, not tamponade physiology. Right bundle branch block is an expected electrocardiographic finding of successful left-sided septal capture, not a hemodynamic complication.
  184. The correct answer is C (A septal perforator branch of the coronary circulation): Septal perforator arteries run within the interventricular septum, and deep lead advancement during left bundle branch area pacing carries a recognized risk of injuring one of these small branches, potentially causing septal hematoma or ischemia, which is why careful depth and trajectory control is emphasized during implantation. The sinoatrial node is located in the right atrium, far from the septal implantation site. The mitral valve annulus is not the structure of concern during septal lead advancement. The main left anterior descending artery trunk itself, rather than its smaller septal perforator branches, is not the primary structure at risk from a septal lead.
  185. The correct answer is B (Left bundle branch area pacing shows lower, more stable thresholds and larger R waves): Comparative data consistently show that left bundle branch area pacing achieves lower and more stable long-term capture thresholds along with larger sensed R wave amplitudes than His bundle pacing, attributed to the more robust septal myocardial fixation site compared with the smaller, more delicate His bundle region. His bundle pacing is the technique more prone to threshold rise, not the reverse. The two techniques are not equivalent in threshold stability. Threshold changes over time are a recognized phenomenon with both techniques, though more pronounced with His bundle pacing.
  186. The correct answer is A (The R wave peak time, measured from stimulus onset to the R wave peak): A short and stable R wave peak time in lead V6, measured from the pacing stimulus to the peak of the R wave, reflects rapid activation of the left ventricular free wall through the captured conduction system and is used as a key criterion to confirm left bundle branch capture rather than isolated right-sided septal myocardial capture. The QT interval reflects repolarization, not depolarization sequence, and is not the criterion used here. The PR interval reflects atrioventricular conduction timing, unrelated to confirming left bundle capture. Total QRS duration alone does not specifically localize activation to the left ventricular free wall the way R wave peak time does.
  187. The correct answer is D (Left bundle branch area pacing as a conduction system resynchronization strategy): Left bundle branch area pacing has growing evidence as an alternative to conventional biventricular cardiac resynchronization therapy in patients with left bundle branch block and reduced ejection fraction, particularly when coronary sinus anatomy precludes a stable left ventricular lead, by directly capturing the conduction system to achieve electrical resynchronization. Right ventricular apical pacing alone would worsen dyssynchrony rather than treat it. A subcutaneous ICD provides no bradycardia or resynchronization pacing. A single-chamber leadless pacemaker in VVI mode does not address ventricular dyssynchrony.
  188. The correct answer is B (A deflectable delivery sheath paired with a lumenless, active-fixation lead): Left bundle branch area pacing requires a steerable, deflectable delivery sheath to achieve the correct trajectory against the septum, combined with a thin, lumenless active-fixation lead that can be screwed progressively deeper into the septal myocardium under fluoroscopic and electrogram guidance. A passive-fixation tined lead cannot be screwed into the septum and is unsuitable for this technique. A balloon-tipped catheter is used for coronary sinus cannulation during biventricular lead placement, not septal pacing. A subcutaneous defibrillation coil is unrelated to pacing lead delivery.
  189. The correct answer is C (Lead migration out of the left bundle capture zone requiring evaluation): A sudden rise in pacing threshold accompanied by loss of the characteristic right bundle branch block pattern on the paced electrocardiogram suggests the lead may have migrated out of the left bundle branch capture zone, back toward pure right-sided septal myocardium or partially withdrawn from the septum, and warrants prompt interrogation and possible imaging or lead revision. This pattern is not consistent with simple battery failure, which would present as loss of output rather than a morphology and threshold change. This is not expected normal variation given the abrupt change described. Atrial fibrillation would not explain a change in paced ventricular QRS morphology or threshold.
  190. The correct answer is D (Lower rates of pacing-induced cardiomyopathy with left bundle branch area pacing): Growing observational and comparative data show that patients requiring a high burden of ventricular pacing have lower rates of pacing-induced cardiomyopathy and better preserved ejection fraction with left bundle branch area pacing compared with conventional right ventricular pacing, supporting its use as a preferred physiologic pacing strategy in this population. The two approaches are not equivalent in cardiomyopathy risk. Heart failure hospitalization rates have trended lower, not higher, with left bundle branch area pacing in these comparisons. Ejection fraction differences have been observed, not absent, favoring the conduction system pacing approach.
  191. The correct answer is B (Cardiac resynchronization therapy with a biventricular device, given her LBBB and QRS duration.): This patient meets the strongest Class I indication for CRT, LVEF 35% or less, LBBB morphology, QRS duration of 150 ms or greater, and NYHA class II to III symptoms despite optimized guideline-directed medical therapy. Randomized trials show this combination of features predicts the greatest reduction in mortality and heart failure hospitalization from biventricular pacing. Escalating diuretics or pursuing transplant evaluation without first offering CRT would bypass an intervention with strong evidence in exactly this clinical profile.
  192. The correct answer is D (His expected benefit is meaningfully lower than a comparable patient with LBBB.): Subgroup analyses of major CRT trials consistently show that non-LBBB QRS morphology, including right bundle branch block and nonspecific intraventricular conduction delay, predicts a smaller reduction in mortality and heart failure events than true LBBB morphology at a similar QRS duration. CRT may still be reasonable in select non-LBBB patients with QRS duration of 150 ms or more, but it is a weaker indication, not a contraindication. This distinction matters clinically because it should temper expectations and inform shared decision making before implant.
  193. The correct answer is A (Yes, ambulatory NYHA class IV patients with LBBB may still benefit from CRT.): Current guidelines support CRT in selected ambulatory NYHA class IV patients who meet LVEF, QRS duration, and morphology criteria and are not dependent on continuous inotropic support, since this population can still gain functional and survival benefit from resynchronization. NYHA class IV status alone does not exclude device therapy, but truly inotrope-dependent or end-stage patients are less likely to benefit and warrant advanced heart failure evaluation instead. Sequencing a ventricular assist device before even attempting CRT is not required when a patient remains ambulatory and reasonably compensated.
  194. The correct answer is C (Her indication is reasonable but somewhat weaker than the patient with the longer QRS.): For LBBB morphology with QRS duration between 120 and 149 ms, current guidelines assign CRT a Class IIa recommendation, reasonable but less strong than the Class I recommendation given for QRS duration of 150 ms or greater. Trial data show a graded relationship between longer QRS duration and greater probability of clinical and echocardiographic response. This patient can still be offered CRT and counseled that benefit is plausible, but expectations should be calibrated to her shorter QRS duration.
  195. The correct answer is B (To visualize the coronary venous anatomy and select a suitable branch for the LV lead.): Balloon-occlusion retrograde coronary sinus venography opacifies the venous tree so the operator can identify branch location, caliber, and takeoff angle before choosing which vein to cannulate for the left ventricular lead. This step directly guides target vein selection, since posterolateral and lateral branches generally provide the most favorable pacing sites. It does not assess right atrial pressure, patent foramen ovale, or right ventricular function, none of which relate to coronary venous mapping.
  196. The correct answer is D (Withdraw the catheter, observe closely, and attempt an alternate venous branch.): A localized coronary sinus dissection without hemodynamic compromise or pericardial effusion is usually managed conservatively by withdrawing the catheter, observing the patient with continued fluoroscopic and hemodynamic monitoring, and then attempting cannulation of a different branch or approach. Emergent surgery is reserved for perforation with tamponade or ongoing hemodynamic instability, which is not present here. Continuing to force catheter advancement through an already dissected segment risks extending the injury and causing perforation.
  197. The correct answer is C (Reprogram to an alternate pacing vector on the multipolar lead electrodes.): Multipolar left ventricular leads allow selection among several electrode pairs, so the first-line management of phrenic nerve stimulation is reprogramming to a different pacing vector that maintains an acceptable pacing threshold while avoiding phrenic capture. This noninvasive step resolves most cases without further intervention. Surgical lead repositioning is reserved for cases where no vector provides an adequate margin between pacing and phrenic thresholds, and simply increasing output would worsen rather than relieve the phrenic stimulation.
  198. The correct answer is A (Complete separation of the E and A mitral inflow waves without truncation.): The iterative echocardiographic method for AV delay optimization aims to find the AV delay that allows complete, non-truncated mitral E and A wave separation, maximizing the contribution of atrial contraction to ventricular filling without premature mitral valve closure. A programmed AV delay that is too long causes E and A wave fusion, while one that is too short truncates the A wave and shortens diastolic filling. Neither fusion nor A wave truncation represents the optimized target.
  199. The correct answer is D (LV-first pacing with a 20 ms offset, matching the highest VTI result.): VV interval optimization selects the interventricular pacing sequence and offset that produces the greatest aortic velocity time integral, which serves as a surrogate for stroke volume. Since the highest VTI was measured with LV pacing 20 ms before RV pacing, that offset should be programmed to maximize hemodynamic benefit from resynchronization. Choosing the opposite sequence or defaulting to simultaneous pacing would abandon the empirically superior setting identified during the optimization study.
  200. The correct answer is B (Roughly 30%, reflecting a substantial minority who show no meaningful improvement.): Across large CRT registries and trials, approximately 30% of patients, even those selected using standard LVEF, QRS duration, and morphology criteria, fail to show meaningful clinical or echocardiographic improvement and are classified as nonresponders. This substantial nonresponse rate is why post-implant optimization, lead position, and biventricular pacing percentage all receive close ongoing attention. Rates as low as 5% or as high as 90% do not reflect the real-world nonresponse burden reported in the literature.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Suboptimal LV lead position outside the typically most effective lateral wall region.): An anterior interventricular vein position is generally less effective than a lateral or posterolateral branch because it does not pace the region of latest mechanical activation in typical LBBB dyssynchrony, and lead position is one of the most common correctable causes of CRT nonresponse even when pacing percentage and timing are otherwise optimal. Battery depletion, new dissection, and atrial lead migration would each present with different findings and do not explain an anatomically anterior LV lead location.
  2. The correct answer is A (Biventricular pacing percentages below roughly 98% are associated with reduced CRT benefit.): Observational data consistently show that patients achieving less than approximately 98% biventricular pacing have attenuated reductions in mortality and heart failure hospitalization compared with those achieving near-complete pacing, so this patient's frequent PVC-related loss of resynchronized beats is a plausible contributor to his worsening symptoms. Pacing percentage is not simply an arbitrary number, and 50% pacing is well below what is needed for reliable benefit. The finding also does not by itself indicate battery depletion, which would be reflected in other interrogation parameters.
  3. The correct answer is D (His bundle pacing, which can correct LBBB by engaging native conduction.): His bundle pacing captures the native His-Purkinje conduction system distal to many sites of LBBB block, and in appropriately selected patients it can narrow or normalize the QRS complex, achieving physiologic resynchronization as an alternative to conventional coronary sinus lead placement when venous anatomy is unfavorable. A leadless pacemaker in the right atrium provides no ventricular resynchronization, and a subcutaneous ICD cannot pace at all, so neither substitutes for the resynchronization this patient needs.
  4. The correct answer is C (Left bundle branch area pacing, delivered from within the septal myocardium.): Left bundle branch area pacing involves advancing a lead through the interventricular septum until it captures the left bundle branch or adjacent conduction tissue, providing a physiologic alternative for resynchronization when coronary sinus branches are inaccessible or unsuitable. This differs from right ventricular apical pacing, which does not engage the native conduction system and can itself cause dyssynchrony, and it is not an epicardial or angioplasty technique. It has become an important rescue strategy for patients with difficult coronary venous anatomy.
  5. The correct answer is B (Atrioventricular node ablation to eliminate competing intrinsic conduction to the ventricles.): In patients with permanent atrial fibrillation whose rapid, irregular intrinsic conduction competes with biventricular pacing despite maximally tolerated rate control, AV node ablation reliably forces near-complete pacemaker dependence and can raise biventricular pacing percentage close to 100%, which is often needed to achieve meaningful CRT benefit. Stopping rate control medication would worsen the competing conduction, and downgrading to single-chamber right ventricular pacing would abandon resynchronization entirely. Repeated cardioversion in permanent atrial fibrillation is unlikely to restore durable sinus rhythm.
  6. The correct answer is A (The posterolateral branch, which typically overlies the latest activated myocardium.): In typical LBBB-related dyssynchrony, the lateral and posterolateral left ventricular wall is usually the region of latest electrical activation, so pacing through a posterolateral coronary sinus branch generally produces greater electrical and mechanical resynchronization than an anterior interventricular branch overlying earlier-activated myocardium. Ease of access and proximity to the ostium are secondary considerations compared with targeting the region of delayed activation, and lead position is a well-established determinant of CRT response, not an irrelevant factor.
  7. The correct answer is C (A probable lead conductor fracture requiring prompt evaluation and imaging.): A sudden, marked rise in lead impedance together with intermittent loss of capture is a classic remote monitoring signature of a lead conductor fracture, and it warrants prompt clinical evaluation, chest radiography, and device interrogation to confirm the diagnosis and plan lead revision. This pattern is distinct from the small, gradual impedance changes considered normal drift, and it is not explained by atrial fibrillation or battery depletion, which would present with different interrogation findings such as declining battery voltage rather than an abrupt impedance jump.
  8. The correct answer is D (Her history of sustained ventricular tachycardia, indicating a defibrillator-level arrhythmia risk.): A documented history of sustained ventricular tachycardia establishes a secondary prevention indication for defibrillator therapy independent of her CRT indication, making a CRT-defibrillator the appropriate device choice to address both resynchronization and sudden cardiac death risk. Age alone, NYHA class, and ischemic etiology are each considered as part of overall risk stratification but none of them independently mandates defibrillator therapy the way a documented sustained ventricular arrhythmia does.
  9. The correct answer is B (Multipoint pacing, intended to capture a larger area of LV myocardium.): Multipoint pacing uses two electrode pairs on a quadripolar left ventricular lead to deliver near-simultaneous stimulation from separate sites, capturing a broader area of left ventricular myocardium and potentially improving resynchronization in patients who do not respond adequately to conventional single-site biventricular pacing. Rate-adaptive pacing addresses chronotropic response, mode switching addresses atrial arrhythmia tracking, and automatic capture management adjusts output thresholds, none of which describes multi-site left ventricular stimulation.
  10. The correct answer is A (Upgrade the system to a biventricular device to treat pacing-induced cardiomyopathy.): Chronic high-burden right ventricular apical pacing is a recognized cause of pacing-induced cardiomyopathy through the same electromechanical dyssynchrony mechanism seen in intrinsic LBBB, and upgrading to a biventricular or conduction system pacing configuration can reverse or improve the resulting LVEF decline and symptoms. Simply reducing the pacing percentage is not feasible in a patient with complete heart block who is pacemaker dependent, and replacing only the generator without addressing the pacing site would not correct the underlying dyssynchrony causing his cardiomyopathy.
  11. The correct answer is D (They represent classic mechanical dyssynchrony features seen in typical LBBB.): Septal flash, an early inward septal motion followed by lateral wall contraction, and apical rocking are well-described echocardiographic markers of the mechanical dyssynchrony characteristic of true LBBB and are associated with a higher likelihood of favorable CRT response. They are not artifacts, and they are not findings typically associated with aortic stenosis or restrictive cardiomyopathy, which have their own distinct echocardiographic signatures. Their presence supports rather than argues against pursuing resynchronization therapy in an appropriately selected candidate.
  12. The correct answer is C (They offer a reasonable, convenient alternative though evidence of outcome benefit is mixed.): Device-based electrogram optimization algorithms provide a convenient, automated way to periodically reassess and adjust AV and VV timing without an in-person echocardiographic visit, but head-to-head trial evidence has not consistently shown superiority over echocardiographic optimization or over nominal settings for hard clinical outcomes. They are a reasonable adjunct rather than a proven replacement, they are not useless, and they can be repeated periodically over the life of the device rather than being limited to a single use.
  13. The correct answer is A (Reposition the lead to a different coronary sinus branch away from the phrenic nerve.): When phrenic nerve capture cannot be separated from adequate myocardial pacing capture across any available electrode vector on a multipolar lead, intraoperative repositioning to an alternate coronary sinus branch is the appropriate next step to find a location with an acceptable safety margin. Accepting an unsafe position would risk chronic symptomatic diaphragmatic stimulation, programming zero output would eliminate resynchronization entirely, and abandoning the procedure is premature when other branches have not yet been tried.
  14. The correct answer is B (Reverse LV remodeling and improved papillary muscle synchrony reduced valve tethering.): Cardiac resynchronization therapy can reduce functional mitral regurgitation by promoting reverse left ventricular remodeling, which decreases annular dilation, and by improving the timing and coordination of papillary muscle contraction, both of which reduce leaflet tethering forces that drive functional regurgitation. Mitral leaflets do not structurally regenerate, and this well-documented CRT effect is not coincidental. This scenario describes secondary functional regurgitation rather than a primary structural valve abnormality like prolapse, which would not be expected to resolve with pacing alone.
  15. The correct answer is D (Lead placement should be directed away from the scarred segment toward viable myocardium.): Pacing directly over dense transmural scar produces poor electrical and mechanical capture and is associated with a substantially higher likelihood of CRT nonresponse, so preprocedural late gadolinium enhancement imaging showing scar in the typically preferred posterolateral region should prompt targeting an alternate, viable myocardial segment for the left ventricular lead. This finding does not by itself exclude the patient from CRT, and abandoning resynchronization for right ventricular-only pacing would forgo a potentially achievable benefit if a viable alternate site exists.
  16. The correct answer is C (A lateral branch overlying the mid-lateral segment identified as viable myocardium.): When preprocedural imaging identifies scar in the usually preferred basal posterolateral region but viable myocardium in the mid-lateral wall, the implanting electrophysiologist should target a coronary sinus branch overlying that viable segment to maximize the likelihood of effective electrical and mechanical capture. The great cardiac vein and middle cardiac vein typically overlie the anterior and inferior septal regions rather than the lateral wall and are not the preferred targets here, and ignoring viability information would risk placing the lead over nonresponsive scarred tissue.
  17. The correct answer is D (A prominent Thebesian valve partially covering the coronary sinus ostium.): A prominent Thebesian valve, a variably developed membranous or crescentic fold at the coronary sinus ostium, is a well-recognized anatomic variant that can obstruct catheter entry and require specific catheter shapes or gentle rotational maneuvers to bypass. A persistent left superior vena cava or left-sided inferior vena cava would alter the overall venous anatomy but would not specifically produce a membranous obstruction at the ostium itself, and an atrial septal defect affects interatrial flow rather than coronary sinus access.
  18. This approach does not eliminate the later need for contrast venography to map venous branches or for fluoroscopy during lead advancement, and it is not intended to measure right atrial pressure, which is unrelated to ostium localization.
  19. The correct answer is D (Upgrade to a biventricular or conduction system pacing system to address dyssynchrony.): New heart failure with reduced ejection fraction developing after years of chronic right ventricular pacing, without another identified cause, is characteristic of pacing-induced cardiomyopathy, and the appropriate management is to upgrade the system to biventricular pacing or conduction system pacing to correct the underlying electromechanical dyssynchrony. Increasing the pacing rate or simply replacing an identical single-chamber generator would perpetuate the problem, and an implantable loop recorder provides diagnostic rhythm monitoring but does not address the pacing-related cause of his cardiomyopathy.
  20. The correct answer is C (Dynamic optimization of resynchronization by fusing native conduction with LV pacing when favorable.): Adaptive CRT algorithms continuously assess intrinsic AV conduction and can switch to LV-only pacing that fuses with native right-sided conduction when this produces more favorable electrical synchrony than conventional biventricular pacing, dynamically optimizing resynchronization beat to beat as conduction varies. The feature still relies on atrial sensing to track intrinsic timing, it is unrelated to tachyarrhythmia detection or shock therapy, and it does not permanently disable right ventricular pacing, since biventricular pacing remains available whenever it is the more favorable configuration.
  21. The correct answer is A (Roughly every three to six months, supplementing an annual in-person evaluation.): Consensus guidance on cardiac implantable electronic device follow-up recommends remote transmissions approximately every three to six months for CRT devices, supplementing rather than replacing at least one comprehensive in-person evaluation annually, allowing earlier detection of lead problems, declining pacing percentage, or arrhythmia burden between visits. A single post-implant transmission or a five-year interval would leave long gaps in which clinically important changes could go undetected, and daily transmission is not the standard recommended cadence for routine surveillance.
  22. Mode switching is an arrhythmia-detection feature, not a battery indicator, and this trend should prompt assessment rather than being dismissed as expected, and it does not by itself confirm a lead fracture.
  23. This progressive improvement is the opposite of a nonresponse or worsening pattern, so lead revision or upgrade evaluation is not indicated. Sustained echocardiographic improvement of this magnitude over a full year is not consistent with a transient or placebo-type effect.
  24. This approach still requires a left ventricular lead to deliver resynchronization, contradicting the claim that it is eliminated, and modern leadless systems have advanced well beyond a strictly single-chamber, no-synchrony design.
  25. The correct answer is A (To further improve electrical resynchronization beyond what biventricular pacing alone achieved.): Combining left bundle branch area pacing with a conventional coronary sinus left ventricular lead in a fusion pacing configuration is an emerging hybrid strategy aimed at achieving greater QRS narrowing and more complete electrical resynchronization in patients whose response to conventional biventricular pacing alone is suboptimal. This hybrid approach adds to rather than replaces the coronary sinus lead, it is unrelated to atrial fibrillation management, and adding a second pacing site increases rather than reduces procedural complexity and time.
  26. His CRT device provides no direct stroke prevention effect, so discontinuing anticoagulation-related measures without an alternative would be unsafe, and there is no such thing as a generator with anticoagulant infusion capability, nor would an additional subcutaneous ICD address his bleeding-related need for an alternative to anticoagulation.
  28. The correct answer is C (120 ms, the delay producing the highest aortic VTI value shown.): AV delay optimization selects the setting that produces the greatest aortic velocity time integral, a surrogate for stroke volume, and the chart shows the highest VTI value at 120 ms compared with the shorter and longer delays tested. Assuming the shortest or longest delay is automatically optimal ignores the actual measured data, since AV optimization does not follow a fixed rule that shorter or longer delays always maximize filling. The 120 ms setting should be programmed because it corresponds directly to the peak measured value.
  29. The correct answer is A (Pulsed field ablation, which uses nonthermal electroporation to achieve tissue-selective injury.): Pulsed field ablation delivers high-voltage electrical pulses that cause nonthermal electroporation of myocardial cells, offering a degree of tissue selectivity that can reduce collateral injury to structures like the esophagus and phrenic nerve compared with thermal energy sources, making it an increasingly used modality for atrial fibrillation ablation in patients such as this CRT candidate. Radiofrequency and cryoballoon ablation both rely on thermal mechanisms, heating and freezing respectively, and surgical epicardial ablation is an invasive open procedure, none of which match the nonthermal, tissue-selective description given.
  30. This is distinct from defibrillation threshold testing, which assesses shock efficacy, from remote monitoring of battery parameters, and from venogram-based coronary sinus imaging used for lead placement, none of which involve electrogram-based substrate identification for ablation.

This does not rely on an external telemetry wand remaining in contact, continuous fluoroscopy during use, or any subcutaneous wire, all of which would be impractical for a permanently implanted system relying on beat-to-beat coordination.

  59.   61. The correct answer is A (Sinus tachycardia, since gradual onset argues against a reentrant ventricular arrhythmia.): Gradual acceleration from a resting sinus rate over several seconds, particularly during exertion, is the classic pattern used by the onset discriminator criterion to identify sinus tachycardia rather than a reentrant ventricular arrhythmia, which typically begins abruptly. Not every rate exceeding the programmed cutoff represents a pathologic ventricular rhythm, onset criteria are applied broadly and are not restricted to irregular rhythms like atrial fibrillation, and gradual onset is a real, expected physiologic pattern rather than an artifact.
  62. The correct answer is A (Supraventricular tachycardia with 1:1 conduction rather than ventricular tachycardia.): The tracing shows nearly identical atrial and ventricular rates throughout the episode, consistent with 1:1 AV conduction, which is a pattern most compatible with a supraventricular tachycardia conducting to the ventricle rather than a primary ventricular arrhythmia. Ventricular tachycardia with retrograde 1:1 conduction can occasionally produce a similar tracing, but it is far less common and additional discriminators would typically be needed to fully exclude it, making the supraventricular explanation the most likely answer here. Atrial fibrillation would show an irregular rather than matched, near-identical rate pattern, and complete heart block would show clearly dissociated rather than matched atrial and ventricular rates.
  63. The correct answer is C (A high percentage morphology match closely resembling the stored sinus template.): This statement is false and is the option to reject, since a high percentage morphology match to the stored sinus template actually supports a supraventricular origin conducted with a similar activation pattern, not ventricular tachycardia. A poor morphology match, reflecting a QRS shape distinctly different from the sinus template, is the finding that supports classifying an episode as ventricular in origin. Identical QRS width to baseline sinus rhythm would similarly argue against VT, and cycle length stability alone is assessed by a different discriminator, not the morphology algorithm.
