  68. The correct answer is A (Schedule elective generator replacement given the elective replacement indicator finding.): Reaching the elective replacement indicator warrants scheduling generator replacement. Urgently extracting the lead system is not warranted since stable lead impedance across six transmissions argues against an acute lead fracture, disabling all detection zones immediately would leave the patient without protection from ventricular arrhythmias during the interim period, and taking no action at all would be inappropriate once the elective replacement indicator has been reached, since replacement should be arranged rather than deferred indefinitely.
  69. The correct answer is C (Reprogram sensing parameters or consider lead repositioning to eliminate T-wave oversensing.): T-wave oversensing causing double-counting of ventricular events and inappropriate shock delivery is best addressed by reprogramming sensing parameters, such as adjusting sensitivity or blanking periods, or in refractory cases considering lead repositioning to reduce the oversensed signal. Increasing shock energy does nothing to address the underlying sensing problem and would not prevent recurrence. Adding a second lead is not a standard corrective measure for this issue, and lowering the detection rate cutoff would worsen rather than improve the situation by increasing sensitivity further.
  70. The correct answer is B (A lead conductor fracture producing intermittent electrical noise misinterpreted as tachycardia.): Nonphysiologic, high-frequency noise with intermittent make-break artifact on the right ventricular lead channel, occurring without corresponding symptoms, is the classic electrical signature of a lead conductor fracture causing intermittent contact disruption that the device misinterprets as a rapid tachyarrhythmia, triggering inappropriate shocks. This pattern is inconsistent with genuine ventricular fibrillation, which would produce a physiologic, organized-appearing electrogram rather than nonphysiologic noise. It is also distinct from typical T-wave oversensing artifact and is not an expected consequence of normal battery depletion.
  71. The correct answer is D (Enable SVT discriminator algorithms such as stability and morphology criteria for the VT zone.): Enabling SVT discriminator algorithms, including stability and morphology criteria, for the VT zone would have allowed the device to recognize the irregular cycle length characteristic of atrial fibrillation and withhold inappropriate therapy despite the rate meeting the VT zone cutoff. Lowering the VT zone rate cutoff would worsen the problem by capturing even more supraventricular rhythms. Disabling the VF zone is unrelated to this issue and would remove needed protection for genuine fast ventricular arrhythmias, and increasing shock energy does not address the underlying rhythm misclassification.
  72. The correct answer is C (An insulation breach, since high-voltage circuit abnormalities predominate while pacing parameters stay normal.): An insulation breach classically produces an abnormally LOW high-voltage impedance (a short circuit allowing current to leak) along with oversensing artifact on the high-voltage circuit, while pacing impedance and thresholds, which depend on the separate low-voltage pace-sense conductor, remain normal. This distinguishes it from a conductor fracture, which classically causes an abnormally HIGH impedance and typically also disrupts pacing parameters. Normal lead aging would not produce these abnormal high-voltage findings, and battery depletion affects voltage output and longevity estimates rather than producing isolated lead impedance abnormalities of this type.
  73. The correct answer is A (Complete hardware extraction, since device pocket infection requires removal of the entire system.): Established guidelines recommend complete removal of the entire device system, including generator and leads, for a confirmed device pocket infection, since localized signs such as erythema and purulent drainage indicate infection extending along the entire device system rather than a superficial process that can be managed with local measures alone. Topical antibiotic ointment and observation alone are inadequate for an established device infection. Reimplanting a new generator into the same infected pocket would only propagate the infection to the replacement hardware.
  74. The correct answer is B (Complete transvenous lead extraction of the entire system along with targeted antibiotics.): Lead-related infective endocarditis with documented vegetations on the intracardiac lead requires complete transvenous extraction of the entire device system in conjunction with targeted antibiotic therapy, since antibiotics alone cannot sterilize infected hardware and leaving any component in place risks persistent bacteremia and treatment failure. Extracting only the generator while leaving infected leads behind would not resolve the underlying source of infection. Immediate contralateral reimplantation without first extracting and clearing the infection risks seeding the new system.
  75. The correct answer is D (Progressive battery or capacitor deterioration warranting closer monitoring or replacement.): A progressive rise in capacitor charge time across successive device checks, from around 8 seconds up to nearly 15 seconds, reflects declining capacitor or battery performance and warrants closer monitoring or consideration of generator replacement before therapy delivery is delayed during a real arrhythmic event. This is not a normal expected variation given the clear upward trend. Improved capacitor efficiency would be reflected by a stable or shortening charge time, not a lengthening one, and lead integrity primarily affects sensing and pacing impedance rather than capacitor charge time.
  76. The correct answer is C (Cardiac perforation by the lead, causing acute pericardial effusion and tamponade.): Acute hypotension and distant heart sounds with a new pericardial effusion showing tamponade physiology immediately after a lead is advanced across the tricuspid valve strongly suggest cardiac perforation by the lead, a recognized mechanical complication of transvenous lead placement. A vasovagal reaction would not typically produce a new pericardial effusion on imaging. Postoperative inflammation develops over a longer time course rather than acutely during the procedure itself, and a pneumothorax would not directly explain a new pericardial effusion with tamponade.
  77. The correct answer is A (Mechanical interference with leaflet coaptation from the lead crossing the tricuspid valve.): Progressive tricuspid regurgitation with the lead directly visualized impinging on leaflet coaptation is a well-recognized mechanism of lead-associated tricuspid regurgitation, caused by mechanical interference with normal valve closure from the lead's course across the tricuspid annulus. This is a distinct, recognized complication rather than an unrelated degenerative process occurring coincidentally. Infective endocarditis is only one of several possible causes of lead-associated regurgitation, not the exclusive one, and excessive lead slack does not reliably improve valve function.
  78. The correct answer is D (Twiddler syndrome, caused by repetitive generator rotation that dislodges the leads.): Twiddler syndrome describes repetitive, often subconscious manipulation and rotation of the generator within its subcutaneous pocket, which progressively coils and retracts the leads, ultimately causing loss of capture or sensing as the leads are pulled back from their intended intracardiac position. This mechanism is distinct from lead fracture caused by external trauma, which would not typically show coiled leads around the generator. It is not a normal expected finding after implant, and while pocket infection can occur separately, coiled leads specifically indicate mechanical twiddling rather than infection.
  79. The correct answer is B (The axillary vein approach, avoiding the costoclavicular space where crush injury occurs.): The axillary vein approach is preferred over a medial subclavian puncture because it avoids passage of the lead through the costoclavicular space between the clavicle and first rib, the site where repetitive mechanical compression classically causes subclavian crush syndrome and lead fracture. A more medial subclavian puncture would increase rather than decrease this crush injury risk. The internal jugular vein is in fact sometimes used for lead placement, and a femoral approach is not standard for routine transvenous ICD implantation.
  80. The correct answer is C (Urgent catheter ablation targeting the ventricular tachycardia substrate causing the storm.): Electrical storm refractory to antiarrhythmic drug therapy is an established indication for urgent catheter ablation targeting the underlying ventricular tachycardia substrate, since eliminating the arrhythmogenic circuit directly addresses the cause of recurrent episodes rather than only suppressing them pharmacologically. Permanently deactivating shock therapies would leave the patient unprotected from a life-threatening arrhythmia. Generator replacement is not indicated based on electrical storm alone without an identified device or battery malfunction, and discontinuing antiarrhythmic medications would likely worsen rather than resolve the storm.
  81. The correct answer is B (The S-ICD lead sits entirely outside the heart and vasculature, parasternal to the sternum.): The defining feature of the subcutaneous ICD is that its lead runs subcutaneously along the left parasternal border with the generator placed laterally, never entering a vein or the heart itself. This avoids all intravascular and intracardiac hardware, unlike a conventional transvenous ICD whose lead travels through the venous system into the right ventricle. Because the lead is entirely extravascular, there is no risk of lead-related venous thrombosis, tricuspid valve interference, or intracardiac lead fracture. This anatomic difference is the basis for most of the S-ICD's advantages and limitations.
  82. The correct answer is D (Implant a separate transvenous or leadless pacemaker, since the S-ICD cannot pace.): The S-ICD has no capability to deliver chronic bradycardia pacing, antitachycardia pacing, or cardiac resynchronization therapy because its lead has no contact with the myocardium. A patient who develops a genuine pacing indication after S-ICD implantation needs a separate pacing system, most often a leadless pacemaker or a transvenous device implanted independently of the existing subcutaneous system. This is one of the central trade-offs against the S-ICD's vascular-sparing design. Device selection at the time of initial implant should anticipate this limitation in patients with borderline conduction disease.
  83. The correct answer is A (The S-ICD can only deliver a high-energy shock, since it lacks pacing capability.): Because the S-ICD lead makes no contact with the myocardium, the device cannot deliver antitachycardia pacing and its only therapy for a detected ventricular arrhythmia is a high-energy defibrillation shock. Patients with a history of recurrent monomorphic VT that responds well to ATP are generally better served by a transvenous system that can attempt painless pacing termination first. This is an important counseling point when choosing between device types for a given patient's arrhythmia substrate. Frequent shocks without an ATP option can meaningfully affect quality of life.
  84. The correct answer is C (It identifies a vector with QRS and T-wave morphology unlikely to cause oversensing.): S-ICD screening exists specifically to find at least one sensing vector, supine and standing, where the QRS-to-T-wave amplitude ratio is favorable enough to avoid double counting or T-wave oversensing, which can otherwise trigger inappropriate shocks. A patient who fails screening in all vectors is not a suitable S-ICD candidate and should be considered for a transvenous system instead. This step has nothing to do with renal function or battery estimation. Postural screening specifically catches morphology changes that occur only with positional shifts in cardiac axis.
  85. The correct answer is B (His young age and long expected lead lifespan favor an extravascular system.): Young patients face many decades of lead exposure, and each transvenous lead revision or extraction carries cumulative vascular and infection risk. An S-ICD avoids placing a permanent lead inside the heart and venous system, which is particularly valuable for someone likely to need multiple generator changes and potential lead-related interventions over a long lifetime. He also has no bradycardia or ATP-responsive arrhythmia indication, removing the two main reasons to prefer a transvenous device. Ventricular fibrillation, the typical arrhythmia in Brugada syndrome, is well treated by shock alone.
  86. The correct answer is A (It avoids central venous access entirely, bypassing her occluded venous pathways.): Because the S-ICD lead is placed subcutaneously along the sternum rather than through a vein, it is an ideal solution for patients whose venous access is compromised by chronic occlusion, prior extractions, or congenital anomalies. This bypasses the need to navigate scarred or occluded central veins altogether. The device does not affect defibrillation shock energy, which is actually higher than most transvenous systems, and it has no antitachycardia pacing capability at all. Venous-access-limited patients are one of the clearest indications for S-ICD in current practice.
  87. The correct answer is D (She has passed screening in at least one vector and may proceed.): S-ICD candidacy requires passing the standardized screening tool in at least one of the three available sensing vectors in both the supine and standing positions, which this patient has achieved in two of three vectors. Failing a single alternate vector while passing the primary and secondary vectors does not disqualify her from implantation. An electrophysiology study is not part of the S-ICD screening pathway and has no role here. Screening is performed on every candidate regardless of syncope history, since its purpose is to prevent postural T-wave oversensing rather than to evaluate syncope.
  88. The correct answer is B (Its lead has no intravascular or intracardiac contact, so fibrous adherence is minimal.): Transvenous leads become densely adherent to venous walls and endocardium over years, which is exactly what makes extraction technically difficult and dangerous, particularly the risk of superior vena cava or myocardial tear. The S-ICD lead lies in a subcutaneous plane with no vascular or cardiac contact, so removal when needed is comparatively straightforward and carries far lower risk of major vascular injury. This is not related to any antibiotic coating or battery chemistry difference. This lower extraction risk is one of the strongest arguments for S-ICD in younger patients anticipating multiple device revisions.
  89. The correct answer is C (The S-ICD provides only brief, fixed-rate backup pacing that cannot sustain prolonged bradycardia.): Some S-ICD models offer a short period of transcutaneous-style backup pacing for roughly thirty seconds immediately following a shock in case of transient post-shock asystole or severe bradycardia, but this feature is limited, non-programmable in rate, and not intended for ongoing bradycardia management. If the bradycardia persists beyond this brief window, the device has no further pacing capability and the patient may need a separate pacing system. This scenario highlights why patients with any risk of significant post-shock bradyarrhythmia require careful consideration before S-ICD selection. It is not a permanent shock-disabling feature.
  90. The correct answer is A (Exercise-induced changes altered the QRS-to-T-wave ratio, causing T-wave oversensing.): Exertion and the accompanying heart rate increase and postural or metabolic changes can transiently alter QRS and T-wave amplitudes even in a vector that passed static preimplant screening, leading to double counting and inappropriate detection. This is a recognized limitation of the S-ICD, since screening cannot fully replicate every physiologic state a patient will experience. Vector reprogramming to an alternate sensing configuration is often the first corrective step. The S-ICD lead cannot migrate into the heart because it is never placed intravascularly, and battery depletion does not typically cause this pattern.
  91. The correct answer is D (The S-ICD generator is generally larger and is placed in a lateral subcutaneous pocket.): The S-ICD generator has historically been larger than contemporary transvenous ICD generators because it must produce a higher-energy shock waveform without the benefit of an intracardiac electrode, and it sits in a lateral subcutaneous pocket along the mid-axillary line rather than a pectoral pocket. In thin patients this can be more noticeable or uncomfortable than a pectoral transvenous device. It is never implanted inside the heart, and only a single generator and a single subcutaneous lead are used. Newer generation devices have reduced this size difference but it remains a consideration in device selection.
  92. The correct answer is C (His complete heart block requires chronic ventricular pacing the S-ICD cannot provide.): A patient with an established indication for chronic bradycardia pacing, such as complete heart block, needs a device capable of continuous ventricular pacing, which the S-ICD structurally cannot deliver since its lead never contacts the myocardium. He would instead need a transvenous ICD with pacing capability or a combination of a leadless or transvenous pacemaker alongside an S-ICD, though the latter combination is uncommon. Neither his ejection fraction, ischemic etiology, nor age is a contraindication to S-ICD in isolation. The pacing requirement is the decisive factor here.
  93. The correct answer is B (Switching is discouraged because the S-ICD cannot deliver antitachycardia pacing at all.): His documented arrhythmia responds well to painless antitachycardia pacing, a therapy the S-ICD cannot deliver because its lead has no myocardial contact. Switching him to an S-ICD would mean every future episode of this monomorphic VT could only be treated with a painful high-energy shock, a significant reduction in quality of life and a step backward from his current effective therapy. Lead lifespan and battery longevity are not determined by generator type in this way and are not the deciding issue here. Patients with ATP-responsive VT are generally poor S-ICD candidates for this reason.
  94. The correct answer is A (An extravascular ICD, a hybrid design combining substernal lead placement with pacing capability.): The extravascular ICD places its lead in the substernal space rather than in a vein or the subcutaneous tissue, giving it proximity to the heart without ever entering the vasculature or cardiac chambers. This substernal position allows the device to deliver low-energy antitachycardia pacing and transient post-shock bradycardia pacing, capabilities the S-ICD lacks, while still avoiding the long-term vascular complications of a transvenous system. It is an implanted device, not a wearable vest, and does not use coronary sinus leads. It represents a genuine hybrid category between transvenous and subcutaneous ICD design.
  95. The correct answer is D (The extravascular ICD can deliver antitachycardia pacing and brief bradycardia pacing.): Because its lead sits in the substernal space in closer proximity to the heart, the extravascular ICD can deliver antitachycardia pacing for monomorphic VT and temporary bradycardia pacing after a shock, capabilities the S-ICD's purely subcutaneous lead cannot provide. It still avoids the venous system and cardiac chambers entirely, preserving the vascular-sparing advantage of the subcutaneous approach. It does not provide true cardiac resynchronization therapy, which still requires a coronary sinus or dedicated left ventricular lead, and it does still require a preimplant screening step similar to the S-ICD. This combination is why it is described as a hybrid device.
  96. The correct answer is C (A possible lead insulation failure given the abrupt drop in impedance.): A lead impedance trend that remains stable for several months and then drops abruptly and steeply, as shown here, is a classic pattern for lead insulation failure, a short circuit that lowers measured impedance, rather than a slow, expected physiologic drift. This finding warrants urgent in-person device interrogation and probably lead replacement or extraction before the lead fails during an actual arrhythmia event. A conductor fracture would instead be expected to raise impedance abruptly, the opposite direction. Battery depletion produces changes in battery voltage and charge time, not this kind of abrupt lead impedance change, and capsule endothelialization affects pacing thresholds gradually over the first weeks after implant, not high-voltage lead impedance months later.
  97. The correct answer is D (The substernal space, anterior to the pericardium and posterior to the sternum.): The extravascular ICD lead is placed in the anterior mediastinum, in the substernal space that lies posterior to the sternum and anterior to the pericardium, accessed via a small subxiphoid incision and a purpose-built delivery sheath. The lead does not enter the pericardial sac, the coronary sinus, or any cardiac chamber, and no venous access is required for the defibrillation lead itself. This extracardiac, extravascular position is what allows the device to avoid the complications associated with both transvenous leads and true epicardial lead placement.
  98. The correct answer is B (It has a shorter track record, with less long-term safety and durability data.): As a newer device category, the extravascular ICD has a much shorter clinical history than either the S-ICD or transvenous ICD systems, so long-term data on lead durability, extraction outcomes, and rare complications such as substernal bleeding or diaphragmatic stimulation are still accumulating. It has shown encouraging early trial results but has not accumulated decades of outcome data, and inappropriate shocks still occur with this platform, just as with other ICD types. Committees adopting new hybrid technology should weigh this evidence gap against the theoretical advantages of the design.
  99. The correct answer is A (Complete extraction of the entire device system, including generator and both leads.): Documented lead vegetation with bacteremia represents definite cardiac implantable electronic device infection with lead endocarditis, and current guidelines mandate complete system removal, meaning the generator and every lead, rather than partial extraction or antibiotic suppression alone. Leaving any hardware in place, including an apparently uninfected lead or a replaced generator, risks persistent or recurrent infection and further embolic events from residual vegetation. Suppressive antibiotics without extraction are reserved only for patients who are not surgical or extraction candidates at all. Reimplantation of a new system is deferred until infection has been adequately treated.
  100. The correct answer is C (Extraction may be considered based on individualized risk, even without current malfunction.): A recalled lead with a known defect that predisposes to future failure is a recognized, though individualized, indication for extraction even when the lead is currently interrogating normally, because the decision balances the ongoing risk of a future life-threatening lead failure against the procedural risk of extraction itself, which rises with lead dwell time. This decision should involve patient-specific factors such as age, dependency on the device, and lead implant duration rather than a blanket rule. Simply capping and abandoning a known defective lead, or extracting no lead ever, ignores this individualized risk assessment that guidelines recommend.
  101. The correct answer is D (An excimer laser sheath, which photoablates fibrous binding along the lead body.): The excimer laser sheath delivers pulsed ultraviolet energy at its tip to photoablate fibrous tissue that has bound the lead to the venous wall or endocardium as the sheath is advanced over the lead, allowing controlled countertraction extraction of chronically implanted leads that fail simple manual traction. Cryoablation and radiofrequency energy are not standard extraction tool mechanisms, and a balloon angioplasty catheter is used for venous stenosis rather than for freeing a bound lead. Laser sheaths, alongside mechanical rotating sheaths, are the mainstay powered tools for extracting leads with significant fibrous adherence.
  102. The correct answer is A (A vascular or myocardial tear, most feared at the superior vena cava.): Acute hypotension with a new pericardial effusion during lead extraction is the classic presentation of a vascular or myocardial tear, most dangerously at the superior vena cava or right atrial junction, and it is a true surgical emergency requiring immediate pericardiocentesis and often emergency sternotomy. This is precisely why every lead extraction procedure with meaningful lead dwell time is performed with cardiac surgical backup and perfusion support immediately available, exactly as described in this scenario. A vasovagal response, contrast allergy, or pulmonary embolism would not typically produce a new pericardial effusion on echocardiography.
  103. The correct answer is B (The mechanical sheath uses a rotating cutting tip, while the laser uses light energy.): Powered mechanical sheaths use a rotating or oscillating cutting mechanism at the sheath tip to mechanically dissect fibrous binding, while excimer laser sheaths use pulsed ultraviolet light to photoablate the same tissue, and operators may choose between them or use them sequentially based on binding site characteristics such as calcification. Neither tool relies on cryotherapy, and both require fluoroscopic guidance throughout the procedure to track sheath advancement safely. Both tool types can be used for leads in any cardiac chamber, and the choice is based on operator experience and binding characteristics rather than lead location alone.
  104. The correct answer is C (Access the contralateral side, or extract the occluding lead if the old side is preferred.): When ipsilateral venous access is occluded, the operator can either implant the new system through the contralateral vein, leaving the old functioning lead undisturbed, or extract the occluding lead first if there is a specific reason to preserve that same-side access, such as an existing functioning generator pocket. Extracting a currently normal-functioning lead purely because of occlusion, without another indication, is not routinely necessary and adds unnecessary extraction risk. Abandoning device therapy is inappropriate when reasonable alternative access exists, and proceeding without imaging guidance through a known occlusion is unsafe.
  105. The correct answer is A (Obtain blood cultures and arrange evaluation for pocket infection or erosion.): Pocket erythema, fluctuance, and skin erosion with drainage after a device procedure are classic findings of cardiac implantable electronic device pocket infection, and any suspicion of this diagnosis warrants blood cultures and prompt evaluation, since pocket infection frequently requires complete system extraction rather than local wound care alone. Skin erosion in particular is considered evidence of infection until proven otherwise, because the device is effectively exposed to the external environment. Reassurance, antihistamines, or an unrelated device programming change would all delay recognition of a potentially serious infection requiring urgent management.
  106. The correct answer is D (Purulent drainage from the incision accompanied by fever and tenderness.): Purulent drainage combined with systemic signs such as fever and localized tenderness is a highly specific combination for true device pocket infection rather than normal postoperative healing, which typically shows resolving bruising or minimal clear serous fluid without fever. Bruising alone or a clean, well-healed incision are reassuring findings consistent with normal recovery. This distinction matters because a genuine pocket infection generally requires complete hardware extraction, while normal postoperative changes need only observation. Prompt recognition of purulent drainage should trigger blood cultures and infectious disease and electrophysiology evaluation without delay.
  107. The correct answer is C (Transesophageal echocardiography to evaluate for lead vegetation or endocarditis.): Persistent Staphylococcus aureus bacteremia without an identified alternative source in a patient with an implanted cardiac device is a strong indication for transesophageal echocardiography to look for lead vegetation, since even a clinically silent, normal-appearing pocket does not exclude lead endocarditis. Transesophageal imaging is more sensitive than transthoracic echocardiography for detecting lead-associated vegetations. Stopping antibiotics based on a normal pocket exam alone would be inappropriate given persistent bacteremia, and reprogramming energy output has no role in infection evaluation. If vegetation is found, complete system extraction is generally required regardless of pocket appearance.
  108. The correct answer is B (Proceed to transesophageal echocardiography for improved sensitivity for lead vegetations.): Transesophageal echocardiography has substantially higher sensitivity than transthoracic imaging for detecting lead vegetations and valve involvement, and an inconclusive transthoracic study in the setting of ongoing high clinical suspicion should prompt transesophageal evaluation rather than simply repeating the less sensitive test or waiting a month. A chest X-ray can show lead position or fracture but cannot identify vegetations, and lead impedance reflects electrical integrity rather than infection. Prompt transesophageal imaging can meaningfully change management by confirming the need for complete system extraction.
  109. The correct answer is D (Recommend complete system extraction, since antibiotics alone rarely eradicate lead infection.): Confirmed lead-related infective endocarditis with vegetation almost always requires complete removal of the entire device system, generator and all leads, because antibiotics alone rarely sterilize a vegetation adherent to intracardiac hardware and relapse or embolic events remain likely if the infected material is left in place. Surgical or extraction risk should be carefully assessed and optimized rather than used as a reason to abandon extraction entirely, and a multidisciplinary heart team approach is often used for higher-risk patients. Antibiotic suppression alone is reserved only for patients truly unable to undergo any extraction procedure.
  110. The correct answer is C (The presence and extent of documented valvular or lead endocarditis.): Antibiotic duration after device extraction is guided primarily by whether there is documented endocarditis and its extent, since patients with confirmed lead or valve vegetation typically require a longer course, often four to six weeks, of targeted intravenous antibiotics after source control, compared with a shorter course for isolated pocket infection without endocarditis. Neither the device manufacturer nor lead implant duration determines antibiotic length, and insurance logistics, while a practical consideration, should not drive the clinical duration decision. Infectious disease consultation is standard for tailoring this decision to the specific organism and extent of infection.
  111. The correct answer is A (An antibiotic-eluting envelope placed around the generator at the time of implant.): A large randomized trial demonstrated that an absorbable antibiotic-eluting envelope, which releases antimicrobial agents locally around the generator over several weeks after implant, significantly reduces the risk of major device infection, particularly in higher-risk procedures such as generator replacement, revision, or upgrade. Extended systemic postoperative antibiotics beyond the standard perioperative dose have not shown similar benefit and add unnecessary antibiotic exposure. Pocket size and arbitrary procedure delay are not established evidence-based infection prevention strategies. The envelope is now commonly used in higher-risk device procedures based on this trial evidence.
  112. The correct answer is B (Staphylococcus species, including both Staphylococcus aureus and coagulase-negative staphylococci.): Staphylococcus aureus and coagulase-negative staphylococci together account for the large majority of cardiac implantable electronic device infections in published series, reflecting skin flora introduced at the time of implant or subsequent hematogenous seeding. Staphylococcus aureus infections tend to present more acutely and are more likely to cause bacteremia and systemic illness, while coagulase-negative staphylococcal infections can be more indolent. Pseudomonas, Candida, and Mycobacterium tuberculosis are all recognized but much less common causes of device infection. Empiric antibiotic coverage for suspected device infection should reliably include activity against staphylococcal species.
  113. The correct answer is C (Bridge with a temporary pacing system, then reimplant at a new site after clearance.): A pacemaker-dependent patient undergoing extraction for infection typically needs temporary pacing support, often with a temporary transvenous wire or, in select cases, a temporary leadless device, to bridge the gap between extraction and definitive reimplantation. The new permanent system should generally be implanted at a different anatomic site once infection markers have cleared and an appropriate observation period has passed, rather than reusing the infected pocket the same day or the identical entry site. Simply withholding pacing support entirely is not safe in a pacing-dependent patient. Reimplantation timing is individualized based on the extent of infection and clinical response.
  114. The correct answer is A (Early subclinical fluid accumulation suggesting impending heart failure decompensation.): A rising composite risk score driven by falling intrathoracic impedance, which reflects increasing lung fluid, together with declining activity and heart rate variability, is the pattern that machine-learning-based remote monitoring algorithms are specifically designed to detect as an early warning of subclinical fluid accumulation before a patient develops overt symptoms of heart failure decompensation. This allows earlier outpatient intervention such as diuretic adjustment, potentially avoiding hospitalization. This pattern is not consistent with battery depletion, benign seasonal variation, or a lead fracture, none of which would produce this specific coordinated multivariate trend.
  115. The correct answer is D (Implantation of an insertable cardiac loop recorder for long-term rhythm monitoring.): For unexplained syncope that remains undiagnosed after appropriate initial short-term monitoring, an insertable cardiac loop recorder provides continuous rhythm monitoring for up to several years, dramatically increasing the chance of capturing an arrhythmia during a rare, unpredictable event. This diagnostic yield far exceeds that of repeating a brief external monitor, which is unlikely to capture such an infrequent event. Proceeding to permanent pacing without documented bradyarrhythmia would be premature, and discharging her without further workup risks missing a serious, potentially recurrent arrhythmic cause of her syncope. Loop recorders are a first-line tool for exactly this clinical picture.
  116. The correct answer is B (Extended rhythm monitoring with an insertable loop recorder to detect occult atrial fibrillation.): In cryptogenic stroke, paroxysmal atrial fibrillation is a leading suspected but frequently occult cause, and studies have shown that extended monitoring with an insertable loop recorder detects atrial fibrillation in a meaningful proportion of patients who had no arrhythmia identified on brief inpatient telemetry. Finding atrial fibrillation would change management toward anticoagulation for secondary stroke prevention. Stopping the workup after only forty-eight hours of telemetry would miss most paroxysmal atrial fibrillation, empiric anticoagulation without a confirmed indication carries unnecessary bleeding risk, and left atrial appendage closure is not appropriate without a confirmed atrial fibrillation diagnosis first.
  117. The correct answer is A (It is inserted through a small subcutaneous incision over the left chest wall.): The insertable cardiac loop recorder is a small, leadless device placed subcutaneously, typically over the left parasternal region, through a minimal incision made under local anesthesia in an outpatient or clinic setting, often using a dedicated insertion tool that shortens procedure time considerably. It requires no venous access, no fluoroscopy, no general anesthesia, and no entry into any cardiac chamber or the pericardial space. This simple, low-risk insertion technique is part of what makes it practical for widespread use in evaluating unexplained syncope or cryptogenic stroke.
  118. The correct answer is D (The loop recorder's multiyear battery life captures infrequent events an external monitor would miss.): An insertable loop recorder has a battery life of several years and continuously monitors rhythm automatically, giving it a far greater chance of capturing a rare, unpredictable event than a wearable external monitor, which is typically worn for only days to a few weeks and can fail if not applied correctly or if the patient forgets to activate it. External monitors are not inherently more accurate for atrial fibrillation detection, and loop recorders automatically detect and store many arrhythmias without requiring patient activation, though patients can also manually trigger recordings during symptoms. This extended passive monitoring window is the central diagnostic advantage.
  119. The correct answer is B (A reduction in staff time spent reviewing clinically insignificant, low-priority alerts.): Machine-learning triage algorithms in remote monitoring are designed to sift through a high volume of transmissions, most of which are clinically insignificant, and prioritize the smaller subset most likely to require action, thereby reducing the alert fatigue and staff burden associated with manually reviewing every single transmission. This does not mean generating more raw alerts, and it does not eliminate the need for human clinical judgment, since a trained clinician still must review and act on flagged events. No algorithm can guarantee perfect sensitivity for every possible arrhythmic event, and overstating that would be clinically misleading.
  120. The correct answer is C (To enable earlier outpatient intervention before overt decompensation requires hospitalization.): Multivariate, machine-learning-derived heart failure risk scores combining several device-based sensors are intended to identify patients trending toward decompensation days before they develop overt symptoms, allowing clinicians to intervene earlier with outpatient measures such as diuretic adjustment or closer follow-up, with the goal of preventing or reducing hospitalization. This technology is not designed to replace in-person follow-up entirely, nor is its purpose related to battery replacement timing. While atrial fibrillation detection is a valuable separate function of many devices, it is not the specific purpose of this composite heart failure risk algorithm. Reducing heart failure hospitalization is the primary intended clinical benefit.
  121. The correct answer is A (Implantable loop recorder for extended ambulatory rhythm monitoring.): Sudden syncope without prodrome in a patient with structural heart disease and reduced ejection fraction raises strong concern for a ventricular arrhythmia, and an implantable loop recorder provides prolonged monitoring to capture an infrequent event. Tilt table testing evaluates for a neurally mediated cause, which is less likely given his cardiac substrate and the abrupt, prodrome-free presentation. Carotid sinus massage and electroencephalography address different mechanisms and would not address the underlying arrhythmic risk suggested by his reduced ejection fraction.
  122. The correct answer is C (Extended ambulatory ECG monitoring during a symptomatic period.): Frequent brief palpitations with a normal resting ECG and echocardiogram are best evaluated with ambulatory monitoring timed to capture symptoms, establishing a documented rhythm correlation before committing to therapy. Empiric beta-blockade without a diagnosis risks masking a treatable arrhythmia or missing the correct target. Electrophysiology study and ablation are premature without first identifying the culprit rhythm, and exercise testing is unlikely to reproduce symptoms that are not exertional.
  123. The correct answer is B (Implantation of a primary prevention implantable cardioverter-defibrillator.): A persistently reduced ejection fraction of 30% despite adequate guideline-directed medical therapy meets established criteria for primary prevention ICD implantation to reduce sudden cardiac death risk. The correct answer is D (Honor the request by deactivating shock therapy while continuing pacing.): Deactivating defibrillation therapy at a competent patient's informed request is an ethically and legally accepted withdrawal of unwanted treatment, distinct from euthanasia, and pacing can be preserved separately since it serves a different symptomatic purpose. Refusing the request disregards patient autonomy and mischaracterizes the ethical framework. The correct answer is B (Type 1 pattern, the only pattern considered diagnostic.): A coved ST-segment elevation of at least 2 mm followed by a negative T wave in the right precordial leads defines the type 1 Brugada pattern, which is the only pattern considered diagnostic of Brugada syndrome without additional criteria. This is not simply a vasovagal phenomenon unrelated to the underlying channelopathy. While dehydration can occur with fever, it does not explain the specific ECG-related arrhythmic mechanism, and fever does have a well-documented direct effect on cardiac sodium channel function.

A normal resting electrocardiogram and an elevated body mass index do not carry the same implication for an inherited genetic cause.
  187. The correct answer is C (Reassess ejection fraction after at least three months of optimized guideline-directed therapy.): Newly diagnosed nonischemic dilated cardiomyopathy should first be treated with optimized guideline-directed medical therapy for at least three months before reassessing ejection fraction, since meaningful recovery can occur and unnecessarily early defibrillator implantation should be avoided. Implanting immediately assumes no possibility of improvement, which is inaccurate for many newly diagnosed patients. Waiting a full two years would be excessive, and basing the decision solely on symptom class ignores the importance of reassessing ejection fraction after therapy.
  188. The correct answer is A (A high-fat, very low-carbohydrate diet combined with a prolonged fasting period beforehand.): A high-fat, very low-carbohydrate diet with an extended preceding fast shifts myocardial metabolism away from glucose utilization, suppressing normal physiologic myocardial fluorodeoxyglucose uptake and allowing abnormal focal uptake from active sarcoid inflammation to be distinguished on cardiac PET. A high-carbohydrate meal would instead increase physiologic myocardial glucose uptake and obscure pathologic findings. Skipping dietary preparation or eating a standard balanced meal beforehand would compromise image interpretability.
  189. The correct answer is D (Substrate-based mapping to identify scar borders and late potentials within the granulomatous region.): Cardiac sarcoidosis produces patchy granulomatous scar that supports reentrant ventricular tachycardia, so substrate-based mapping to identify scar borders, late potentials, and other abnormal electrograms is the standard approach for guiding ablation in this disease. Sarcoidosis frequently involves the ventricular myocardium, not only the atrium. Relying only on activation mapping during sinus rhythm without pacing maneuvers, or performing empiric ablation without electroanatomic guidance, would not adequately localize the arrhythmogenic substrate.
  190. The correct answer is C (Implantable cardioverter defibrillator placement given his history of resuscitated cardiac arrest.): A patient with short QT syndrome who has survived a cardiac arrest has established secondary prevention indication for implantable cardioverter defibrillator placement, since pharmacologic therapy alone does not reliably prevent recurrent life-threatening arrhythmia in this channelopathy. Beta blocker monotherapy without a device would leave him inadequately protected. Observation alone is inappropriate given his documented life-threatening event, and sinoatrial node ablation is not an accepted treatment for this condition.
  191. The correct answer is B (Combined inferior and lateral lead distribution of the early repolarization pattern.): An early repolarization pattern that spans both the inferior and lateral leads has been associated with higher arrhythmic risk in patients resuscitated from ventricular fibrillation compared with a pattern confined to a single lead group. Isolated involvement of one anterior precordial lead is a much lower-risk and less specific pattern. Confinement to the right precordial leads or an ascending pattern limited to the limb leads do not describe the higher-risk inferolateral distribution being asked about here.
  192. The correct answer is A (Cardioselective beta blockers, which carry a more favorable fetal safety profile overall.): Cardioselective beta blockers have an extensive record of relative safety in pregnancy and are generally preferred as an initial antiarrhythmic choice for symptomatic premature ventricular contractions when lifestyle measures alone are insufficient. Amiodarone carries substantial fetal thyroid and neurodevelopmental risk despite its efficacy. Dofetilide lacks established long-term pregnancy safety data, and combining sotalol with amiodarone increases risk without being an appropriate first-line strategy in this setting.
  193. The correct answer is C (Low molecular weight heparin, avoiding warfarin because of first-trimester teratogenic risk.): Low molecular weight heparin is preferred for anticoagulation during early pregnancy because warfarin is teratogenic when used in the first trimester and can cause a recognized embryopathy. Continuing warfarin unchanged through this period would expose the fetus to that known risk. Direct oral anticoagulants lack sufficient safety data in pregnancy and are generally avoided. Withholding anticoagulation entirely would leave a patient with an elevated stroke risk score inadequately protected.
  194. The correct answer is B (A common benign early repolarization variant frequently observed in trained athletes.): J-point and ST segment elevation in the lateral precordial leads is a frequent, typically benign electrocardiographic finding in trained endurance athletes and does not by itself indicate an underlying channelopathy or structural disease when the heart is structurally normal and the patient is asymptomatic. It does not meet diagnostic criteria for Brugada syndrome, which requires a distinct coved ST pattern. It is not evidence of arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy, and it does not warrant automatic disqualification from competition.
  195. The correct answer is D (Exertional syncope together with a family history of unexplained sudden death before age 50.): Exertional syncope combined with a family history of unexplained sudden death at a young age is a recognized red flag combination that should prompt further cardiac evaluation before athletic clearance, since it raises concern for an underlying inherited arrhythmia syndrome or cardiomyopathy. A resting heart rate of 48 beats per minute is a common and expected finding in trained athletes. Mild respiratory sinus arrhythmia and a single isolated premature atrial contraction are both benign incidental findings that do not warrant further workup on their own.
  196. The correct answer is A (Transbaffle puncture to access the pulmonary venous atrium when standard routes are blocked.): When the surgical baffle in a Fontan circulation obstructs standard venous access to the pulmonary venous atrium, a transbaffle puncture is often required to reach ablation targets located in that chamber. Standard femoral venous access alone does not reliably reach all Fontan atrial targets given the altered anatomy. Transseptal puncture from the coronary sinus is not the conventional Fontan access route, and epicardial access is not the primary strategy for reaching this specific intracardiac target.
  197. The correct answer is A (It should be included as it may guide both symptom management and outflow-directed therapy.): A markedly elevated Valsalva-provoked gradient in a symptomatic patient is clinically meaningful even when the resting gradient is normal, since provocable obstruction can explain exertional symptoms and may guide decisions about outflow-directed pharmacologic or septal reduction therapy. Ignoring provocable gradients would overlook a genuine contributor to his dyspnea. A single provocable gradient finding does not automatically mandate urgent surgery without considering his overall symptom burden and response to medical therapy, and provocable gradients are well established to correlate with symptoms in many patients.
  198. The correct answer is D (Lamin A/C variants carry disproportionately high arrhythmic risk independent of ejection fraction.): Lamin A/C cardiomyopathy is well recognized to produce ventricular arrhythmia and sudden death risk that is disproportionate to the degree of ejection fraction reduction, so progressive conduction disease and nonsustained ventricular tachycardia in this genotype warrant earlier defibrillator consideration than standard ejection fraction-based thresholds. Describing this genotype as low risk is inaccurate. Conduction disease in these carriers is a recognized precursor to ventricular arrhythmia, and ejection fraction alone does not fully capture risk across all genetic cardiomyopathies.
  199. The correct answer is B (Histologic evidence of extracardiac sarcoidosis together with a compatible cardiac imaging abnormality.): Expert consensus diagnostic criteria allow a diagnosis of probable cardiac sarcoidosis when histologically confirmed extracardiac sarcoidosis is present alongside a compatible cardiac abnormality such as unexplained ventricular tachycardia and patchy late gadolinium enhancement, even without a positive cardiac biopsy. A nondiagnostic biopsy alone does not exclude the diagnosis given the disease's patchy distribution. Isolated ventricular tachycardia without supporting imaging or tissue findings, or entirely normal imaging studies, would not support this diagnosis.
  200. The correct answer is B (Complex venous baffles that separate systemic and pulmonary venous pathways requiring baffle puncture.): The Mustard procedure reroutes systemic and pulmonary venous return using intra-atrial baffles, and this altered, compartmentalized anatomy often requires baffle puncture to access the chamber where the arrhythmogenic substrate lies during electroanatomic mapping and ablation. The anatomy is distinctly abnormal rather than a normal biatrial arrangement. Venous access routes to the atria are not entirely absent, since ablation is being performed, and no procedure in this complex anatomy guarantees freedom from arrhythmia recurrence.
